**5.33.12**  Since Sañjaya has returned from the Pāṇḍavas, my mind is not fully at rest; all my senses have gone out of their natural state — my anxiety today is only this: what will he say?

**5.33.13**  Vidura said: 'Sleeplessness visits the weak man who is attacked by the strong, one who lacks means, one whose wealth has been taken, one tormented by desire, and a thief.'

**5.33.14**  O lord of men, I hope you are not touched by these great faults; I hope you are not burning with longing for the wealth of others.

**5.33.15**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: 'I wish to hear your righteous words of supreme well-being, for in this lineage of royal sages you alone are acknowledged as truly wise.'

**5.33.16**  Vidura said: 'One who is devoted to what is commendable and does not pursue what is censured, who is not a skeptic but full of faith — this is the mark of a wise man.'

**5.33.17**  He whom anger, elation, arrogance, false modesty, vanity, and self-conceit do not drag away from his purposes — he is truly called a wise man.

**5.33.18**  He whose intentions and deliberated counsel others do not know, but only learn of what he has already accomplished — he is truly called a wise man.

**5.33.19**  He whose purposes are not obstructed by cold, heat, fear, pleasure, prosperity, or adversity — he is truly called a wise man.

**5.33.20**  He whose active intelligence follows righteousness and worldly gain, and who chooses wealth through proper desire — he is truly called a wise man.

**5.33.21**  The wise undertake tasks according to their ability and execute them according to their ability; they despise nothing, O bull of the Bharatas.

**5.33.22**  He comprehends quickly, listens patiently, and having understood the matter pursues it free from selfish desire; he does not interfere in another's affairs unbidden — this is the foremost wisdom of a wise man.

**5.33.23**  Men of wise intellect do not desire what is unattainable, do not grieve over what is lost, and are not bewildered in times of calamity.

**5.33.24**  He who sets out only after deliberating fully, who does not halt midway in an undertaking, who wastes no time and is master of himself — he is truly called a wise man.

**5.33.25**  The wise delight in noble actions, perform deeds that bring prosperity, and do not envy what is beneficial to others, O bull of the Bharatas.

**5.33.26**  He who does not exult at personal honour and is not distressed by dishonour, remaining unshakeable like a deep pool of the Gaṅgā — he is called a wise man.

**5.33.27**  A man who knows the true nature of all beings, who understands the right application of all actions, and who knows the proper means among men — he is called a wise man.

**5.33.28**  He who is eloquent in speech, a vivid narrator, gifted with inference, possessed of ready insight, and able to expound a text swiftly — he is truly called a wise man.

**5.33.29**  He whose learning is in accord with wisdom and whose wisdom is in accord with learning, and who does not transgress the boundaries of noble conduct — he attains the designation of wise man.

**5.33.30**  One who is unlearned yet arrogant, one who is poor yet haughty, and one who seeks wealth without effort — the learned call him a fool.

**5.33.31**  He who abandons his own interest to pursue another's, and he who acts deceitfully in the name of friendship — such a one is called a fool.

**5.33.32**  He who desires those who do not desire him, who hates those who do desire him, and who harbours enmity toward the powerful — they call him a man of foolish mind.

**5.33.33**  He who makes a friend of an enemy, who hates and injures a friend, and who undertakes wicked actions — they call him a man of foolish mind.

**5.33.34**  He who entangles his tasks in endless complications, who hesitates about everything, and who takes a long time over what should be done quickly — he is a fool, O bull of the Bharatas.

**5.33.35**  He who enters without being invited, speaks at length without being asked, and places trust in the vigilant — such a fool is the lowest of men.

**5.33.36**  He who blames another for a fault while committing the same himself, and he who grows angry though he has no power — he is the most foolish of men.

**5.33.37**  He who does not know his own strength, who, devoid of righteousness and wealth, desires what cannot be obtained while doing nothing — he is called one of foolish intellect in this world.

**5.33.38**  O king, he who instructs one who is not a worthy pupil, who attends upon a worthless person, and who serves a miser — they call him a man of foolish mind.

**5.33.39**  He who, having attained great wealth, learning, or lordship, moves about without arrogance — he is called a wise man.

**5.33.40**  He who eats the finest food alone, who has a fine dwelling all to himself, and does not share with his dependants — who is more cruel than he?

**5.33.41**  One alone commits the sins, yet a great multitude enjoys the fruit; the enjoyers are freed, but the doer is tainted by the fault.

**5.33.42**  An arrow shot by an archer may or may not slay one man; but intelligence unleashed by an intelligent man can destroy a whole kingdom together with its king.

**5.33.43**  Having resolved the one, determine the two; bring the three under control with the four; having conquered the five and understood the six, abandon the seven and be happy.

**5.33.44**  Poison kills one person; a weapon kills one person; but a breach of counsel kills a king together with his whole kingdom and people.

**5.33.45**  One should not enjoy sweet things alone, nor deliberate on important matters alone, nor travel a road alone, nor stay awake alone among those who sleep.

**5.33.46**  O king, that which you fail to understand — the one without a second — is truth, which is the stairway to heaven, like a boat across the ocean.

**5.33.47**  There is one fault in those who are patient, and a second is not found in them: people take one who is endowed with patience to be powerless.

**5.33.48**  Dharma alone is the supreme good; patience alone is the highest peace; knowledge alone is the supreme vision; non-violence alone brings happiness.

**5.33.49**  The earth swallows these two as a serpent swallows creatures that dwell in holes: a king who does not resist his foes, and a brahmin who does not go abroad to study.

**5.33.50**  A man who performs two actions shines in this world: speaking no harsh word, and not seeking favour from the wicked.

**5.33.51**  O tiger among men, these two act according to another's disposition: women toward those who love them, and the world toward those who honour it.

**5.33.52**  These two are sharp thorns that wither the body: one who is poor yet consumed by desires, and one who is powerless yet given to anger.

**5.33.53**  O king, these two men stand above heaven itself: the mighty one endowed with patience, and the poor man who is generous in giving.

**5.33.54**  With regard to wealth lawfully acquired, two transgressions should be understood: giving it to an unworthy recipient, and withholding it from a worthy one.

**5.33.55**  O bull of the Bharatas, three standards are spoken of for men; those who know the Vedas understand them as the inferior, the middling, and the best.

**5.33.56**  O king, men are of three kinds — superior, inferior, and middling; one should employ them properly in tasks of three corresponding kinds.

**5.33.57**  O king, only three are without independent wealth: a wife, a slave, and a son; whatever they acquire belongs to the one to whom they belong.

**5.33.58**  Four things a king of great power should know and avoid, say the wise: he should not deliberate in counsel with those of little understanding, nor with those who are dilatory, nor with the slothful, nor with wandering minstrels.

**5.33.59**  Dear Dhṛtarāṣṭra, let these four dwell in your house, in accordance with the householder's duty for one blessed with prosperity: an aged kinsman fallen on hard times, a friend of noble birth who is poor, and a childless sister.

**5.33.60**  O great king, Bṛhaspati told Indra, lord of the thirty gods, four things that bear immediate fruit — hear these from me now.

**5.33.61**  They are: the resolve of the gods, the majesty of the wise, the humility of those accomplished in knowledge, and the destruction of those who commit wicked deeds.

**5.33.62**  O bull of the Bharatas, five fires must be tended by a man with great care: father, mother, fire, the self, and the teacher.

**5.33.63**  He who honours these five alone attains glory in the world: the gods, the ancestors, men, mendicants, and guests as the fifth.

**5.33.64**  Five will follow you wherever you go: friends, enemies, those who are neutral, those on whom one depends, and those who depend on one.

**5.33.65**  If even one of the five senses of a mortal man has a breach, his wisdom drains away through it as water from a hole at the foot of a water-bag.

**5.33.66**  Six faults must be abandoned by a man who desires prosperity in this world: sleep, drowsiness, fear, anger, laziness, and procrastination.

**5.33.67**  A man should abandon these six as one abandons a broken boat on the ocean: a teacher who does not impart instruction, and a priest who does not study —

**5.33.68**  — a king who does not protect his people, a wife who speaks unkindly, a cowherd who desires the village, and a barber who desires the forest.

**5.33.69**  Yet these six qualities a man should never abandon at any time: truth, generosity, diligence, freedom from envy, patience, and steadfast courage.

**5.33.70**  He who masters within himself these six that are ever-present — he who has conquered his senses — is not afflicted by sins, much less by misfortunes.

**5.33.71**  These six live off six others; a seventh is not found: thieves live off the careless, physicians live off the sick —

**5.33.72**  — women of loose conduct live off those consumed by desire, officiating priests live off those who perform sacrifices, a king lives off those who are quarrelsome, and the wise always live off fools.

**5.33.73**  Seven faults, which give rise to vices, should always be abandoned by a king; it is by these that kings, even well-established ones, mostly meet their ruin.

**5.33.74**  Women, dice, hunting, drinking, harsh speech as the fifth, great severity of punishment, and corruption of wealth — these are the seven.

**5.33.75**  There are eight forewarning signs of a man who is about to perish: first, he hates brahmins, and he quarrels with brahmins.

**5.33.76**  He seizes the property of brahmins, he desires to slay brahmins, he takes delight in reviling them and does not rejoice in their praise.

**5.33.77**  He does not think of them in times of duty, and when asked he responds with envy. A wise man, having understood these faults with his intelligence, should abandon them.

**5.33.78**  O Bharata, these eight are the fresh butter of joy — they are seen to be present in life, and each one brings great happiness.

**5.33.79**  The coming together with friends, the great arrival of wealth, the embrace of a son, and the union in conjugal love.

**5.33.80**  Sweet conversation at the right moment, reverence among one's own people, the attainment of what is desired, and honour in a public assembly.

**5.33.81**  This dwelling of nine gates, supported by three pillars, witnessed by five, presided over by the knower of the field — he who knows it is the supreme sage.

**5.33.82**  Ten do not know dharma, O Dhṛtarāṣṭra — hear them: the intoxicated, the heedless, the mad, the exhausted, the angry, the hungry.

**5.33.83**  The one in haste, the coward, the greedy, and the lustful — those are the ten. Therefore a wise man should not fall into these states.

**5.33.84**  On this very point they cite this ancient tale, the song sung by the lord of the asuras, Sudhanvan, for the sake of his son.

**5.33.85**  The king who abandons desire and anger, who places wealth in a worthy vessel, who is discerning, learned, and swift in action — him all the world regards as an authority.

**5.33.86**  He knows how to inspire confidence in people; on those whose faults are known he inflicts punishment; he knows both measure and forbearance — such a one is graced by Śrī in her fullness.

**5.33.87**  He does not despise even the very weak; vigilant, he manages his enemy with forethought; he does not relish conflict with the powerful; yet he who acts with valour at the right time — he is the wise one.

**5.33.88**  Having encountered calamity, he is never shaken; vigilant, he seeks out effort; the self-conqueror endures suffering in its time — the burden-bearer whose enemies are thus vanquished.

**5.33.89**  Fruitless absence from home, alliance with the wicked, violation of another's wife, deceit, theft, slander, drinking wine — he who does not resort to these is always happy.

**5.33.90**  He does not undertake enterprises in anger; when asked, he speaks only the truth; he does not relish dispute over a trivial matter; nor, when not honoured, does the undeluded one grow angry.

**5.33.91**  He who neither envies nor lacks compassion; who, when weak, does not act as guarantor; who says nothing excessive and tolerates dispute — such a one wins praise everywhere.

**5.33.92**  He who never adopts an arrogant demeanour; who does not boast over others even of his own valour; who even when overcome says nothing harsh — people always hold such a one dear.

**5.33.93**  He does not rekindle an enmity that has been pacified; he does not rise to arrogance nor sink to abasement; he does not harbour anger saying 'I am in distress' — him they call foremost, the one of noble character.

**5.33.94**  He truly does not exult in his own happiness; he is not pleased at another's suffering; having given, he does not afterwards repent — the good man of noble character does not boast.

**5.33.95**  He who, knowing high and low, seeks to honour the customs of the land, the conventions of the time, and the duties of caste — he, wherever he goes, always exercises lordship over the multitude.

**5.33.96**  Hypocrisy, delusion, envy, wicked deeds, what is hateful to kings, slander, enmity with groups, and dispute with the intoxicated, the mad, and the wicked — the prudent man who avoids these is foremost.

**5.33.97**  Self-restraint, purity, worship of the gods, auspicious rites, expiation, and the various observances of the world — he who performs these daily: the gods prosper his undertakings.

**5.33.98**  He contracts marriage with equals, not with inferiors; with equals he maintains friendship, dealings, and conversation; and he places before him those distinguished by virtue — the policies of such a wise man are truly well-guided.

**5.33.99**  He eats moderately, having distributed to his dependants; he sleeps moderately though his work is immeasurable; being asked, he gives even to enemies — misfortunes forsake that self-possessed man.

**5.33.100**  He whose intended and accomplished deeds no other persons know at all, whose counsel, once kept secret and properly executed — no affair of his, however small, goes amiss.

**5.33.101**  He who is devoted to the peace of all beings, truthful, gentle, generous, and pure of heart — he shines out exceedingly among his kinsmen, like a great gem of purest breed, radiant.

**5.33.102**  The man who is deeply ashamed before himself becomes the teacher of all the world; of infinite lustre, cheerful of mind, composed — he shines by his own radiance like the sun.

**5.33.103**  Born in the forest, the sons of King Pāṇḍu — that king consumed by a curse — those five, each equal to Indra, were raised and trained by you yourself as children, O son of Ambikā; they abide by your command.

**5.33.104**  Having given them their rightful kingdom, O father, you shall live happy, rejoicing with your sons; neither among the gods nor among men, O lord of kings, shall you be found wanting.

**5.34.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Tell me what course of action you perceive for one who is awake and burning with anxiety — for you, dear one, are our expert in dharma and worldly affairs, and you are pure.

**5.34.2**  Instruct me properly, O Vidura; tell me with full wisdom everything concerning Ajātaśatru — whatever you consider wholesome, O man of undaunted spirit; speak what will bring welfare to the Kurus.

**5.34.3**  Fearing evil and seeing only evil, I ask you with a troubled mind, O sage. Tell me everything exactly as it is — everything that Ajātaśatru has in mind.

**5.34.4**  Vidura said: Whether it be good or evil, whether hateful or pleasing, one should speak that unasked to one whose ruin one does not wish.

**5.34.5**  Therefore, O king, wishing welfare for the Kurus, I shall speak to you words that are beneficial and righteous — hear them from me as I speak.

**5.34.6**  O Bharata, those deeds which, though falsely undertaken, might succeed without application of the proper means — do not set your mind upon them.

**5.34.7**  Likewise, O king, a deed ordained by application of means may still not succeed; the intelligent man should not weary his mind over that either.

**5.34.8**  In undertakings that have consequences, one should consider those consequences; having deliberated, one should act — one should not proceed with haste.

**5.34.9**  Having looked at the consequences and the fruits of actions, and at his own capacity to rise to the task, the steadfast man should act or not act accordingly.

**5.34.10**  He who does not know the measure in matters of maintenance, growth and decline, treasury, subjects, and the rod of punishment — such a one does not endure in kingship.

**5.34.11**  But he who observes these measures as described, engaged in the knowledge of dharma and worldly affairs — he attains to sovereignty.

**5.34.12**  One should not conduct oneself improperly merely because a kingdom has been obtained; for insolence destroys prosperity as old age destroys the finest beauty.

**5.34.13**  The fish, rushing at the appearance of the thing, swallows the iron hook hidden beneath the finest bait, and does not look to the consequence.

**5.34.14**  What can be swallowed, what ought to be swallowed, what once swallowed will digest, and what is beneficial in its digestion — that is what the man who desires prosperity should consume.

**5.34.15**  He who plucks the unripe fruits of a tree obtains no juice from them, and the seed within is also destroyed.

**5.34.16**  But he who takes the ripe fruit, mature in its season — from the fruit he obtains its juice, and from the seed he obtains fruit again.

**5.34.17**  Just as the bee gathers honey while protecting the flowers, so should one take what one needs from people without harming them.

**5.34.18**  One should gather flower by flower and not cause the cutting of the root — like a garland-maker in a garden, not like the charcoal-burner.

**5.34.19**  What will accrue to me from doing this? What will accrue to me from not doing it? — having thought through his actions in this way, a man should act or refrain from acting.

**5.34.20**  Some goals should never be undertaken, and come to nothing regardless; in such matters even human effort exerted proves fruitless.

**5.34.21**  Certain goals — those of small cost but great fruit — a wise man swiftly undertakes to accomplish; he does not obstruct such endeavours.

**5.34.22**  He who sees everything plainly, as if drinking it in with his eyes, even sitting in silence — the people become attached to him.

**5.34.23**  He who pleases the world in these four ways — by eye, by mind, by speech, and by deed — the world in turn becomes gracious toward him.

**5.34.24**  He from whom living beings shrink away, as deer shrink from a hunter — even having obtained the earth to the edge of the ocean, such a one falls away.

**5.34.25**  He who has obtained the ancestral kingdom by his own power, yet stands in wickedness, scatters it away as the wind scatters a cloud.

**5.34.26**  For the king who practises dharma following the conduct of the good from the beginning, the earth full of riches grows and multiplies in prosperity.

**5.34.27**  But for the one who abandons dharma and practises adharma, the earth shrinks back as a hide placed in fire shrinks.

**5.34.28**  Whatever effort is made in crushing the kingdom of another, that very same effort should be made in protecting one's own kingdom.

**5.34.29**  By dharma one should acquire a kingdom; by dharma one should protect it; having attained prosperity rooted in dharma, one does not abandon it nor is diminished.

**5.34.30**  Even from a babbling madman or a crawling child, one should extract the essence from everywhere, as gold is extracted from stones.

**5.34.31**  A steadfast man should sit gathering the well-spoken words and well-done deeds of the wise from here and there, as a gleaner gathers grain.

**5.34.32**  Cows perceive by smell, brahmins perceive by the Vedas, kings perceive through spies, and other people perceive through their eyes.

**5.34.33**  A cow that is hard to milk gets more suffering inflicted on her; but the cow that is easy to milk, O king, they do not discipline even at all.

**5.34.34**  Wood that bends without being heated — they do not try to heat it either; and wood that is already bent of itself — they do not try to bend it either.

**5.34.35**  By this analogy, the wise man should bend toward the stronger — he who bows to the stronger bows, as it were, to Indra.

**5.34.36**  Cattle have rain as their lord; kings have friends and kinsmen; husbands are the kinsmen of women; the Vedas are the kinsmen of brahmins.

**5.34.37**  Dharma is protected by truth; learning is protected by practice; beauty is protected by cleanliness; lineage is protected by conduct.

**5.34.38**  Grain is protected by proper storage; horses are protected by orderly exercise; cows are protected by frequent inspection; women are protected by plain clothing.

**5.34.39**  Lineage is no authority for one devoid of good conduct — such is my view; for even among those born in the lowest station, conduct itself is what is distinguished.

**5.34.40**  He who is envious of another's wealth, beauty, valour, lineage, happiness, good fortune, and honour — his disease is without end.

**5.34.41**  That by which one should not be intoxicated — one should not drink it: from fear of doing what should not be done, from abandoning what should be done, and from untimely betrayal of counsel.

**5.34.42**  Intoxication by learning, intoxication by wealth, and thirdly intoxication by noble birth — these are the three intoxications of the puffed-up, and these very same things are the self-disciplines of the good.

**5.34.43**  The ignoble, when asked by the noble to do any task at any time, think of themselves as virtuous even while being ignoble and of ill repute.

**5.34.44**  The good are the refuge of the self-possessed; the good themselves are the refuge of the good; the good are also the refuge of the ignoble — but the ignoble are not the refuge of the good.

**5.34.45**  The assembly is conquered by the well-dressed; equal hope is conquered by the one who has cattle; the road is conquered by the one who has a vehicle; everything is conquered by the one of good character.

**5.34.46**  Character is foremost in a man; for one in whom it perishes here, there is no use for him in life, nor in wealth, nor in kinsmen.

**5.34.47**  For the wealthy the highest food is meat; for those of middling means the best is dairy produce; for the destitute, O bull of the Bharatas, salt is what tops their meal.

**5.34.48**  The poor always eat food that is in fact more excellent; for hunger produces sweetness, and that sweetness is very hard to obtain among the rich.

**5.34.49**  In the world, the prosperous generally lack the capacity to enjoy food; but for the poor, O lord of kings, even wood is digested.

**5.34.50**  For the lowest, the greatest fear is loss of livelihood; for those of middling station, the fear is of death; but for the highest among mortals, the greatest fear is dishonour.

**5.34.51**  The intoxications of power are the most sinful of intoxications — the pride of drinking and the rest. For one intoxicated by the pride of power does not recover his senses until he has fallen.

**5.34.52**  By the senses, ungoverned, roaming among their objects, this world is afflicted, even as the stars are afflicted by the planets.

**5.34.53**  He who is conquered by the fivefold group of senses that are innate and tormenting of the self — his calamities grow like the moon in the bright fortnight.

**5.34.54**  He who, without having conquered himself, desires to conquer his ministers and his enemies, without having subdued his ministers, becomes powerless and comes to ruin.

**5.34.55**  He who first conquers himself by fitting means, and thereafter his ministers and his enemies — his desire for conquest does not go in vain.

**5.34.56**  He whose senses are subdued, whose ministers are conquered, who applies punishment with firmness against offenders, who acts after deliberation and is steadfast — upon such a one prosperity eternally waits.

**5.34.57**  O king, the body of a man is his chariot; the self is not the driver — his senses are the horses. Vigilant and skilled, with good and well-trained horses, the steadfast man travels comfortably, like a charioteer.

**5.34.58**  These senses, if ungoverned, are capable of killing a man, like unruly untamed horses on the road that destroy a poor charioteer.

**5.34.59**  The fool, led astray by the senses, sees harm as benefit and benefit as harm; he regards great suffering as pleasure.

**5.34.60**  He who, abandoning righteousness and worldly good, follows in the wake of his senses — he quickly comes to ruin in respect of prosperity, life, wealth, and wife.

**5.34.61**  He who is master of wealth but not master of his senses — from the lack of mastery over his senses he falls from that very mastery of wealth.

**5.34.62**  One should seek the self by the self, with mind, intellect, and senses disciplined; for the self alone is the friend of the self, and the self alone is the enemy of the self.

**5.34.63**  O king, desire and anger — those two, like fish trapped in a net with small meshes — rob a person of his wisdom.

**5.34.64**  He who, having duly considered righteousness and worldly good in this world, acquires the means for both — he who has gathered the proper means thrives in happiness always.

**5.34.65**  He who, without conquering the five inner enemies that destroy the mind, desires to conquer external foes — those very enemies overcome him.

**5.34.66**  Indeed, evil-souled kings are seen being slain by their own deeds, losing their kingdoms through lack of mastery over the senses.

**5.34.67**  Because of not abandoning the company of evil-doers, punishment touches the innocent equally, through association. Because of mixing with the dry, the moist is burned; therefore one should never make alliance with the wicked.

**5.34.68**  The man who, through delusion, does not restrain the five arising inner enemies — each with its five effects — him calamity devours.

**5.34.69**  Freedom from envy, straightforwardness, purity, contentment, pleasant speech, self-restraint, truthfulness, and ease of conduct — these do not belong to the evil-natured.

**5.34.70**  Self-knowledge, ease of conduct, forbearance, constant adherence to duty, guarded speech, and generosity — O Bharata, these are not found among the lowest.

**5.34.71**  The ignorant injure the wise through abuse and slander; the speaker acquires sin, while the one who forgives is set free.

**5.34.72**  Violence is the strength of the wicked; the application of punishment is the strength of kings; service is the strength of women; forbearance is the strength of the virtuous.

**5.34.73**  O king, restraint of speech is considered the most difficult of all disciplines; to speak what is meaningful and varied at length is not possible.

**5.34.74**  Various kinds of well-spoken speech bring auspiciousness; that same speech, ill-spoken, O king, leads only to harm.

**5.34.75**  A forest pierced by arrows heals; one felled by an axe heals. But a wound inflicted by ugly speech does not heal — a wound made by words does not close.

**5.34.76**  Barbed arrows, reeds, and iron shafts can be removed from the body; but the dart of speech cannot be removed — for it lodges in the heart.

**5.34.77**  Word-arrows fly from the mouth; struck by them, another grieves by night and by day. They do not fall on the vital spots of others alone — the wise man should not release them against others.

**5.34.78**  When the gods wish to bring a man to ruin, they first take away his understanding; he then perceives all things inversely.

**5.34.79**  When the intellect has become turbid and destruction is imminent, wrong policy, wearing the appearance of right policy, does not depart from the heart.

**5.34.80**  Such, O Bharata, is the understanding that has seized you — through hostility to your sons, the Pāṇḍavas — and you do not perceive it.

**5.34.81**  O Dhṛtarāṣṭra, let Yudhiṣṭhira — who is endowed with kingly qualities and could be king of all three worlds — be your pupil and rule under your guidance.

**5.34.82**  He surpasses all your sons in fortune, endowed with splendor and wisdom, and knowing the truth of righteousness and worldly good.

**5.34.83**  He who is the foremost among the upholders of righteousness, by virtue of compassion and mercy, O king of kings, endures many sufferings out of reverence for you.

**5.35.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Speak again, O great intellect, words that accord with righteousness and worldly good. Listening to you, I am not satisfied — you speak such varied and wondrous things.

**5.35.2**  Vidura said: Bathing at all sacred fords, and straightforwardness toward all beings — these two may be regarded as equal; or rather, straightforwardness is superior.

**5.35.3**  Adopt straightforwardness toward your sons always, O lord; having gained the highest fame here, you shall obtain heaven hereafter.

**5.35.4**  As long as a man's meritorious glory is sung in the worlds, O tiger among men, so long is he honored in the world of heaven.

**5.35.5**  On this point they cite this ancient history — the dialogue between Virocana and Sudhanvan over Keśinī.

**5.35.6**  Keśinī said: Are Brāhmaṇas superior, or are the sons of Diti, O Virocana? And why does Sudhanvan not mount the couch?

**5.35.7**  Virocana said: We, the offspring of Prajāpati, are indeed the foremost, O Keśinī, the best of beings. These worlds belong to us — what are the gods, what are the twice-born?

**5.35.8**  Keśinī said: Stay right here, let us wait in attendance, O Virocana — Sudhanvan will come in the morning; I wish to see you two together.

**5.35.9**  Virocana said: So shall I do, O gracious one, as you, O timid one, say; in the morning you shall see both Sudhanvan and me together.

**5.35.10**  Sudhanvan said: I touch your golden seat, O son of Prahlāda; I have come here alone, but I will not sit together with you.

**5.35.11**  Virocana said: Let them bring a plank, a bundle of grass, or a mat — O Sudhanvan, you are not worthy to sit together with me.

**5.35.12**  Sudhanvan said: Even your father would attend upon me seated beside him from below. You are a child, reared in comfort at home — you understand nothing.

**5.35.13**  Virocana said: O Sudhanvan, let us wager all the gold, cattle, and horses that are our wealth among the asuras, and then let those who know arbitrate our question.

**5.35.14**  Sudhanvan said: Let the gold, cattle, and horses be yours, O Virocana. But let us wager our very lives and then ask those who know to arbitrate.

**5.35.15**  Virocana said: Where shall we two go, with our lives as the stake? I would not stand among the gods, nor among humans at any time.

**5.35.16**  Sudhanvan said: We shall go to your father, with our lives as the stake; for even for the sake of his son, Prahlāda would not speak falsehood.

**5.35.17**  Prahlāda said: These two appear before me — who have not walked together — come here by the same road like two angry venomous serpents.

**5.35.18**  Why do they come together now who did not walk together before? O Virocana, I ask you this: what is your friendship with Sudhanvan?

**5.35.19**  Virocana said: There is no friendship between Sudhanvan and me — we have wagered our lives. O Prahlāda, I ask you this: do not speak falsely in answering.

**5.35.20**  Prahlāda said: Let water and honey-mixture be brought for Sudhanvan. O Brahmin, you are worthy of honor; a white, well-nourished cow shall be given.

**5.35.21**  Sudhanvan said: Water and honey-mixture have already been offered to me on the road. O Prahlāda, please speak the truth in answer to our question.

**5.35.22**  Prahlāda said: O Brahmin, either my son or you would be placed in the position of a witness. How can one like me speak to the question of two who are in dispute?

**5.35.23**  Now, O Sudhanvan, I ask you this: one who would speak neither truth nor falsehood — where does such a poor witness dwell?

**5.35.24**  Sudhanvan said: The same night as a woman whose husband has taken another wife, as a man defeated at dice, as one whose limbs are exhausted from bearing a heavy load — in that same condition does the poor witness dwell.

**5.35.25**  Shut out in a city, hungry outside the gate, seeing his enemies in the majority — in that condition does the poor witness dwell.

**5.35.26**  Lying about animals kills five; lying about cows kills ten; lying about horses kills a hundred; lying about a man kills a thousand.

**5.35.27**  Lying for the sake of gold kills those already born and those yet unborn; lying about land destroys everything — therefore never speak falsehood about land.

**5.35.28**  Prahlāda said: Sudhanvan, born of Aṅgiras, is superior to me, O Virocana; his mother is superior to your mother. Therefore you are indeed defeated by him.

**5.35.29**  O Virocana, Sudhanvan is now the master of your life. But O Sudhanvan, I desire that Virocana be given back by you.

**5.35.30**  Sudhanvan said: Because you chose righteousness and did not speak falsehood out of desire, therefore I give back to you your son, O Prahlāda — the one hard to obtain.

**5.35.31**  This Virocana, your son, O Prahlāda, is given back by me. Let him wash the feet of the maiden in my presence.

**5.35.32**  Vidura said: Therefore, O king of kings, you should not speak falsehood for the sake of land. Do not lead yourself with sons and ministers to ruin while pursuing your sons.

**5.35.33**  The gods do not protect one with a staff in hand, as a cowherd guards cattle; rather, him whom they wish to protect they endow with wisdom.

**5.35.34**  In the exact measure that a man sets his mind on virtue, in that measure all his purposes succeed — there is no doubt in this.

**5.35.35**  The sacred hymns do not save the deceitful man who lives by deceit from his sins; as fledglings leave the nest once their wings have grown, the sacred hymns abandon him at the time of death.

**5.35.36**  Drinking in a state of intoxication, quarrel, enmity with a guild, dispute between husband and wife, estrangement from kinsmen, acting against the king's wishes, controversy between men and women — these are to be avoided, as is a corrupt path.

**5.35.37**  A soothsayer, a merchant, a former thief, a dice-cheat, a physician, an enemy, and a friend — these seven one should not employ as witnesses.

**5.35.38**  An agnihotra performed out of pride, silence practiced out of pride, learning studied out of pride, and sacrifice performed out of pride — these four, which should bring fearlessness, bring fear when performed in the wrong spirit.

**5.35.39**  An arsonist, a poisoner, one who eats from a paramour's household, a seller of soma, a maker of impure festivals, an informer, one who betrays friends, and an adulterer —

**5.35.40**  — a slayer of an embryo, one who violates his guru's bed, a Brahmin who drinks liquor, an excessively harsh person, a crow-natured person, an atheist, and a reviler of the Vedas —

**5.35.41**  — one who seizes the sacrificial ladle, an outcaste, a miser even if wealthy, and one who harms what he was told to protect — all these are equal to slayers of Brahmins.

**5.35.42**  Gold is tested by a fire of grass; a good one is tested by conduct in the yoke of life; a hero is tested by fearful and financially distressing situations; and in grave calamities, friends and enemies alike are tested.

**5.35.43**  Old age steals beauty; hope steals steadiness of mind; death takes away life; envy destroys righteous conduct; anger destroys prosperity; association with the ignoble destroys character; lust destroys modesty; and pride destroys everything.

**5.35.44**  Prosperity springs from auspiciousness; it grows further through boldness; it takes root through skill; and it becomes established through self-restraint.

**5.35.45**  Eight qualities illuminate a man: wisdom, noble birth, self-control, learning, valor, speaking little, generosity according to one's ability, and gratitude.

**5.35.46**  All these great and glorious qualities are subsumed by one quality that overwhelms them: when a king honors a person, that single quality outshines all other qualities.

**5.35.47**  O king, these eight things in the world of men are manifestations of the world of heaven; four of these are followed by the good, and four of these are pursued by the righteous.

**5.35.48**  Sacrifice, charity, study, and austerity — these four are ever attended by the good. Self-restraint, truth, straightforwardness, and non-cruelty — these four ever follow the righteous.

**5.35.49**  That is no assembly where there are no elders; those are no elders who do not speak of righteousness; that is no righteousness where there is no truth; that is no truth which is riddled with deceit.

**5.35.50**  Truth, beauty, learning, knowledge, noble birth, good conduct, strength, wealth, valor, and eloquent speech — these ten are the sources of social association.

**5.35.51**  One who does evil, renowned for evil, reaps only evil as fruit. One who does good, renowned for good, reaps only good as fruit.

**5.35.52**  Sin, committed again and again, destroys wisdom. The man whose wisdom is destroyed ever undertakes only sin.

**5.35.53**  Virtue, practiced again and again, increases wisdom. The man whose wisdom has grown ever undertakes only virtue.

**5.35.54**  A man who is envious, venomous, cruel, and given to enmity meets with great distress before long, by his sinful conduct.

**5.35.55**  One who is free from envy, possessed of wisdom, and ever practicing noble deeds attains happiness without difficulty and shines forth everywhere.

**5.35.56**  He is truly learned who acquires wisdom from the wise. Indeed, a wise man, having attained righteousness and wealth, is able to prosper in happiness.

**5.35.57**  In a single day one should do that by which one may dwell in comfort at night; in eight months one should do that by which one may dwell in comfort during the rains.

**5.35.58**  In one's earlier years one should do that by which one may dwell in comfort when old; throughout one's life one should do that by which one may dwell in comfort after death.

**5.35.59**  People praise food only after it has been digested, a wife only after her youth has passed, a warrior only after the battle is over, and an ascetic only after he has crossed to the other shore.

**5.35.60**  When a gap is stopped up with wealth gained by unrighteousness, that gap remains uncovered, and from there another crack opens up.

**5.35.61**  A preceptor is the chastiser of those who are self-controlled; a king is the chastiser of the wicked; but Yama, the son of Vivasvat, is the chastiser of those who commit secret sins.

**5.35.62**  The origin of sages, rivers, great-souled lineages, women, and of evil conduct — these should not be probed.

**5.35.63**  A Kṣatriya who delights in honoring the twice-born, who is generous, and who is straightforward toward his kinsmen shares in heaven and guards the earth for a long time, O king.

**5.35.64**  Three kinds of men gather the golden flowers of the earth: the brave, the learned, and one who knows how to serve.

**5.35.65**  Deeds of intellect are the highest, O Bharata; deeds of the arms are middling; those of the legs are the lowest, being akin to burden-bearing.

**5.35.66**  Having placed sovereignty in the foolish Duryodhana, Śakuni, Duḥśāsana, and Karṇa, how do you seek prosperity?

**5.35.67**  The Pāṇḍavas, O bull of the Bharatas, are endowed with every virtue and conduct themselves toward you as toward a father; conduct yourself toward them as toward sons.

**5.36.1**  Vidura said: On this point they cite this ancient legend — the dialogue between Atreya and the Sādhyas, as we have heard it.

**5.36.2**  In ancient times the Sadhya gods questioned the great sage of sharpened vows as he wandered in the form of a goose — that great knower of wisdom.

**5.36.3**  The Sadhya gods said: We are the Sādhyas, O great sage. Having seen you, we cannot form a full estimate of you. In our view you are wise and intelligent through your learning; you deserve to speak a noble and inspired word.

**5.36.4**  The goose said: This is the duty I have ever upheld, O immortals — steadiness, tranquility, adherence to truth and righteousness. Having loosened every knot of the heart, bring the pleasant and the unpleasant under your own control.

**5.36.5**  When reviled one should not revile in return; anger that is endured burns up the reviler and gathers his good deeds.

**5.36.6**  One should not be abusive, nor contemptuous of others, nor a betrayer of friends, nor a frequenter of the low; one should not be arrogant, nor of base conduct; one should avoid harsh and wounding speech.

**5.36.7**  Terrible words burn up the vital points, bones, heart, and very life-breaths of men here. Therefore one who delights in righteousness should ever avoid speech that is wounding and harsh in nature.

**5.36.8**  One who pierces the vital spots, is harsh, speaks rough words, and stings people with thorns of speech — know him as the most miserable of men, carrying ruin bound upon his face.

**5.36.9**  If another pierces him deeply with arrows blazing like fire and sun, the wise man, though diminished, knows while gaining even more: 'He is piling up merit for me.'

**5.36.10**  Whether one serves the good, the wicked, an ascetic, or even a thief, just as a garment takes on the color of its dye, so one comes under their influence.

**5.36.11**  He who does not begin a quarrel nor incite others to quarrel, who does not strike back when unstruck nor slay, who does not desire the sin of one who wishes to kill him — to such a one the gods aspire when he arrives.

**5.36.12**  They say silence is better than speech. If one must speak, let what is said be true — that is second. Let what is said be pleasant — that is third. Let what is said be righteous — that is fourth.

**5.36.13**  With whomever a man associates in argument, whomever he serves, and whatever he wishes to become — such does a man become.

**5.36.14**  From whatever a man turns away, from that he is released. By turning away from everything he does not know even a particle of suffering.

**5.36.15**  He is neither defeated nor seeks to defeat others; he makes no enemies and retaliates against none; his nature is equal in blame and praise alike; he neither grieves nor rejoices.

**5.36.16**  He who wishes the well-being of all, whose mind is not set on harm, who speaks truth, is gentle and self-controlled — that one is a highest person.

**5.36.17**  He who does not soothe with empty words, who gives what he has promised, and who understands success and failure — that one is a middling person.

**5.36.18**  One who is a vexer and not a chastiser, who does not turn back from the grip of anger, who is ungrateful, no one's friend, and of wicked soul — these are the qualities of the lowest man in this world.

**5.36.19**  He who does not trust in good even from others, who is suspicious even of himself, and who rejects his friends — he indeed is the lowest person.

**5.36.20**  One who wishes his own good should serve only the best; in due time also the middling; but should never serve the lowest.

**5.36.21**  One gains wealth by the strength of the wicked, through constant rising, through wisdom and manliness; yet such a one does not rightly earn praise, nor does one acquire the conduct of great families.

**5.36.22**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: The gods desire great families, as do those grown in righteousness and wealth, and the greatly learned. I ask you this question, Vidura: what indeed are great families?

**5.36.23**  Vidura said: Austerity, self-restraint, knowledge of Brahman, spreading of learning, auspicious marriages, constant gift of food — in those families where these seven qualities prevail and righteous conduct is well-practiced, those are great families.

**5.36.24**  Those whose conduct does not waver and whose lineage is unshaken, who walk in righteousness through the grace of their conduct, who desire distinguished fame within the family, and who have abandoned untruth — those are great families.

**5.36.25**  By absence of sacrifice, by improper marriages, by the suppression of the Vedas, and by transgression of righteousness, families fall into the state of non-families.

**5.36.26**  By destruction of the property of gods, by seizure of priestly wealth, and by transgression against Brahmins, families fall into the state of non-families.

**5.36.27**  By disrespect toward Brahmins, by slander of them, O Bharata, and by misappropriation of deposits entrusted to one's care, families fall into the state of non-families.

**5.36.28**  Families well endowed with cattle, men, and horses do not attain the rank of great families if they are deficient in right conduct.

**5.36.29**  But families that are not deficient in conduct, even if they are of small wealth, do attain the rank of great families and draw great glory to themselves.

**5.36.30**  Let there be no enemy-maker in our family; let there be no minister of a king who seizes others' property; no betrayer of friends, no deceitful one, no speaker of falsehood, no one who eats before the ancestors, gods, and guests.

**5.36.31**  Let the one who would kill our Brahmin, the one who would hate our Brahmins, and the one who would render our farming useless — let none such enter our assembly.

**5.36.32**  Grass, ground, water, and as a fourth — kindly and true speech: these in the houses of the good are never absent at any time.

**5.36.33**  O king, these are offered with the highest faith, properly honored, practiced among the righteous of great wisdom and meritorious deeds, O greatly wise one.

**5.36.34**  Even a slender chariot, O king, is able to bear a load, while other earthly things cannot bear it in the same way. In the same way those endowed with noble birth are able to bear burdens, while other men cannot bear them similarly.

**5.36.35**  That is no friend whom one fears because of anger; nor is that a friend who must be approached with suspicion. The friend in whom one can take confidence as in a father — that indeed is a friend; others are merely associates.

**5.36.36**  If even an unrelated person conducts himself with the spirit of friendship, he alone is a kinsman, that is a friend, that is a refuge, that is one's ultimate resort.

**5.36.37**  For a man of unsteady mind who does not serve the elders, whose understanding is ever wavering, the acquisition of friends is always insecure.

**5.36.38**  Riches pass beyond a man of unsteady mind, lacking self-mastery, who follows the lead of his senses — just as geese pass beyond a dried-up lake.

**5.36.39**  They grow angry without cause and become pleased without reason — this is the character of the wicked, like a drifting cloud.

**5.36.40**  Those who, when honored and when their purposes are served, do not become friends — even flesh-eaters, when they are dead, do not consume such ungrateful ones.

**5.36.41**  One should petition one's friends whether there is wealth or not; one who does not petition does not come to know the soundness or shallowness of friends.

**5.36.42**  Through anguish beauty fades, through anguish strength fades, through anguish knowledge fades, through anguish one falls into disease.

**5.36.43**  Nothing is attained through grief, and the body is afflicted; enemies rejoice. Therefore do not set your mind on grief.

**5.36.44**  Again a man dies and is born; again a man declines and rises again; again a man begs and is begged of; again a man grieves and is grieved over.

**5.36.45**  Happiness and suffering, existence and non-existence, gain and loss, death and life — all these touch everything here in turn. Therefore the wise man should neither rejoice nor grieve.

**5.36.46**  These six senses are unstable and restless; wherever and toward whatever each of them moves, from there and there flows away his understanding — like water ever flowing from a cracked pot.

**5.36.47**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: The slender, lofty, crested king Yudhiṣṭhira has been wrongly treated by me; he will bring the end of my dull-witted sons through battle.

**5.36.48**  All this is ever agitated, this mind is ever agitated; tell me, O greatly wise one, of that state which is free from agitation.

**5.36.49**  Vidura said: I see peace for you nowhere other than in knowledge and austerity, nowhere other than in the restraint of the senses, nowhere other than in the abandonment of greed, O sinless one.

**5.36.50**  Through wisdom one drives away fear; through austerity one attains greatness; through service to elders one attains knowledge; through renunciation one attains peace.

**5.36.51**  Unattached to the merit of giving, unattached to the merit of Vedic learning, freed from passion and hatred — those seeking liberation wander here.

**5.36.52**  At the end of that which is well studied, well fought, well done as a deed, and austerity well practiced — happiness flourishes.

**5.36.53**  Even when they have attained well-spread couches, the disunited never truly obtain sleep; they do not find pleasure in women, O king, nor when praised by bards and panegyrists.

**5.36.54**  The disunited never truly walk in righteousness; the disunited never truly attain happiness here; the disunited never truly honor dignity; the disunited never truly love peace.

**5.36.55**  Beneficial counsel does not taste sweet to them; security and prosperity are not arranged for them; for the disunited, O lord of men, there is no resort whatsoever other than ruin.

**5.36.56**  Abundance is to be expected from cattle, austerity from a Brahmin, inconstancy from women, and danger from one's own kinsmen.

**5.36.57**  Threads, though individually slender and always extended, when they are many, because of their multitude bear many hardships — this is the simile of the righteous.

**5.36.58**  Kindred separated from one another smolder and die out; united they blaze — like firebrands, O Dhṛtarāṣṭra, O bull of the Bharatas.

**5.36.59**  Those who are violent toward Brahmins, women, kinsmen, and cattle — they fall, O Dhṛtarāṣṭra, like a ripe fruit from its stalk.

**5.36.60**  Even a great tree that stands alone, mighty and well-rooted, has its branches and trunk crushed outright by the wind.

**5.36.61**  But those trees that stand together in a grove, firmly rooted, endure even the swiftest winds by leaning upon one another.

**5.36.62**  In the same way, enemies think they can destroy a man standing alone, however many his virtues, just as the wind can fell a solitary tree.

**5.36.63**  By mutual support and by sheltering one another, kinsmen flourish, just as lotuses thrive in a lake.

**5.36.64**  Brahmins, cows, women, children, and kinsmen must never be slain, nor those whose food one has eaten, nor those who have sought one's refuge.

**5.36.65**  There is no virtue in a man, however wealthy, that surpasses being free from disease — may it be well with you — for the sick are as good as dead.

**5.36.66**  O great king, drink and be calmed — drink wrath, that bitter ailment of the head, born without disease, sharp, harsh, and fierce, attended by sin, the drink that the good imbibe and the wicked refuse.

**5.36.67**  Those tormented by illness pay no heed to the fruits of action; they cannot experience the truth of pleasures. The sick, always oppressed by pain, are aware of neither the enjoyment of wealth nor of happiness.

**5.36.68**  O king, once before you did not heed my words when, having seen Draupadī won at dice, I told you to restrain Duryodhana in the gambling hall — wise men shun cheating at dice.

**5.36.69**  That is no strength which fights against the gentle; dharma, mixed with vigor, must be practiced. Prosperity assembled through cruelty is soon destroyed, while prosperity built on gentleness combined with firmness passes on to sons and grandsons.

**5.36.70**  May the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra protect the Pāṇḍavas, and may the sons of Pāṇḍu protect your sons. Let the Kurus be of one enemy and one counsel; may they live, O king, in happiness and prosperity.

**5.36.71**  You are today the central pillar of the Kauravas; the Kuru house depends on you, O descendant of Ajamidha. Protect the young sons of Pṛthā, scorched by their forest exile — protect them, dear one, guarding your own honor.

**5.36.72**  Make peace between the Kauravas and the sons of Pāṇḍu; let not enemies exploit the breach between you. They are all steadfast in truth, O lord of men — do you yourself restrain Duryodhana, O king of kings.

**5.37.1**  Vidura said: O king of kings, Manu, the self-born's son, spoke of these seventeen men, O son of Vicitravīrya, as those who beat the air with their fists.

**5.37.2**  He called them like one who bows while claiming Indra's bow cannot be bent, or like one who bows while saying the feet of Marīci cannot be made to bow.

**5.37.3**  He who instructs one unfit to learn, he who grows angry at the wrong time, he who overly serves an enemy, he who does not protect women — may it be well with you — he who begs from one who should not be begged, and he who boasts.

**5.37.4**  He who, though well-born, does what should not be done; he who, being weak, is always at war with the strong; he who speaks to one who has no faith; he who desires what is undesirable, O lord of men.

**5.37.5**  He who, as father-in-law, takes pleasure in his daughter-in-law's laughter; he who, while living with his daughter-in-law, is desirous of honor; he who sows seed in another's field; and he who slanders a woman excessively.

**5.37.6**  He who, having received something, says 'I do not remember it'; he who boasts when asked for a gift after giving; he who in this world seeks the favor of the wicked — these men are followed by the wind with a noose in hand.

**5.37.7**  Whatever way a man behaves toward another, in the same way should one behave toward him — that is dharma. One who acts with deceit should be met with deceit; one who acts virtuously should be met with virtue.

**5.37.8**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: A man is said in all the Vedas to live a hundred years — when he does not attain all that lifespan, what is the cause of it here on earth?

**5.37.9**  Vidura said: Excessive speech, excessive pride, and likewise excessive abandonment, O lord of men, excessive anger, excessive curiosity, and betrayal of friends — these are the six.

**5.37.10**  These sharp weapons cut short the lifespans of embodied beings — these things kill men, not death itself — may it be well with you.

**5.37.11**  He who violates the wife of one who trusts him, he who sleeps with his guru's wife, the brahmin who has married a low-caste woman, and he who is addicted to drink, O Bharata —

**5.37.12**  — and he who kills one who has sought refuge — all these are equal to slayers of brahmins. The Vedic teaching declares that expiation must be performed by those who associate with such persons.

**5.37.13**  The householder who is generous, whose speech is never contemptible, who eats only the remnants of food, who is harmless, who does nothing purposeless, who has abandoned strife, who is grateful, truthful, and gentle — such a wise man attains heaven.

**5.37.14**  O king, men who always speak pleasantly are easy to find; but one who speaks what is unpleasant yet wholesome, and one who listens to it, are both rare.

**5.37.15**  He who, relying on dharma and setting aside the lord's likes and dislikes, speaks what is unpleasant yet wholesome — with such a man the king has a true ally.

**5.37.16**  For the family's sake abandon a man; for the village's sake abandon a family; for the kingdom's sake abandon a village; for the sake of one's own self abandon the whole earth.

**5.37.17**  Protect wealth against calamity; protect one's wife even at the cost of wealth; protect oneself always, even at the cost of wife and wealth.

**5.37.18**  I spoke thus also at the time of the dicing, O king, and yet you did not accept such fitting counsel — like a sick man, O son of Vicitravīrya, it did not please you, though it was wholesome medicine.

**5.37.19**  You let crows defeat these splendid peacocks — these Pāṇḍavas were defeated by the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra — while hiding jackals and abandoning lions. When the time comes, you shall grieve, O lord of men.

**5.37.20**  He, dear one, who does not anger at any time toward a servant who is devoted and intent on his master's welfare — in such a master do servants place their trust, and they do not abandon him in times of calamity.

**5.37.21**  One should not try to win outsiders by cutting off the livelihood of one's servants — for ministers who are loving but deprived of their customary enjoyments will abandon such a master, accustomed as they are to their due.

**5.37.22**  Having first carefully enumerated all undertakings, and income and expenditure, and a livelihood proportionate to them, one should then gather helpers suited to the work — for difficult tasks are accomplished with the aid of helpers.

**5.37.23**  He who, knowing his master's intent, carries out all tasks without laziness — a devoted, noble speaker of what is beneficial, one who knows his own capacity — such a man, like one's very self, deserves compassion.

**5.37.24**  But a servant who, when instructed, disregards the command, who talks back when assigned a task, who is conceited about his own wisdom and speaks contrarily — such a servant should be dismissed without delay.

**5.37.25**  They say a messenger endowed with eight qualities is: unhaughty, not timid, not dilatory, compassionate, smooth in manner, not to be bought by others, healthy in constitution, and noble in speech.

**5.37.26**  A wise man should never go to another's house out of blind trust, especially at an improper time; he should not linger concealed in a public square at night; nor should he court a woman of the warrior class.

**5.37.27**  He should not become an accomplice in concealing what happened in a sacrifice; nor of one who has shared secret counsel with bad associates. He should not say 'I have no trust in you'; rather, let him give a reason for his refusal.

**5.37.28**  A king who is squeamish, a servant of the king who is unchaste, a son, a brother, a widow with young children, a soldier, and one who has had his rations taken away — these should all be avoided in business dealings.

**5.37.29**  Ten qualities attend one who is devoted to bathing: strength, beauty, clarity of voice and complexion, touch, fragrance, purity, auspiciousness, delicacy, and the best of women.

**5.37.30**  Six qualities attend one who eats in moderation: health, long life, happiness, strength, clean progeny, and men do not reproach him saying 'he is gluttonous.'

**5.37.31**  One should not lodge these persons in one's house: one who is idle, a glutton, one hated by the world, one full of deceit, one who is cruel, one who does not know the right place and time, and one who is ill-dressed.

**5.37.32**  Even in severe distress, one should never beg from these: a miser, one who reviles others, one without learning, one born of a wretched family, one who claims respect while deserving none, one who is harsh, one who bears grudges, and the ungrateful.

**5.37.33**  One should not associate with these six lowest of men: one whose deeds are corrupt, one who speaks too much, one who is habitually false, one whose devotion is unstable, one whose attachment is prolonged in wrong directions, and one who is excessively proud.

**5.37.34**  Undertakings are bound by helpers, and helpers are bound by undertakings — these two are bound to each other, and neither succeeds without the other.

**5.37.35**  Having fathered sons and freed himself from debt, having arranged some livelihood for them, and having properly given all daughters in marriage, a man should desire to dwell in the forest like a sage.

**5.37.36**  What is beneficial to all beings and conducive to one's own happiness — a capable man should do that, for this is the root of the attainment of dharma and wealth.

**5.37.37**  He who possesses intelligence, power, energy, courage, enterprise, and determination — from what source could there be any fear of poverty for him?

**5.37.38**  See the faults in going to war with the Pāṇḍavas — a conflict wherein even the gods together with Indra would be shaken. Eternal enmity with your sons, a life of constant anxiety, destruction of glory, and joy for your enemies.

**5.37.39**  The wrath of Bhīṣma, your own wrath equal to Indra's, the wrath of Droṇa, and the wrath of King Yudhiṣṭhira, fully roused, would destroy this world, like a white planet falling aslant across the sky.

**5.37.40**  Your hundred sons, Karṇa, and the five Pāṇḍavas together could rule the entire earth girdled by the ocean.

**5.37.41**  The sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra are the forest, O king, and the sons of Pāṇḍu are considered the tigers — do not cut down the forest along with its tigers, and do not destroy the tigers apart from the forest.

**5.37.42**  There would be no forest without tigers, and there would be no tigers without the forest — the forest is protected by the tigers, and the tigers are protected by the forest.

**5.37.43**  The wicked do not wish to discern the virtues of others as much as those evil-minded ones wish to discover the absence of virtue in them.

**5.37.44**  One who desires the highest attainment of wealth should first and foremost practice dharma — for wealth does not depart from dharma any more than nectar departs from the world of heaven.

**5.37.45**  He whose self has ceased from sin and is established in what is good — by him all this is understood, both nature and its modifications.

**5.37.46**  He who properly pursues dharma, wealth, and desire each in its proper time — the combination of dharma, wealth, and desire — attains that both here and hereafter.

**5.37.47**  He who restrains the impulse that arises from anger and joy, and he who is not bewildered in times of calamity — such a man is a vessel of prosperity, O king.

**5.37.48**  Learn from me of the five kinds of strength that are always present in men. What is called physical strength — strength of the arm — is said to be the lowest strength.

**5.37.49**  The acquisition of good ministers — may it be well with you — is said to be the second strength. The acquisition of wealth, those who desire conquest say, is the third strength.

**5.37.50**  What is the innate strength of a man, O king, descended from father and grandfather — that is called the strength of noble birth and is remembered as the fourth strength.

**5.37.51**  But that by which all these are gathered together, O Bharata — that which is the best of all strengths — is called the strength of wisdom.

**5.37.52**  When a man is capable of doing great harm to another man, having incurred his enmity, one should not take comfort saying 'I am far away.'

**5.37.53**  What wise man should place trust in women, in kings, in serpents, in Vedic recitation, in those who serve enemies, and in pleasure and in life?

**5.37.54**  For a creature struck by the arrow of wisdom, there are no healers, no medicines, no oblation-mantras, no auspicious rites, no Atharvavedic remedies, not even highly perfected antidotes.

**5.37.55**  A serpent, fire, a lion, and a son of good family, O Bharata — none of these should be treated with contempt by a man, for all of them are of exceeding power.

**5.37.56**  Fire, that great power in the world, lies hidden in wood and does not consume that wood as long as it is not kindled by others.

**5.37.57**  But when that same fire is churned forth from wood and blazes up, it then swiftly burns both that wood and all the forest around it with its energy.

**5.37.58**  In just the same way, those born in noble families, whose energy is like fire, patient and showing no outward sign, lie dormant like fire in wood.

**5.37.59**  You and your sons have the nature of creepers, while the sons of Pāṇḍu are considered the great sala trees — no creeper ever grows without leaning on a great tree.

**5.37.60**  You and your sons are the forest, O king, O son of Ambikā, and know the Pāṇḍavas to be the lions in the forest, dear one — for a forest without lions would perish, and the lions would perish without the forest.

**5.38.1**  Vidura said: The vital breaths of a young man actually rise upward when an elder approaches, and through standing to receive him and through respectful salutation, he recovers them again.

**5.38.2**  Having offered a seat to the virtuous guest who has arrived, having brought water and washed his feet, having inquired after his welfare and informed him of one's own condition — then let the wise man give food after duly attending to him.

**5.38.3**  He in whose house water, the honey-mixture, and the cow are received by a guest without the proper mantras — whether out of greed, fear, or parsimony for the sake of wealth — the noble declare his life to be a life of futility.

**5.38.4**  A physician, a surgeon, one who has broken his vow of chastity, a thief, a cruel man, a drunkard, one who kills an embryo, one who lives by military service, and one who sells his Vedic learning — however dear such a guest may be, he does not deserve the water-offering.

**5.38.5**  Things that should not be sold are: salt, cooked food, curds, milk, honey, oil, clarified butter, sesame seeds, meat, roots and fruits, vegetables, red garments, all fragrant substances, and molasses.

**5.38.6**  He who is free from anger, who looks on clod and gold as equal, who has relinquished grief, who has gone beyond both alliance and conflict, who has ceased from blame and praise alike, who is indifferent to the pleasant and the unpleasant — such a wandering mendicant walks as one who is neutral.

**5.38.7**  He who subsists on wild rice, roots, ingudi nuts, and vegetables, who is well self-controlled, who needs no prompting in the rites of the sacred fire, who lives in the forest and is ever attentive to guests, who bears the burden of austerity, and who performs meritorious acts — he is the true ascetic.

**5.38.8**  Having wronged a man of wisdom, one should not take comfort saying 'I am far away' — the arms of a wise man are long, and with them he strikes the one who has struck him.

**5.38.9**  One should not trust the untrustworthy, nor should one trust the trustworthy too absolutely; for fear born of misplaced trust cuts even roots asunder.

**5.38.10**  A man should be free from jealousy, guard his wife, be generous, speak pleasantly, be gentle and sweet-spoken toward women, yet never be enslaved by them.

**5.38.11**  Women are said to be the Lakṣmī of the household — greatly fortunate, holy, and the very light of the home; therefore they are to be protected with especial care.

**5.38.12**  One should entrust the inner apartments to the father, the kitchen to the mother, and the cattle to one equal to oneself; one should personally oversee agriculture, conduct trade through servants, and honor Brahmins through one's sons.

**5.38.13**  Fire arises from water, the Kṣatriya from Brahman, and iron from stone; the all-pervading energy of each is quenched in its own origin.

**5.38.14**  The good who are born in noble families, blazing like fire, yet patient and without outward display, lie dormant like fire hidden within wood.

**5.38.15**  That king whose counsel is unknown to both outsiders and those within his own circle — he who sees in all directions — enjoys sovereignty for a long time.

**5.38.16**  One should not speak of what one is about to do, but display what has already been accomplished; when matters of dharma, desire, and wealth are kept thus, counsel remains unbroken.

**5.38.17**  Counsel should be taken on the summit of a mountain, in a secluded palace chamber, or in a forest free of stakes and thorns — in such private places.

**5.38.18**  A non-friend does not deserve to know the highest counsel, O descendant of Bharata; nor does an unwise friend, nor a wise man lacking self-control; for in a minister both the desire for gain and the safeguarding of counsel must coexist.

**5.38.19**  For that king whose counselors know all affairs already concluded — that king who keeps his deliberations secret — success is without doubt.

**5.38.20**  He who through delusion pursues ignoble deeds, when those deeds recoil upon him, falls from life itself.

**5.38.21**  The performance of praiseworthy actions brings happiness; the non-performance of those very same actions causes great remorse thereafter.

**5.38.22**  For the king who knows the increase and decline of positions, who understands the six royal policies and knows his own self, and whose conduct is never held in contempt — the earth is under his sway, O king.

**5.38.23**  For him whose anger and joy are never in vain, who personally supervises his own duties, and whose treasury is under his own control — the earth becomes a giver of wealth.

**5.38.24**  A lord of the earth should not be satisfied with mere name and royal umbrella; he should distribute wealth among his servants and not be the sole taker of everything.

**5.38.25**  A Brahmin knows a Brahmin; a husband knows his wife; a king knows his minister; and a king knows another king.

**5.38.26**  An enemy who has fallen into one's lap and is fit for destruction should not be released; for from one who has not been slain, fear arises again very soon.

**5.38.27**  Anger should always be carefully restrained toward the gods, toward kings, toward Brahmins, and toward the aged, children, and the sick.

**5.38.28**  A wise man avoids pointless quarrel, which is the practice of fools; thereby he gains fame in the world and is not joined to calamity.

**5.38.29**  He whose grace yields no fruit and whose anger too is purposeless — servants do not desire such a master, just as women do not desire an impotent husband.

**5.38.30**  Intellect alone does not bring wealth, nor does dullness alone lead to poverty; the wise man understands the cyclic turns of the world's affairs — others do not.

**5.38.31**  A fool always disregards those great in learning, character, and age, those great in wisdom, and those great in wealth and lineage, O Bharata.

**5.38.32**  Misfortunes swiftly come to him who behaves ignobly, who is unwise, envious, unrighteous, foul-tongued, and wrathful.

**5.38.33**  Non-deception, generosity, not violating agreements, and speech that is properly directed — these turn living beings back toward one in good will.

**5.38.34**  One who does not deceive, who is capable, grateful, intelligent, and straightforward — even if his treasury is diminished — still gains a following.

**5.38.35**  Fortitude, tranquility, self-restraint, purity, compassion, speech that is not harsh, and non-betrayal of friends — these seven are the fuel-sticks of prosperity.

**5.38.36**  One who does not share, who is wicked at heart, ungrateful, and shameless — such a basest of men is to be shunned in this world, O lord of men.

**5.38.37**  He who, being himself at fault, angers blameless members of his own household, does not sleep in peace at night — like one whose house contains a serpent.

**5.38.38**  Those whose enmity would threaten one's prosperity and security, O Bharata — one should always propitiate them as one propitiates the gods.

**5.38.39**  Wealth that is entrusted to women, wealth placed in the hands of those newly risen, and wealth dependent on the ignoble — all such wealth is in a state of uncertainty.

**5.38.40**  Where a woman rules, where a gambler rules, where a child gives commands — those lands sink helplessly, like stones in a river.

**5.38.41**  Those who are devoted to essential purposes and not to outward distinctions, O Bharata — those I consider truly wise; for distinctions are the province of attachment.

**5.38.42**  The man whom gamblers praise, whom wandering bards praise, whom prostitutes praise — such a man does not truly live.

**5.38.43**  Having abandoned those supreme archers, the Pāṇḍavas of boundless might, you have placed the great sovereignty of the Bharatas in Duryodhana's hands.

**5.38.44**  You will very soon see him fallen from that sovereignty — him who is stupefied by the pride of power — just as Bali was cast down from the three worlds.

**5.39.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: This man is not his own master in prosperity or adversity; like a wooden doll strung on threads, he is subject to the ordainer's decree of fate. Therefore, speak — I am attentive, all ears.

**5.39.2**  Vidura said: Even Bṛhaspati, if he speaks at the wrong moment, receives only contempt for his wisdom and disrespect, O Bharata.

**5.39.3**  One person becomes dear through giving, another through pleasant speech, another through the strength of counsel and fundamental support; he who is dear in that last way is truly dear.

**5.39.4**  One who is disliked does not appear virtuous, intelligent, or learned; to one who is loved, auspicious deeds are attributed; to one who is disliked, evil ones, O Bharata.

**5.39.5**  That is not truly a loss, O great king, which brings growth in its wake; what should be regarded as true loss here is that which, once suffered, destroys much more.

**5.39.6**  Some are prosperous in virtue, others in wealth; those who are rich in wealth but poor in virtue — avoid them, O Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**5.39.7**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: All that you speak is fitting for the future and is approved by the wise; yet I have not the heart to abandon my son. Where there is dharma, there is victory.

**5.39.8**  Vidura said: One who is endowed with natural virtue and imbued with humility will never inflict even the slightest harm upon any living being.

**5.39.9**  There are those who are devoted to slandering others, who delight when others suffer misfortune, and who are always striving to create mutual discord among people.

**5.39.10**  Those whose very sight is tainted with fault, in whose company there is immense fear, whose taking of wealth is full of vice, and whose giving is also fraught with great danger —

**5.39.11**  Those men who are notorious as sinful, who are reproached in society, and who are tainted by other grave faults — such men one should avoid.

**5.39.12**  When friendship with a base person wanes, the affection vanishes; and so too does the fulfillment of results from that friendship, and whatever joy existed in that bond.

**5.39.13**  Such a person strives to defame others and makes effort toward their destruction; even when the wrong done is slight, he does not, out of delusion, find peace.

**5.39.14**  Having carefully observed with discerning intelligence such base, cruel, and uncontrolled persons, a learned man should avoid them from afar.

**5.39.15**  He who supports a kinsman who is poor, wretched, or afflicted — he gains prosperity in sons and cattle, and imperishable fame.

**5.39.16**  Kinsmen should be nurtured by those who desire their own welfare and the growth of their family, O lord of kings; therefore conduct yourself well.

**5.39.17**  By honoring your kinsmen you will be united with what is best, O king; even kinsmen who are lacking in virtue must be protected, O bull among the Bharatas.

**5.39.18**  How much more so then, when they are virtuous and yearn for your grace? Show grace to the afflicted Pāṇḍavas, O lord of the people.

**5.39.19**  Grant them some villages for their livelihood, O lord; by doing so you will attain fame in this world, O ruler of men.

**5.39.20**  For you, as an elder, the protection of your sons is your duty, dear one; and by me too what is beneficial must be spoken — know me to be one who desires your good.

**5.39.21**  Conflict with kinsmen should not be made by one who desires prosperity, dear one; pleasures are to be enjoyed together with one's kinsmen, O bull of the Bharatas.

**5.39.22**  Eating together, conversing together, and mutual affection — these are the things to be done with kinsmen; conflict with them should never occur under any circumstances.

**5.39.23**  Kinsmen can uplift in this world, and kinsmen can cause one to sink; those of good conduct uplift, while those of bad conduct cause one to sink.

**5.39.24**  Conduct yourself well toward the Pāṇḍavas, O lord of kings, O bestower of honor; surrounded by them, you will be invincible to your enemies.

**5.39.25**  The kinsman who, though near a prosperous kinsman, still sinks into misery — he incurs that prosperous kinsman's sin, like a deer caught by one whose hand is smeared with poison.

**5.39.26**  You will grieve afterward, O best of men, upon hearing that they have been slain or that your own sons have been slain — reflect on this now.

**5.39.27**  The action on account of which, lying on his deathbed, a man would be tormented — that action he should not do from the very beginning, given the uncertainty of life.

**5.39.28**  There is no man who does not commit some wrong act, save Bhārgava alone; but the remedy for what remains — the capacity to set right — rests only in the wise.

**5.39.29**  If that sin was done to the Pāṇḍavas earlier by Duryodhana, then it is for you, O lord of men, as the elder of the family, to remedy it.

**5.39.30**  By restoring them to their rightful position, you will be free of sin in this world, O best of men, and worthy of worship by the wise.

**5.39.31**  He who, having carefully reflected on the well-spoken words of the steadfast and their fruits, determines his course of action accordingly — such a man abides long in glory.

**5.39.32**  Humility destroys poverty; valor destroys calamity; forbearance always destroys anger; and right conduct destroys ill-omened qualities.

**5.39.33**  By one's retinue, one's field, one's dwelling, one's service, and by one's food and clothing — from these can a family's true nature be assessed, O king.

**5.39.34**  Between those two whose minds meet mind to mind in quietness and whose wisdom meets wisdom — the friendship between them does not decay.

**5.39.35**  A man of intelligence should avoid one of wicked understanding and undeveloped wisdom, as one avoids a well covered with grass; friendship with such a person perishes.

**5.39.36**  With the arrogant, the foolish, the violently reckless, and those who have abandoned dharma — a wise man should not cultivate friendship.
