**5.39.37**  A friend who is grateful, righteous, truthful, not mean-spirited, of firm devotion, master of his senses, steadfast in his steadfastness, and who never abandons — such a one is a desired friend.

**5.39.38**  Total suppression of the senses is no different from death; but excessive indulgence of them could afflict even the gods.

**5.39.39**  Gentleness toward all beings, freedom from envy, forbearance, firmness, and not disrespecting friends — these the wise declare to be life-prolonging virtues.

**5.39.40**  He who, through skillful policy, seeks to recover what was lost through poor policy, by taking firm and resolute counsel — that is the vow of a true man, not a coward.

**5.39.41**  The man who knows how to remedy future adversity, who is firm in resolve in the present moment, and who knows what remains to be done when past affairs are concluded — such a man is not abandoned by fortune.

**5.39.42**  Whatever one constantly practices in deed, thought, and speech — that very thing carries one away; therefore one should practice what is auspicious.

**5.39.43**  Resorting to auspicious things, disciplined practice, learning, diligence, straightforwardness, and the constant company of the virtuous — these bring prosperity.

**5.39.44**  Non-despondency is the root of prosperity and of the destruction of sorrow and the arising of joy; the one who is never disheartened becomes great and attains supreme happiness.

**5.39.45**  There is nothing more auspicious than this, nothing more beneficial for one who seeks prosperity: O dear one, forbearance everywhere and always.

**5.39.46**  The powerless man forgives everything out of weakness; the powerful man forgives for the sake of dharma. For one to whom gain and loss are equal, forbearance is always beneficial.

**5.39.47**  Whatever pleasure one pursues without thereby falling away from dharma and artha — that desire one may indulge; one should not practice the vow of a fool.

**5.39.48**  Prosperity does not dwell among those afflicted by sorrow, those who are heedless, those who deny the next world, the idle, the unrestrained, and those devoid of enterprise.

**5.39.49**  Those of wicked intellect, taking a man endowed with straightforwardness for a weakling — ashamed of his very straightforwardness — presume to affront him.

**5.39.50**  Prosperity does not approach, out of fear, one who is exceedingly noble, exceedingly generous, exceedingly heroic, excessively given to vows, or one who is arrogant of his own wisdom.

**5.39.51**  The Vedas bear fruit in the agnihotra sacrifice; learning bears fruit in good conduct and practice; a wife bears fruit in love and offspring; wealth bears its fruit in giving and enjoyment.

**5.39.52**  He who performs funeral rites with wealth unrighteously acquired does not enjoy the fruit of that wealth after death, because the means of acquisition was wrong.

**5.39.53**  In impassable forests and wildernesses, in severe calamities and turmoil, and even when weapons are raised — those who have a remainder of merit know no fear.

**5.39.54**  Effort, self-restraint, skill, heedfulness, firmness, memory, and undertaking after deliberation — know these to be the root of prosperity.

**5.39.55**  Austerity is the strength of ascetics; Brahman-knowledge is the strength of those who know Brahman; violence is the strength of the wicked; forbearance is the strength of the virtuous.

**5.39.56**  These eight do not break a vow: water, roots, fruit, milk, oblation, the wish of a brahmin, the word of a teacher, and medicine.

**5.39.57**  One should not impose on another what is disagreeable to oneself — this in brief is the essence of dharma; all other conduct proceeds from desire.

**5.39.58**  Conquer anger by the absence of anger; conquer the unrighteous man by righteousness; conquer the miser by generosity; conquer falsehood by truth.

**5.39.59**  One should never place trust in a woman who is a cheat, in the lazy, in the coward, in the violent, in one who fancies himself a great man, in a thief, in an ungrateful person, or in a nihilist.

**5.39.60**  For one who is habitually respectful and who always serves elders, four things increase: fame, lifespan, glory, and strength.

**5.39.61**  Do not set your mind on wealth that comes through excessive suffering, through transgression of dharma, or through supplication of an enemy.

**5.39.62**  An unlearned man is pitiable; a childless couple is pitiable; subjects without food are pitiable; a kingdom without a king is pitiable.

**5.39.63**  The road is the old age of those with bodies; water is the old age of mountains; lack of union is the old age of women; a sharp word is the old age of the mind.

**5.39.64**  The Vedas are tainted by lack of recitation; a brahmin is tainted by lack of observance of vows; a virtuous woman is tainted by curiosity; women are tainted by prolonged separation from their husbands.

**5.39.65**  Silver is the dross of gold; tin is the dross of silver; lead is known as the dross of tin; and dross itself is the dross of lead.

**5.39.66**  One does not conquer sleep by sleeping; one does not conquer a woman by desire; one does not conquer fire by adding fuel; one does not conquer liquor by drinking it.

**5.39.67**  He whose friends are won by generosity, whose enemies are conquered in battle, and whose wife is won by food and drink — his life bears full fruit.

**5.39.68**  Even those who possess thousands live; even those who possess hundreds live. O Dhṛtarāṣṭra, give up desire; there is no way one cannot live.

**5.39.69**  Whatever rice and barley, gold, cattle, and women there are on this earth — all that is not enough for one person alone. Seeing this truth, one is not deluded.

**5.39.70**  O king, I say to you again: act equally toward your sons. O king, let there be equity between your own sons and the sons of Pāṇḍu.

**5.40.1**  Vidura said: He who, when requested by good men, without hesitation accomplishes the task without diminishing his capacity — glory quickly comes to that virtuous man. For when good men are pleased, it leads to happiness.

**5.40.2**  He who, of his own accord without being reproached, relinquishes even great wealth tainted with unrighteousness — he sleeps happily, freed from sorrows, like a serpent that has shed its old skin.

**5.40.3**  Falsehood in self-aggrandizement, tale-bearing that reaches the king's ears, and persistent false accusation of one's teacher — these are equal to the slaying of a brahmin.

**5.40.4**  Envy is death in a single step; harsh speech is the slayer of prosperity. Disobedience, haste, and boastfulness are the three enemies of learning.

**5.40.5**  Where is knowledge for one who seeks comfort? There is no comfort for one who seeks knowledge. The seeker of comfort must abandon knowledge, or the seeker of knowledge must abandon comfort.

**5.40.6**  Fire is not satiated by fuel; the great ocean is not satiated by rivers; Death is not satiated by all creatures; nor is a fair-eyed woman satiated by men.

**5.40.7**  Hope destroys steadfastness; Death destroys prosperity; anger destroys glory; miserliness destroys fame. Neglect destroys cattle, O king; and a single enraged brahmin destroys a kingdom.

**5.40.8**  A goat, a bronze vessel, a chariot always ready, a honey-drawing bee, a learned brahmin, an elder kinsman fallen on hard times, and a friend — may these always be in your house.

**5.40.9**  A she-goat, sandalwood, a lute, a mirror, honey and ghee, poison, an udumbara fruit, a conch shell, gold, a navel piece, and yellow pigment —

**5.40.10**  — these auspicious items ought to be kept in the house, said Manu, O Bharata, for the worship of gods and brahmins, and for the reception of guests.

**5.40.11**  And this supreme counsel I declare to you, the most meritorious and distinguished of teachings, O dear one: Never abandon dharma — not for desire, not for fear, not for greed, not even for the sake of your own life.

**5.40.12**  Dharma is eternal; pleasure and pain are transient. The self is eternal; the body that sustains it is transient. Abandoning the transient, be established in the eternal. Be content — for contentment is the highest gain.

**5.40.13**  Behold those mighty and illustrious men — having ruled the earth full of wealth and grain, having abandoned kingdoms and vast enjoyments — those kings have gone into the power of Death.

**5.40.14**  Men lift a dead son — one nourished with such sorrow — and carry him out of their own homes, O king; with hair disheveled and crying piteously, they cast him into the midst of the pyre like a log of wood.

**5.40.15**  Another enjoys the wealth of one who has gone to the realm of the dead; birds and fire consume the elements of his body. He departs to the other world with only two companions — merit and sin — wrapped around him.

**5.40.16**  Kinsmen, friends, and sons turn back after abandoning him; but the man who has been cast into the fire — his own deeds follow him.

**5.40.17**  Above this world and below that other world a great darkness stands — a blinding gloom. That is the great delusion of the senses. Awaken, O king — let it not deceive you.

**5.40.18**  If you are able to hear all this counsel and act accordingly, you will attain supreme glory in the world of the living; and there will be no fear for you in the next world, nor in this one.

**5.40.19**  The self is a river of sacred fords, O Bharata — its waters are truth, its banks are fortitude, its waves are self-restraint. One who bathes in it, performing virtuous deeds, is purified. For the self is always holy water itself.

**5.40.20**  Making a boat of steadfastness, cross the river whose waters are the five senses and whose crocodiles are desire and anger — cross the difficult straits of rebirth.

**5.40.21**  He who, honouring and propitiating one who is great in wisdom, great in dharma, a kinsman, great in learning, and also great in years — who consults such a one in matters of what should and should not be done — such a person will never be deluded.

**5.40.22**  By steadfastness guard the loins and belly; by the eyes guard the hands and feet; guard the eyes and ears by the mind; and guard the mind by action.

**5.40.23**  One who always performs the water-rite, always wears the sacred thread, always studies the scriptures, avoids the food of the fallen, speaks the truth, and performs his duty to his teacher — such a brahmin never falls from the world of Brahman.

**5.40.24**  A kshatriya who has studied the Vedas, tended the sacred fires, performed sacrifices, protected his subjects, whose inner self is purified by the sword for the sake of cows and brahmins, and who is slain in battle — he goes to heaven.

**5.40.25**  A vaishya who has studied the brahmins and kshatriyas, who in due season shares his wealth with his dependants, who inhales the auspicious smoke purified by the Tretā sacrifice — after death he enjoys the pleasures of heaven among the gods.

**5.40.26**  A shudra who duly and in proper order honours the brahmin, kshatriya, and vaishya varnas — when these are pleased, he, untroubled and with his sins burned away, abandons the body and enjoys the pleasures of heaven.

**5.40.27**  This dharma of the four varnas has been declared to you. Now hear the reason I am telling you this: the son of Pāṇḍu is being deprived of his kshatriya dharma. O king, engage him in the duty of a king.

**5.40.28**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: It is indeed as you always instruct me, O gentle one; my own mind too becomes thus, even as you say to me.

**5.40.29**  But that resolve, though formed in this way, always turns around when it meets with Duryodhana in regard to the Pāṇḍavas.

**5.40.30**  No mortal is able to transcend what is fated. I consider that only fate acts; human effort is without purpose.

**5.41.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: If there is anything, O Vidura, that you have left unsaid, tell me who am eager to hear. You speak of many wondrous things.

**5.41.2**  Vidura said: O Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the ancient and eternal youth Sanatsujata declared, O Bharata, that there is no death.

**5.41.3**  He, the foremost among all the wise, will expound to you all the secret and the manifest teachings that reside in the heart, O great king.

**5.41.4**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Is there anything more that the eternal one would tell me that you yourself do not know? You yourself speak, O Vidura, if there is any remainder of wisdom in you.

**5.41.5**  Vidura said: I am born of a shudra womb and therefore dare not say more than this. But I know the eternal wisdom that is the understanding of that youth.

**5.41.6**  For one born into a brahmin womb, even speaking what is most secret — he is not censured by the gods for that. Therefore I tell you this much.

**5.41.7**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Tell me, O Vidura, of that ancient and eternal one. How can a meeting with him happen right here through this body?

**5.41.8**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Vidura meditated upon that sage of sharpened vows; and that sage, knowing he had been thought of, revealed himself, O Bharata.

**5.41.9**  Vidura received him with the rites prescribed by the scriptures. When the sage was comfortably seated and rested, Vidura spoke to him.

**5.41.10**  O blessed one, there is a certain doubt in the mind of Dhṛtarāṣṭra which I am not able to resolve. Please speak to him of it — hearing which, this lord of men may transcend pleasure and pain.

**5.41.11**  Speak so that gain and loss, the dear and the hateful, old age and death, fear and anger, hunger and thirst born of intoxication, discontent and sloth, desire and anger, decline and rise — may not torment him.

**5.42.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then the wise king Dhṛtarāṣṭra, that great soul, having honoured the words spoken by Vidura, questioned Sanatsujata in private, desiring the highest wisdom.

**5.42.2**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: O Sanatsujata, I have heard this teaching of yours — that there is no death. And yet gods and asuras both practised brahmacharya for the sake of immortality. Which of these is truly true?

**5.42.3**  Sanatsujata said: Some say that through ritual action one attains immortality; others say that there is no death. O king, listen as I explain this — do not doubt it.

**5.42.4**  Both are true, set in motion from the time of kshatriyas and others. Delusion is what the wise consider to be death. I declare heedlessness to be death; I declare constant vigilance to be immortality.

**5.42.5**  Through heedlessness indeed the asuras were defeated; through heedlessness men become Brahman. Death does not devour beings like a tiger; for its form cannot be perceived.

**5.42.6**  Some call Yama death, others call it something else. Immortality rests in the self; brahmacharya is immortality. That god rules the kingdom in the realm of the ancestors — auspicious to the auspicious, inauspicious to the inauspicious.

**5.42.7**  From the mouth of men this issues forth: anger, heedlessness, and delusion — these are death's very form. Deluded, moving under its sway, the dead fall from here and fall there again.

**5.42.8**  Then the gods too are swept along by it; hence death comes to bear the name of dying. Those attached to the fruits of action at the arising of action — they follow along there and do not cross over death.

**5.42.9**  He who, while brooding, would strike down the impulses that arise — he who, through disregard, remains unawakened — he becomes death itself, consuming like death. Such is the one who, knowing, destroys desires.

**5.42.10**  A man who follows after desires is destroyed along with those desires. He who casts off desires shakes off whatever dust of rajas remains in him.

**5.42.11**  This is the hell of beings — darkness without light — that is seen ahead. They rush forward as if being seized, heading face-first into the pit.

**5.42.12**  Covetousness first slays a man; then desire and anger seize him afterward. These bring the foolish to death. But the wise cross over death by their steadfastness.

**5.42.13**  Not thinking of anything other than himself, O kshatriya, one does not study — like a tiger among blades of grass. That one whose inner self is made of anger, greed, and delusion — that very one dwelling in your body is death.

**5.42.14**  Having understood death as it arises, he who stands firm in knowledge does not fear death here. Death is destroyed in its own domain, just as a mortal, upon entering the realm of death, is destroyed.

**5.42.15**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Some in this world do not practice righteousness here, while others practice righteousness here. Is righteousness obstructed by sin, or does righteousness overcome sin?

**5.42.16**  Sanatsujata said: In that case the fruit of both is experienced — of righteousness and of its opposite. The wise man repels unrighteousness by means of righteousness here. Know this: righteousness is the stronger of the two.

**5.42.17**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Those worlds of one's own righteousness which they speak of — the eternal worlds of twice-born men of meritorious deeds — those who tell of their courses and then speak of others have not done this deed, O wise one, nor has it truly been done.

**5.42.18**  Sanatsujata said: Those brahmanas who do not compete in strength as if rivaling the strong — they shine in the heavenly world after departing from here.

**5.42.19**  Where a brahmana should think of food and drink as being as plentiful as grass and fuel during the rainy season — living there, he should not grieve over it.

**5.42.20**  Where one, without boasting, gives away what is inauspicious and fearful, acting as though doing something superfluous — that person is better, not the other.

**5.42.21**  One who, without boasting, does not grieve over himself, who does not enjoy the property of brahmanas — that food is approved by the good.

**5.42.22**  Just as a dog always eats what it has vomited, to its own destruction, so those who live by exploiting their own prowess eat what has been vomited.

**5.42.23**  A brahmana should always think: "My conduct is unknown." But dwelling in the midst of kinsmen, nothing whatsoever would be known of him.

**5.42.24**  For who deserves to slay such a brahmana in his inner self? Therefore, O kshatriya, Brahman dwells in something and perceives it.

**5.42.25**  He should be untiring through non-acquisition, respected and free from affliction. Disciplined, yet not posturing as disciplined — such should be the brahmana who knows Brahman, the wise seer.

**5.42.26**  Those twice-born who are not wealthy in human riches but are wealthy in the Vedas — they are hard to assail, hard to shake. Know them as the body of Brahman.

**5.42.27**  Whoever here knows all the gods who have performed proper sacrifices — there is no equal to the brahmana in whom Brahman strives of its own accord.

**5.42.28**  He who is honored even when not striving to be — he is truly honored. The one truly worthy of honor should not consider himself disrespected, nor should he grieve over disrespect.

**5.42.29**  The one who is honored should think: "The learned honor me here." The one who is not honored should think: "The fools ignorant of righteousness, though skilled in worldly scriptures, will not honor the one worthy of honor."

**5.42.30**  Indeed, honor and silence do not go together always. This world belongs to honor, and that world too belongs to honor — so the wise know.

**5.42.31**  Prosperity is the companion of happiness here, and yet she is also an obstacle. The Brahmic prosperity is very hard to attain, O kshatriya, for one lacking in wisdom.

**5.42.32**  The good declare her doors to be many in kind and hard to enter: truth, honesty, modesty, self-restraint, purity, and knowledge — six that obstruct pride and delusion.

**5.43.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: He who, being a twice-born, studies the Rig verses, the Yajus, and the Sāmaveda — when he commits sins, is he tainted by sin or is he not tainted?

**5.43.2**  Sanatsujata said: The Sāmans, nor even the Rig verses, nor the Yajus, O discerning one, protect him from sinful deeds. I do not speak falsely to you.

**5.43.3**  The Vedic meters do not carry across one who practices deceit through deceit — as fledglings leave the nest when their wings have grown, the meters abandon him at the time of death.

**5.43.4**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: If the Vedas are not capable of saving even the knower of the Vedas, O discerning one, then why does this eternal teaching of the brahmanas persist?

**5.43.5**  Sanatsujata said: Austerity practiced in this world bears fruit seen in another. These worlds of brahmanas are attained when austerity is abundant and restraint is observed.

**5.43.6**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: How does austerity become complete and fully abundant, O Sanatsujata? Tell me that, so that we may understand it.

**5.43.7**  Sanatsujata said: Anger and eleven other faults, and the six beginning with cruelty, O king — and the twelve qualities beginning with righteousness that are well known to the twice-born in the scriptures —

**5.43.8**  Anger, desire, greed, delusion, the wish to obtain, grief, envy, pride, sorrow, longing, jealousy, and disgust — these twelve faults of humans should always be avoided by a man.

**5.43.9**  Each one of these, O king of kings, stalks human beings one by one, seeking an opening among them, like a hunter among deer.

**5.43.10**  The boastful, the envious, the arrogant, one who harbors anger, the fickle, and the unguarded — these six, when they prevail, lead men into evil conduct. The good do not do this, even in dire straits.

**5.43.11**  One who grows in enmity through shared enjoyment, one who regrets what he has given, the miser, the weak, the praiser of factions, and the one who despises women — these are the other seven cruel traits.

**5.43.12**  Righteousness, truth, self-restraint, austerity, absence of jealousy, modesty, forbearance, freedom from envy, sacrifice, charity, steadfastness, and sacred learning — these are the twelve great vows of a brahmana.

**5.43.13**  But one who has fallen away from these twelve — he would instruct the entire earth; distinguished in three, two, or even one — know that he has nothing of his own.

**5.43.14**  Self-restraint, renunciation, and vigilance — in these the immortal is established. Those wise brahmanas call them the gateways to truth.

**5.43.15**  Self-restraint has eighteen faults: acting contrary to what is done and undone, falsehood, envy, desire, self-interest, and longing —

**5.43.16**  Anger, sorrow, thirst, greed, slander, ill-will, the desire to acquire, anguish, and attachment —

**5.43.17**  Forgetfulness, excessive speech, and conceit about oneself — one who is freed from these faults is called self-restraint by the good.

**5.43.18**  But renunciation is superior in six ways: on obtaining what is dear, one does not rejoice; and when the unpleasant arises, one does not ever reach distress.

**5.43.19**  Beloved wives and sons — and whatever speech is appropriate, whatever should be given to one who begs, that speech should be spoken. Even what should not be spoken, he speaks nonetheless — that is remembered as the third quality.

**5.43.20**  One who exists with things given up and does not use them through desire, who is not diminished in actions on that account — like a student in mind — such a man who is endowed with all these qualities, even if he possesses wealth, would be so.

**5.43.21**  Vigilance has eight faults that should be abandoned: from the five senses and from the mind, O Bharata, and from past and future — one freed from these becomes happy.

**5.43.22**  That which is freed from these faults and endowed with these qualities — this is the complete and fully abundant austerity alone, about which you ask me, O king. What more do you wish to hear?

**5.43.23**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: By the Vedas with the epics as fifth, most people are largely described. Likewise there are others with four Vedas, others with three Vedas —

**5.43.24**  And those with two Vedas, those with one Veda, and yet others with no Rig verses at all. Among them, who is the one I should know as a true brahmana?

**5.43.25**  Sanatsujata said: Through not knowing the one Veda, those many Vedas came to be. Of the one truth, O king, one who is established in truth — thus, having set aside the Veda, they place wisdom in the great.

**5.43.26**  Charity, study, and sacrifice — these proceed from greed. For those who fall away from truth, their intention becomes fruitless.

**5.43.27**  Then sacrifice extends from the very firm grasp of truth. One whose mind is different, whose speech is different, and whose action is different — a man accomplished in resolve stands above mere resolutions.

**5.43.28**  One should practice the vow of initiation without servility. This indeed — established in its root — is the name; truth alone is the highest for the good. Knowledge is indeed what is called the direct; austerity is what is generated as the indirect.

**5.43.29**  One should know that one who reads much is merely a brahmana by much-reading. Therefore, O kshatriya, do not regard someone as a brahmana by mere chatter. Only one who never departs from truth — know him as the true brahmana.

**5.43.30**  The Vedic meters, O kshatriya — Atharvan sang those first; this is the stream of seers. Those who know the meters and have studied them do not know the knowable of the Veda, nor the knowable.

**5.43.31**  There is no one who is truly the knower of the Vedas; someone may comprehend the Vedas, O king. But one who knows the Vedas does not know the knowable — only one established in truth knows the knowable.

**5.43.32**  I recognize the brahmana as the wise declarer who, with all doubt cut away, elucidates all uncertainties.

**5.43.33**  In search of him one should go eastward, not southward, not toward the near, how much less diagonally, and in no direction in any manner whatsoever.

**5.43.34**  Having become silent, one should attend, not even striving with the mind. Brahman should be returned to — it is indeed lodged in the inner self.

**5.43.35**  A sage is truly a sage because of silence, not because of dwelling in the forest. But one who knows that imperishable — he is called the supreme sage.

**5.43.36**  Through the analysis of all meanings one is called a grammarian. One who sees the worlds directly becomes one who sees all.

**5.43.37**  A brahmana standing in truth truly sees Brahman, O kshatriya, in the sequential order of the Vedas. Knowing this, I declare it to you.

**5.44.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: O Sanatsujata, since you speak this all-pervading brahmic speech for the sake of another — this supreme discourse, so very rare among desires — declare that word to me, O youth.

**5.44.2**  Sanatsujata said: This Brahman is not to be attained by one in a hurry — you ask me about it and are overly excited. I shall expound the ancient unmanifest knowledge, perfected by understanding and by the brahmacharya of those who have realized it.

**5.44.3**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: This eternal unmanifest knowledge which you speak of — perfected by brahmacharya, not to be begun here at just any time, O noble one — how then does one attain brahminhood and immortality?

**5.44.4**  Sanatsujata said: Those who conquer desires in this world, who endure seeking the brahmic state — they extract the self from the bodies here, established in pure being, like the reed from the munja grass.

**5.44.5**  The father and mother make this body, O Bharata. The teacher-instructor gives birth to a true birth — that birth is real, that birth is ageless and immortal.

**5.44.6**  Those who enter here into the womb of the teacher, becoming as an embryo and practicing brahmacharya — they become makers of scripture in this very world; casting off the body, they attain the supreme yoga.

**5.44.7**  One who covers the ears with truth, making them veiled, and grants the immortal — him one should regard as both father and mother. One who knows what has been done by him should not wrong him.

**5.44.8**  A student who always honors the teacher, who is pure and diligent in desiring self-study, who does not act with pride and does not harbor anger — this is the first quarter of brahmacharya.

**5.44.9**  One should do what is dear to the teacher, even at the cost of one's life, even with wealth, in deed, in mind, and in speech — this is called the second quarter.

**5.44.10**  Just as one's conduct toward the teacher, so should it be toward the teacher's wife. One who does what is instructed and is pleasing to them — this is called the third quarter.

**5.44.11**  Having done a service for the teacher, the wise man should not boast of it; he should think and not say "I am doing this." That is indeed the fourth quarter of brahmacharya.

**5.44.12**  Whatever wealth accrues to one living thus — one should pass that on to the teacher. One thus acquires the conduct of the good with its many virtues; and this same conduct applies to the teacher's son.

**5.44.13**  One living thus grows in every direction here; he obtains many sons and renown. The quarters and directions rain blessings upon him, and people dwell within this brahmacharya of his.

**5.44.14**  By this brahmacharya the gods attained divinity, and the greatly fortunate sages, the wise ones, attained the world of Brahman.

**5.44.15**  By this very brahmacharya came the beauty of the Gandharvas and the Apsaras. By this brahmacharya the sun is born anew each day.

**5.44.16**  One who lies on the ground and burns the entire body with austerity, O king — by this brahmacharya the wise man transcends childhood and likewise holds back death at the hour of death.

**5.44.17**  Those worlds that men win through finite deeds are finite, O kshatriya. But the wise man attains Brahman itself through brahmacharya, the whole of it. There is no other path for going there.

**5.44.18**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Does it appear white, or red, or dark, or like collyrium, or tawny — what the knowledgeable brahmana sees who is skilled in this? What form has that immortal, imperishable state?

**5.44.19**  Sanatsujata said: It does not appear white, nor red, nor dark, nor like iron, nor sun-colored. It does not abide on the earth, nor in the atmosphere. The ocean does not hold it in its waters.

**5.44.20**  It does not reside in the stars, nor in lightning. Its form is not seen in the clouds, nor in the wind, nor among the gods. It is not seen in the moon, nor in the sun.

**5.44.21**  It is not in the Rig verses, nor in the Yajus, nor in the Atharvans. It is not seen even in the pure Sāmans, nor in the Rathantara or Bṛhatsāman, O king. Even in the Great Vow it cannot surely be seen.

**5.44.22**  It is beyond crossing over, beyond darkness. Even death reaches it at the time of dissolution. Its form is subtler than the edge of a razor, yet its form is greater than the mountains.

**5.44.23**  That is the foundation, that is the immortal, those are the worlds, that is Brahman, that is glory. Beings were born from that, and to that they return at dissolution.

**5.44.24**  That is free from affliction, that great and exalted glory — the poets call it a transformation of speech. In that, all this universe is established. Those who know that become immortal.

**5.45.1**  Sanatsujata said: That radiant, supreme brilliance — that great light blazing, that great glory — the gods themselves worship it; from it the sun shines forth. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.2**  From that radiance Brahman is born; by that radiance Brahman grows. That radiance, in the midst of all lights, blazes without itself being heated, burning all others. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.3**  The waters, then from the waters, in the midst of the flood — both deities dwell in the atmosphere; bearing united and diverse forms, he upholds both the earth and heaven. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.4**  Both deities — earth and heaven — and the directions: the radiant one upholds the entire universe. From him the directions and rivers flow; from him the great oceans are established. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.5**  Dwelling at the hub of the wheel of the imperishable Dhruva — him who bears the banner, the horses carry that divine, ageless one in the sky. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.6**  His form does not abide in any likeness; no one sees him with the eye. Through wisdom, through mind, and through the heart — those who know him thus become immortal. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.7**  Through the twelve-month stream guarded by the gods, the masters of honey move fearfully then. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.8**  The bee, having gathered it, drinks that honey for half a month; the Lord, who is oblation in all beings, ordained it so. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.9**  Having approached the golden-leafed Aśvattha tree, those without wings become birds there and fly off in their respective directions. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.10**  From the full, fullnesses are drawn; from the full, fullnesses are made and taken away; from the full, fullnesses arise — and yet fullness alone remains. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.11**  From him indeed the wind comes forth, and is ever reverently devoted to him; from him fire and soma arise, and in him the life-breath is extended.

**5.45.12**  One should know that all of this comes from him — yet all that cannot be spoken. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.13**  The out-breath swallows the in-breath; the moon swallows the in-breath; the sun swallows the moon; the Supreme swallows the sun. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.14**  The swan rising out of the water does not lift even one foot — if it were constantly worshipped as a priest, there would be neither death nor immortality. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.15**  Thus that great-souled god, the Puruṣa, swallows the purifying fire; whoever truly knows that Puruṣa — his self is not destroyed here. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.16**  One who were to spread a thousand thousand wings and fly, arriving at the very middle of the middle — even if swift as thought — the yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.17**  His form does not abide in vision, yet those of perfectly pure nature see him. The wise, the benevolent, behold him with the mind; those who take refuge in him become immortal. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.18**  As serpents hide in caverns, mortals hide behind their own learning and conduct; in them, confused people are deluded — just as one misleads travelers on a path to their fear. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.19**  One who is always treated as non-existent — there is no death for him, much less immortality. Truth and untruth, bound equally by truth; he is the sole womb of both the existent and the non-existent. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.20**  Neither by good deeds nor by evil — this is seen equally among humans; one should know this equally as the nectar of immortality. Thus united, one should seek that honey indeed. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.21**  Harsh words do not burn his heart; unlearned texts and unoffered fire-sacrifices do not trouble him. The mind should take on the lightness of Brahman; the wise attain the name of wisdom for him. The yogis behold him, the blessed, the eternal one.

**5.45.22**  One who thus sees the Self in all beings, and all beings engaged in various pursuits — what more should such a one grieve for?

**5.45.23**  Just as in a great flood inundated everywhere a small well becomes superfluous, so are all the Vedas to a brahmin who truly knows.

**5.45.24**  The great-souled Puruṣa, the size of a thumb, dwelling in the heart, is not seen. Unborn, moving day and night, without fatigue — knowing him thus, the wise one abides in serenity.

**5.45.25**  I alone am your mother; I am your father; I am your son again. I am the Self of all — both what does not exist and what does exist.

**5.45.26**  I am the aged grandfather, the father, and the son, O Bharata. You all dwell in my Self — yet you are not mine, nor am I yours.

**5.45.27**  The Self alone is my abode; the Self is my birth. I am declared by the Vedas, established in the ageless one.

**5.45.28**  Smaller than the smallest, well-disposed in mind, I watch over all beings. They know the father of all beings as placed in the lotus of the heart.

**5.46.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: While Sanatsujata and the wise Vidura were thus conversing with the king, that night passed away.

**5.46.2**  When that night had passed, all those kings entered the assembly hall gladly, eager to see the charioteer's son.

**5.46.3**  Desiring to hear the message of the Pārthas — filled with righteousness and purpose — with Dhṛtarāṣṭra at their head, all went to the auspicious royal assembly.

**5.46.4**  The hall was white as plaster, vast, adorned with golden courtyards, radiant as the moon, most beautiful, sprinkled with the finest water.

**5.46.5**  Spread with beautiful seats — golden, wooden, made of the hardest stone, and of ivory — well-covered with upper coverings.

**5.46.6**  Bhīṣma, Droṇa, Kṛpa, Śalya, Kṛtavarmā, Jayadratha, Aśvatthāmā, Vikarṇa, Somadatta, and Bahlika —

**5.46.7**  And the greatly wise Vidura, and Yuyutsu the great warrior, and all those assembled heroes, the kings — placing Dhṛtarāṣṭra in front, they entered that auspicious assembly, O bull of the Bharatas.

**5.46.8**  Duḥśāsana, Citrasena, Śakuni the son of Subala, Durmukha, Duhsaha, Karṇa, Ulūka, and Viviṃśati —

**5.46.9**  Placing before them the Kuru king Duryodhana the intolerant, they entered that assembly, O king, like gods entering Indra's court.

**5.46.10**  As those heroes with arms like iron bars entered, O king, the assembly shone, O king, like a mountain cave filled with lions.

**5.46.11**  Those great archers, splendid in assembly, having entered, took their places on the most worthy seats, resplendent as the sun.

**5.46.12**  When all those kings were seated on their thrones, O Bharata, the doorkeeper announced that the charioteer's son had arrived.

**5.46.13**  That chariot comes which went to the Pāṇḍavas on our behalf; the envoy has returned quickly, drawn by excellent Sindhu horses.

**5.46.14**  Having come near, he quickly leapt from the chariot — wearing his earrings — and entered the full assembly thronged with great-souled kings.

**5.46.15**  Sañjaya said: I have returned from going to the Pāṇḍavas — know this, O Kauravas. The Pāṇḍavas greet all the Kurus according to their age.

**5.46.16**  They bow to the elders and treat equals as equals; the sons of Pṛthā addressed the young ones, honouring each according to age.

**5.46.17**  As I was instructed by Dhṛtarāṣṭra when I departed hence, so I spoke to the Pāṇḍavas when I went. Hear that, O kings.

**5.47.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: I ask you, Sañjaya, in the midst of the kings — what did the great-spirited Dhanañjaya say, dear one? He is the leader of warriors, the cutter of the lives of the wicked, the great-souled one.

**5.47.2**  Sañjaya said: Let Duryodhana hear these words that Arjuna, preparing to fight, the great-souled one with Yudhiṣṭhira's approval, spoke — Dhanañjaya, while Keśava listened.

**5.47.3**  Knowing his own arm-strength, unafraid, the steady one, in private with Vāsudeva, Kiriti spoke to me while preparing for battle: 'Speak this in the midst of the Kurus to the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra.'

**5.47.4**  All the kings who have been assembled for battle against the Pāṇḍavas, let them also hear: exactly as I spoke my complete message, convey it to that king along with his ministers.

**5.47.5**  Just as all the gods attend upon the king of gods, the thunderbolt-armed Indra, so the Pāṇḍavas and the Sṛñjayas listened to the well-reasoned words spoken by the diademed one.

**5.47.6**  Thus spoke Arjuna, the Gāṇḍīva-bowman with eyes like red lotuses, preparing for battle: 'If the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra does not release the kingdom of Yudhiṣṭhira — king of the Ajamidha line — then there is indeed some evil deed committed before by the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra that has not yet been expiated.'

**5.47.7**  Those who face battle with Bhīmasena and Arjuna, with the twin Aśvins, with Vāsudeva, with Saineya surely weapon in hand, with Dhṛṣṭadyumna and Śikhaṇḍī, and with Yudhiṣṭhira who is like Indra himself — who could burn earth and heaven with his very wrath —

**5.47.8**  If the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra thinks he should fight with them, the entire aim of the Pāṇḍavas is accomplished. Do not do that for the sake of the Pāṇḍavas' cause; but if you think so, then come to battle.

**5.47.9**  The Pāṇḍava who follows dharma and was sent into exile — the hard bed on which he lay in the forest — the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will meet with a far worse, miserable, and disastrous final bed, bereft of life.

**5.47.10**  With modesty, knowledge, austerity, and self-restraint, with wrath, with protection of dharma and with wealth — acting wrongfully, the evil-minded son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra has been lording it over the Kuru Pāṇḍavas.

**5.47.11**  Deceitful in foundation, with spies and with straightforwardness, with austerity and self-control, with protection of dharma and strength, speaking truth yet joining lies with affection, forbearing and suffering excessively long —

**5.47.12**  When the eldest Pāṇḍava of disciplined soul, his mind provoked to excess among the Kurus, shall release that terrible wrath long restrained through many years — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.13**  Blazing like a fire with a black trail when fully kindled, as fire would burn dry grass in summer — so Yudhiṣṭhira, blazing with wrath, will burn the army of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son upon encountering it.

**5.47.14**  When he shall see Bhīmasena standing on the battlefield, mace in hand, vomiting the poison of his wrath, that intolerant Pāṇḍava of terrible speed — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.15**  Like a great lion entering among cattle, mace in hand, approaching the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra — when Bhīma of terrible form becomes the slayer — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.16**  Fearless in great fear, accomplished in arms, a crusher of enemy forces in combat — at once charging the masses of chariots with his chariot, striking down crowds of foot-soldiers with his mace —

**5.47.17**  Swiftly crushing many soldiers, when he hurls himself to scatter the army of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son, cutting like a hero with an axe through a forest — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.18**  When the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra beholds his own vast host of soldiers scattered and deluged — like a village burned by fire as though mere grass, like a ripe crop burned by lightning —

**5.47.19**  Its heroes slain, turning away, seized with fear, mostly with backs turned, its warriors mostly daunted, burned by the flame of Bhīmasena's weapons — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.20**  When Nakula the skillful warrior, drawing from his quiver with his right hand, slaying hundreds — the foremost of chariot fighters, the knowing warrior — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.21**  When Nakula — accustomed to comfort but who long lay on the hard bed of the forests — hisses greatly like an enraged venomous serpent — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.22**  Heroes who have given up their lives for battle with the kings, commanded by Dharmarāja, charging the army with gleaming chariots — seeing them, the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will afterwards repent.

**5.47.23**  When the Kaurava shall see those five young heroes accomplished in arms yet not appearing as mere children, the Kekayas charging with lives renounced — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.24**  When the relentless one, Sahadeva, mounts the chariot whose wheels move silently, with golden stars, yoked with ivory-white horses, and will hurl off the heads of kings with streams of arrows —

**5.47.25**  When a great terror erupts, beholding him standing on his chariot, whirling in battle, accomplished in arms, flying out in every direction — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.26**  He who restrains by shame, subtle, truth-speaking, of great strength, endowed with all dharmas — Sahadeva the swift-acting, the swift one, hurling men, striking in the tumult towards Gāndhārī.

**5.47.27**  When he shall see the mighty archers, the sons of Draupadī, heroes skilled in arms, expert in chariot warfare, approaching like venomous serpents of terrible venom — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.28**  When Abhimanyu, slayer of enemy heroes, raining down arrows on foes like a cloud, plunging deep into battle, equal to Kṛṣṇa in arms — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.29**  When he shall see the boy of no boyish valor, Subhadrā's son, equal to Indra in arms, falling upon the enemy army like death itself — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.30**  The Prabhadrakas — swifter than swift, young, skilled, of lion-like valor — when they are the ones hurling themselves upon the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra with their armies — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.31**  When he shall see the two aged great warriors Virāṭa and Drupada, each advancing with his own division against the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra and their armies — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.32**  When Drupada, accomplished in arms, shall cut off the heads of young warriors in battle, standing on his chariot, in his wrath, with arrows released from his bow — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.33**  When Virāṭa the slayer of enemy heroes shall pierce into the vitals of the enemy host together with the Matsyas of fierce appearance — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.34**  When he shall see the eldest of the Matsyas, of fierce appearance, the son of Virāṭa, a chariot warrior, armored and deployed at the front for the cause of the Pāṇḍavas — then the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra will repent of battle.

**5.47.35**  When the foremost hero of the Kauravas — the best of Shantanu's sons — is slain in battle by Śikhaṇḍī, our enemies will never be able to hold out. I declare this without doubt, as truth.

**5.47.36**  When Śikhaṇḍī, a chariot-fighter advancing against Bhīṣma in his chariot, fully armored, crushes the enemy's chariot-masses with celestial horses — then Dhārtarāṣṭra will repent of the war.

**5.47.37**  When he sees Dhṛṣṭadyumna shining at the forefront of the Sṛñjaya army — he to whom the wise Droṇa taught his secret weapon — then Dhārtarāṣṭra will repent.

**5.47.38**  When that immeasurable commander, overwhelming the Dhārtarāṣṭras with his arrows, advances against Droṇa in battle, bearing down on his foe — then Dhārtarāṣṭra will repent of the war.

**5.47.39**  He is modest, wise, strong, and resolute — endowed with fortune, the foremost among the Somakas. No other enemies could ever endure him, if that lion of the Vṛṣṇis were to lead them.

**5.47.40**  Though the world might say 'do not choose him,' we choose Sātyaki, the grandson of Śini, as our unrivaled ally on the chariot in battle — mighty, fearless, and skilled in arms.

**5.47.41**  When the lord of the Śinis, as commanded by me, rains arrows on the foe like a cloud pouring rain and covers the warriors in a net of shafts — then Dhārtarāṣṭra will repent of the war.

**5.47.42**  When that long-armed, firm-bowed great soul girds himself for battle, enemies shrink away from him as cattle scatter on smelling a lion, and as from fire.

**5.47.43**  That long-armed, firm-bowed great soul could cleave mountains and destroy all worlds; expert in weapons, skillful, swift-handed — he blazes in the sky like the sun.

**5.47.44**  The mastery in arms of the lion of the Vṛṣṇis — the Yādava — is varied, subtle, and well-wrought. Just as they speak of the highest discipline perfected in every virtue, so Sātyaki is endowed with all those qualities.

**5.47.45**  When Suyodhana sees in battle the golden chariot of Mādhava yoked with four white horses — and sees Sātyaki — then that foolish, unrestrained man will repent.

**5.47.46**  When he sees in battle the chariot gleaming with gold and jewels, yoked with white horses, bearing the terrible monkey-banner, governed by Keśava — then that foolish, unrestrained man will repent.

**5.47.47**  When that dull-witted one hears the terrible, thunderous slap of the bowstring against my palm as I brandish the Gāṇḍīva in the great battle — a sound equal to the crash of the thunderbolt —

**5.47.48**  — then that foolish son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, that evil-minded one with wretched allies, will be scorched in battle as he watches his army scatter like a herd of cattle before a rain-darkness of arrows at the battle's front.

**5.47.49**  Rising like lightning from a thundercloud, slaying thousands of foes in their midst, I will pour forth bone-piercing, vital-point-splitting arrows — then Dhārtarāṣṭra will repent of the war.

**5.47.50**  When he sees the multitudes of sharp-tipped arrows loosed from the mouth of the Gāṇḍīva's string swooping down to take elephants, horses, and armored warriors — then Dhārtarāṣṭra will repent of the war.

**5.47.51**  When that dullard sees the enemy's released arrows being carried back against their course and cut crosswise, being intercepted and severed by my razor-headed shafts — then Dhārtarāṣṭra will repent of the war.

**5.47.52**  When the broad-bladed arrows released from my arm, like birds from a treetop, sever the heads of young warriors — then Dhārtarāṣṭra will repent of the war.

**5.47.53**  When he sees warriors struck by arrows falling from their chariots, from great elephants, and from horses, and brought down on the arena of battle — then Dhārtarāṣṭra will repent of the war.

**5.47.54**  Like Death with his mouth agape and his bow drawn, I will drive off masses of infantry and masses of chariots on all sides with blazing showers of arrows against the enemy — then the dull-witted one will repent.

**5.47.55**  When, as I range across all directions with my chariot, he sees his own army bewildered and cut down by the Gāṇḍīva, overwhelmed by dust — then afterward that dull-witted one will repent.

**5.47.56**  Duryodhana will see his entire army maddened, its limbs severed, unconscious, with its foremost horses, heroes, men, and elephants slain — thirsty, its wings broken, afflicted with fear.

**5.47.57**  Crying out in agony, being slaughtered and slain, the army will be strewn with scattered hair, bones, and skulls, as if Prajāpati's work had been left half-done — seeing this, the dull-witted one will repent.

**5.47.58**  When Dhārtarāṣṭra sees gathered on the chariot in battle: the Gāṇḍīva, Vāsudeva, the divine conch Pāñcajanya, the horses, the two inexhaustible quivers, Devadatta, and me —

**5.47.59**  — when at the end of the age I shall burn the Kauravas like fire, routing the assembled bands of plunderers and ushering in a new age — then Dhṛtarāṣṭra along with his son will be scorched.

**5.47.60**  Together with his brothers, together with his son, together with his army, bereft of sovereignty, a slave to anger, of small intelligence — trembling at the end of his pride when it has been ordained — that fool Dhārtarāṣṭra will afterward repent.

**5.47.61**  Once at dawn, after I had completed my recitations, a brahmin spoke to me pleasantly at the water's edge: 'O Pārtha, a hard task is appointed for you — you must fight against your enemies, O Savyasācin.'

**5.47.62**  'May Indra, the thunderbolt-wielder with the tawny horses, go before you in battle, slaying your enemies; and may Kṛṣṇa Vāsudeva protect you from behind, on your chariot yoked with Sugrīva.'

**5.47.63**  I chose Vāsudeva as my ally in this war from Mahendra, the thunderbolt-wielder. That Kṛṣṇa has been granted to me for the slaying of the plunderers — I deem this ordained for me by the gods.

**5.47.64**  He in whose victory Kṛṣṇa rejoices even in his mind, without himself fighting — that man will certainly overcome all his enemies; of gods led by Indra there is no concern in the human world.

**5.47.65**  He who wishes to defeat Vāsudeva Kṛṣṇa — so splendid, so utterly heroic — in battle would be trying to cross the great ocean, the immeasurable mass of waters, with his bare arms.

**5.47.66**  He who wishes to split a mountain — that immense white mass of rock — with his palm: his own hand, nails and all, would crumble, and he would do nothing at all to the mountain.

**5.47.67**  He who wishes to defeat Vāsudeva in battle would be trying to quench a blazing fire with his arms, to stop the moon and sun in their courses, and to seize by force the immortal nectar of the gods.

**5.47.68**  He who, on a single chariot, overwhelmed the Bhoja kings and forcibly seized their territory, then carried off as his wife Rukmiṇī blazing with glory — from whom the great-souled son of Rukmiṇī was born —

**5.47.69**  He swiftly crushed the Gāndhāras and conquered all the sons of Nagnajit; forcibly releasing, bellowing, the one who had been bound — that jewel of the gods, fair to behold.

**5.47.70**  He slew the Pāṇḍya at Kavāṭa and crushed the Kaliṅgas at Dantakūra. By him was the city of Vārāṇasī burned, rendered masterless for many long years.

**5.47.71**  Ekalavya, the Niṣāda king whom others deemed unconquerable in battle — he lies dead, slain by Kṛṣṇa who struck him like Jambha striking a mountain with speed.

**5.47.72**  Likewise the wicked son of Ugrasena, who oppressed the Vṛṣṇis and Andhakas in their midst — he felled him, together with Baladeva, and having slain him gave the kingdom to Ugrasena.

**5.47.73**  He battled the flying city Saubha, and through his illusion-power struck down Śālva the terrible. At the gate of Saubha he caught the śataghni with his bare arms. What mortal could endure him?

**5.47.74**  There was a fortress called Prāgjyotiṣa, a terrible city of the demons, impossible to storm. There the mighty Naraka, son of Earth, stole the beautiful jeweled earrings from the Ādityas.

**5.47.75**  The gods assembled with Indra could not endure him and were afraid to retrieve the earrings. But when they saw the prowess of Keśava — his irresistible strength and weaponry —

**5.47.76**  — knowing the nature of Keśava, they commissioned Kṛṣṇa for the slaying of the plunderer. He, Vāsudeva — sovereign in his accomplishments — undertook that difficult task.

**5.47.77**  At Nirmocana he slew six thousand, instantly cutting through the razor-edged snares. Having killed Mura and slaughtered Ogha the demon, the hero went on to Nirmocana.

**5.47.78**  Right there a battle took place between him and Viṣṇu of surpassing might. He lies dead, slain by Kṛṣṇa, like a karṇikāra tree knocked down by the wind.

**5.47.79**  Having retrieved those jeweled earrings, and having slain Bhauma Naraka and Mura, the wise one, attended by Śrī and by glory, returned — he of matchless majesty.

**5.47.80**  The gods there bestowed boons upon him, having witnessed that tremendous feat accomplished in battle: 'May you feel no fatigue when fighting; may you walk freely in the sky and in the waters.'

**5.47.81**  'And may weapons find no purchase on your body.' Thus Kṛṣṇa was there fulfilled in his purpose. In Vāsudeva, immeasurable and of great might, the fullness of virtue is ever present.

**5.47.82**  Dhārtarāṣṭra believes he can overcome with force that unendurable Viṣṇu of boundless valor — yet when that evil-souled one reasons about him, he tolerates even that and looks upon us.

**5.47.83**  Whoever thinks that by picking a quarrel with Kṛṣṇa and me in our combined strength it is possible to deprive the Pāṇḍavas of what is theirs — let him go there and find out in the clash of battle.

**5.47.84**  Having paid homage to King Śāntanava, and likewise to Droṇa together with his son, and to Śāradvatī who has no equal in combat — I will fight, desiring the kingdom.

**5.47.85**  I deem the weapons destined by righteousness for the one who will fight the Pāṇḍavas in accordance with dharma. The sons of Pāṇḍu were defeated in a fraudulent game by cruel men and spent twelve years in exile.

**5.47.86**  Having endured the ordained hardship in the forest for a long time, and one year of living incognito — the Dhārtarāṣṭras, now firmly established, cannot suddenly tolerate the lives of the Pāṇḍavas.

**5.47.87**  If they were to defeat us in battle even with gods led by Indra as allies, then adharma practiced would outweigh dharma — that is certain; nothing done would be good.

**5.47.88**  If this man is not bound by his actions, if he does not deem us superior — I, together with Vāsudeva, expect to slay Duryodhana along with all his kin.

**5.47.89**  And if action is not ordained among men, if one's own karma does not exist for a person — then reflecting on both these arguments, the defeat of Dhārtarāṣṭra is surely right.

**5.47.90**  O Kurus, what I declare to you is plain: the Dhārtarāṣṭras will not survive the fighting. The Kurus will not attain what they seek by any means other than battle; there is no survivor for those who refuse to fight here.

**5.47.91**  But I, having slain the Dhārtarāṣṭras along with Karṇa, will win back the entire kingdom of the Kurus. Do each of you what must be done; enjoy your beloved wives and children.

**5.47.92**  Moreover, we have among us aged brahmins who are widely learned, of good character, and of noble lineage — yearly astronomers skilled in astrology, and knowers of the decisive configurations of the asterisms.

**5.47.93**  The high and the low ordained by fate, the divine questions, the MṛgaCakra, the auspicious hours — all of these declare a great destruction for the Kurus and Sṛñjayas, and victory for the Pāṇḍavas.

**5.47.94**  Thus does Ajātaśatru think of us — his purpose accomplished, ready to suppress the enemy. And Janārdana, who knows things not hidden from him, the lion of the Vṛṣṇis sees no doubt in this.

**5.47.95**  And I too know the shape of what is to come; I see it with my own clear-eyed intelligence, ever vigilant. My ancient sight does not waver: the Dhārtarāṣṭras will not survive the battle.

**5.47.96**  Without being touched, the Gāṇḍīva bow yawns open; without being touched, my bowstring quivers; and my arrows, springing themselves from the mouths of the quivers, yearn again and again to fly.

**5.47.97**  My sword emerges brightly from its scabbard, as if a snake shedding its old skin. Voices of fearsome aspect speak from the banner: 'When will your chariot be yoked, O Crowned One?'

**5.47.98**  Packs of jackals cry out in the night; demons spring down from the sky; deer and jackals, blue-necked peacocks and crows, vultures, herons, and hyenas —

**5.47.99**  — and Suparṇas descend and circle behind, as they observe my chariot yoked with white horses. For I alone, raining arrows, could lead all the earthly warriors to the realm of Death.

**5.47.100**  Drawing upon different paths of weaponry — like fire blazing through a thicket in the dry season — the Sthūṇākarṇa, the terrible Pāśupata, and the Brahmāstra that Indra himself knows:

**5.47.101**  — bent on slaughter, releasing them swiftly, I would leave scarcely any people remaining. I will attain peace — for that is the highest state, and this is my firm resolve. O son of Gavalgaṇa, tell them this.

**5.47.102**  He who was constantly counseled again and again by those ministers, and yet believes he can pick a quarrel with the gods led by Indra as his allies — that is Dhārtarāṣṭra. Behold his delusion.

**5.47.103**  The aged Bhīṣma son of Śāntanu, and Kṛpa, and Droṇa together with his son, and the wise Vidura — may whatever all of them say come to pass. May all the Kurus live long.

**5.48.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: O descendant of Bharata, when all those kings had assembled, Bhīṣma the son of Śāntanu spoke these words to Duryodhana.

**5.48.2**  Bṛhaspati and Uśanā had drawn near to Brahmā, and the Maruts together with Indra, and the Vasus together with the Aśvins.

**5.48.3**  The Ādityas, the Sādhyas, and the seven sages in heaven, Viśvāvasu the gandharva, and the lovely hosts of apsarases —

**5.48.4**  — they all came near, bowing to the ancient grandfather of the worlds. Surrounding the lord of all the worlds, the heavenly ones took their seats.

**5.48.5**  Drawing away the minds and the radiance of those heavenly ones, the two primal gods, the sages Nara and Nārāyaṇa, passed by.

**5.48.6**  Bṛhaspati asked Brahmā, 'Who are these two who do not attend upon you? Tell us about them, O Grandfather.'

**5.48.7**  Brahmā said: These two who illuminate the earth and the sky, these ascetics, blazing and shining, who have pervaded and transcended all — they are of great might.

**5.48.8**  These two are Nara and Nārāyaṇa, moving from world to world, vigorous through their own austerity, of great being and valor.

**5.48.9**  These two, by their deeds, have ever delighted the worlds; they are constant; they are worshipped by gods and gandharvas, and they exist for the destruction of the demons.

**5.48.10**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Hearing this, Śakra went to where those two were performing austerities, together with all the hosts of gods headed by Bṛhaspati.

**5.48.11**  Then, when a terrible fear had arisen for the heavenly ones in the battle between gods and demons, he besought the great-souled Nara and Nārāyaṇa for a boon.

**5.48.12**  Those two said, 'Choose.' Then, O best of the Bharatas, Śakra spoke to those two: 'Let your aid be given to us.'

**5.48.13**  Then those two said to Indra, 'We shall do whatever you wish.' Together with them, Indra conquered the Daityas and Dānavas.

**5.48.14**  Nara, the scorcher of foes, in Indra's battle, slew enemies by the hundreds of thousands — the Paulomas and the Kālakhañjas.

**5.48.15**  This one, Arjuna, standing on a swaying chariot, severed the head of Jambha with a broad arrow even as that demon was swallowing the sacrifice in battle.

**5.48.16**  This one shattered the golden city beyond the ocean, slaying sixty thousand Nivata-kavacas in battle.

**5.48.17**  This great-armed Arjuna, conqueror of enemy cities, having vanquished the gods together with Indra, gratified the fire-god. And Nārāyaṇa likewise slew yet many more there.

**5.48.18**  Thus behold these two of great valor come together — Vāsudeva and Arjuna, two heroes, two great warriors united.

**5.48.19**  These two divinities Nara and Nārāyaṇa are the primeval gods, as the scriptures declare — unconquerable in the human world even by gods and demons together with Indra.

**5.48.20**  This Kṛṣṇa is Nārāyaṇa, while Phalguna is known as Nara. Nārāyaṇa and Nara are one essence divided into two.

**5.48.21**  These two, through their deeds, attain imperishable and eternal worlds; they are born again and again wherever they are needed at the time of battle.

**5.48.22**  'Therefore, action alone must be performed,' thus spoke Nārada. He who knows the Vedas declared all this about the wheel of the Vṛṣṇis.

**5.48.23**  When you shall behold Keśava bearing conch, discus, and mace, and Arjuna of the terrible bow taking up his weapons,

**5.48.24**  the two eternal great souls, the two Kṛṣṇas standing on one chariot — then, Duryodhana my child, you shall remember my words.

**5.48.25**  If this destruction of the Kurus were not already at hand, your mind would not be so overwhelmed against both your interest and righteousness.

**5.48.26**  If you do not accept this counsel, you shall hear of very many slain. For all the Kurus attend upon your opinion alone.

**5.48.27**  Yet you alone approve as truth the counsel of only three — including Karṇa who has been cursed by Rāma as well, O bull of the Bharatas.

**5.48.28**  The base-born son of a charioteer, Śakuni the son of Subala, and likewise your wretched, sinful brother Duḥśāsana.

**5.48.29**  Karṇa said: 'Such words should not be spoken by you, longlived one, as you speak to me, O Grandsire. For I stand in the duty of a warrior, not having deviated from my own dharma.'

**5.48.30**  'What other misconduct do you find in me for which you reproach me? The sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra know no fault of mine anywhere.'

**5.48.31**  'I have always done everything pleasing to King Dhṛtarāṣṭra, and likewise to Duryodhana, for he is established in the kingdom.'

**5.48.32**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having heard Karṇa's words, Bhīṣma, the son of Shantanu, again addressed the great king Dhṛtarāṣṭra with these words:

**5.48.33**  'Though this one constantly boasts, "I shall slay the Pāṇḍavas," he is not even a fraction complete compared to the great-souled Pāṇḍavas.'

**5.48.34**  'Know the conduct of this evil-minded son of a charioteer for what it is — the ruination that is coming upon your wicked sons.'

**5.48.35**  'Relying on this one, your slow-witted son Suyodhana has despised those heroes, the sons of gods, those tamers of foes.'

**5.48.36**  'And what supremely difficult deed has this one ever done that could compare to what each one of all the Pāṇḍavas accomplished individually in the past?'

**5.48.37**  'When at the city of Virāṭa he saw his dear brother slain by the valor of Dhanañjaya, what did this one do then?'

**5.48.38**  'When Dhanañjaya attacked all the Kurus together, routed them, and recaptured the cattle — where was this one then, gone away?'

**5.48.39**  'When your son was being carried away by the Gandharvas during the cattle-expedition, where was this son of a charioteer who now struts about like a bull?'

**5.48.40**  'Was it not there too that those Gandharvas were defeated by Pārtha, by the great-souled Bhīma, and by the twins who came to the rescue?'

**5.48.41**  'These are the many false boasts of this man, O bull of the Bharatas — he who is ever a braggart, ever transgressing dharma and artha. May good fortune attend you.'

**5.48.42**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having heard Bhīṣma's words, the great-minded son of Bharadvaja — Droṇa — honoring him, spoke thus to Dhṛtarāṣṭra in the midst of the kings:

**5.48.43**  'What the foremost of the Bharatas, Bhīṣma, has said — let that be done, O king. You should not act on the word of those who are greedy for gain at the cost of desire.'

**5.48.44**  'Before the battle, I think it good to come to terms with the Pāṇḍavas. The words that were spoken by Arjuna and reported by Sañjaya —'

**5.48.45**  'All that I fully know, and the Pāṇḍava will do it. For there is no equal to him as an archer in all three worlds.'

**5.48.46**  But disregarding that meaningful counsel of Droṇa and Bhīṣma, the king then questioned Sañjaya about the Pāṇḍava.

**5.48.47**  From that very moment all the Kurus became hopeless of life, when the king did not properly heed Bhīṣma and Droṇa.

**5.49.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: 'What did that Pāṇḍava king, the son of Dharma, say upon hearing that these many armies had gathered against him?'

**5.49.2**  'What does Yudhiṣṭhira seek, preparing to fight with passion? Upon whose face among his brothers and sons does he look in deliberation?'

**5.49.3**  'Who dissuade him saying "make peace," and who urge "fight"? Who, by treacherous means, have angered this knower of dharma, this follower of righteousness?'

**5.49.4**  Sañjaya said: 'The Pāñcālas along with the Pāṇḍavas look to the face of King Yudhiṣṭhira — may good fortune be yours — and he commands them all.'

**5.49.5**  'The chariot-hosts of the Pāṇḍavas and Pāñcālas, assembled separately, welcome Yudhiṣṭhira, the son of Kuntī, as he approaches.'

**5.49.6**  'The Pāñcālas welcome the son of Kuntī blazing with splendor as though the sun rising from darkness, like a rising mass of radiance.'

**5.49.7**  'From the cowherds and shepherds upward, all rejoice in Yudhiṣṭhira; the Pāñcālas, the Kekayas, and the Matsyas welcome the Pāṇḍava.'

**5.49.8**  'Brahmin women, princesses, and the daughters of the merchant class come out at play to see Pārtha arrayed for battle.'

**5.49.9**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: 'Tell me, Sañjaya, with whom have the Pāṇḍavas engaged against us? The Somakas with Dhṛṣṭadyumna as their general — like what?'

**5.49.10**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Gavalgaṇa's son, questioned thus in the assembly of the Kurus, heaved a long and heavy sigh, seeming to reflect again and again; then, for no apparent reason, by the force of fate, a swoon came over the charioteer.

**5.49.11**  Then a man declared in the royal assembly: 'O great king, this Sañjaya has fainted and fallen to the ground; he utters no words, his awareness diminished, his consciousness faint.'

**5.49.12**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: 'Sañjaya must have beheld the great chariot-warriors who are the sons of Kuntī; his mind has been greatly agitated by those tiger-like men.'

**5.49.13**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Sañjaya, having regained consciousness and composure, said this to Dhṛtarāṣṭra, O great king, in the assembly of the Kurus:

**5.49.14**  'I have seen, O king of kings, the great chariot-warriors who are the sons of Kuntī, emaciated from their confinement during their stay in the house of the Matsya king. Listen, O great king, to those with whom the Pāṇḍavas have engaged.'

**5.49.15**  'He who, for the sake of neither anger, nor fear, nor desire, nor material gain, nor logical argument, would ever abandon truth — the righteous-souled one —'

**5.49.16**  'He who is the standard in righteousness, O great king — the foremost among upholders of dharma — by that Ajātaśatru, the Pāṇḍavas have engaged.'

**5.49.17**  'He who has no equal on earth in the strength of his arms, that bowman who brought all kings under his sway — by that Bhīmasena the Pāṇḍavas have engaged against you.'

**5.49.18**  'When they escaped from the house of lac and from Hiḍimba, the man-eater, he who became their refuge — Vṛkodara, the son of Kuntī —'

**5.49.19**  'And where the king of the Sindhus dragged away Yājñasenī, there too he became their refuge — Vṛkodara, the son of Kuntī.'

**5.49.20**  'And he who freed all the Pāṇḍavas assembled together while burning at Vāraṇāvata — by him they have engaged against you.'

**5.49.21**  'By whom, to bring delight to Kṛṣṇa, those demons overcome by rage were slain, having entered the terrible and impassable mountain Gandhamādana —'

**5.49.22**  'He whose strength equal to ten thousand elephants is concentrated in the essence of his arms — by that Bhīmasena the Pāṇḍavas have engaged against you.'

**5.49.23**  'The hero who, with Kṛṣṇa as his second, once conquered Indra himself who was fighting him, in order to gratify the fire-god —'

**5.49.24**  'He who gratified the great god himself, Giresha, the trident-bearer, the god of gods, the husband of Umā, by combat —'

**5.49.25**  'That bowman who brought all the world-guardians under his sway — by that Vijaya in battle the Pāṇḍavas have engaged against you.'

**5.49.26**  'He who brought the western quarter under his sway together with its multitudes of barbarians — that Nakula, a fighter of varied combat, stands there as warrior.'

**5.49.27**  'By that handsome hero, that supreme bowman, the son of Mādrī, O Kauravya, the Pāṇḍavas have engaged against you.'

**5.49.28**  'He who conquered by battle the Kāśis, the Aṅgas, the Māgadhas, and the Kaliṅgas — by that Sahadeva the Pāṇḍavas have engaged against you.'

**5.49.29**  'Those who equal him in valor are only four men on earth: Aśvatthāmā, Dhṛṣṭaketu, Pradyumna, and Rukmin.'

**5.49.30**  'By that Sahadeva, the younger, the hero among men, the joy of Mādrī, the Pāṇḍavas have engaged against you.'

**5.49.31**  'She who was the virtuous princess of Kāśi and performed terrible austerities of old, even after death wishing for the slaying of Bhīṣma, O bull of the Bharatas —'

**5.49.32**  'She was born as the daughter of the Pāñcāla king, and by fate he was born again as a male — that tiger among men who knows the qualities and defects of both woman and man.'

**5.49.33**  'By Śikhaṇḍī, that Pāñcāla, maddened for battle, who attacked the Kaliṅgas, trained in arms, you Kurus have been engaged.'

**5.49.34**  'The one whom a yaksha made into a man — for the purpose of slaying Bhīṣma, it is said — by that great bowman of fierce power the Pāṇḍavas have engaged.'

**5.49.35**  'The great bowmen, the five royal brothers of Kekaya, their armor well-polished, those heroes — by them too they have engaged against you.'

**5.49.36**  'He who is long-armed, swift with weapons, steadfast, truly valiant — by that hero of the Vṛṣṇis, Yuyudhāna, battle has been engaged against you.'

**5.49.37**  'He who was a refuge for the great-souled Pāṇḍavas at the time of need — by that Virāṭa in battle the Pāṇḍavas have engaged.'

**5.49.38**  'He who is the lord of Kāśi, the king, the great chariot-warrior of Vārāṇasī — he became their fighter; by him they have engaged against you.'

**5.49.39**  'By the young sons of Draupadī, difficult to conquer in battle, great-souled, whose touch is like that of a venomous serpent — by them the Pāṇḍavas have engaged.'

**5.49.40**  'He who equals Kṛṣṇa in valor and Yudhiṣṭhira in self-restraint — by that Abhimanyu in battle the Pāṇḍavas have engaged.'

**5.49.41**  'And he who is matchless in valor, Dhṛṣṭaketu of great fame, unbearable when angry in battle, the great chariot-warrior son of Śiśupāla — by that king of the Cedis the Pāṇḍavas have engaged against you.'

**5.49.42**  'He who is the refuge of the Pāṇḍavas as Vāsava is the refuge of the gods — by that Vāsudeva the Pāṇḍavas have engaged against you.'

**5.49.43**  'Likewise the brother of the Cedi king, Śarabha, O bull of the Bharatas, together with Karakarṣa — by those two they have engaged against you.'

**5.49.44**  'Jarāsandha's son Sahadeva, and Jayadratha — both of them — and Drupada of great splendor, surrounded by a great force, having dedicated himself, stands ready to fight for the sake of the Pāṇḍavas.'

**5.49.45**  'These and many others — kings of the east and the north — hundreds of them, relying upon whom the king of righteousness stands arrayed.'

**5.50.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: All these warriors of great valor whom you have enumerated — even if all of them were gathered together on one side, Bhīma alone would stand on the other.

**5.50.2**  My fear of Bhīmasena arises ever greater, Sañjaya — of that enraged, implacable warrior, as of a tiger before a great roebuck.

**5.50.3**  I lie awake through all the nights, breathing long hot sighs, afraid of Vṛkodara, dear Sañjaya, as a weak animal is afraid before a lion.

**5.50.4**  For in this army I see no one equal in might to that mighty-armed one whose splendor rivals Indra's — no one who could withstand him in battle.

**5.50.5**  That son of Kuntī, that Pāṇḍava, is implacable and relentless in enmity — he does not laugh without cause, he seems frenzied, he glances sidelong, and his voice is thunderous.

**5.50.6**  Of tremendous speed, tremendous energy, mighty-armed, of immense strength — he will bring an end through battle to my foolish sons.

**5.50.7**  Vṛkodara, bearing the mace seized in the grip of his thighs, stands like Death with rod in hand as the bull among the Kurus in battle.

**5.50.8**  In my mind I see that terrible mace of his — made of iron and steel, adorned with gold — raised like the staff of Brahmā.

**5.50.9**  As a powerful lion roams among herds of deer, so will Bhīma range through my armies.

**5.50.10**  Of all my sons he alone is of fierce valor — a great eater, contrary and always impetuous even in childhood.

**5.50.11**  My heart trembles when I recall how even in childhood Duryodhana and the others, fighting against him, were crushed like creatures by an elephant.

**5.50.12**  My sons have always been tormented by his prowess. He alone — Bhīma of terrible valor — has been the cause of the breach between us.

**5.50.13**  I seem to see Bhīma before me in battle, swallowing whole the armies of men, elephants, and horses, maddened with rage.

**5.50.14**  Tell me, Sañjaya, of that hero Bhīmasena the implacable — equal to Droṇa and Arjuna in weaponry, equal to the wind in speed.

**5.50.15**  I count it a great gain that that slayer of enemies, that high-spirited one, did not kill all my sons right then and there.

**5.50.16**  He by whose might the Yakṣas and Rākṣasas of great strength were slain — how shall any human withstand his onslaught in battle?

**5.50.17**  He never remained under my control even as a child, Sañjaya — how much less now, when he has been wronged and tormented by my wicked sons?

**5.50.18**  Hard and cruel by nature, he would sooner break than bend; with his sidelong glance and knitted brows — how could Vṛkodara ever be pacified?

**5.50.19**  Broad-shouldered, matchless in strength, fair-complexioned, towering like a palm tree — Bhīmasena by measure exceeds Arjuna by a span.

**5.50.20**  In speed he surpasses horses; in strength he surpasses elephants; the middle Pāṇḍava of muted speech, with the eyes of a bee, is immensely powerful.

**5.50.21**  Thus did I hear from the mouth of Vyāsa himself in former times of this Pāṇḍava as a child — his form and his valor described truly and accurately.

**5.50.22**  With his iron rod, Bhīma — the foremost of strikers — when enraged will destroy in battle chariots, elephants, horses, and men.

**5.50.23**  Implacable, ever-wrathful, fierce, of terrible valor — he who previously acted against me and was dishonored, dear Sañjaya.

**5.50.24**  His mace of iron and steel — massive, well-jointed, gold-adorned, a slayer of hundreds, resounding with a hundred peals — how shall my sons be able to withstand it?

**5.50.25**  Like a boundless, unfathomable ocean swift as arrows — Bhīmasena is a fortress impossible to breach, dear Sañjaya, yet the fools wish to cross him.

**5.50.26**  The young fools who fancy themselves wise do not listen even as I cry out; seeing only the honey, they do not perceive the precipice ahead.

**5.50.27**  Those who engage in battle with this Wind in human form are surely urged on by fate — like great deer goaded toward a lion.

**5.50.28**  His iron-and-steel mace — four cubits long, six-edged, of immeasurable power, whose touch brings agony — how shall my sons endure its blow, dear Sañjaya?

**5.50.29**  As he whirls his mace and splits the heads of elephants, licking the corners of his mouth, tears flowing again and again —

**5.50.30**  — charging headlong in aimed attacks, uttering terrible roars, lunging against maddened elephants, roaring back at them —

**5.50.31**  — plunging into the chariot lanes and striking down warriors one by one — could my people escape even from blazing fire such as his?

**5.50.32**  That mighty-armed one, clearing a path, routing my army, dancing as it were with mace in hand, will show them the end of an age.

**5.50.33**  Like a rut-maddened elephant breaking flowering trees, Vṛkodara will plunge into the army of my sons in battle.

**5.50.34**  That tiger among men will strip the chariots of their warriors and banners and shatter their wheels, and he will tear down chariot-fighters and cavalry alike.
