**5.70.13**  What could be more painful than this — that I cannot provide for my mother, for my father, or for my friends, O Janārdana?

**5.70.14**  With the Kāśi, the Cedi, the Pāñcāla, and the Matsya as allies, and with you as our lord, O Madhusūdana, I have sought only five villages.

**5.70.15**  Kushastala, Vrukasthala, Asandi, Vāraṇāvata, and O Govinda, whatever the fifth place among these may be.

**5.70.16**  Let five be given to us, my dear — whether villages or towns — in which we may dwell together, so that the Bharatas may not perish.

**5.70.17**  Yet even those the wicked-souled Dhārtarāṣṭra will not consent to, considering himself the sole lord. What could be more painful than this?

**5.70.18**  In one well-born, grown old, yet covetous of another's wealth — greed destroys wisdom; and wisdom, once destroyed, slays shame.

**5.70.19**  Shame, once slain, oppresses dharma; dharma, once slain, destroys prosperity; prosperity, once destroyed, slays the man; and the destruction of a man is his destitution.

**5.70.20**  From the destitute man, kinsmen, friends, and priests depart, O dear one — as birds depart from a tree that has neither flowers nor fruit.

**5.70.21**  This indeed is death, O dear one — that kinsmen turn away from one who has fallen, as the vital breaths withdraw from a dying being.

**5.70.22**  Śambara declared that there is no more wretched condition than this — where no food is seen either today or tomorrow.

**5.70.23**  They say that wealth is the highest dharma; in wealth all things are established. The wealthy live in the world; those men without wealth are as good as dead.

**5.70.24**  Those who, relying on their own strength, strip a man of his wealth — they crush dharma, artha, and kama, and that man himself.

**5.70.25**  Reaching this condition, some men have chosen death; some have gone to the village, some to the forest, and some have taken to wandering in despair.

**5.70.26**  Some cultivate madness; others fall under the sway of enemies; some fall into servitude to others for the sake of wealth.

**5.70.27**  Calamity is heavier for a man than death itself — the destruction of prosperity is indeed the cause of the ruin of both dharma and desire for him.

**5.70.28**  For that death which is his righteous end, that eternal way of the world — no being whatsoever escapes it on all sides.

**5.70.29**  A man who is poor by nature is not afflicted so greatly, O Kṛṣṇa, as one who has obtained good fortune and then, having been nourished in ease, is deprived of it.

**5.70.30**  He, having reached great misfortune through his own fault, blames the gods along with Indra — but never blames himself in any way.

**5.70.31**  And no amount of scripture can restrain him from blame; he grows angry at his servants and becomes envious of his friends.

**5.70.32**  Wrath then overtakes him; he becomes further deluded; and having fallen under the power of delusion, he pursues cruel deeds.

**5.70.33**  Through excess of sinful deeds he nurtures confusion; confusion leads only to hell — that is the final destination of sinful deeds.

**5.70.34**  If he does not awaken, O Kṛṣṇa, he goes only to hell; his awakening is wisdom itself, and the eye of wisdom is never destroyed.

**5.70.35**  When a man gains wisdom he then studies the scriptures; and one ever devoted to scripture regains dharma — the highest limb of which is shame.

**5.70.36**  The man endowed with shame hates sin; his prosperity grows; and as long as that man prospers, so long does he truly be a man.

**5.70.37**  Ever devoted to dharma, calm in soul, ever diligent in the performance of duty — he does not turn his mind to adharma and does not dwell in sinful acts.

**5.70.38**  One who is without shame, or who is thoroughly deluded — such a one is neither woman nor man; he has no claim to dharma, just as a Śūdra has none.

**5.70.39**  The person endowed with shame protects the gods, the ancestors, and himself as well; through that he attains immortality — that is the final destination of virtuous deeds.

**5.70.40**  All this you can see directly in my case, O Madhusūdana — how, fallen from my kingdom, I dwell in these lodgings.

**5.70.41**  We cannot rightly abandon prosperity under any argument; and if death comes upon us while we strive for it, even that would be good.

**5.70.42**  The first option for us would be, O Mādhava, that we and they become peaceful and equal in spirit, and that we together enjoy that prosperity.

**5.70.43**  The ultimate extreme — born from the destruction of terrible deeds — would be that we slay the Kauravas and then enjoy those kingdoms.

**5.70.44**  Even enemies who are unrelated and ignoble must be slain — how much more so those who are of this kind?

**5.70.45**  For our allies are mostly kinsmen and elders — the slaying of them is exceedingly sinful. What then is glorious in battle?

**5.70.46**  This dharma of the Kṣatriya is sinful, and we are bound by kinship to Kṣatriyas — it is our own prescribed duty, whether dharma or adharma, since any other livelihood is condemned.

**5.70.47**  The Śūdra lives by service, the Vaiśyas by trade and commerce; we warriors live by slaying. The skull-bowl is what the Brahmins seek — each order follows its own mode of life.

**5.70.48**  A Kṣatriya slays a Kṣatriya, a fish lives by devouring other fish, a dog kills a dog — O Dāśārha, behold how dharma has come about in this world.

**5.70.49**  In battle, O Kṛṣṇa, strife is ever-present and lives perish in the clash; strength serves only to guide strategy, while in obstinate combat both victory and defeat arise.

**5.70.50**  Neither life nor death of living beings comes about by their own will alone; nor can pleasure or pain be obtained outside their proper time, O best of the Yadus.

**5.70.51**  One man alone may slay many, and many may together slay one; a coward may kill a hero, and a man of no glory may kill one who is glorious.

**5.70.52**  Victory and defeat are seen for both sides; likewise decline is seen for both — in retreat come diminution and loss.

**5.70.53**  War is sinful in every way — who strikes without being struck in return? And for one who is slain, O Hrishikesha, victory and defeat are equal.

**5.70.54**  I consider defeat to be no better than death. And even for one who gains victory, O Kṛṣṇa, decline is certain in the end.

**5.70.55**  In the end some people kill even those who are beloved; when that weakened man sees his sons and brothers no more, O Kṛṣṇa, disgust with life arises on every side.

**5.70.56**  Those heroes who are modest, noble, and sensitive to compassion are precisely the ones who fall in battle; it is the lesser men who survive.

**5.70.57**  Even after slaying enemies, O Janārdana, there is ever remorse; and in this, sinful consequence follows after, and a remnant always remains.

**5.70.58**  For that remnant, gaining strength, would not leave a remnant of its own; it strives for total annihilation, wishing to put an end to the enmity.

**5.70.59**  Victory begets enmity; the defeated one abides in sorrow; one who has laid aside both victory and defeat sleeps in peace and happiness.

**5.70.60**  A man who has given birth to enmity sleeps in misery every day, his mind without contentment, like a man in a house where a serpent dwells.

**5.70.61**  One who uproots everything is bereft of fame; he acquires eternal infamy among all living beings.

**5.70.62**  Enmities do not cease, even those created over a long time; there are those who recount them, and men are born again in the clan to avenge them.

**5.70.63**  Nor is enmity ever quelled by enmity, O Keśava; just as fire fed with oblations, O Kṛṣṇa, only grows ever greater.

**5.70.64**  There is no peace other than through a different way; those who always seek an opening for attack — this fault is unceasing for them.

**5.70.65**  The affliction born of manly striving is powerful and torments the heart; peace from it comes either through renunciation or through the withdrawal of the mind.

**5.70.66**  Or by striking at the very root of enemies, O Madhusūdana, a blazing cessation of fruit might come about — but that would be even more cruel.

**5.70.67**  The peace that comes through renunciation — without that, it is slaughter itself; and likewise there is the complete annihilation of enemies, and of oneself, from uncertainty.

**5.70.68**  We do not wish to abandon that, nor do we wish the destruction of the clan. In this situation, the peace that comes through humble submission is indeed the most worthy course.

**5.70.69**  For those who strive in every way and desire non-war, when conciliation is repulsed, war becomes well-established — the result of lack of valor for one side.

**5.70.70**  Through the rejection of conciliation something terrible is set in motion; wise men have observed this — it is like the behavior of dogs over a footprint.

**5.70.71**  Tail-wagging, snarling, growling back and forth, turning around, baring of teeth, and howling — from these comes the start of a fight.

**5.70.72**  There the stronger one, O Kṛṣṇa, having conquered, devours that prey. Even so among men there is no distinction whatsoever — it is the same.

**5.70.73**  In every way this is fitting behavior of the strong toward the weak — disregard and opposition; for the weak man indeed submits in supplication.

**5.70.74**  A father, a king, and an elder deserve honor in every way; therefore Dhṛtarāṣṭra is worthy of honor and worship, O Janārdana.

**5.70.75**  But the love for his son is powerful in Dhṛtarāṣṭra, O Mādhava; fallen under the sway of his son, he will abandon any submission to us.

**5.70.76**  In that situation, what do you think, O Kṛṣṇa, is the course befitting the moment? How may we not fall short in both material purpose and righteousness, O Mādhava?

**5.70.77**  In this difficulty of purpose, O Madhusūdana, whom else could I fittingly consult apart from you, O best of men?

**5.70.78**  You are dear to us and you desire our welfare; you know the right course in all matters — who is there for us like you, O Kṛṣṇa, a friend who knows every resolution?

**5.70.79**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Thus addressed, Janārdana replied to the king of dharma: 'For the benefit of you both, I will go to the assembly of the Kurus.'

**5.70.80**  'If I achieve peace there without sacrificing your interests, O king, that would be a very great meritorious act for me, bearing a great reward.'

**5.70.81**  'I would free from the noose of death the enraged Kurus and Sṛñjayas, the Pāṇḍavas, the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, and this entire earth.'

**5.70.82**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: It is not my view, O Kṛṣṇa, that you should go to the Kurus — Suyodhana will not act upon your words even if they are well-spoken.

**5.70.83**  The assembled royal Kṣatriyas who follow Suyodhana's will — I do not welcome your entering into their midst, O Kṛṣṇa.

**5.70.84**  Neither wealth nor divine status — still less happiness — nor even the sovereignty of all the gods would please us if it came at the cost of obstructing you, O Mādhava.

**5.70.85**  The Blessed Lord said: I know, O great king, the wickedness of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son; yet we shall become beyond reproach in the eyes of all kings in every world.

**5.70.86**  Nor indeed are all the kings assembled together sufficient to stand before me when I am angered — just as lesser creatures cannot stand before a lion.

**5.70.87**  And if they were to act against me in any improper way, I would burn all the Kurus to ashes — so my mind resolves.

**5.70.88**  The journey there will certainly not be fruitless, O Pārtha; either the attainment of the purpose will come at some point, or at the very least there will be freedom from reproach.

**5.70.89**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: Whatever pleases you, O Kṛṣṇa — go well to the Kauravas; I shall see you return with your purpose accomplished and in good health.

**5.70.90**  O Viṣvaksena, O lord, going to the Kurus, may you pacify the Bharatas so that we may all be together with glad minds and serene hearts.

**5.70.91**  You are a brother, you are a friend, and you are dear to Bībhatsu and to me; by reason of our friendship you are beyond suspicion — go well for the good of all.

**5.70.92**  You know us, you know the others, you know the purpose, you know the proper words — whatever is for our benefit, O Kṛṣṇa, that is what you should say to Suyodhana.

**5.70.93**  Whatever words joined with righteousness and beneficial counsel may be appropriate, those speak, O Keśava — whether conciliatory or otherwise.

**5.71.1**  The Blessed Lord said: I have heard Sañjaya's words and I have heard yours; I know in full the intentions both of them and of you.

**5.71.2**  Your understanding is grounded in dharma; their mind is grounded in enmity. What can be gained without war — that would be greatly esteemed by you.

**5.71.3**  But that is not the final duty of a Kṣatriya, O lord of peoples; all those who live in the stages of life say that a Kṣatriya should not live by begging.

**5.71.4**  Victory or death in battle — this is eternally ordained by the Creator; this is the own-duty of a Kṣatriya. Weakness of spirit is not commended.

**5.71.5**  Resorting to faint-heartedness, livelihood cannot be sustained, O Yudhiṣṭhira. Show valor, O mighty-armed one; slay the enemies, O subduer of foes.

**5.71.6**  The sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra are excessively greedy, they have cultivated alliances, they have dwelt together for a long time, they have made friends and built up strength, O scorcher of foes.

**5.71.7**  There is no means by which they would treat you as an equal, O lord of peoples; for they consider themselves powerful on account of Bhīṣma, Droṇa, Kṛpa, and others.

**5.71.8**  And for as long as you attend to them with gentleness, O king, for that long will they take away your kingdom, O subduer of foes.

**5.71.9**  Neither out of compassion, nor out of weakness, nor for the sake of dharma or material interest are the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra capable of fulfilling your desire, O subduer of foes.

**5.71.10**  This very reason — that the Pāṇḍavas did not repent even of wearing a loincloth for your sake, difficult as that was — this is the cause that now stands before you.

**5.71.11**  Before the grandfather Bhīṣma, before Droṇa, before the wise Vidura, before the assembled chiefs of the Kurus who saw everything as it truly was —

**5.71.12**  — he who is given to charity, gentle, self-controlled, devoted to dharma, and faithful — that you, O king, he deceived by fraud through gambling; and that wicked, cruel man feels no shame on account of that act.

**5.71.13**  With one of such character and conduct, O king, do not show affection; he deserves to be slain by all the world — how much more so by you, O Bharata.

**5.71.14**  With words utterly unfitting, he taunted one who did not deserve it; he boasts and speaks joyfully among his brothers.

**5.71.15**  'The Pāṇḍavas have nothing at all of their own here — not even their name and lineage will remain to them.'

**5.71.16**  'After a great length of time their ruin will come; those people who have lost their following will return to their natural state.'

**5.71.17**  Uttering these and other harsh words, he boasts among his kinsmen now that you have gone out to the forest.

**5.71.18**  Those who were gathered there, seeing you blameless yet wronged, sat in the assembly hall at that time with tears in their throats, weeping.

**5.71.19**  Those kings did not applaud him; together with the Brahmins, all the members of that assembly condemned Duryodhana there.

**5.71.20**  The censure of a man of noble birth, and slaying — O crusher of enemies, slaying is a greater virtue, O king; but censure is a base way of living.

**5.71.21**  He was slain at that very moment, O king, the moment he became shameless and was condemned, O great king, among all the kings of the earth.

**5.71.22**  The slaying of one whose character is like this requires little effort; propped up and stiffened artificially, he is like a tree whose roots have been cut.

**5.71.23**  That ignoble, evil-minded man deserves to be slain by all the world, like a serpent. Slay him, O destroyer of enemies — do not hesitate, O king.

**5.71.24**  In every way this is fitting for you, and it pleases me also, O sinless one — that you should offer humble submission to your father and to Bhīṣma.

**5.71.25**  But I, going there, will cut away the doubt of all those in the world who are of two minds regarding Duryodhana, O king.

**5.71.26**  In the midst of the kings assembled there, I will proclaim your virtues befitting a true man, and also his transgressions.

**5.71.27**  Having heard my words spoken there — words joined with righteousness and purpose, and truly beneficial — all the kings and rulers of various countries —

**5.71.28**  — will recognize in you a soul of dharma and a speaker of truth; and in him they will discover how he acted out of greed.

**5.71.29**  And I will reproach him even among the townspeople and country folk, taking in the old and the young alike, in the assembly of the four orders of society.

**5.71.30**  If, while you are seeking peace there, dharma is not obtained, the kings will censure the Kurus and Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**5.71.31**  When he who is abandoned by the world is slain — when Duryodhana is slain — O king, what other task will remain to be done?

**5.71.32**  And going to all the Kurus, without sacrificing your interests, I will strive to bring about peace and I will observe their conduct closely.

**5.71.33**  And going there, having observed the Kauravas' course of action — one bent on war — I will return for the sake of your victory, O Bharata.

**5.71.34**  In every way I foresee war with our enemies; for all the omens appear to me as portending just that.

**5.71.35**  Beasts and birds cry out in dreadful tones; at dusk, terrible forms appear among the foremost elephants and horses; fire blazes forth in many ghastly hues — destruction-bearing and fearsome. Were the destroyer not already at hand, it would not be so.

**5.71.36**  Having arrayed weapons, armour, chariots, and elephants, and all banners, and with every warrior rested and ready — let them be alert upon elephants, horses, and chariots. Whatever must be won in battle, make all of it complete, O lord of men.

**5.71.37**  Duryodhana is utterly unable to restore it to you while he yet lives, O king — that prosperous realm that was once yours, O foremost of the Pāṇḍavas, stolen from you at the dice game.

**5.72.1**  Bhīmasena said: Speak only in whatever way peace may come about for the Kurus, O Madhusūdana; do not threaten them with war.

**5.72.2**  Intolerant, perpetually enraged, contemptuous of what is good — though high-minded — Duryodhana must not be addressed harshly; deal with him through conciliation alone.

**5.72.3**  He is wicked by nature, of the same mind as brigands, intoxicated with the pride of sovereignty, and one who has made enmity with the Pāṇḍavas.

**5.72.4**  Shortsighted, cruel, contemptuous, ferocious in his valour, long-bearing in anger, ungovernable, evil-souled, and fond of deceit —

**5.72.5**  Even if he were to die, he would not relent, nor would he ever abandon his own view. Peace with such a one, O Kṛṣṇa, I consider supremely difficult.

**5.72.6**  He is ungrateful even toward friends, has abandoned righteousness, is fond of falsehood, and strikes back against the words and minds of his own well-wishers.

**5.72.7**  Fallen under the sway of anger and fixed in his corrupt nature, he pursues evil according to his very character — like a serpent pricked by blades of grass.

**5.72.8**  Duryodhana along with his army is known to you in every way — what his disposition is, what his nature, what his strength, and what his prowess.

**5.72.9**  In former days the Kurus with their sons, and we as well, lived joyfully together with our kinsmen, as though with Indra foremost among us.

**5.72.10**  Through Duryodhana's wrath, O Madhusūdana, the Bharatas will be consumed like forests by fire at the close of winter.

**5.72.11**  These eighteen renowned kings, O Madhusūdana, utterly destroyed their kinsmen, friends, and relatives alike —

**5.72.12**  Among the flourishing Asuras blazing with splendour, when the appointed time of righteousness arrived, Bali was born —

**5.72.13**  Udavarta among the Haihayas, Janamejaya among the Nipas, Bahula among the Tālajaṅghas, Uddhata Vasu among the Krimis —

**5.72.14**  Ajabindu among the Suviras, Kusardhika among the Saurashtras, Arkaja among the Balihas, Dhautamulaka among the Chinas —

**5.72.15**  Hayagrīva among the Videhas, Varapra among the mighty Mahaujasas, Bahu among the Sundaravegas, Pururava among the Diptakshas —

**5.72.16**  Sahaja among the Cedi-Matsyas, Bṛhadbala among the Pracetas, Dhāraṇa among the Indravatsas, Vigahana among the Mukutas —

**5.72.17**  And Shama among the Nandivegas — these disgracers of their families, O Kṛṣṇa, were born at the end of their respective ages as the lowest of men within their clans.

**5.72.18**  Could this Duryodhana be for us Kurus that brand upon the family, nurtured by time at the end of the age — the lowest of men, a sinful wretch?

**5.72.19**  Therefore speak to him gently and gradually, with words rooted in righteousness and benefit, richly invoking his desires — not harshly, for he is ferocious in valour.

**5.72.20**  O Kṛṣṇa, let all of us even bow low before Duryodhana; let us descend to humility and follow along — only let the Bharatas not perish.

**5.72.21**  If only there could be a neutral standing — O Vāsudeva, act in whatever way allows us to live alongside the Kurus without dishonour touching them.

**5.72.22**  Speak also to the aged grandfather Bhīṣma and to the members of the assembly, O Kṛṣṇa: let there be brotherhood among the brothers, and let the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra be appeased.

**5.72.23**  I say this, and the king agrees; Arjuna likewise is no seeker of war — for compassion runs deep in Arjuna.

**5.73.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Hearing these unprecedented words of Bhīma, who had fallen into such uncharacteristic softness, the mighty-armed Keśava smiled.

**5.73.2**  Reckoning it like lightness in a mountain, or coolness in fire, the younger brother of Rāma — Śauri, bearer of the Śārṅga bow — addressed Vṛkodara:

**5.73.3**  Enkindling him with words as the wind kindles fire, Kṛṣṇa spoke to Bhīma who sat there overwhelmed with pity.

**5.73.4**  At other times, O Bhīmasena, you praise only war, eager to slay and crush those cruel sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**5.73.5**  You do not sleep but lie awake face-down, O scorcher of foes, ever speaking harsh, disturbing, and wrathful words.

**5.73.6**  Breathing out breath the colour of fire, consumed by your own rage, with your mind unquenched, O Bhīma — like a fire still trailing smoke.

**5.73.7**  You lie alone in corners groaning, like one weighed down and weary; some who do not know you even think you have lost your mind.

**5.73.8**  Wrenching up trees and uprooting them like an elephant snapping branches, striking the ground with your feet, O Bhīma, you roam about groaning.

**5.73.9**  You take no pleasure in the company of people, O Pāṇḍava, but dwell in solitude; neither night nor day do you ever greet anyone with joy.

**5.73.10**  Suddenly you smile for no reason in private, then sit as though weeping; you sit for long with your head resting on your knees, eyes half-closed.

**5.73.11**  Again and again you are seen knitting your brows and licking your lips — all of it, O Bhīma, is the work of rage.

**5.73.12**  Just as the sun is seen rising brilliant in the east and, once released, revolves with its rays steadfastly in the west —

**5.73.13**  So truly I say: there is no deviation from it — 'I will approach the intolerant Duryodhana with my mace and slay him!' —

**5.73.14**  Thus you once solemnly grasped your mace in the midst of your brothers, pledging it. Yet now, O scorcher of foes, your mind turns toward peace with him.

**5.73.15**  Ah — when the time of battle is upon us, you see as contrary to war what is not contrary to it at all. Has fear overtaken you, O Bhīma?

**5.73.16**  Ah, O Pārtha, you see omens as reversed — both in the borderland of sleep and in full waking — and therefore you desire peace.

**5.73.17**  Ah — you hope for nothing of your own manliness, like a eunuch! You are seized by faint-heartedness, and by that your mind is perverted.

**5.73.18**  Your heart trembles, your mind sinks into despondency, you are gripped by the paralysis of your thighs — and therefore you desire peace.

**5.73.19**  The mind of a mortal is indeed impermanent, O Pārtha, ever wavering — like the seed pod of a silk-cotton tree shaken loose by a gust of wind.

**5.73.20**  This corrupted understanding of yours — like the speech of cattle become human — is drowning the minds of the sons of Pāṇḍu like men who cannot swim.

**5.73.21**  This is a great wonder to me — like a mountain moving — that you, Bhīmasena, should speak words so unlike yourself.

**5.73.22**  Seeing your own deeds and your birth in this lineage, O Bharata, rise up! Do not give way to despondency, O hero — be steadfast.

**5.73.23**  This faint-heartedness is not fitting for you, O crusher of foes. What a warrior cannot win through prowess, he cannot enjoy either.

**5.74.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Thus addressed by Vāsudeva, the ever-wrathful and intolerant one sprang forward like a fine horse and then spoke in reply.

**5.74.2**  You think of me as different from what I intend, O Acyuta — one who is utterly disciplined in spirit and of proven valour in battle.

**5.74.3**  You know my character, O descendant of Dāśārha, having lived with me a long time — or do you perhaps not know me, like shallow water mistaking itself for a deep lake? Therefore you address me with words that do not suit me.

**5.74.4**  How could anyone who knows me as Bhīmasena, O Mādhava, speak to me in such unsuitable terms — just as you have seen fit to speak to me?

**5.74.5**  Therefore I shall declare this, O joy of the Vṛṣṇis — my own manliness and strength, which are not equal to those of my enemies but surpass them.

**5.74.6**  Self-praise is in no way a noble act; yet pierced by those sharp words, I shall speak of my own strength.

**5.74.7**  Look upon these two — heaven and earth, O Kṛṣṇa — within whom all these creatures dwell, both immovable and infinite, established as the mothers of all.

**5.74.8**  If these two, suddenly enraged, were to rush together like two slabs of rock, I could hold them apart with my two arms — along with all the moving and unmoving creation between them.

**5.74.9**  Look at the span between these arms of mine, like two great iron bolts — I see no man who, once caught within it, could escape.

**5.74.10**  Even the Himālayas, the ocean, and Indra the thunderbolt-wielder himself — if I were to attack, those three could not rescue anyone by relying on their strength.

**5.74.11**  I could fight all the warriors who have set upon the Pāṇḍavas and crush them underfoot upon the ground.

**5.74.12**  Surely you do not fail to know my prowess, O Acyuta — how I subdued kings and brought them under our sway.

**5.74.13**  But if you do not know me — as men do not know the risen sun's brilliance — you shall come to know me, O Janārdana, in the thick and press of battle.

**5.74.14**  Why did you pierce me with those harsh words, like a needle into a wound, O sinless one? I speak as best I can — know that I am greater still than what I say.

**5.74.15**  You shall see it on the day of carnage, when battle is joined in fierce press — elephants driven before me, and likewise charioteers and cavalry.

**5.74.16**  You and the world shall behold me furiously slaying men and foremost warriors, dragging away the best of them one after another.

**5.74.17**  My marrow does not melt, my mind does not tremble; I feel no fear even when all the world turns against me in rage.

**5.74.18**  But it is only this friendship, O Madhusūdana — compassion — that makes me endure all these hardships: let not the Bharatas perish.

**5.75.1**  The Blessed Lord said: It was out of affection, wishing to test your spirit, that I spoke thus — not out of insult, not out of pedantry, not out of anger, and not out of a desire to argue.

**5.75.2**  I know your greatness; I know the strength that is yours; I know your deeds. I do not disparage you.

**5.75.3**  Whatever good qualities you perceive in yourself, O Pāṇḍava, I reckon them in you thousandfold.

**5.75.4**  And in such a lineage — honoured by all kings, by kinsmen and by friends — O Bhīma, you are exactly as that birth demands.

**5.75.5**  For men who seek to understand the turn of doubtful dharma, O Vṛkodara, cannot resolve the alternation of the divine and the human.

**5.75.6**  That very same cause which becomes the means of a person's success becomes the cause of his ruin as well — and thus human effort remains uncertain.

**5.75.7**  What is seen one way by discerning poets who perceive flaws turns out another way — like the gusts of the wind veering unpredictably.

**5.75.8**  Even what is well-counselled, well-ordered, properly supported, and rightly done by human effort is opposed by fate.

**5.75.9**  Divine fate too, if unassisted by action, is foiled by human exertion — just as cold, heat, rain, hunger, and thirst are overcome, O Bharata.

**5.75.10**  Whatever a person who is disposed to his appointed fate accomplishes through his own doing — beyond that there is no obstruction; that is the mark of it.

**5.75.11**  The sustenance of the world rests on nothing else than action, O Pāṇḍava. One who understands this should act accordingly — the fruit would follow from the confluence of both.

**5.75.12**  He who, with this understanding firmly established, engages in action alone — neither grieves in failure nor is elated in success.

**5.75.13**  O Bhīmasena, this much is what I wish to convey: victory in battle against the Kurus is not to be considered absolutely certain.

**5.75.14**  One should not draw the reins too tight, for in a reversal of fortune that very effort leads to despair or exhaustion — it is for this reason that I speak thus to you.

**5.75.15**  O Pāṇḍava, when tomorrow comes and I approach Dhṛtarāṣṭra, I shall strive to bring about peace on your behalf, without sacrificing your interests.

**5.75.16**  If they agree to peace, then boundless glory shall be mine, your desire shall be fulfilled, and the highest good shall accrue to them as well.

**5.75.17**  But if the Kurus persist in their course and do not accept my words, then there shall be dreadful war and terrible deeds.

**5.75.18**  O Bhīmasena, in this war the chief burden rests on you; the yoke is to be borne by Arjuna, and all others shall follow their lead.

**5.75.19**  I shall be the charioteer of Arjuna in the battle — this is the wish of Dhanañjaya himself; for I do not desire war.

**5.75.20**  Therefore, O Vṛkodara, being apprehensive, I have been piercing your mind and rousing your valour with bold words to kindle your spirit.

**5.76.1**  Arjuna said: O Janārdana, O scorcher of foes, Yudhiṣṭhira has said all that was to be said; but having heard your words, a reflection arises in my mind.

**5.76.2**  O lord, you yourself do not consider peace here to be easy — whether because of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's greed or the wretchedness that has come upon him.

**5.76.3**  And yet you hold that human valour is fruitless, and that actions do not bear fruit without manly effort.

**5.76.4**  This speech you have given is in one sense true and in another not quite so; yet nothing in it should be regarded as unachievable.

**5.76.5**  And do you regard our hardship as the sin from the beginning? Only the deeds of those bear no fruit who have sinned.

**5.76.6**  O lord, an action rightly performed shall surely be fruitful; therefore, O Kṛṣṇa, act in such a way that the enemy may find peace.

**5.76.7**  O hero, you are the supreme well-wisher of both the Pāṇḍavas and the Kurus, just as Prajāpati is to the gods and the asuras.

**5.76.8**  Secure the welfare of both the Kurus and the Pāṇḍavas; I do not think it difficult for you to accomplish what is good for us.

**5.76.9**  O Janārdana, if the mission becomes accomplished thus, you shall achieve it by your very going there — of this there is no doubt.

**5.76.10**  And whatever else you intend regarding that wicked one, O hero, all shall come to pass exactly as you desire.

**5.76.11**  Whether there be peace with them or not, whatever is your purpose — when carefully considered, O Kṛṣṇa, your desire is our highest authority.

**5.76.12**  That wicked-souled one, along with his sons and kinsmen, surely deserves death — he who could not tolerate the prosperity of the son of Dharma even when witnessed.

**5.76.13**  And he who, O Madhusūdana, seeing no righteous means, seized what was ours through a cruel stratagem — by winning at a crooked game of dice.

**5.76.14**  How can a man born among Kṣatriyas, bearing a bow, turn back when challenged, even when the surrender of life itself is at hand?

**5.76.15**  Having seen us defeated by unrighteous means and forced to wander in the forest, that Suyodhana has become fit to be slain by me, O Vārṣṇeya.

**5.76.16**  O Kṛṣṇa, there is nothing wondrous in what you wish to do for the sake of a friend. How can the foremost action be achieved — whether by gentle means or by other means?

**5.76.17**  Or if you deem their immediate slaughter to be superior, then let that be done at once — you need not deliberate further.

**5.76.18**  For you know how Draupadī was tormented in the assembly hall by that evil-minded one — and even that was endured by her.

**5.76.19**  O Mādhava, I cannot bring myself to believe that he would conduct himself rightly toward the Pāṇḍavas — like seed sown on barren ground.

**5.76.20**  Therefore, O Vārṣṇeya, whatever you deem proper and beneficial for the Pāṇḍavas — do that quickly; whatever must be done for us, do it without delay.

**5.77.1**  The Lord said: O mighty-armed Pāṇḍava, it is as you say; yet, O Bībhatsu, all of this depends upon the two kinds of action — human and divine.

**5.77.2**  A field, though rich in sap and well prepared by the farmer, O Kaunteya, will never yield a harvest without rain.

**5.77.3**  There, men call the irrigation and cultivation human effort; but there too one can clearly see the drying up caused by fate.

**5.77.4**  This has been ascertained by wise men of old: the cause of things in the world lies in the combination of divine and human action.

**5.77.5**  I shall do what is supremely possible through human effort; but the working of fate I cannot control in any way.

**5.77.6**  For that evil-minded one has abandoned both dharma and truth and conducts himself accordingly; he does not grieve over deeds of such a nature.

**5.77.7**  And his counsellors — Śakuni, the son of a charioteer, and his brother Duḥśāsana — encourage and strengthen that sinful resolve of his.

**5.77.8**  O Pārtha, Suyodhana, together with his allies, will not attain peace by relinquishing the kingdom; short of his slaughter there is no other way.

**5.77.9**  Nor will that evil-minded king of dharma consent to give up the kingdom even when entreated; even when begged, he will not give it.

**5.77.10**  Yet I do not think he is the one who should be told the instruction of Yudhiṣṭhira — the purpose has already been stated there by the king of dharma, O Bharata.

**5.77.11**  Yet that sinful Kaurava will not do all of that; and when he refuses to act accordingly, he shall become worthy of death in the eyes of the world.

**5.77.12**  And he is deserving of death at my hands as well, O Bharata — and in the eyes of the world — for he wronged all of you even in your boyhood.

**5.77.13**  Your kingdom has been seized by that cruel, wicked-souled one; and the sinner is not appeased even when he beholds the prosperity of Yudhiṣṭhira.

**5.77.14**  And more than once he has tried to sow discord between us for your sake, O Pārtha; but I did not accept that sinful design of his.

**5.77.15**  You know well, O mighty-armed one, his deepest intent; and you also know that I desire what is dear to the king of dharma and to myself.

**5.77.16**  Knowing his own nature and my supreme intent, you nevertheless suspect me — as though unknowing — along with Arjuna and others, without any cause.

**5.77.17**  And that which is supremely divine — you have also understood that, O Pārtha — ordained by fate; how then could peace come from the enemy?

**5.77.18**  Whatever I am able to do by word and by deed, O Pāṇḍava, that I shall do, O Pārtha; but I do not expect peace from the enemy.

**5.77.19**  How could one who desires such peace speak of stealing cattle? — even when Bhīṣma pleaded with him, even after a year had passed.

**5.77.20**  You were already defeated the moment you resolved to divide things bit by bit and moment by moment — and still Suyodhana was not satisfied.

**5.77.21**  Yet in every way I must carry out the command of the king of dharma, and once again make manifest the sinful deeds of that wicked-souled one.

**5.78.1**  Nakula said: O Mādhava, the king of dharma — knower of dharma and generous of speech — has spoken at length and in conformity with truth and righteousness.

**5.78.2**  O Mādhava, understanding the king's intention, Bhīmasena has made known both the desire for peace and the strength of his arms.

**5.78.3**  Likewise, what Phalguna has said — you have heard it; and your own opinion, O hero, has been expressed by you more than once.

**5.78.4**  O best of men, having heard all this and setting it aside, whatever you consider timely — do that.

**5.78.5**  O Keśava, O subduer of foes, in each specific circumstance what is timely must be done by a person of his own accord.

**5.78.6**  O best of men, a matter thought of one way turns out differently — men in this world are of changeable minds.

**5.78.7**  Their dispositions toward us were one way when we were dwelling in the forest unseen, O Kṛṣṇa, and altogether different when we were visible — and now different again.

**5.78.8**  O Vārṣṇeya, even our own attachment to the kingdom was not as great when we were wandering in the forest as it is now.

**5.78.9**  O hero, upon hearing that our forest-exile had ended, seven akshauhinis have assembled to our cause, O Janārdana, through your grace.

**5.78.10**  Seeing these tiger-like men, of inconceivable strength and valour, who have taken up their weapons for battle — what man here would not tremble?

**5.78.11**  May you, in the midst of the Kurus, speak words first with conciliation, so that even the dull-witted Suyodhana, filled with fear, does not tremble.

**5.78.12**  O Keśava — Yudhiṣṭhira, Bhīmasena, the unconquered Bībhatsu, Sahadeva and myself, you and Rāma as well —

**5.78.13**  — and the greatly valiant Sātyaki, Virāṭa with his son, Drupada with his ministers, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the son of Pṛṣata —

**5.78.14**  — and the valiant king of Kāśi, and Dhṛṣṭaketu of the Cedis — what mortal, made of flesh and blood, could stand against these in battle?

**5.78.15**  O mighty-armed one, you will undoubtedly accomplish the desired purpose of the king of dharma by your very going there alone.

**5.78.16**  O sinless one, Vidura, Bhīṣma, Droṇa, and Bahlika are capable of discerning what is good when it is spoken by you.

**5.78.17**  And they will prevail upon King Dhṛtarāṣṭra and upon that wicked-actioned Suyodhana together with his ministers.

**5.78.18**  O Janārdana, with Vidura as the listener and you as the speaker, what matter going astray could the two of you not set on the right path?

**5.79.1**  Sahadeva said: What the king has said is eternal dharma; yet, O subduer of foes, whatever must be done to ensure that war takes place — that should be done.

**5.79.2**  O Dāśārha, even if the Kurus were to desire peace with the Pāṇḍavas, you should still arrange for war against them.

**5.79.3**  How indeed can my rage against Suyodhana be quelled without his death, when I have seen Pāñcālī so tormented in that assembly?

**5.79.4**  O Kṛṣṇa, if Bhīma, Arjuna, and the righteous king of dharma were to abandon dharma for his sake, even then I wish to fight him in battle.

**5.79.5**  Sātyaki said: O mighty-armed one, the great-minded Sahadeva speaks the truth; my anger would find peace only in the death of Duryodhana.

**5.79.6**  For you know how, upon seeing the Pāṇḍavas clad in bark and deerskin in the forest, suffering in misery, your own anger too arose.

**5.79.7**  Therefore, O best of men, what that brave bull among men — the son of Mādrī — has said represents the view of all the warriors.

**5.79.8**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: While the great-minded Yuyudhāna was speaking thus, a tremendous lion-roar arose from all the warriors gathered there.

**5.79.9**  All the heroes on every side honoured those words, cheering Shaineyya with cries of "Well done! Well done!" — all eager for battle.

**5.80.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having heard the king's words — full of dharma and prudence and full of counsel — Kṛṣṇa (Draupadī), tormented by grief, spoke to Dāśārha who was seated there.

**5.80.2**  The daughter of King Drupada, with her long dark hair flowing, having honoured Sahadeva and the great warrior Sātyaki —

**5.80.3**  — and seeing Bhīmasena composed and still, in deepest anguish, her eyes filled with tears, that noble-minded woman spoke these words:

**5.80.4**  O mighty-armed one, O knower of dharma, O Madhusūdana, you know how the Pāṇḍavas were cast down from their happiness by resorting to treachery.

**5.80.5**  O Janārdana, how the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, together with his ministers, had Sañjaya convey the king's secret counsel to you privately —

**5.80.6**  O descendant of Daśārha, that too is known to you — what Sañjaya was instructed to say, and all of it has been heard by you.

**5.80.7**  "Let five villages be given to us" — so it was said, O greatly resplendent one: Kuśasthala, Vṛkasthala, Āsandi, and Vāraṇāvata,

**5.80.8**  and any fifth place as the last, O mighty-armed one. Thus Duryodhana and his allies are to be addressed, O Keśava.

**5.80.9**  Yet even upon hearing that appeal, O Kṛṣṇa, Suyodhana did not act upon it — the appeal of Yudhiṣṭhira, O descendant of Daśārha, that modest one who desires peace.

**5.80.10**  If Suyodhana should desire peace without restoring the kingdom, O Kṛṣṇa, then on no account should any peace be made upon going there.

**5.80.11**  For the Pāṇḍavas together with the Sṛñjayas are capable, O mighty-armed one, of facing the terrible and wrathful army of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son.

**5.80.12**  For no purpose whatsoever can be achieved among them through conciliation or gifts; therefore no mercy should be shown toward them by you, O Madhusūdana.

**5.80.13**  O Kṛṣṇa, enemies who are not pacified by conciliation or by gifts — the rod of punishment must be unleashed upon them by one who wishes to preserve his life.

**5.80.14**  Therefore a great punishment must be swiftly meted out upon them, O Acyuta, by you yourself, O mighty-armed one, together with the Pāṇḍavas and the Sṛñjayas.

**5.80.15**  This course of action is befitting the sons of Pṛthā and brings glory to you as well, O Kṛṣṇa; when carried out, it will bring happiness to the kṣatriya order.

**5.80.16**  For a kṣatriya overcome by greed must be slain by a kṣatriya — or even by a non-kṣatriya, O descendant of Daśārha, when he is upholding his own dharma.

**5.80.17**  Excepting the brahmin, dear one, even one steeped in all sins — for the brahmin is the guru of all castes, the one who receives the first share of offerings.

**5.80.18**  Just as there is fault in slaying the undeserving, O Janārdana, that same fault is seen in sparing one who deserves to be slain — so those who know dharma declare.

**5.80.19**  Act in such a way that this fault does not touch you, O Kṛṣṇa, O descendant of Daśārha — together with the Pāṇḍavas and the Sṛñjayas and their forces.

**5.80.20**  And again I shall speak with confidence, O Janārdana: what woman like me exists upon this earth, O Keśava?

**5.80.21**  I am the daughter of King Drupada, risen from the midst of the sacrificial altar, the sister of Dhṛṣṭadyumna, your dear friend, O Kṛṣṇa.

**5.80.22**  I entered the lineage of Ājamīḍha; I am the daughter-in-law of the great-souled Pāṇḍu; I am the queen of the sons of Pāṇḍu, who are equal in splendour to the five Indras.

**5.80.23**  By five heroes five sons were born to me — great warriors all; and as Abhimanyu is to you, O Kṛṣṇa, so are they yours in point of dharma.

**5.80.24**  I — I suffered the seizing of my hair and, afflicted, was dragged into the assembly hall, while the sons of Pāṇḍu looked on, O Keśava, even while you lived.

**5.80.25**  While the Kurus, the Pāñcālas, and the Vṛṣṇis all still lived, I was made a slave by sinners and placed in the midst of the assembly.

**5.80.26**  While the sons of Pāṇḍu stood by without anger, without action, merely watching, I cried out in my heart: "Save me, O Govinda" — it was you I longed for.

**5.80.27**  Then my father-in-law, the venerable king, spoke these words to me: "Choose a boon, O Pāñcālī — you are worthy of boons, so I regard you."

**5.80.28**  When I said, "Let the Pāṇḍavas be free from slavery, with their chariots and weapons," they were released — only to be sent to forest exile, O Keśava.

**5.80.29**  You are acquainted with all such sufferings of mine, O Janārdana. Save me, O lotus-eyed one — together with my husbands, my kinsmen, and my relations.

**5.80.30**  Am I not, O Kṛṣṇa, the rightful daughter-in-law of both Bhīṣma and Dhṛtarāṣṭra? And yet I was made a slave.

**5.80.31**  Shame on the strength of Bhīmasena! Shame on the archery of the son of Pṛthā! That Duryodhana still lives even for a moment, O Kṛṣṇa!

**5.80.32**  If I am worthy of your grace, if you have compassion for me, then let all your wrath, O Kṛṣṇa, be directed against the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**5.80.33**  Having spoken thus, the dark-cornered-eyed one took in her left hand her tress of hair — gently bunched together, beautiful to behold at its tip, deep blue in hue, fragrant with sacred perfume —

**5.80.34**  endowed with every auspicious mark, radiant as a great serpent — that mass of hair, she the beautiful-hipped one held it in her left hand,

**5.80.35**  and approaching the lotus-eyed one, she who walks like an elephant and whose eyes were filled with tears — Kṛṣṇā spoke these words to Kṛṣṇa:

**5.80.36**  "This, O lotus-eyed one, was seized by Duḥśāsana's hand — it must be remembered at all times by you who desire peace with the enemy."

**5.80.37**  "If Bhīma and Arjuna are wretched and eager for peace, O Kṛṣṇa, my aged father will fight along with his sons, those great warriors."

**5.80.38**  "And my five sons of great valour, O Madhusūdana, with Abhimanyu at their head, will fight against the Kurus."

**5.80.39**  "If I do not see Duḥśāsana's dark arm severed and rolled in the dust, what peace can there be for my heart?"

**5.80.40**  "Thirteen years have passed for me while I waited, carrying in my heart a fury blazing like fire."

**5.80.41**  "My heart is being torn apart, pierced by the arrow of Bhīma's words — he who, mighty-armed as he is, today looks to dharma alone."

**5.80.42**  Having spoken thus, Kṛṣṇā, she of the wide eyes, wept with a voice choked with tears — trembling, with a voice broken by sobs.

**5.80.43**  She of the full breasts and rounded hips, her two breasts drenched together, shed tears from her eyes as if releasing water made burning hot and melted.

**5.80.44**  The mighty-armed Keśava spoke to her, consoling her: "O Kṛṣṇā, before long you shall see the women of the Bharata line weeping."

**5.80.45**  "Thus, O timid one, O passionate lady, they shall weep — those whose kinsmen and relatives have been slain, whose allies are slain, whose armies are destroyed — they at whom your wrath is directed."

**5.80.46**  "And I shall bring this about, together with Bhīma, Arjuna, and the twins, by the command of Yudhiṣṭhira and by the ordinance fashioned by destiny."

**5.80.47**  "If the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, ripened by Time, do not heed my words, they shall lie slain upon the earth, made food for jackals and dogs."

**5.80.48**  "Though the Himālaya mountain may tremble, though the earth may shatter into a hundred pieces, though the sky may fall with all its stars — my words shall not be in vain."

**5.80.49**  "I swear this truth to you, O Kṛṣṇā — restrain your tears. Before long you shall see your husbands, adorned with glory, their enemies slain."

**5.81.1**  Arjuna said: You are today the best of friends to all the Kurus, a beloved kinsman always, on both sides alike.

**5.81.2**  You are capable, O Keśava, of bringing about the well-being that must be secured between the Pāṇḍavas and the sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, and of bringing peace to them.

**5.81.3**  From here, O lotus-eyed one, O slayer of enemies, you could speak to wrathful Suyodhana whatever must be said for the sake of peace among the Bhāratas.

**5.81.4**  If what is spoken by you — auspicious, wholesome, grounded in dharma and artha — is not accepted by that fool, then he will fall under the power of fate.

**5.81.5**  The Blessed One said: That which is righteous, beneficial to us, and conducive to the well-being of the Kurus — I shall go to King Dhṛtarāṣṭra with that intent.

**5.81.6**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then, when the darkness had departed, when the pure sun had risen, when the auspicious hour of Mitra had arrived, when the sun shone with gentle rays,

**5.81.7**  in the month of Kaumuda, under the asterism Revatī, at the end of autumn when winter was setting in, in the pleasant season of abundant crops — the best of the vigorous ones arose hale.

**5.81.8**  Hearing the auspicious, holy, and agreeable words of confident brahmins — like Vāsava hearing the words of the seers —

**5.81.9**  having performed the morning rites, bathed, purified, and adorned, Janārdana worshipped the sun Vivasvān and fire.

**5.81.10**  Having stroked a bull on its back, having saluted the brahmins, having circumambulated the sacred fire, seeing auspiciousness before him,

**5.81.11**  Janārdana, having pledged that word of the Pāṇḍava, spoke to Sātyaki, the grandson of Śini, who was seated there.

**5.81.12**  "Let the conch, the discus, and the mace be placed on the chariot, along with the quivers, the lances, and all the weapons."

**5.81.13**  "For Duryodhana is of wicked soul, and so is Karṇa together with Śakuni's son; and an enemy, however ordinary, must not be treated with contempt even by the powerful."

**5.81.14**  Then, having understood the intent of Keśava, his forerunners went forth to harness the chariot of him who bears the discus and the mace.

**5.81.15**  That chariot — blazing like the fire of dissolution, like a traveller moving through the sky — was adorned with two wheels that shone with the brilliance of the moon and the sun,

**5.81.16**  decorated with half-moons and full moons, with fish, with deer and birds, with flowers of various kinds, and on all sides with gems and jewels — a wondrous sight.

**5.81.17**  Resembling the newly risen sun, vast, and beautiful to behold, with body parts wrought from gems and gold, with a fine standard and fine pennants —

**5.81.18**  well-equipped, invincible, draped with tiger skins — the destroyer of the glory of enemies and the increaser of joy among the Yadus —

**5.81.19**  they harnessed it with the horses Sainya, Sugrīva, Meghapuṣpa, and Balāhaka — bathed and endowed with every excellence.

**5.81.20**  Further magnifying the greatness of Kṛṣṇa, the chariot was yoked with the banner Sughoṣa bearing the king of birds.

**5.81.21**  That chariot, resembling the peak of Mount Meru, rumbling like the thundering drums of storm clouds, was mounted by the hero Śauri as if it were a divine vehicle by one who has earned merit.

**5.81.22**  Then, having taken Sātyaki up with him, the Best of Men set forth, making both the earth and the sky resound with the roar of the chariot.

**5.81.23**  In a moment the sky became clear of dense clouds; a gentle wind blew in his wake; the dust settled.

**5.81.24**  At the departure of Vāsudeva, auspicious beasts and birds moving clockwise and in the right direction followed in his train.

**5.81.25**  Cranes, lotuses, and swans followed Madhusūdana on every side with cries full of auspicious import and meaning.

**5.81.26**  And the sacred fire, being fed with great oblations of mantras and offerings, became smokeless and turned its flame clockwise.

**5.81.27**  Vasiṣṭha, Vāmadeva, Bhūridyumna, Gaya, Krātha, Śukra, Nārada, Vālmīki, the Maruts, Kuśika, and Bhṛgu —

**5.81.28**  together with the divine seers and brahmic sages, they all circled clockwise around Kṛṣṇa, the younger brother of Vāsava, the bringer of joy to the Yadus.

**5.81.29**  Thus honoured by these greatly blessed and virtuous hosts of great seers, Kṛṣṇa set forth toward the abode of the Kurus.

**5.81.30**  As he set forth, Yudhiṣṭhira the son of Kuntī followed him, and both Bhīmasena and Arjuna, and the two Pāṇḍava sons of Mādrī.

**5.81.31**  The valiant Cekitāna, Dhṛṣṭaketu the king of the Cedis, Drupada, the king of Kāśi, and the great warrior Śikhaṇḍī —

**5.81.32**  Dhṛṣṭadyumna with his sons, Virāṭa together with the Kekayas — these warriors went forth to accompany that bull among warriors.

**5.81.33**  Then the resplendent Dharmarāja Yudhiṣṭhira, following Govinda, spoke these words in the presence of the kings:

**5.81.34**  "He who, firm of mind and free from greed, does not follow unrighteousness out of desire, or fear, or greed, or for the sake of material gain —

**5.81.35**  that knower of dharma, resolute, wise — Keśava, the lord of all beings in all creatures, god of gods, resplendent in might."

**5.81.36**  Embracing him who was endowed with all virtues and bore the Śrīvatsa mark, the son of Kuntī began to give his instructions.

**5.81.37**  She who, from our very childhood, reared us — that woman of fasting and austerity, ever devoted to our well-being and welfare —

**5.81.38**  devoted to the worship of gods and guests, diligent in service to elders, affectionate, fond of her sons, and dear to us, O Janārdana —

**5.81.39**  she who saved us from the great peril of death out of fear of Suyodhana, O crusher of foes, like a ship lifting one from the ocean —

**5.81.40**  she who, though undeserving of sorrow, has continually suffered hardships on our account — please inquire of her welfare, O Mādhava.

**5.81.41**  Greatly console her who is overwhelmed by grief for her sons; salute her and embrace her, proclaiming the names of the Pāṇḍavas.

**5.81.42**  From the time of her marriage, O crusher of foes, she has witnessed the indignities and sufferings of her fathers-in-law, undeserving as she was of such grief.

**5.81.43**  Will there ever come a time, O Kṛṣṇa, O crusher of foes, when I might place my afflicted mother in happiness?

**5.81.44**  When we went forth into the forest, she, pitiable and attached to her sons, followed after us weeping; yet we departed into the forest leaving her behind in tears.

**5.81.45**  Surely grief does not kill — for if she still lives, O Keśava, she has been deeply afflicted by sorrow over her sons, yet honored by the people of Ānarta.

**5.81.46**  She, O Kṛṣṇa, O lord, must be saluted on my behalf; and so must Dhṛtarāṣṭra of the Kuru line, and all the kings who are older in years.

**5.81.47**  Bhīṣma, Droṇa, and Kṛpa, the great king Bāhlika, Drauṇi, Somadatta, and all the Bharatas individually —

**5.81.48**  and Vidura, the greatly wise, keeper of counsel for the Kurus, of unfathomable intellect and knower of dharma — embrace him, O Madhusūdana.

**5.81.49**  Having said this to Keśava there in the midst of the kings, Yudhiṣṭhira, given leave, circumambulated Kṛṣṇa and withdrew.

**5.81.50**  Even as he was departing, Bībhatsu addressed his friend, that bull among men, the slayer of enemy heroes, the undefeated scion of the Dāśārhas.

**5.81.51**  What transpired for us, O lord, in the former deliberation regarding half the kingdom, O Govinda — that is known among all the kings.

**5.81.52**  If he should give that without obstruction, honoring us and not slighting us, it would be dear to me, O mighty-armed one, and they would be freed from great peril.

**5.81.53**  But if the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, ignorant of proper means, acts otherwise than this, I shall surely bring the kṣatriyas to their end, O Janārdana.

**5.81.54**  When the Pāṇḍava had spoken thus, Vṛkodara rejoiced greatly; again and again that Pāṇḍava trembled, overcome with anger.

**5.81.55**  The son of Kuntī trembled and uttered great loud cries; having heard the words of Dhanañjaya, his mind was greatly swollen with joy.

**5.81.56**  Hearing that roar of his, the bowmen trembled; all the animals voided their dung and urine.

**5.81.57**  Having thus spoken to Keśava there, and having declared his resolve, he was given leave, embraced Janārdana, and withdrew.

**5.81.58**  When all those kings had departed, Janārdana, bold, mounted swiftly on his chariot drawn by Śaibya and Sugrīva.

**5.81.59**  Those horses of Vāsudeva, urged on by Dāruka, seemed to devour the road and swallow the sky.

**5.81.60**  Then the mighty-armed Keśava saw sages on the road, blazing with brahmic splendor, standing on both sides of the path.

**5.81.61**  Descending quickly from his chariot, Janārdana saluted and addressed all those sages duly, honoring them.

**5.81.62**  "Is all well in the worlds? Is dharma properly observed? Do the three varṇas remain under the guidance of the brāhmaṇas?"

**5.81.63**  Having offered them that reverence, Madhusūdana said: "O venerable ones, where are you perfected ones coming from? What is your path here?"

**5.81.64**  "Or what is the business of the venerable ones? What may I do for you? For what purpose have you, O venerable ones, come to this earth?"

**5.81.65**  Jāmadagnya approached Madhusūdana and, embracing Govinda — a friend from long ago, of noble conduct — spoke to him.

**5.81.66**  "Divine seers, doers of merit, brāhmaṇas of great learning, and royal seers, O scion of Dāśārha, honoring the ascetics —

**5.81.67**  — witnesses to the ancient struggle of gods and asuras, O greatly resplendent one, and wishing to behold the assembled earthly kṣatriyas from all sides —

**5.81.68**  — and the kings in the assembly, and you yourself in truth, O Janārdana — we go to witness this great spectacle, O Keśava."

**5.81.69**  "We wish to hear words joined with dharma and purpose, O Mādhava, spoken by you among the Kurus in the midst of kings, O scorcher of foes."

**5.81.70**  "Bhīṣma, Droṇa and the others, and Vidura of great mind, and you yourself, O tiger among Yādavas, will assemble in that hall."

**5.81.71**  "Your divine words there, and theirs as well, O Mādhava, we wish to hear, O Govinda — words that are true and auspicious."

**5.81.72**  "You have been asked, O mighty-armed one; we shall see you again. Go without obstacle, O hero; we shall see you when you have arrived at the assembly."

**5.82.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Ten great chariot-warriors, weapons in hand, followed the son of Devakī, the mighty-armed crusher of enemy heroes, as he departed.

**5.82.2**  A thousand foot soldiers and cavalry as well, O scorcher of foes, and abundant provisions, O king, and hundreds of servants besides.

**5.82.3**  Janamejaya said: How did the great-souled Madhusūdana, scion of the Dāśārhas, depart? And what omens appeared for that one of mighty power as he traveled?

**5.82.4**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: The wondrous signs that attended the departure of that great soul — hear those from me: divine, heaven-sent, and portentous.

**5.82.5**  In a cloudless sky a peal of thunder with lightning arose; and immediately after, rain fell heavily in a thick downpour.

**5.82.6**  The great rivers, those best of streams flowing eastward, reversed their course; all directions were inverted and nothing could be distinguished.

**5.82.7**  Fires blazed up, O king; the earth trembled; wells and water jars poured forth water in hundreds of streams.

**5.82.8**  The entire world was enveloped in darkness at that time; the directions and intermediate directions, O king, could not be discerned because of the dust.

**5.82.9**  A great sound arose in the sky with no body visible; in all regions, O king, that seemed like a wonder indeed.

**5.82.10**  A south-westerly wind battered Hāstinapura, harsh and dreadful in sound, uprooting trees in great numbers.

**5.82.11**  But wherever the Vārṣṇeya traveled on the road, O Bhārata, there the wind was pleasant and all things were auspicious.

**5.82.12**  A rain of flowers fell and lotuses in great abundance; the road was level, free of hardship, free of sharp grass and thorns.

**5.82.13**  As he traveled, O king, that mighty-armed giver of wealth was worshipped by brāhmaṇas here and there with honey-offerings and flowers.

**5.82.14**  Women came together along the road to scatter fragrant wild flowers upon that great soul who was devoted to the welfare of all beings.

**5.82.15**  He passed through a beautiful tract of rice fields filled with all manner of crops, pleasant and most righteous, O bull of the Bharatas.

**5.82.16**  Beholding villages rich in cattle, lovely and heart-pleasing, he passed through towns and various kingdoms.

**5.82.17**  Always joyful and cheerful of mind, protected by the Bhāratas, the people were untroubled, unaware of the misdeeds of enemy forces.

**5.82.18**  Then the townsfolk, having learned of his coming from Upaplavya, stood together on the road, eager to see Viṣvaksena.

**5.82.19**  All of them worshipped him, the worshipful lord of beautiful name, like a blazing fire — that honored guest who had arrived in their land.

**5.82.20**  Keśava, slayer of enemy heroes, reached Vṛkasthala as the sun, clear and reddening, was spreading its rays at sunset.

**5.82.21**  Descending swiftly from his chariot, performing his ablutions according to rule, ordering the unharnessing of the chariot, he sat down to perform the evening rite.

**5.82.22**  Dāruka too released the horses, tended to them according to scripture, removed all armor, and set them free to roam.

**5.82.23**  Having completed all of that, Madhusūdana said: "For the sake of Yudhiṣṭhira's mission we shall spend the night here."

**5.82.24**  Understanding his wish, the men prepared lodgings for him; and in a moment they gathered food and drink of fine quality.

**5.82.25**  The leading brāhmaṇas of that village, O king — noble, of good lineage, modest, observing the brahmic way of life —

**5.82.26**  — they approached the great-souled Hṛṣīkeśa, crusher of foes, and offered worship duly, accompanied by blessings and auspicious rites.

**5.82.27**  Having honored the scion of Dāśārha, who is worshipped in all worlds, they offered their gem-filled dwellings to the great soul.

**5.82.28**  The lord, saying "it is done," honored them duly; going to their dwellings he returned again together with them.

**5.82.29**  Having fed the brāhmaṇas there with well-prepared food, and having eaten himself together with all of them, Keśava passed that night pleasantly.

**5.83.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having thus learned through messengers of Madhusūdana's arrival, Dhṛtarāṣṭra addressed the mighty-armed Bhīṣma with reverence.

**5.83.2**  And Droṇa, Saṃjaya, the greatly wise Vidura, and Duryodhana with his ministers — with hair standing on end he spoke these words:

**5.83.3**  "A great wonder and marvel is being reported, O joy of the Kurus; women, children, and the aged are speaking of it house by house."

**5.83.4**  "Others recount it with praise; still other groups have gathered; diverse discussions are going on in the crossroads and assemblies."

**5.83.5**  "The heroic scion of Dāśārha will come on behalf of the Pāṇḍavas; that Madhusūdana is deserving of our honor and reverence in every way."

**5.83.6**  "For in him lies the journey of the world; he is indeed the lord of all beings. In that Mādhava reside steadiness, valor, wisdom, and might."

**5.83.7**  "Let that best of men be honored; for he is eternal dharma itself. When honored, he brings happiness; when not honored, he brings unhappiness."

**5.83.8**  "If that scion of Dāśārha, that crusher of foes, is pleased by our attentions, we shall attain all our intentions among all the kings."

**5.83.9**  "Arrange today itself for his worship, O scorcher of foes; let rest-halls be established on the road, furnished with all desirable things."

**5.83.10**  "Do whatever will cause his affection to arise toward you, O mighty-armed Gāndhārī's son; and what is your opinion, O Bhīṣma?"

**5.83.11**  Then Bhīṣma and all the others said to Dhṛtarāṣṭra, lord of the people: "Excellent" — thus honoring his words.

**5.83.12**  Knowing their approval, King Duryodhana then proceeded to designate beautiful sites for the halls of reception.

**5.83.13**  Then in pleasant places, portion by portion, they constructed numerous halls filled with all manner of jewels.

**5.83.14**  Variegated seats furnished with various fine qualities, women with perfumes, ornaments, and fine garments —

**5.83.15**  Wholesome food and drink of many kinds, diverse delicacies, and fragrant garlands — all these the king then bestowed.

**5.83.16**  And in particular, for a residence, the Kaurava king arranged a charming assembly hall adorned with many jewels in the village of Vṛkasthala.

**5.83.17**  Having made all these arrangements — worthy of the gods and beyond human — King Duryodhana then reported them to Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**5.83.18**  Without even inspecting those assembly halls or the various jewels, Keśava of the Dāśārha line made his way to the Kuru palace.

**5.84.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Kṣattṛ, Janārdana has arrived here from Upaplavya; he is staying at Vṛkasthala and will come here tomorrow morning.

**5.84.2**  Janārdana, lord of the Āhukas, foremost among all the Sātvatas, great of soul, great of valor, and great of measure —

**5.84.3**  Mādhava, the sustainer and protector of the prosperous Vṛṣṇi lineage, the blessed lord who is the great forefather of all three worlds —

**5.84.4**  The Vṛṣṇis and Andhakas of glad heart revere his wisdom as the Ādityas, Vasus, and Rudras revere the intellect of Bṛhaspati.

**5.84.5**  I shall bestow worship upon that great-souled one of the Dāśārha line. O knower of dharma, hear me as I describe it before you.

**5.84.6**  I will give him sixteen golden chariots, each yoked with four of the finest horses of Bāhlīka breed, uniform in color with beautiful dark limbs.

**5.84.7**  I will give Keśava eight elephants, perpetually in rut, with tusks like poles, fierce strikers, each accompanied by eight attendants.

**5.84.8**  I will give him a hundred fair maidservants who have not borne children, glowing like gold, and as many male servants likewise.

**5.84.9**  I will give him eighteen thousand woolen cloths, soft to the touch, brought from the mountain folk.

**5.84.10**  Thousands of hides from the land of China — these too I will give him, as many as Keśava deserves.

**5.84.11**  This gem, blazing with great radiance, pure and shining by day and night alike — that too I will give him, for Keśava deserves it too.

**5.84.12**  And I will give him a conveyance yoked with mules that can cover fourteen yojanas in a single day.

**5.84.13**  Whatever vehicles he has and however many men accompany him, I will always provide him provisions eight times that number.

**5.84.14**  All my sons and grandsons — all except Duryodhana — will go forth to receive the Dāśārha hero, adorned and mounted on gleaming chariots.

**5.84.15**  And auspicious women of the foremost courtesans, finely adorned, will go forth on foot by the thousands to receive the illustrious Keśava.

**5.84.16**  And whatever auspicious maidens from the city may go to see Janārdana — they too shall go forth unveiled.

**5.84.17**  Indeed, the city — men, women, and children together — gazes upon the great-souled Madhusūdana as the people gaze upon the sun.

**5.84.18**  Let great banners and flags be raised in all directions, and let the road be sprinkled with water and made spotless — so he commanded.

**5.84.19**  And the house of Duḥśāsana, finer than Duryodhana's own — let it be quickly swept clean and adorned for him.

**5.84.20**  For it is graced with beautiful palaces of pleasing appearance, auspicious and delightful, well-appointed with great wealth in every season.

**5.84.21**  Whatever jewel or treasure in this house — mine or Duryodhana's — that hero of the Vṛṣṇis may deserve, that without doubt must be given.

**5.85.1**  Vidura said: O king, you are held in high esteem as the best among people even in all three worlds; you are honored and respected by the world, O Bhārata.

**5.85.2**  That you, standing in the last stage of life, should speak thus — whether from scripture or sound reasoning — is fitting, for you are a steadfast elder.

**5.85.3**  As sunlight upon a touchstone, as a great wave upon the ocean — so great is the dharma in you, O king: thus the people are convinced.

**5.85.4**  The world has ever been nourished by the flood of your virtues, O king; strive always to protect those virtues, together with your kinsmen.

**5.85.5**  Embrace uprightness; do not lose in many ways through childishness — your kingdom, your sons and grandsons, and your dearest friends.

**5.85.6**  What you wish to give Kṛṣṇa as a guest is much, and more besides — the Dāśārha hero deserves the very earth itself.

**5.85.7**  Yet it is not for dharma's sake nor out of love for him that you desire to do this for Kṛṣṇa — I swear this by my own self in truth.

**5.85.8**  This is illusion, not reality; this is deceit, O giver of great gifts. I know your hidden intention, O king, concealed beneath this outward act.

**5.85.9**  The Pāṇḍavas seek just five villages, O king, and you will not give even those to them — who then will bring about peace?

**5.85.10**  By wealth you wish to win over the mighty-armed Vārṣṇeya, and by this very stratagem you seek to separate him from the Pāṇḍavas.

**5.85.11**  He cannot be won over by wealth, nor by effort, nor by reproach — no one other than Dhanañjaya can accomplish this. I speak this truth to you.

**5.85.12**  He knows Kṛṣṇa's greatness; he knows Kṛṣṇa's steadfast devotion. I know that Dhanañjaya is inseparable from him — equal to his own life.

**5.85.13**  Beyond a pot filled with water, the washing of his feet, and courteous inquiry after his well-being, Janārdana will accept nothing more.

**5.85.14**  Whatever hospitality is dear to that great-souled one who is worthy of honor — let that be rendered to him, O king, for Janārdana is worthy of honor.

**5.85.15**  Keśava comes to the Kurus hoping for an auspicious outcome; for that very purpose he comes, O king — serve him accordingly.

**5.85.16**  The Dāśārha hero desires peace — between you, Duryodhana, and the Pāṇḍavas, O lord of kings. Do as he says.

**5.85.17**  You are their father, O king; your sons are grown but the others are as children. Act toward them as a father, for they act toward you as sons.

**5.86.1**  Duryodhana said: What Vidura says about Kṛṣṇa is all truly spoken — Janārdana is devoted to the Pārthas and cannot be swayed.

**5.86.2**  But the manifold wealth and honor that would be given to Janārdana as tribute — that, O lord of kings, must never be given.

**5.86.3**  The place, the time, the occasion — Keśava is no doubt deserving. But, O king, Adhokṣaja will think: 'He honors me out of fear.'

**5.86.4**  And where there is humiliation for a Kṣatriya, O lord of the people, a wise man should not do such a deed — this is my firm conviction.

**5.86.5**  For Kṛṣṇa the lotus-eyed is the most adorable of gods throughout all three worlds — this I know fully and in every way.

**5.86.6**  But according to the course of affairs, nothing should be given to him now, O lord. War once begun does not subside except through war.

**5.86.7**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Hearing those words of his, Bhīṣma the grandsire of the Kurus spoke these words to King Vaicitravīrya.

**5.86.8**  Whether honored or dishonored, Janārdana would not grow angry; even when slighted, Keśava is not one to slight another in return.

**5.86.9**  But what purpose has been determined in his mind as to be accomplished, O mighty-armed one, cannot by any means be altered by anyone.

**5.86.10**  Whatever the mighty-armed one says should be done without hesitation; make peace with the Pāṇḍavas quickly through Vāsudeva as the sacred ford.

**5.86.11**  That dharma-souled Janārdana will surely speak what is just and beneficial; to him must you and your kinsmen speak pleasing words.

**5.86.12**  Duryodhana said: There is no alternative, O king, that I, while living among the living, O grandsire, should enjoy this sovereignty in full together with them.

**5.86.13**  But hear this great plan I have devised: I will bind Janārdana, the mainstay of the Pāṇḍavas.

**5.86.14**  With him bound, the Vṛṣṇis, the earth, and the Pāṇḍavas will all be subject to me — and he is coming here tomorrow morning.

**5.86.15**  Here, tell me the means by which Janārdana shall not perceive the plan in time, and whereby no harm may befall us.

**5.86.16**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Hearing those terrible words aimed against Kṛṣṇa, Dhṛtarāṣṭra together with his ministers was tormented and became despondent.

**5.86.17**  Then Dhṛtarāṣṭra spoke these words to Duryodhana: Do not speak thus, O protector of the people — this is not eternal dharma.

**5.86.18**  For Hṛṣīkeśa comes as an envoy, is our kinsman, and is dear to us; he is blameless among the Kauravas — how could he deserve imprisonment?

**5.86.19**  Bhīṣma said: O Dhṛtarāṣṭra, surrounded by your counselors, this dim-witted son of yours chooses calamity and not benefit, even when implored by his friends.

**5.86.20**  This one walks a crooked path, sinful and attended by sin, and having abandoned the counsel of friends — and you too follow along after him.

**5.86.21**  Having dared to lay hands on Kṛṣṇa whose deeds are without effort, this utterly wicked son of yours together with his ministers will cease to exist in a moment.

**5.86.22**  I cannot in any way bear to listen to words full of such ruinous intent from this sinful, cruel, dharma-forsaking, evil-minded one.

**5.86.23**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having spoken thus, Bhīṣma, best of the Bharatas, old but filled with great fury, rose from his seat and departed, he whose valor is in truth.

**5.87.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Rising early in the morning, Kṛṣṇa performed all his daily rites; then, blessed by the Brāhmaṇas, he set out toward the city.

**5.87.2**  Having bid farewell to that mighty-armed one as he departed, O king, all the residents of Vṛkasthala turned back.

**5.87.3**  The Dhārtarāṣṭras went forth adorned to receive him as he came — all of them except Duryodhana: Bhīṣma, Droṇa, Kṛpa, and the rest.

**5.87.4**  And many citizens, O king, eager to see Hṛṣīkeśa, came out — some in various kinds of vehicles, and others simply on foot.

**5.87.5**  Meeting on the road with Bhīṣma of effortless deeds, with Droṇa, and with those Dhārtarāṣṭras, he proceeded to the city, surrounded by them all.

**5.87.6**  For the sake of honoring Kṛṣṇa, the city was beautifully adorned and the royal roads were strewn with many jewels.

**5.87.7**  Not a single person remained at home, O king, O bull of the Bharatas — no woman, no elder, no child — all out of eagerness to see Vāsudeva.

**5.87.8**  The men on the royal road could scarcely touch the ground, so great, O king, was the press at the entry of Hṛṣīkeśa.

**5.87.9**  Even the great houses, thronged by the finest women who crowded their rooftops, appeared as if trembling under the weight, as seen from the earth.

**5.87.10**  And the swift horses of Vāsudeva lost all their swiftness, the royal road being so packed with people.

**5.87.11**  The lotus-eyed one, crusher of foes, entered the white palace of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, beautified with its many halls.

**5.87.12**  Passing through three courtyards of the royal dwelling, Keśava the subduer of enemies approached King Vaicitravīrya.

**5.87.13**  As the Dāśārha hero approached, the king who sees with wisdom's eye, that lord of men of great renown, rose along with Droṇa and Bhīṣma.

**5.87.14**  Kṛpa, Somadatta, and the great king Bāhlika — all rose from their seats in honor of Janārdana.

**5.87.15**  Then, having approached the illustrious king Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the Vārṣṇeya paid homage to Bhīṣma with fitting words.

**5.87.16**  Having performed among them that proper greeting according to custom, Madhusūdana, the scion of Madhu, met with the kings there according to their respective seniority.

**5.87.17**  Then Janārdana greeted Droṇa along with his son, and the glorious Bāhlīka, and Kṛpa, and Somadatta.

**5.87.18**  There stood a grand, polished, golden seat of great magnificence; by the command of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, Acyuta sat upon it.

**5.87.19**  Then the priests of Dhṛtarāṣṭra duly offered Janārdana a cow, the honey-and-curd mixture (madhuparka), and water, as prescribed.

**5.87.20**  Govinda, having been received as a guest, sat cheerfully and conversationally, making friendly exchanges with all the Kurus, surrounded by them.

**5.87.21**  Honored and worshipped by Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the greatly renowned subduer of foes then took leave of the king and departed.

**5.87.22**  Having duly met with the Kurus in the Kuru assembly, Mādhava then went to the pleasant residence of Vidura.

**5.87.23**  Vidura approached Janārdana and honored the descendant of Dāśārha with all auspicious gifts and every comfort that was at hand.

**5.87.24**  Then Vidura, the knower of all dharma, having received Govinda as his guest, asked Madhusūdana about the welfare of the sons of Pāṇḍu.

**5.87.25**  Of the pleased friend Vidura, the foremost in intelligence, ever firm in dharma, free of faults, and wise —

**5.87.26**  — to him the Dāśārha, who sees all things directly, narrated in full detail everything about the doings of the Pāṇḍavas.

**5.88.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then, in the afternoon, Janārdana went to Vidura and then Govinda, the subduer of foes, went to his father's sister (Kuntī).

**5.88.2**  She, upon seeing Kṛṣṇa coming with the serene radiance of the sun, took him by the neck and wept aloud, remembering the Pārthas.

**5.88.3**  Among those men of valor, Pṛthā shed tears upon seeing, after a long time, the Vārṣṇeya Govinda, their companion.

**5.88.4**  She spoke to Kṛṣṇa, the lord of warriors, seated after being received as a guest, with a face full of choked sobs and dried by grief:

**5.88.5**  "Those who, from childhood on, were devoted to serving their elders, who were friends to one another, honored by all, and equal in spirit —

**5.88.6**  — those who deserved to be kings, deprived of their kingdom by treachery and gone to the wilderness, who had subdued anger and elation, who were devoted to the Brāhmaṇas and spoke the truth —

**5.88.7**  — the Pārthas, abandoning their beloved comforts, left me weeping and went to the forest, tearing my heart out by the root.

**5.88.8**  How, O Keśava, dear one, did those noble Pāṇḍavas, who did not deserve such a fate, dwell in the great forest teeming with lions, tigers, and elephants?
