**5.103.13**  "But because I serve diligently, stationed as your emblem on the flagstaff, and carry your younger brother — on that account you disrespect me."

**5.103.14**  "Who else can bear such a burden? Who else is mightier than I — I who, though superior, carry him along with his kin?"

**5.103.15**  "Because I have been deprived of my food out of disregard for me, my honor is destroyed — both before you and before the others, O Vāsava."

**5.103.16**  "Those born of Aditi who are endowed with strength and valor — among all of them it is said that you are especially the mightiest."

**5.103.17**  "Yet I carry you with just a portion of one wing, without exertion. Reflect slowly, dear one — who then is truly powerful here?"

**5.103.18**  Kaṇva said: Hearing those words of the bird — terrible in their consequences — he who bears the wheel on his chariot, though unshakeable, spoke agitating Tārkṣya.

**5.103.19**  "O Garutman, you think yourself powerful — you are utterly weak. Enough of this self-praise before us, O egg-born one."

**5.103.20**  "The entire three worlds are unable to bear my body; I sustain even myself by my own power — and I also uphold you."

**5.103.21**  "Bear then just this one arm of mine — the right one; if you can hold even that single one, then your boasting shall have borne fruit."

**5.103.22**  Then the blessed one placed his arm upon Garuḍa's shoulder — and he fell, crushed by the weight, overwhelmed and bereft of consciousness.

**5.103.23**  For he felt a weight equal to that of the entire earth together with its mountains — all pressing upon that single limb of his body.

**5.103.24**  Yet the Infallible One, though far mightier in strength, did not crush him utterly; and thus Acyuta did not cause the bird's life to perish.

**5.103.25**  With wings pinioned, his body drooping, senses scattered, and mind reeling, the bird let fall his feathers, overwhelmed by that immense burden.

**5.103.26**  Then the bird, son of Vinatā, bowed his head before Viṣṇu and, bewildered, dazed, and pitiful, spoke these words.

**5.103.27**  "O Lord, with an arm worthy of one who is the essence of all worlds—an arm released at will—I have been ground into the earth."

**5.103.28**  "Deign to forgive me, O God—a bird confused and feeble-minded, scorched by the fire of arrogance, who dwells upon your banner."

**5.103.29**  "I did not know your supreme might, O Lord, O Pervader—therefore I thought my own prowess unmatched by others."

**5.103.30**  Then the Blessed Lord showed grace to Garuṭmān; out of affection he said to him, "Be not thus again."

**5.103.31**  "Likewise you, O son of Gāndhāra—as long as you do not encounter those heroic sons of Pāṇḍu in battle, so long shall you live, my child."

**5.103.32**  "Bhīma, son of Vāyu, the foremost of warriors, of immense strength, and Dhanaṃjaya, son of Indra—whom could those two not slay in battle?"

**5.103.33**  "Viṣṇu, Vāyu, Śakra, Dharma, and the two Aśvins—by what means could you stand to face these gods?"

**5.103.34**  "Therefore, enough of this enmity—seek peace, O son of a king; by the sacred ford of Vāsudeva you ought to preserve your lineage."

**5.103.35**  "Indeed, Nārada of great austerity is a direct witness to all of this—to the majesty of that Viṣṇu who bears the discus and mace."

**5.103.36**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: But Duryodhana, having heard all this, only heaved a sigh with furrowed brow; he glanced at Rādheya and then laughed aloud.

**5.103.37**  The evil-minded one, having dismissed the words of the seer Kaṇva as worthless, slapped his thigh—thick as an elephant's trunk—and said this:

**5.103.38**  "As I was created by the Lord, as whatever is destined to happen will happen, and as my course is fixed—so I conduct myself, O great seer. What will this babbling accomplish?"

**5.104.1**  Janamejaya said: He who is fixedly bent upon ruin, who is blinded by greed in the affairs of others, who delights in ignoble acts, who has resolved on destruction—

**5.104.2**  —who is a source of pain to his kinsmen, who increases the grief of relatives, who gives sorrow to friends and joy to enemies—

**5.104.3**  —how is it that his kinsmen do not restrain this one gone astray? Or out of affection a devoted friend, or the venerable Pitāmaha himself?

**5.104.4**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: What was fitting was spoken by the Blessed One; what was fitting was spoken by Bhīṣma; and what was manifold in kind was spoken also by Nārada—hear that.

**5.104.5**  Nārada said: Rare indeed is the friend who truly listens, and rare the friend who truly means well. A friend stands where a kinsman does not stand.

**5.104.6**  I see that the words of friends ought to be heeded, O joy of the Kurus; and one should not cling stubbornly—for stubborn insistence is utterly terrible.

**5.104.7**  On this point, too, they cite this ancient tale—how Gālava came to grief through stubborn insistence.

**5.104.8**  Long ago, Dharma, wishing to test Viśvāmitra as he performed austerities, approached him himself, taking the form of the venerable seer Vasiṣṭha.

**5.104.9**  Assuming the guise of one of the seven sages, O Bhārata, O king, hungry and craving food, he came to the hermitage of Kauśika.

**5.104.10**  Viśvāmitra, greatly agitated, set about cooking a dish of the finest food with great effort—but that guest did not wait for it.

**5.104.11**  When the food he had taken was eaten—given to him by other ascetics—Viśvāmitra came forward bearing the food that was still very hot.

**5.104.12**  "Wait for me—I have eaten," said the Blessed One, and departed; and Viśvāmitra, the greatly resplendent, stood there and waited, O king.

**5.104.13**  Holding that food reverently on his head, he stood to one side with his arms; motionless as a pillar nearby, he subsisted on air alone.

**5.104.14**  The sage Gālava made every effort to serve him—out of reverence, deep respect, heartfelt devotion, and desire to please.

**5.104.15**  Then, when a full hundred years had passed, Dharma came again, assuming the form of Vasiṣṭha, desiring food from Kauśika.

**5.104.16**  He saw the food being held reverently on the head of that great seer—the wise Viśvāmitra, who had stood there subsisting on air alone.

**5.104.17**  Then Dharma accepted the food—still hot, still fresh—and having eaten it, said to him "I am pleased, O seer among brahmin sages," and that sage departed.

**5.104.18**  Having moved beyond the nature of a kṣatriya and attained brahmanhood, Viśvāmitra was then filled with joy at Dharma's words.

**5.104.19**  And Viśvāmitra, pleased by the service and devotion of his ascetic disciple Gālava, said to him: "You have my leave, my child—go wherever you wish, Gālava."

**5.104.20**  Addressed thus, Gālava spoke these words in reply to that best of sages, to the resplendent Viśvāmitra, with a sweet and joyful voice:

**5.104.21**  "What fee shall I give you for this work of a guru? For a human undertaking succeeds only when accompanied by the proper gift."

**5.104.22**  "For it is taught that the fruit of a sacrifice in heaven is enjoyed only through the completion of gifts; by the good, the fee is called peace itself. What shall I bring for my teacher's sake? Let the Blessed One say."

**5.104.23**  But the Blessed One, though knowing, conquered by Gālava's service, urged him again and again: "Go, go."

**5.104.24**  Being told again and again "go, go" by Viśvāmitra, Gālava replied over and over: "What shall I give?"

**5.104.25**  But pressed again and again by the ascetic Gālava's insistence, Viśvāmitra, somewhat roused to irritation, said this:

**5.104.26**  "Give me eight hundred horses—white as the moon, each with one black ear—and go, Gālava, delay no more."

**5.105.1**  Nārada said: Thus addressed by the wise Viśvāmitra, Gālava neither sat nor lay down nor took food after that.

**5.105.2**  Reduced to skin and bones like a deer, wholly given over to worry and grief, lamenting beyond measure, and burning with anguish,

**5.105.3**  "Where are wealthy friends? Where is accumulated wealth? Where, for me, are eight hundred horses white as the moon?"

**5.105.4**  "Where is my desire for food? Where my desire for happiness? My very will to live is severed. What use is life to me?"

**5.105.5**  "I shall go to the far shore of the ocean, or to what lies beyond the farthest edge of the earth, and abandon myself there. What fruit is there in living?"

**5.105.6**  "For one who is poor, whose purpose is unfulfilled, who has been abandoned by every kind of fruit, who carries a debt—where is happiness in listlessness?"

**5.105.7**  "For one who has enjoyed the wealth of friends, who has received their cherished affection, and who is unable to repay them—death is better than life."

**5.105.8**  "For one who has promised 'I will do it' and does not do what must be done—burned by the fire of false speech—the merit of sacrifices and charitable works is destroyed."

**5.105.9**  "Untruth has no form; untruth has no progeny; untruth has no sovereignty—how then could it have an auspicious end?"

**5.105.10**  "Where is the glory of the ungrateful? Where his station? Where his happiness? The ungrateful cannot be trusted—and for ingratitude there is no atonement."

**5.105.11**  "The sinful man without wealth does not truly live. Where is the governance of sin? The sinful one surely reaches destruction, destroying what was done for him."

**5.105.12**  "I am that sinful, ungrateful, wretched, and untruthful one who, though benefited by my teacher, does not do what I promised. I shall release my vital breaths after making one final supreme effort."

**5.105.13**  "I have never before petitioned the gods; and yet all the thirty gods honor me in the midst of sacrificial assemblies."

**5.105.14**  "I shall go to Viṣṇu—the foremost of the wise, the god who is lord of the three worlds, to Kṛṣṇa, the best refuge for those who seek refuge."

**5.105.15**  "He from whom all enjoyments are established, pervading all gods and asuras—I wish to behold, with a pure heart, that great Yogin, the imperishable."

**5.105.16**  When he had spoken thus, his friend Garuḍa, the son of Vinatā, appeared before him and spoke joyfully, wishing to please him:

**5.105.17**  "You are regarded as my friend and as a friend among friends; a friend with resources should be engaged with the cherished desire of a friend."

**5.105.18**  "I have resources, O brahmin—the younger brother of Vāsava has previously been spoken to on your behalf, and he has granted my wish for your sake."

**5.105.19**  "So come, let us go—I will carry you comfortably to any place, or even to the far shore of the earth. Go, Gālava, delay no more."

**5.106.1**  Suparṇa said: I have been instructed by the Lord—by him who knows all origins and causes, O Gālava. Tell me: which direction shall I follow to visit first?

**5.106.2**  "Shall I go east, south, west, or north, O best of twice-born? Where shall I go, O Gālava?"

**5.106.3**  "In that direction where the Sun who illumines all worlds first rises, where at twilight the Sādhyas perform their austerities—

**5.106.4**  "—in which direction was the primordial mind first born, by which this world is pervaded; where are the two eyes of Dharma, where he himself is established—

**5.106.5**  "—from whose mouths the oblation offered glides in every direction: this is the door, O best of twice-born, of the day and of the path itself—

**5.106.6**  "—where the daughters of Dakṣa and their offspring were first born, where the sons born of Kaśyapa's own self grew mighty—

**5.106.7**  "—from where the glory of the gods has its root, where Śakra was consecrated to the sovereignty of the gods, O seer among brahmins, and where austerities were accumulated by the gods—

**5.106.8**  "—for this reason, O brahmin, this direction is called 'East,' because in a more ancient time the gods first frequented it.

**5.106.9**  "And for this very reason, for those of ancient lineage who look toward the eastern quarter, the primary works to be done are the divine works—by one who desires happiness."

**5.106.10**  "Here the Lord, the sustainer of worlds, first chanted the Vedas; here the Sāvitrī was first spoken by Savitṛ among those who discourse on Brahman."

**5.106.11**  "Here the Yajus were given by the Sun, O best of twice-born; here, by the gods who have obtained their boons, Soma is drunk at sacrifices."

**5.106.12**  "Here the fire-gods, satisfied, enjoy their own source; here, resorting to the netherworld Pātāla, Varuṇa attained his sovereignty."

**5.106.13**  "Here, O best of twice-born seers, were made manifest the ancient Vasiṣṭha's birth, his establishment, and his dissolution."

**5.106.14**  "Here a hundred decades of Oṃkāra's revelations are born; here the sages, drinkers of Soma, drink at the altar of the oblation shed."

**5.106.15**  "Here many animals in the forest—boars and others—were consecrated and assigned by Śakra to the gods as their share of the sacrifice."

**5.106.16**  "Here the impure and ungrateful ones—whether human or demonic—who arise are slain in wrath by the Blazing One himself."

**5.106.17**  "This is the door of the three worlds, of heaven, and of happiness. This is the eastern quarter—enter it if you wish."

**5.106.18**  "It is dear to me to serve him in whose word I abide. Say, Gālava, I will go—but also listen to the next direction."

**5.107.1**  Suparṇa said: This is the direction in which, it is said, Vivasvān formerly gave the sacrificial fee to his teacher according to Vedic rite—therefore it is called the South.

**5.107.2**  "Here the ancestral side of all these three worlds is established; here, it is heard, is the dwelling of the gods who drink the warm oblation, O twice-born."

**5.107.3**  Here the Viśvedevas always dwell together with the ancestors; being worshipped in the worlds, they have attained an equal share of the oblations.

**5.107.4**  This, O twice-born, is declared to be the second gate of righteousness; here the reckoning of time is counted in the smallest fractions of a moment.

**5.107.5**  Here divine sages dwell perpetually, and likewise the sages of the ancestors' realm, and all the royal sages, free from sorrow.

**5.107.6**  Here dharma and truth are observed, and deeds are witnessed; this is the path, O foremost of twice-born, for one whose self is brought low by its own actions.

**5.107.7**  This is that direction, O foremost of twice-born, which all must enter; yet being shrouded in ignorance, it cannot be traversed in happiness.

**5.107.8**  Here, O bull among twice-born, thousands of demons have been let loose and adverse things must be seen by those who have not mastered themselves.

**5.107.9**  Here in the groves of Mandara and in the dwellings of seer-sages, the Gandharvas sing songs that captivate the heart and mind, O twice-born.

**5.107.10**  Here, having heard the Sāma hymns sung together with ballads, Raivata abandoned wife, ministers, and kingdom and went to the forest.

**5.107.11**  Here a boundary was established by Sāvarṇi and the son of Yavakrīta, O brahmin, which even the sun does not transgress.

**5.107.12**  Here the great-souled king of the rākṣasas, Rāvaṇa, son of Pulastya, having performed austerities, chose immortality over the gods.

**5.107.13**  Here Vṛtra too, by his conduct, attained enmity with Indra; here all vital breaths, once spent, return and again disperse fivefold.

**5.107.14**  Here, O Gālava, men of evil deeds are tormented; here is the river named Vaitaraṇī, surrounded by those who help in crossing; having gone there, one reaches the end of happiness and the end of sorrow.

**5.107.15**  Here the sun, turning back, pours out the nectar of the gods; having reached the point of the Dhaniṣṭhā constellation, it releases frost once more.

**5.107.16**  Here I, O Gālava, in a former time, tormented by hunger and deliberating, found two great creatures locked in battle — an elephant and a tortoise.

**5.107.17**  Here the great sage named Śakradhanus was born from the sun; they know him as the divine Kapila, by whom the sons of Sagara were consumed.

**5.107.18**  Here dwell perfected brahmins called Śiva who have mastered the Vedas; having studied the entire corpus of the Vedas, they attain the realm of Yama.

**5.107.19**  Here is the city named Bhogavatī, guarded by Vāsuki, and likewise by the serpent Takṣaka and by Airāvata.

**5.107.20**  Here at the time of departing, a great darkness is encountered that cannot be pierced even by the sun or by fire, which travels on the dark path.

**5.107.21**  This too is the path of your anguish, O Gālava; tell me whether we should go west, or else listen to me.

**5.108.1**  Suparṇa said: This direction is the beloved realm of King Varuṇa, lord of the waters, always the foundation and very source of the king of the sea.

**5.108.2**  Here the sun releases its own light behind the day; this direction is renowned as the west, O foremost of twice-born.

**5.108.3**  Here the blessed divine Kaśyapa anointed Varuṇa with sovereignty over the creatures of the water and to guard the waters.

**5.108.4**  Here, having drunk all six essences of Varuṇa, the young Moon is born at the start of the bright fortnight, destroyer of darkness.

**5.108.5**  Here the demons were formerly bound by the wind; gasping and tormented by great serpents, they fell into deep sleep, O twice-born.

**5.108.6**  Here the mountain named Asta receives the devoted sun; it is from here that the western twilight creeps forth.

**5.108.7**  From here night and sleep emerge at the close of day, arising as if to take away half the life of the living world.

**5.108.8**  Here Indra caused the goddess Diti to miscarry while she slept, bearing within her the offspring of her own self; from that act the host of Maruts was born.

**5.108.9**  Here the root of Himavat reaches eternally to Mount Mandara; even in a thousand years its end cannot be reached.

**5.108.10**  Here, reaching the shore of the ocean by the golden mountain and the river of golden water, the divine cow Surabhī pours out her milk.

**5.108.11**  Here in the midst of the ocean the headless trunk of Svarbhānu is seen, that sun-like being who seeks to devour the sun and the moon.

**5.108.12**  Here too the vast resonance of the invisible and immeasurable golden-headed, tawny-haired one who sings is heard.

**5.108.13**  Here a maiden named Dhvajavatī, daughter of Harimedhas, stood suspended in the sky, halted there by the command of the sun.

**5.108.14**  Here, O Gālava, wind, fire, water, and ether release the touch of pain — both day's and night's; from this point onward the sun's course turns obliquely.

**5.108.15**  Here all the luminaries enter the solar orb, and having wandered together with the sun for twenty-eight nights, they issue forth again from the sun through union with the moon.

**5.108.16**  Here perpetually is the origin of rivers — the rising of the ocean; here the waters of the three worlds rest, sheltered by Varuṇa.

**5.108.17**  Here too is the dwelling of Ananta, king of serpents, and here is the unsurpassed abode of Viṣṇu, who has no beginning and no end.

**5.108.18**  Here too is the dwelling of Pavana, friend of fire, and here is the dwelling of the great sage Kaśyapa, son of Marīci.

**5.108.19**  This western path has been described to you through the gateway of the direction; tell me, O Gālava, where shall we go — what is your mind, O foremost of twice-born?

**5.109.1**  Suparṇa said: Because one is lifted from sin here, because one obtains the highest good here, therefore the wise call it uttarā — the north — from the fruit of being uplifted.

**5.109.2**  The path of the northern golden region, O Gālava, is indeed regarded as the middle way between the western and eastern directions.

**5.109.3**  In this most excellent northern direction, O bull among twice-born, no one who is disagreeable, no one of disobedient nature, no unrighteous person dwells.

**5.109.4**  Here dwell Nārāyaṇa, Kṛṣṇa, and the best of men Jiṣṇu at the hermitage of Badarī, and likewise the eternal Brahmā.

**5.109.5**  Here on the slopes of the Himālaya the great lord Maheśvara always abides; here the Moon was anointed with sovereignty over the brahmins.

**5.109.6**  Here the great god received the Gaṅgā as she fell, descended from the heavens, and gave her to the human world, O foremost knower of brahman.

**5.109.7**  Here the goddess performed austerities in her desire to win Maheśvara; here Kāma, Roṣa, the mountain, and Umā all came into being.

**5.109.8**  Here, O Gālava, Dhanada was anointed with lordship over the rākṣasas, yakṣas, and Gandharvas on Mount Kailāsa.

**5.109.9**  Here is the lovely garden of Caitraratha, here the hermitage of the Vaikhānasas, and here the Mandākinī river and Mount Mandara, O bull among twice-born.

**5.109.10**  Here the Saugandhika forest, lush with grass and stands of plantain, is guarded by the demons of the southwest; here grow the Santānaka trees.

**5.109.11**  Here are the celestial chariots suited to the perfected ones who are ever self-restrained and yet wander freely — chariots that fulfil every pleasure, O Gālava.

**5.109.12**  Here are those seven sages and the goddess Arundhatī as well; here the star Svāti stands, and here its rising is remembered.

**5.109.13**  Here the grandfather Brahmā, having mounted sacrifice, is established as the fixed Pole Star; the luminaries and the sun and moon revolve around him perpetually.

**5.109.14**  Here the foremost among twice-born guard the gate called Gāyantikā — great-souled sages named Dhāmā, ever truthful in speech.

**5.109.15**  Their birth is not known, nor their form, nor the austerities they have accumulated; thousands of cycles of pleasure await them, O Gālava.

**5.109.16**  The further a man ventures beyond that point, O foremost of twice-born, the more he dissolves away, O Gālava.

**5.109.17**  That region has never before been traversed by anyone else, O bull among twice-born, save the divine Nārāyaṇa or Nara, the imperishable Jiṣṇu.

**5.109.18**  Here is that place called Kailāsa, the abode of Ailavila; here ten apsarasas named Vidyutprabhā were born.

**5.109.19**  Here is what is called Viṣṇupada — the footprint made by Viṣṇu as he strode through the three worlds — situated in the northern direction, O brahmin.

**5.109.20**  Here King Marutta performed sacrifice, O foremost of twice-born, O seer among sages, at Uśīrabīja, where lies the lake of Jāmbūnada gold.

**5.109.21**  Here the sacred, pure, lotus-filled lake of the Himālayas attended in person upon the great-souled seer-sage Jīmūta.

**5.109.22**  Having donated all his great wealth to brahmins, that great sage chose the forest; hence that forest became known as Jaimūta — the forest of Jīmūta.

**5.109.23**  Here the guardians of the directions call out perpetually each morning and evening, O bull among twice-born: "Whose task? What is it?" — O Gālava.

**5.109.24**  Thus, O foremost of twice-born, this northern direction is renowned as uttarā — the north — for these and other virtues, and it is uttarā — superior — in all undertakings.

**5.109.25**  These four directions have been described to you at length, my friend, in due order; now tell me in which direction you wish to go according to your desire.

**5.109.26**  I am ready, O foremost of twice-born, to show you the directions and the entire earth, O brahmin; therefore mount me, O twice-born.

**5.110.1**  Gālava said: O Garuḍa, foe of the serpent lord, O Suparṇa, son of Vinatā, take me eastward, O Tārkṣya, to where the two eyes of dharma are.

**5.110.2**  Go first to that eastern direction which was described first; for it is there that you proclaimed the presence of the gods.

**5.110.3**  Truth and dharma have been well proclaimed there by you; I desire to come together with all the gods, and to behold those devas once more, O younger brother of Aruṇa.

**5.110.4**  Nārada said: The son of Vinatā said to that twice-born: "Mount me"; and then the sage Gālava mounted Garuḍa.

**5.110.5**  Gālava said: As you stride forward, O devourer of serpents, your form appears like that of the thousand-rayed Vivasvat in the forenoon.

**5.110.6**  I see here the motion of trees driven forward by the wind of your wings, all following together, as if they had set out on a journey, O bird.

**5.110.7**  You seem to be drawing the earth — with its oceans and forests, its mountains, woods, and thickets — toward you with the wind of your wings, O traveller of the sky.

**5.110.8**  The water with its fish, serpents, and crocodiles seems to be lifted up into the sky by the great wind and by your wing-gust, ceaselessly.

**5.110.9**  I see fish that all look alike, whales that eat fish, creatures that swallow even whales, and serpents with human faces, all churned up as it were.

**5.110.10**  My ears have been deafened by the roar of the great ocean; I neither hear nor see, and I do not know the reason for my own state.

**5.110.11**  Please go slowly, keeping in mind the sin of slaying a brahmin; the sun cannot be seen, my friend, nor the directions, nor the sky, O bird.

**5.110.12**  I see only darkness and cannot make out your body; your two eyes I can see, O egg-born, like a pair of precious gems.

**5.110.13**  I cannot see your body, nor indeed my own; at every step I see fire rising up from the water.

**5.110.14**  That fire suddenly extinguishes my sight and then it calms; therefore turn back — a great time has passed in our going, O son of Vinatā.

**5.110.15**  I have no purpose left in this journey, O devourer of serpents; turn back, O one of great speed — I cannot bear your velocity.

**5.110.16**  For I have promised my teacher eight hundred horses here — white horses with one dark ear each, radiant as the moon.

**5.110.17**  I see no way to bring those to an end, O bird. Therefore this is the path I have found for myself — to abandon life itself.

**5.110.18**  I have no wealth at all, nor any friend possessed of wealth; nor can this debt be discharged even by a great sum.

**5.110.19**  Nārada said: While Gālava was speaking thus at length and in misery, the son of Vinatā, even as he flew on, laughed and replied.

**5.110.20**  You are not very wise, O brahmin-seer, that you wish to cast away your life. Moreover, one must not force time; for Time is the supreme lord.

**5.110.21**  Why did you not prompt me about this matter from the very beginning? There is a great means by which this can be accomplished.

**5.110.22**  There on the shore of the ocean is a mountain named Ṛṣabha. Let us rest and eat there, and then we shall turn our course, O Gālava.

**5.111.1**  Nārada said: The two — the brahmin and the bird — then alighted on the peak of Ṛṣabha, and there they saw the brahmin woman Śāṇḍilī, endowed with austerity.

**5.111.2**  Suparṇa saluted her and Gālava honoured her respectfully; and welcomed by her, the two sat down upon seats she offered.

**5.111.3**  She quickly prepared food, enriched with sacred offerings and mantras; and having eaten and become satisfied, both of them lay down on the ground, overcome by the food.

**5.111.4**  After a while Suparṇa awoke, wishing to depart; but then the bird found himself stripped of the limbs of his body.

**5.111.5**  The bird had become like a lump of flesh, retaining only his beak and feet. Gālava, seeing him in that state, was distressed and questioned him.

**5.111.6**  What fruit of coming here has befallen you? And for how long shall we have to remain here?

**5.111.7**  What inauspicious and dharma-violating thought did you harbour in your mind? For this transgression of yours is surely not a trifling one.

**5.111.8**  Suparṇa then said to the brahmin: I had indeed meditated, O brahmin, on carrying this perfected woman away from here to the place where Prajāpati dwells.

**5.111.9**  Where the god Mahādeva is, where eternal Viṣṇu is, where Dharma and Sacrifice dwell — there, I thought, let her reside.

**5.111.10**  Therefore I bow and beg the gracious lady with a mind well disposed; indeed my mind pondered this wish, though I am now filled with remorse.

**5.111.11**  Out of deep reverence for you I here sought what was not to be sought; whether it was well done or ill done, by your great-souled grace please forgive it.

**5.111.12**  Pleased, she then said to the two — the lord of birds and the best of brahmins: Fear not; you are Suparṇa — O Suparṇa, set aside your agitation.

**5.111.13**  You have reproached me, my child, and I do not lightly forgive reproach; the sinful one who disparages me falls from the worlds.

**5.111.14**  Though lacking in all auspicious marks, and not censured by me, having accepted righteous conduct, I have attained this supreme perfection.

**5.111.15**  From right conduct one gains dharma; from right conduct one gains wealth; through right conduct one attains prosperity; right conduct destroys inauspiciousness.

**5.111.16**  Therefore, O long-lived lord of birds, go from here as you wish. And women who are worthy of blame should nonetheless never be blamed by you.

**5.111.17**  You shall be as before, endowed with strength and valour. Thereupon his wings became even more splendid than before.

**5.111.18**  Permitted by Śāṇḍilī, he set out as he had come; yet he was unable to find horses of that description.

**5.111.19**  Viśvāmitra then, seeing Gālava standing on the road in the presence of the son of Vinatā, spoke — the foremost of the eloquent.

**5.111.20**  The matter which you yourself promised me, O brahmin — the time for its fulfillment has come, or so you may reckon.

**5.111.21**  I shall wait this much time and a little beyond, as the means for accomplishing it may be found, O brahmin. That course of action, however, should be heard now.

**5.111.22**  Suparṇa then said to the wretched and deeply distressed Gālava: What Viśvāmitra said is now plainly before me.

**5.111.23**  So come, O best of brahmins, let us deliberate together, O Gālava; it is not possible for you to live without having given the guru-gift in full.

**5.112.1**  Nārada said: Then the best of birds addressed the wretched Gālava: What was fashioned by fire on the earth and ordered by the wind — because everything is made of gold-stuff, it is called hiraṇya.

**5.112.2**  It bears and sustains everything; for that reason it is called wealth (dhana). This wealth abides eternally in the three worlds.

**5.112.3**  Ever through the two Proṣṭhapada stars and through Śukra the lord of wealth, Śukra takes from men the wealth earned by the mind.

**5.112.4**  It is guarded by Ajaikapāda, Ahirbudhnya, and Dhanada; thus what is not destined to be obtained cannot be obtained, O best of brahmins.

**5.112.5**  Apart from the wealth of horses there is no fulfillment for you. Beg here from some king born of royal-seer lineage — a king who, without oppressing his people, would make both of us successful in our aim.

**5.112.6**  There is in the lunar lineage a certain king, a friend of mine. Let us go to him; he has great prosperity on earth.

**5.112.7**  He is Yayāti, the royal seer, son of Nahuṣa, true in valour; spoken to by me and personally petitioned by you, he will give.

**5.112.8**  His prosperity had been exceedingly great, like that of the lord of wealth; knowing the nature of wealth, he purified himself through charity alone.

**5.112.9**  Thus deliberating and reflecting on what was proper, the two presented themselves at the abode of the lord of men, Yayāti.

**5.112.10**  Having received the hospitality of welcome-gift and excellent food, and being asked the reason for coming, the son of Vinatā spoke.

**5.112.11**  This is my friend, O son of Nahuṣa — Gālava, a treasury of austerity; he was the disciple of Viśvāmitra for tens of thousands of years, O king.

**5.112.12**  Released by him and wishing to offer something in return, this brahmin asked the venerable one: What guru-gift shall I give you?

**5.112.13**  Being asked repeatedly by him who was slightly angered, and knowing his own modest means, he was told: Give this.

**5.112.14**  Give me eight hundred horses of uniform dark ear, white-bodied, of pure birth, brilliant as the moon.

**5.112.15**  Let this be given as the guru-gift, if you concur, O Gālava — thus spoke Viśvāmitra, the wealth of whose power lay in austerity, with some anger.

**5.112.16**  This best of brahmins, scorched by great grief and unable to fulfil it, has come to you for refuge.

**5.112.17**  Having received alms from you, O tiger among men, freed from distress, having discharged the debt to his guru, he shall practise great austerity.

**5.112.18**  Through a share of his austerity he will connect you as well; with his own austerity befitting a royal seer he will fill you who are already full.

**5.112.19**  As many hairs as there are on a horse, O lord of men, so many worlds do the givers of horses attain, O lord of the earth.

**5.112.20**  He is the worthy vessel for receiving, and you likewise are the worthy vessel for giving; let this be like milk poured into a conch — well fitted in comparison.

**5.113.1**  Nārada said: Thus addressed by Suparṇa with true and excellent words, the king, attentive and having deliberated again and again, resolved.

**5.113.2**  Yayāti, performer of thousands of sacrifices, lord of giving, the master, the ruler of Vatsakā and Kāśī, spoke these words.

**5.113.3**  Having seen Tārkṣya my dear friend, and Gālava the best of brahmins, and the manifest proof of austerity, and the praiseworthy begging mission proclaimed —

**5.113.4**  And having bypassed other kings born of the solar lineage, these two have come to my presence — weighing my judgment —

**5.113.5**  Today my birth is fulfilled; today my lineage is redeemed; today this my country is redeemed by you, O sinless Tārkṣya.

**5.113.6**  Yet I wish to speak, my friend, as you know me from before — I am not as wealthy as I was; my wealth is depleted, O friend.

**5.113.7**  I am not able to make your coming here in vain, O bird; nor do I have the heart to make the hope of this brahmin-seer empty.

**5.113.8**  Yet I will give what will accomplish this task; for one who goes to a patron and returns with hope destroyed burns down the lineage.

**5.113.9**  Nothing more sinful than this is spoken of, O son of Vinatā — as the word 'give' is the destroyer of expectation in the world, so is the word 'there is not.'

**5.113.10**  For a respected man, if he fails to act and leaves a petitioner with dashed hopes and unaccomplished aim, destroys his sons and grandsons.

**5.113.11**  Therefore my daughter — like the daughter of the gods, enriching all dharma, the establisher of four lineages — this one I offer.

**5.113.12**  Ever desired by gods, men, and asuras alike for her beauty, O Gālava — let this young daughter of mine be accepted.

**5.113.13**  Kings will give even their kingdoms as her bride-price without doubt; how much more will they give two hundred of the dark-eared horses?

**5.113.14**  You, sir, please accept this my daughter Mādhavī; I shall become possessed of grandsons — that indeed is my boon, O lord.

**5.113.15**  Gālava accepted that maiden, and with the bird said 'We shall meet again,' then set out with the maiden.

**5.113.16**  'This door to the horses has been found,' said the egg-born one; and bidding farewell to Gālava, he went to his own home.

**5.113.17**  When the lord of birds had gone, Gālava, together with the maiden, reflecting on who among kings was capable of giving the bride-price, set out.

**5.113.18**  He went mentally toward Haryaśva of the Ikṣvāku line, the best of kings, of great valour, endowed with a four-fold army at Ayodhyā.

**5.113.19**  Possessed of treasury, grain, and military strength, beloved by his citizens, dear to brahmins, eager to protect his subjects, practicing supreme austerity in tranquility.

**5.113.20**  Approaching him, Gālava the brahmin said to Haryaśva: O king of kings, this is my maiden — she who through her offspring increases dynasties.

**5.113.21**  Accept her, O Haryaśva, as a wife in exchange for bride-price. I shall declare the bride-price to you; hear it and then deliberate.

**5.114.1**  Nārada said: Then King Haryaśva, having pondered deeply and heaving a long hot sigh for the sake of progeny — that best of kings — spoke.

**5.114.2**  Elevated among the six elevated things; subtle among the seven subtle things; deep among the three deep things; and red in five respects — she has all this.

**5.114.3**  Beheld by many gods and asuras, seen by many gandharvas, endowed with many auspicious marks, bearing the capacity for many births.

**5.114.4**  She is capable of giving birth to a world-ruling son. Tell me the bride-price, O best of brahmins, having considered my resources.

**5.114.5**  Gālava said: Give me eight hundred horses of uniform dark ear, moon-white, born of the proper country, of handsome form.

**5.114.6**  Then she will be yours — the auspicious mother of sons, the womb of your fires like the araṇī is the source of the sacrificial flame, the large-eyed one.

**5.114.7**  Nārada said: Hearing these words, King Haryaśva, bewildered by desire, the royal seer spoke in distress to Gālava the best of sages.

**5.114.8**  Two hundred horses of the kind you describe are at hand for me; other horses of mine roam by the hundreds.

**5.114.9**  I shall beget a single offspring upon her, O Gālava; let you fulfil this desire of mine as a boon.

**5.114.10**  Hearing this, the maiden spoke these words to Gālava: A certain boon was granted to me by a speaker of brahman —

**5.114.11**  After each birth, after each birth, you shall become a maiden again. Therefore give me to the king, having first received those finest horses.

**5.114.12**  From four kings there will indeed be eight full hundreds of horses for you, and four sons for me.

**5.114.13**  Let my deliverance be accomplished for the sake of my teacher, O best of twice-born. This is my judgment for now — or think as you see fit, O brahmin.

**5.114.14**  Thus addressed by the maiden, the sage Gālava then spoke these words to Haryaśva, guardian of the earth.

**5.114.15**  O best of men, O Haryaśva, accept this maiden. In exchange for a quarter of the bride-price, beget one son upon her.

**5.114.16**  He accepted that maiden, praised Gālava joyfully, and at the proper time and place obtained the desired son.

**5.114.17**  Then there was born a king named Vasumanas, richer than the wealthy, comparable to wealth itself, a bestower of wealth.

**5.114.18**  Then in due time the wise Gālava came back again and, approaching Haryaśva with a glad heart, spoke to him.

**5.114.19**  O king, your son has been born, blazing like the infant sun. The time has come, O best of men, to go to another king for the rest of the gift.

**5.114.20**  Haryaśva, firm in his word and steadfast in his manly duty, and knowing the rarity of such horses, gave Mādhavī back again.

**5.114.21**  And Mādhavī, renouncing the splendid royal majesty once more, willingly became a virgin again and followed behind Gālava.

**5.114.22**  "Let the horses remain with you for now," said the brahmin, and he departed with the maiden toward Divodāsa, lord of his people.

**5.115.1**  Gālava said: There is a king of mighty valor, guardian of the earth, lord and master of the Kāśis, known as Divodāsa, son of Bhīmasena, ruler of men.

**5.115.2**  Let us go there, dear one. Come along gently; do not grieve. He is righteous, devoted to self-restraint, and truthful — that lord of his people.

**5.115.3**  Nārada said: The sage approached him and was duly honored by him. Then Gālava urged that king for the purpose of procreating.

**5.115.4**  Divodāsa said: I have heard all of this before — why speak at length, O brahmin? This very matter has indeed been desired by me upon hearing it, O best of twice-born.

**5.115.5**  And this is highly agreeable to me: that passing over other kings you have come to me in this way. Without doubt, this outcome was destined.

**5.115.6**  The same wealth of horses is ours as well, O Gālava. I too shall beget only one royal son upon her.

**5.115.7**  Nārada said: Saying "so be it," the best of brahmins gave the maiden to the lord of the earth, and the king received that maiden according to proper rite.

**5.115.8**  That royal sage sported with her radiantly as the sun sports with his consort, as Fire sports with Svāhā, as Vāsava sports with Śacī.

**5.115.9**  As the Moon with Rohiṇī, as Yama with Dhumorna, as Varuṇa with Gaurī, as the lord of wealth with his prosperity.

**5.115.10**  As Nārāyaṇa with Lakṣmī, as the ocean with the Jāhnavī, as Rudra with Rudrani, as the Grandfather with the sacrificial altar-Goddess.

**5.115.11**  As Vasiṣṭha with Adrishyanti, and Vasiṣṭha also with Akshamala, as Cyavana with Sukanyā, as Pulastya with Sandhya.

**5.115.12**  As Agastya with the maiden of Vidarbha, as Satyavan with Sāvitrī, as Bhṛgu with Pulomā, as Kaśyapa with Aditi.

**5.115.13**  As Richika with Renuka, as Kauśika with the daughter of the Himālaya, as Bṛhaspati with Tara, as Śukra with Shataparvā.

**5.115.14**  As the lord of earth with the Earth-Goddess, as Purūravas with Urvaśī, as Richika with Satyavatī, as Manu with Sarasvatī.

**5.115.15**  Thus while King Divodāsa sported with her, Mādhavī gave birth to a single son named Pratardana.

**5.115.16**  Then the blessed Gālava came to Divodāsa when the appointed time had arrived and spoke these words.

**5.115.17**  "Let your lordship return the maiden to me; let the horses remain with you while I go elsewhere for the bride-price, O lord of the earth."

**5.115.18**  Then Divodāsa, righteous in soul, at Gālava's appointed time, returned that maiden — that king standing firm in his truth.

**5.116.1**  Nārada said: Just as before, she renounced her royal splendor and became a maiden again — the glorious Mādhavī, true to her vow, followed the brahmin Gālava.

**5.116.2**  Gālava, reflecting and with his mind fixed on his purpose, went to the city of the Bhojas to see the king Uśīnara.

**5.116.3**  Going to that king of true valor, he said: "This maiden will bear you two royal sons."

**5.116.4**  "Begetting two sons equal to the Moon and Sun upon her, you shall gain your object both in this world and the next, O king."

**5.116.5**  "But the bride-price to be paid to me, O knower of all dharma, is four hundred horses of moon-like splendor, each with one black ear."

**5.116.6**  "This undertaking is for the sake of my teacher; I have no personal use for the horses. If it is possible, O great king, let it be done without hesitation."

**5.116.7**  "You are without a son, O royal sage. Beget sons, O king; ferry your ancestors and yourself across by the raft of sons."

**5.116.8**  "For he who enjoys the fruit of having sons, O royal sage, does not fall from heaven; he does not go to the terrible hell where the sonless go."

**5.116.9**  Having heard all this and much else besides from Gālava's speech, Uśīnara the king of men gave him his reply.

**5.116.10**  Uśīnara said: I have heard your words as you have spoken them, O Gālava. Fate is powerful, O brahmin — my mind is indeed inclined toward this.

**5.116.11**  "Of horses like these I have two hundred, O best of twice-born. Thousands upon thousands of others roam in my possession."

**5.116.12**  "I too shall beget only one son upon her, O Gālava, O brahmin. I shall travel the path that others have gone."

**5.116.13**  "I could even make up the full price for you, O best of twice-born — but my wealth belongs to my townspeople and country folk, not for my own enjoyment."

**5.116.14**  "For a king who gives away others' wealth out of desire, O righteous one, is not associated with virtue or with glory."

**5.116.15**  "So I will accept her — let your lordship give me this maiden, radiant as a goddess, to beget one son upon her."

**5.116.16**  The best of brahmins, Gālava, honored King Uśīnara in return for speaking so auspiciously in so many ways.

**5.116.17**  Having given her over to Uśīnara, Gālava departed to the forest. Uśīnara sported with her, who had come to him, like one who has earned good merit delighting in prosperity.

**5.116.18**  In the gorges of mountains and the cascades of rivers, in pleasure-gardens of all kinds, in forests and groves,

**5.116.19**  In delightful mansions and on the pinnacles of palaces, in airy upper-chambers and in inner apartments,

**5.116.20**  Then in due time there was born to him a son blazing like the young sun — he who became widely renowned as Śibi, that best of kings.

**5.116.21**  The brahmin Gālava came to him, received the maiden back, and as he departed, O king, he saw the son of Vinatā.

**5.117.1**  Nārada said: The son of Vinatā then laughingly said to Gālava: "By good fortune I see you here with your purpose accomplished, O brahmin."

**5.117.2**  Gālava, hearing the words spoken by the son of Vinatā, reported to him that a quarter of his task still remained undone.

**5.117.3**  Suparṇa, best among birds, said to him: "No effort need be made by you — this will not succeed for you."

**5.117.4**  "For long ago, at Kanyakubja, Richika chose the maiden Satyavatī, daughter of Gādhi, as his wife, and being spoken to said:"

**5.117.5**  "'Let a thousand horses of moon-like splendor, each with one black ear, be given to me, O lord' — so spoke he, O Gālava."

**5.117.6**  "Richika, saying 'so be it,' went to the abode of Varuṇa, obtained the horses at the Ashvatirtha ford, and gave them to the king."

**5.117.7**  "Those horses were given away by the king to brahmins after a Pundarika sacrifice. Two hundred each were then bought from them by kings."

**5.117.8**  "The remaining four hundred, O best of twice-born, were swept away by the Vitastā river while being ferried across. Thus the unattainable can never be attained, O Gālava."

**5.117.9**  "Present this maiden along with these two hundred horses to Viśvāmitra, O righteous one — six hundred horses in all. Then you shall be free of your confusion, your duty done, O bull among twice-born."

**5.117.10**  Gālava said "so be it" to him, and then together with Suparṇa, taking the horses and the maiden, he came to Viśvāmitra.

**5.117.11**  Gālava said: Here are six hundred of the desired horses. Together with the remaining two hundred, accept this maiden, your lordship.

**5.117.12**  "By royal sages three righteous sons have already been born upon her. Let your lordship also beget a fourth single one, O best of men."

**5.117.13**  "In this way eight full hundreds of horses will be yours. Being freed from your debt to me, I shall then practice austerities at my ease."

**5.117.14**  Nārada said: Viśvāmitra, seeing Gālava along with the bird and that woman of finest form, spoke these words.

**5.117.15**  "Why was this maiden not given to me here at the very beginning, O Gālava? All four sons could have been mine, nurturing the lineage."

**5.117.16**  "I accept your maiden for the fruit of one son. And let the horses all go to my hermitage and remain there."

**5.117.17**  Then the great-splendored Viśvāmitra, sporting with her, begat a son — Aṣṭaka, born of Mādhavī.

**5.117.18**  As soon as that son was born, the great-splendored Viśvāmitra invested him with wealth, with duties, and with those very horses.

**5.117.19**  Then Aṣṭaka went to his city, which shone like the city of the Moon, while Kauśika, after giving the maiden to his disciple, went to the forest.

**5.117.20**  Gālava too, setting out southward together with Suparṇa, his mind at peace, said this to the maiden.

**5.117.21**  "A son who is a lord of giving has been born of you, and another who is a hero, yet another devoted to truth and dharma, and likewise one who is a great sacrificer."

**5.117.22**  "Therefore come, O fine-hipped one — your father has been ferried across by his sons, and four kings as well, and I too, O slender-waisted one."

**5.117.23**  Gālava took leave of Suparṇa, devourer of serpents, delivered the maiden to her father, and then departed to the forest.

**5.118.1**  Nārada said: But the king, wishing to hold a svayamvara for her again, came to the hermitage at the confluence of the Gaṅgā and Yamunā.

**5.118.2**  Placing Mādhavī, garland-wreath in hand, upon a chariot, Puru and Yadu ran around their sister in the hermitage.

**5.118.3**  There was a great gathering there of serpents, yakshas, and humans, of flying creatures, beasts, and birds, of those dwelling in mountains, trees, and forests.

**5.118.4**  The forest was crowded with lords of many human realms and surrounded on all sides by sages equal to Brahmā.

**5.118.5**  But when the suitors were being named, she of finest complexion passed over all of them and chose the forest as her mate.

**5.118.6**  Descending from the chariot, the maiden paid obeisance to her kinsmen, and entering the sacred forest, that daughter of Yayāti performed austerities.

**5.118.7**  Through various fasts, consecrations, and vows, having made herself light in body, she became one who roamed with the deer.

**5.118.8**  She wandered about eating tender green shoots resembling beryl gemstones, the finest young grasses, both bitter and sweet.

**5.118.9**  And drinking the fragrant, pure, choicest waters of holy rivers — cold and crystal clear.

**5.118.10**  In forests where lions had once roamed and then departed, in thickets freed from forest fires, in desolate and dense groves —

**5.118.11**  roaming with the deer like a doe ranging through the forest, she practiced great dharma, fully covered by the vow of brahmacharya.

**5.118.12**  Yayāti too, having followed the conduct of kings of old, after a life spanning thousands of years, met the law of time — death.

**5.118.13**  The two lineages of Pūru and Yadu, growing and foremost among men — through these two, the son of Nahuṣa was established in this world and the next.

**5.118.14**  King Yayāti, having ascended to heaven, was honored — that king, equal to a great ṛṣi, the mighty one who enjoyed the foremost fruits of heaven.

**5.118.15**  When a span of many thousands of years had passed, replete with virtues, and he sat amid honored royal seers and great sages —

**5.118.16**  Yayāti, his understanding obscured by folly, his mind seized by conceit, began to despise all men, gods, and hosts of seers.

**5.118.17**  Then the god Śakra, slayer of Bala, became aware of him; and all those royal sages said, "Shame! Shame!" thus they spoke.

**5.118.18**  And a discussion arose as they looked upon the son of Nahuṣa: "Who is this? Of which king? How did he come to heaven?"

**5.118.19**  "By what deed has he attained this state? Where did he accumulate austerity? How is he known in heaven, and by whom is he known?"

**5.118.20**  Thus deliberating, those kings dwelling in heaven, having seen him, questioned one another about King Yayāti.

**5.118.21**  The hundreds of keepers of the celestial chariots, the guardians of heaven's gates, were asked — and so were the keepers of the thrones — and they replied: "We do not know."

**5.118.22**  All of them, their knowledge veiled, did not recognize that king. Then after a moment the king's vigor was struck down.

**5.119.1**  Nārada said: Then he was dislodged from his place and fell from his seat, his mind trembling, assailed by the fire of grief.

**5.119.2**  His garland withered, his understanding departed, his crown and armlets fallen, he spun about with all his limbs slack, his ornaments and garments dropping away.

**5.119.3**  Becoming invisible to them as he gazed at them, and then unable to see them again and again, empty, with an empty mind, he was about to plunge to the earth.

**5.119.4**  "What inauspicious thing, what corruption of dharma, did I contemplate in my mind, by which I have been dislodged from my place?" — thus the king reflected.

**5.119.5**  And those kings, siddhas, and apsarases, still there in that place, saw Yayāti falling without support, dislodged.

**5.119.6**  Then a certain person, the one who casts down those whose merit is exhausted, came and spoke to Yayāti by command of the king of gods:

**5.119.7**  "You are utterly intoxicated with pride; there is no one you do not despise. Fallen through arrogance, heaven is not for you, O son of an earthly king — you are not recognized. Go — fall!" Thus he addressed him.

**5.119.8**  Having spoken thrice the words "May I fall among the virtuous," the son of Nahuṣa, as he was about to fall, reflected on a destination — he who was foremost among those with discernment.

**5.119.9**  At that very moment, the king saw four foremost kings in Naimiṣa forest, and he fell down in their midst.

**5.119.10**  Pratardana, Vasumanā, Śibi of the Uśīnaras, and Aṣṭaka — they were satisfying the lord of the gods with the Vājapeya sacrifice.

**5.119.11**  Yayāti, inhaling the sacrificial smoke from their rite as it rose to the gate of heaven, fell down toward the earth.

**5.119.12**  That lord of the world, taking hold of the river of smoke — which connected earth and heaven and flowed like the Gaṅgā — descended.

**5.119.13**  The king fell into the midst of those four splendid kinsmen, who had just completed the final bath of their sacrifice and who were equal to guardians of the worlds.

**5.119.14**  The royal sage Yayāti, his merit exhausted, fell into the midst of those four, who were like oblation fires, like lions among kings, blazing greatly.

**5.119.15**  All those kings addressed him — he who was like an image of splendor: "Who are you? Whose kinsman? Of what land or city?"

**5.119.16**  "Are you a yakṣa, or a god, a gandharva, or perhaps a rākṣasa? You are not of human form. What purpose do you seek?"

**5.119.17**  Yayāti said: I am Yayāti, the royal sage, fallen from heaven with my merit exhausted. Meditating on the words "May I fall among the virtuous," I fell here, among you.

**5.119.18**  The kings said: Let that which you desired be true, O bull among men. Let the fruit of all our sacrifices and the merit of our dharma be accepted by you.

**5.119.19**  Yayāti said: I am no brahmin whose wealth is accepting gifts — I am a kṣatriya. Nor is my mind inclined toward the destruction of another's merit.

**5.119.20**  Nārada said: At that very time, those kings, seeing Mādhavī who had come there in the course of her wandering with the deer, greeted her and said this:

**5.119.21**  "What is the purpose of your coming? What shall we do? Your command — for we are all at your command, your sons, O you rich in austerity."

**5.119.22**  Hearing their words, Mādhavī with great joy approached her father, and she bowed to Yayāti.

**5.119.23**  Seeing her sons bowing their heads, the ascetic woman spoke these words: "Your grandsons, O king of kings, my own sons — they are not strangers to you. These will rescue you — this is the ancient decree of fate."

**5.119.24**  "I am your daughter, O king — Mādhavī, the one who roams with the deer. I too have accumulated merit; let half of that be accepted by you."

**5.119.25**  "Because, O king, all men share in the fruit of their offspring, therefore they desire their daughter's sons — even as you do, O lord of the earth."

**5.119.26**  Then all those kings, bowing their heads in reverence to their mother, and paying homage, then spoke to their maternal grandfather:

**5.119.27**  The kings, filling the earth with lofty, incomparable, affectionate voices, strove to rescue their maternal grandfather who had fallen from heaven.

**5.119.28**  Then Gālava, coming near, also spoke to that king: "Let Your Majesty ascend to heaven by an eighth part of my austerity."

**5.120.1**  Nārada said: The very moment he was recognized by those virtuous men, that bull among men Yayāti assumed a divine form and became free of affliction.

**5.120.2**  Wearing a divine garland and divine garments, adorned with divine ornaments, endowed with divine fragrance, his feet no longer touched the earth.

**5.120.3**  Then Vasumanā, renowned in the world as the lord of generosity, spoke loudly first and addressed the king:

**5.120.4**  "Whatever I have gained in this world through giving without censure to all classes of people — that I shall give; may you be joined with that merit."

**5.120.5**  "Whatever fruit belongs to one given to generosity, whatever fruit belongs to one given to forbearance, and whatever fruit I have in sacred deposits — may you be joined with that."

**5.120.6**  Then Pratardana, that bull among kṣatriyas, who was always devoted to dharma and always intent on battle, also spoke:

**5.120.7**  "Whatever glory arising from kṣatriya dharma I have gained in this world, and the fruit of being called a hero — may you be joined with that."

**5.120.8**  Śibi of the Uśīnaras, the wise one, spoke sweet words: "Just as toward children, toward women, and toward the helpless —"

**5.120.9**  "— in battles, in falls from fortune, in adversities and calamities alike — I have never previously spoken an untruth. By that truth, ascend to heaven."

**5.120.10**  "As I would give up life, kingdom, action, and pleasures, O king, but never truth — by that truth, ascend to heaven."

**5.120.11**  "As my dharma rests on truth, as fire is pleased by truth, as Śakra is pleased by truth — by that truth, ascend to heaven."

**5.120.12**  Then Aṣṭaka, the royal sage, the Kauśika, son of Mādhavī, the knower of dharma who had performed hundreds of sacrifices, spoke these words:

**5.120.13**  "Hundreds of Puṇḍarīka sacrifices and Gosava rites have been performed by me, O lord, and Vājapeya sacrifices — receive the fruit of all these."

**5.120.14**  "No jewels of mine, no wealth, nor any other possessions have remained unused in sacrifices. By that truth, ascend to heaven."

**5.120.15**  And as the grandsons spoke thus to that king, step by step, leaving the earth, the king ascended to heaven.

**5.120.16**  Thus all those assembled kings, through their meritorious deeds, swiftly rescued Yayāti who had fallen from heaven.

**5.120.17**  The grandsons, through their own dharma earned by sacrifice and gifts, born in four royal lineages and enhancers of their families, they lifted that supremely wise maternal grandfather to heaven.

**5.120.18**  The kings said: Endowed with the qualities of royal dharma, possessed of all virtuous qualities, we are your grandsons, O king. Ascend to heaven, O earthly lord.

**5.121.1**  Nārada said: Lifted to heaven by those virtuous kings of great gifts, Yayāti took leave of his grandsons and ascended to the sky.

**5.121.2**  He was showered with a rain of various fragrant flowers, and embraced by a pure, holy-scented wind.

**5.121.3**  Ascending to an immovable station won by the merit of his grandsons, enriched by his own deeds, he blazed with supreme splendor.

**5.121.4**  Sung to and danced around by hosts of gandharvas and apsarases, he was welcomed with joy in heaven to the sound of divine drums.

**5.121.5**  Praised with various eulogies by gods, royal sages, and cāraṇas, he was honored with the finest tribute and celebrated by the gods.

**5.121.6**  Having attained the fruit of heaven, the Grandsire (Brahmā) spoke to him — who was content and of tranquil mind — as if satisfying him with words:

**5.121.7**  "The four-footed dharma has been gathered by you through meritorious deeds. This world of yours is imperishable, and your fame in heaven imperishable — moreover, O royal sage, today through your good deed here."

**5.121.8**  "The minds of all the dwellers in heaven had been veiled in darkness, by which they did not recognize you — thus, not being known, you were cast down."

**5.121.9**  "And through the love of your grandsons you have been rescued and come here, and you have regained the station won by your own deeds — immovable, eternal, holy, supreme, permanent, and imperishable."

**5.121.10**  Yayāti said: O Blessed One, there is a certain doubt in me; please resolve it. For there is no other I am fit to ask, O Grandsire of the worlds.

**5.121.11**  "The great fruit I had earned — spanning thousands upon thousands of years, built by the protection of subjects, accumulated through floods of sacrifices and gifts —"

**5.121.12**  "— how was that exhausted in so short a time, causing my fall? O Blessed One, you know the eternal worlds that I had won."

**5.121.13**  The Grandsire said: That great fruit you had earned — spanning thousands upon thousands of years, built by the protection of subjects, accumulated through floods of sacrifices and gifts —

**5.121.14**  "— that was exhausted by this very fault by which you were cast down: by arrogance, O king of kings, you were condemned by the dwellers in heaven."

**5.121.15**  "This eternal world, O royal sage, is not won by pride, nor by force, nor by violence, nor by cunning, nor by deceit."

**5.121.16**  "The inferior, the exalted, and the middling are not to be despised by you, O king. For among those burned by contempt, there is no one who is at peace anywhere."

**5.121.17**  "Those men who shall recount this fall and rising again — even when they encounter adversities, they will cross over them without doubt."

**5.121.18**  Nārada said: This is the fault that was incurred long ago by Yayāti through arrogance, and also by Gālava through excessive insistence, O lord of the earth.

**5.121.19**  Those who wish well-being for their friends and desire their prosperity should listen: one must not persist stubbornly, for stubborn insistence is the seed of ruin.

**5.121.20**  Therefore you too, O son of Gāndhārī, abandon pride and anger; make peace with the Pāṇḍavas, O hero; relinquish your fury, O king.

**5.121.21**  Whatever a king gives, whatever he does, whatever austerity he practises, whatever oblation he offers — none of it is lost or diminished; no other reaps it: the doer alone enjoys the fruit.

**5.121.22**  This great narrative is held to be unsurpassed by the learned who are free from passion and anger; having considered it, the one who pursues the three ends of life in their right order enjoys the earth in its entirety.

**5.122.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: Revered sage, it is exactly as you say; I too desire this very thing, yet I am not master of it, O blessed one.

**5.122.2**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having spoken thus, he then addressed Kṛṣṇa, O Bhārata: "You have told me what leads to heaven and to worldly good, what is righteous and just, O Keśava."

**5.122.3**  "But I am not my own master, dear one; what is being done is not pleasing to me — Duryodhana, O Kṛṣṇa, is dull-witted and transgresses the scripture, yet he is my son."

**5.122.4**  "Strive to appease him, O mighty-armed one, O best of men; you would thereby perform a very great service to your friends, O Janārdana."

**5.122.5**  Then the Vṛṣṇi hero, knowing the truth of all dharma and worldly good, turned and spoke gentle words to the impatient Duryodhana.

**5.122.6**  "Hear this my word, O best of the Kurus; it is especially beneficial to you and to those bound to you, O Bhārata."

**5.122.7**  "O greatly wise one, born of a noble lineage, endowed with learning and right conduct, praised for all your qualities — it is fitting that you do what is good."

**5.122.8**  "Those of bad family, evil-minded, cruel, and shameless — they would act in the way you are now contemplating, dear boy."

**5.122.9**  "In this world, the conduct of the righteous is seen to be joined with dharma and worldly good; the conduct of the wicked is seen to be the reverse, O bull among Bhāratas."

**5.122.10**  "This reverse conduct is again and again observed in you; the unrighteousness bound up with it is dreadful and great — it destroys one's very life."

**5.122.11**  "Because of you, infamy has arisen many times, O Bhārata; by avoiding that harmful course of action you will act for your own welfare."

**5.122.12**  "You will thereby free yourself, your brothers, your servants, and your friends from unrighteous and infamous action, O scorcher of foes."

**5.122.13**  "Make peace with the Pāṇḍavas — wise, brave, highly spirited, self-controlled, and learned men — O tiger among men, O bull among Bhāratas."

**5.122.14**  "That would be beneficial and dear to the wise Dhṛtarāṣṭra, to the grandsire, to Droṇa, and to the great-minded Vidura."

**5.122.15**  "And to Kṛpa, to Somadatta, to the wise Bāhlīka, to Aśvatthāmā, to Vikarṇa, and to Sañjaya, O lord of the people."

**5.122.16**  "And it would bring happiness to the majority of your kinsmen and friends, O scorcher of enemies; in peace there would be welfare for all the world as well, dear boy."

**5.122.17**  "You are modest, born of a noble house, learned and not cruel; abide, dear boy, within the instruction of your father and your mother, O bull among Bhāratas."

**5.122.18**  "Whatever a father commands is held to be the greatest good, O Bhārata; every person in the highest adversity remembers a father's instruction."

**5.122.19**  "Your father together with his ministers desires, dear boy, a meeting with the Pāṇḍavas, O best of Kurus; let that be pleasing to you as well, dear boy."

**5.122.20**  "The mortal who, having heard the counsel of friends, does not follow it — at the end it burns him, like the kiṃpāka fruit when eaten."

**5.122.21**  "He who, out of delusion, does not accept advice that leads to the highest good — he is dilatory, falls short of his aims, and is yoked to remorse."

**5.122.22**  "But he who, having heard advice leading to the highest good, acts upon what is at hand and sets aside his own wilfulness — he prospers in happiness in this world."

**5.122.23**  "He who, out of contrariness, will not tolerate the counsel of one who seeks his benefit, hears only things contrary to his good, and falls into the power of his enemies."

**5.122.24**  "He who, transgressing the view of the virtuous, acts according to the view of the wicked — his friends soon grieve for him in his calamity."

**5.122.25**  "He who abandons his principal ministers and resorts to those of low character, on falling into terrible danger finds no way out."

**5.122.26**  "He who associates with the wicked, who is idle in conduct, who never listens to friends, who chooses strangers and hates his own — the earth curses him, O Bhārata."

**5.122.27**  "Yet you, having set yourself against those heroes, seek protection from the uncultured, the incompetent, and the foolish, O bull among Bhāratas."

**5.122.28**  "What other man on earth, besides you, would pass over kinsmen equal to Indra, great warriors all, and hope for protection from others?"

**5.122.29**  "From birth the sons of Kuntī have been constantly wronged by you, yet they never become angry with you — for the Pāṇḍavas are righteous-souled."

**5.122.30**  "The illustrious Pāṇḍavas have been treated with falsehood since birth, dear boy, yet they have responded to you rightly, O mighty-armed one."

**5.122.31**  "You too should respond in kind, O bull among Bhāratas; do not follow the lead of anger toward your own foremost kinsmen."

**5.122.32**  "The undertakings of the wise are joined with the three ends of life, O bull among Bhāratas; men follow dharma and artha when the three together cannot be achieved."

**5.122.33**  "Among those who pursue them separately: the wise man follows dharma, the middling man follows artha, the quarrelsome man follows kali, and the childish man follows kāma alone."

**5.122.34**  "He who, led by his senses out of greed, abandons dharma, and seeks kāma and artha by improper means — he perishes."

**5.122.35**  "But he who seeks kāma and artha should follow dharma first of all; for neither artha nor kāma ever departs from dharma."

**5.122.36**  "Dharma itself is declared to be the means to all three ends, O lord of the people; for one who seeks them through it grows swiftly, like fire in dry grass."

**5.122.37**  "Yet you, dear boy, are seeking by improper means, O bull among Bhāratas, the great resplendent supremacy renowned among all kings."

**5.122.38**  "He who acts wrongly toward those who act rightly cuts himself down, O king, as an axe cuts a forest."

**5.122.39**  "One should not cut down the mind of one whose defeat one does not desire; the mind of the steadfast whose understanding is unbroken is set upon the good."

**5.122.40**  "One who has abandoned his own self cannot harm even an ordinary creature in the three worlds, O Bhārata — how much less those bulls among the Pāṇḍavas."

**5.122.41**  "A man fallen under the sway of wrath discerns nothing; all extended judgment is cut away — observe this, O Bhārata."

**5.122.42**  "Meeting with the Pāṇḍavas is better for you than keeping bad company, dear boy; finding delight in them, you will obtain all your desires."

**5.122.43**  "You enjoy the earth won by the Pāṇḍavas, O best of kings, yet placing the Pāṇḍavas behind your back, you seek protection elsewhere."

**5.122.44**  "You place your sovereignty in Duḥśāsana, in the unbearable Karṇa, and in the son of Subala, and through them you seek prosperity, O Bhārata."

**5.122.45**  "Yet these are not adequate for you in wisdom, in dharma and artha, nor are they adequate in valour against the Pāṇḍavas, O Bhārata."

**5.122.46**  "Indeed, all these kings together with you are not capable of facing the wrathful Bhīmasena in battle."

**5.122.47**  "All this assembled royal army that stands here, dear boy — Bhīṣma, and Droṇa, and Karṇa, and Kṛpa —"

**5.122.48**  "Bhūriśravas, Saumadatti, Aśvatthāmā, and Jayadratha — all of these are incapable of standing against Dhanañjaya in battle."

**5.122.49**  "Arjuna when enraged is unconquerable by all gods and demons, by humans and by Gandharvas alike — do not set your heart on war."

**5.122.50**  "Let us see if there is any man in all this assembled royal army who, having encountered Arjuna in battle, would return home unharmed."

**5.122.51**  "What good to you is the slaughter of people here, O bull among Bhāratas? Let that one man be pointed out by whose defeat you would be victorious."

**5.122.52**  "He who at Khāṇḍavaprastha conquered the gods, the Gandharvas, the Yakṣas, the Asuras, and the Nāgas together — what mortal would fight him?"

**5.122.53**  "Likewise at the city of Virāṭa a great wonder is heard — of one against many; that example is sufficient proof."

**5.122.54**  "Yet you hope to conquer in battle that unconquerable, unapproachable, victorious, unwavering, mighty hero Arjuna."

**5.122.55**  "Who would dare challenge Pārtha when I am his companion, advancing in battle against them? Not even Indra himself could do so."

**5.122.56**  "He who defeats Arjuna in battle would have to lift the earth with his bare arms, burn these people in his wrath, and hurl the gods from heaven."

**5.122.57**  "Consider your sons, your brothers, your kinsmen and relatives — let them not perish on your account, O best of the Bhāratas."

**5.122.58**  "Let there remain a remnant of the Kauravas; let this lineage not be destroyed; let it not be said of you 'destroyer of the family,' your fame ruined, O lord of men."

**5.122.59**  "The great warriors will establish you in the position of crown prince and your father Dhṛtarāṣṭra, lord of the people, as sovereign king."

**5.122.60**  "Do not disregard the prosperity that is coming toward you, already poised; by giving half to the Pārthas you will obtain great prosperity."

**5.122.61**  "Having made peace with the Pāṇḍavas, having heeded the counsel of your friends, and delighting in your allies, you will long enjoy good fortune."

**5.123.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu, having heard the words of Keśava, spoke to the impatient Duryodhana, O bull among Bhāratas.

**5.123.2**  "You have been addressed in words by Kṛṣṇa, who desires the welfare of his friends; reflect on that, dear boy; do not follow the lead of anger."

**5.123.3**  "Without acting on the word of the great-souled Keśava, dear boy, you will never obtain the highest good, nor happiness, nor well-being."

**5.123.4**  "The mighty-armed Keśava has spoken to you a righteous and beneficial purpose, dear boy; adopt that purpose, O king; do not destroy your people."

**5.123.5**  "Through your wickedness you will cause to fall this blazing Bhārata sovereignty renowned among all kings, while Dhṛtarāṣṭra yet lives."

**5.123.6**  "Through your evil counsel you will cause to fall from life yourself together with your ministers, sons, cattle, kinsmen, and allies."

**5.123.7**  "By transgressing the true and purposeful counsel of Keśava, and that of your father, O best of Bhāratas, and of the wise Vidura —"

**5.123.8**  "— do not go the wrong path as a destroyer of your family, a last man, a wretch; do not give your aged father and mother over to grief."

**5.123.9**  Then Droṇa spoke there these words to Duryodhana, who had fallen under the sway of wrath and was sighing again and again.

**5.123.10**  "Keśava has spoken to you a word joined with dharma and worldly good, dear boy, and Bhīṣma son of Śaṃtanu likewise; accept it, O lord of men."

**5.123.11**  "Two wise men, intelligent, self-controlled, caring for your welfare, and learned, have spoken to you a beneficial word; accept it, O scorcher of foes."

**5.123.12**  "Follow what Kṛṣṇa and Bhīṣma have said, O greatly wise one; do not follow the words of the shallow-minded, O scorcher of foes."

**5.123.13**  "Those who are inciting you — they are never of any use to you; in battle they will fasten the enemy's enmity around their own necks."

**5.123.14**  "Do not slay all the Kurus — their sons and brothers alike; where Vāsudeva and Arjuna stand, know that their force is unconquerable."

**5.123.15**  "This indeed is the true view of your two well-wishers, Kṛṣṇa and Bhīṣma; if you do not accept it, dear boy, you will repent hereafter, O Bhārata."

**5.123.16**  As was declared by Jamadagnya, Arjuna is even greater than that; for Kṛṣṇa, the son of Devakī, is one impossible to withstand even for the gods.

**5.123.17**  What use is there in saying to you what is pleasing and comfortable, O bull of the Bharatas? All of this has been told to you; do as you wish. I do not dare to say more to you, O best of the Bharatas.

**5.123.18**  While that speech was still unfinished, Vidura the Kṣattā also spoke a word, looking toward Duryodhana, the wrathful son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra.

**5.123.19**  O Duryodhana, I do not grieve for you, O bull of the Bharatas; but I grieve for these two elders — Gāndhārī and your father.

**5.123.20**  Those two shall wander without a protector, with you as their protector but hard-hearted, their friends slain, their ministers slain, like two birds with clipped wings.

**5.123.21**  They shall roam this earth as beggars, lamenting, having given birth to such a sinful destroyer of the family line, a base man like you.

**5.123.22**  Then King Dhṛtarāṣṭra addressed Duryodhana, who was seated surrounded by his brothers and encircled by kings.

**5.123.23**  O Duryodhana, understand what has been said by the great-souled Śauri; accept thoroughly what is good, as something that brings security and welfare, and is imperishable.

**5.123.24**  For with this ally Kṛṣṇa, whose deeds are untiring, we shall attain all our desired aims among all the kings.

**5.123.25**  Well united with Keśava, go, my son, to Yudhiṣṭhira; perform entirely all the auspicious rites for the welfare of the Bharatas.

**5.123.26**  My son, go to the meeting with Vāsudeva as your guide; I consider this to be the moment appointed by time — do not transgress this, O Duryodhana.

**5.123.27**  If you reject Keśava who is asking for peace and speaking earnestly on your behalf, there is no humiliation that you will not suffer.

**5.124.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having heard the words of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, Bhīṣma and Droṇa, endorsing them, spoke this word to Duryodhana, who had transgressed their guidance.

**5.124.2**  As long as the two Kṛṣṇas are not fully armed, as long as the Gāṇḍīva bow stands ready, as long as Dhaumya has not offered the enemy's forces as oblation into the battle-fire —

**5.124.3**  — as long as Yudhiṣṭhira, the great archer restrained by decorum, does not look in fury upon your army — until then let the carnage be appeased.

**5.124.4**  As long as Bhīmasena, the great archer, son of Pṛthā, is not seen stationed in his own battle arrays — until then let the carnage be appeased.

**5.124.5**  As long as he does not range the battle lines, rousing the army to joy, as long as he does not cut off in battle the heads of the fighters on elephants —

**5.124.6**  — with his mace, the slayer of heroes, striking them down like ripe fruits of a tree in season — until then let the carnage be appeased.

**5.124.7**  Nakula and Sahadeva, and Dhṛṣṭadyumna the Pārṣata, and Virāṭa, and Śikhaṇḍī, and the son of Śiśupāla — all clad in armor —

**5.124.8**  — as long as these, trained in weapons and shooting swiftly, do not plunge in like crocodiles into a great ocean — until then let the carnage be appeased.

**5.124.9**  As long as the terrible vulture-feathered arrows do not fall upon the tender bodies of kings — until then let the carnage be appeased.

**5.124.10**  Upon the chests of warriors anointed with sandalwood and aloe paste and adorned with garlands and golden ornaments — as long as the great arrows of great archers —

**5.124.11**  — the iron shafts shot swiftly by those trained in weapons, far-flying and aimed true, are not driven down — until then let the carnage be appeased.

**5.124.12**  Let Yudhiṣṭhira the Dharma-king, that elephant among kings, receive you with both hands as you bow your head in obeisance.

**5.124.13**  Let the dexterous one place his right arm, marked with the signs of flag and goad and banner, upon your shoulder — for peace, O bull of the Bharatas.

**5.124.14**  Let him stroke your back with a hand bearing jeweled finger-rings and adorned with gems and medicinal substances, as you sit before him.

**5.124.15**  Let Vṛkodara, your kinsman, with shoulders like a sala tree and mighty arms, also address you with conciliatory words — for peace, O bull of the Bharatas.

**5.124.16**  Having been saluted by Arjuna and the twin sons of Mādrī, those three, smell their heads in affection and greet them with love, O king.

**5.124.17**  Let the lords of men shed tears born of joy upon seeing you reunited with those heroic Pāṇḍava brothers.

**5.124.18**  Let all the prosperity of the kings be proclaimed in the royal capitals; let the earth be enjoyed in brotherly accord — be free of fever.

**5.125.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Having heard the unpleasant speech in the Kuru assembly, Duryodhana replied to the glorious and mighty-armed Vāsudeva.

**5.125.2**  You ought to say this after proper consideration, O Keśava; for you single me out in particular for detailed censure.

**5.125.3**  Out of devotion to the Pārthas, without cause, O Madhusūdana, you constantly reproach me — what balance of strength do you consider?

**5.125.4**  You, the Kṣattā Vidura, the King Dhṛtarāṣṭra, the preceptor Droṇa, or the grandsire Bhīṣma — all of them censure only me, and no other king.

**5.125.5**  And I perceive no transgression on my part in any respect; yet all of you together with the kings hate me.

**5.125.6**  And even reflecting carefully, I do not perceive any offense at all, however minute, O subduer of enemies, O Keśava.

**5.125.7**  The Pāṇḍavas came willingly to the dice game, O Madhusūdana, and were defeated by Śakuni in their kingdom — what evil deed is mine in that?
