**5.154.17**  Escorted by the chief Vṛṣṇis who came like mighty tigers, the great-armed one was protected as Vāsava is by the Maruts.

**5.154.18**  Clad in dark silk garments, resembling a peak of mount Kailāsa, moving with the leisurely gait of a lion, glorious, with eyes reddened at the corners by wine—

**5.154.19**  seeing him, the king of dharma and the greatly radiant Keśava rose, and so did the son of Pṛthā the terrible-actioned Vṛkodara.

**5.154.20**  The Gāṇḍīva-bowman and whatever other kings were there—all of them approached and honored Halayudha.

**5.154.21**  Then the Pāṇḍava king touched his hand with his own hand, and all the others with Vāsudeva at their head saluted him.

**5.154.22**  Halayudha, the subduer of foes, paid homage to the elders Virāṭa and Drupada, and then sat down together with Yudhiṣṭhira.

**5.154.23**  Then, when the kings had sat down on all sides, Rauhineya gazed upon Vāsudeva and spoke:

**5.154.24**  "There will be a great and terrible slaughter of men—this I regard as fated and certain; it cannot be averted.

**5.154.25**  My wish is that I might see you, along with your dear ones, emerge safe from this battle, healthy and with bodies unharmed.

**5.154.26**  The assembled royal Kṣatriya clan is ripened by Time without doubt; a great crushing is coming, a mire of flesh and blood.

**5.154.27**  Again and again in private I urged Vāsudeva: 'Madhusūdana, maintain equal conduct toward your kinsmen on both sides.'

**5.154.28**  "'The Pāṇḍavas are as much ours as is king Duryodhana; show him too the proper honor'—this I urged again and again.

**5.154.29**  But Madhusūdana did not heed that counsel of mine; he was wholly committed with all his being, with eyes fixed on Dhanañjaya.

**5.154.30**  Victory of the Pāṇḍavas is certain—this is my firm conviction; for such indeed is the absorption of Vāsudeva, O Bharata.

**5.154.31**  And I cannot bear to witness the world without Kṛṣṇa; therefore I shall follow what Keśava intends to do.

**5.154.32**  For both are my heroic pupils, skilled in mace-fighting; my affection is equal for Bhīma and for king Duryodhana.

**5.154.33**  Therefore I shall go to visit the sacred fords along the Sarasvatī; I cannot bear to stand by as the Kauravas perish."

**5.154.34**  Having spoken thus, the mighty-armed Rāma, with the permission of the Pāṇḍavas, departed on his pilgrimage to the sacred fords, taking leave of Madhusūdana.

**5.155.1**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: At that very time, the son of the great-souled Bhīṣmaka, king of the golden-fleeced, who was himself a friend of Indra—

**5.155.2**  the son of that lord of abundance, the exceedingly glorious Bhoja, lord of the southern regions—known in all directions as Rukmi—

**5.155.3**  he who was the pupil of the lion among Kimpurushas who dwells on Gandhamādana, and had mastered the complete four-footed science of archery,

**5.155.4**  the mighty-armed one who obtained the bow Mahendra, equal in splendor to the Gāṇḍīva, and the divine inexhaustible Śārṅga which is on a par with it—

**5.155.5**  these three are the only divine bows that traverse the heavens: the Varuṇa bow called Gāṇḍīva, the Mahendra called Vijaya,

**5.155.6**  and Śārṅga, they say, the Vaiṣṇava bow, divine and made of pure radiance—which Kṛṣṇa bore, striking terror into enemy armies.

**5.155.7**  The son of Pāka's slayer received the Gāṇḍīva from the fire in the Khandava forest; the greatly splendid Rukmi obtained Vijaya from a tree.

**5.155.8**  Having severed the bonds of Mura, having slain Mura by force, having conquered Naraka the earth-born, and having recovered the jeweled earrings,

**5.155.9**  Hrishikesha recovered sixteen thousand women and various jewels and the supreme bow Śārṅga.

**5.155.10**  But Rukmi, having obtained the bow Vijaya whose sound was like a thundercloud, came to the Pāṇḍavas as though threatening the whole world.

**5.155.11**  This hero, proud of the strength of his own arms, had in the past not forgiven the abduction of Rukmiṇī by the wise Vāsudeva.

**5.155.12**  Having made the vow "I shall not return without slaying Keśava," he then pursued the Vṛṣṇi hero, foremost among all weapon-bearers.

**5.155.13**  With a great four-fold army, far-reaching, equipped with varied weapons and armor, swollen like the flooding Gaṅgā,

**5.155.14**  he encountered the Vṛṣṇi hero, lord and master of yogas, was utterly defeated and humiliated, O king, and returned to Kundina.

**5.155.15**  Right there where he had been defeated in battle by Kṛṣṇa, the slayer of enemy heroes, he built a fine city named Bhojakata.

**5.155.16**  With that great army rich in elephants and horses, that city became renowned on earth by the name Bhojakata, O king.

**5.155.17**  That Bhoja king, of great valor, surrounded by a great army comprising a full akshauhini division, came to the Pāṇḍavas.

**5.155.18**  Then, clad in armor, bearing sword and arrows and bow and palm-shield, riding his chariot, with a banner the color of the sun, he entered the great host.

**5.155.19**  Known to the Pāṇḍavas, and desiring to please Vāsudeva, king Yudhiṣṭhira went out to meet him and honored him.

**5.155.20**  Duly honored and well-received by the sons of Pāṇḍu, he honored them all in return; then, rested, along with his soldiers, he spoke in the midst of the heroes to Dhanañjaya, son of Kuntī:

**5.155.21**  "I stand ready as your ally in battle. If you are afraid, O Pāṇḍava, I shall render you in combat an assistance that your enemies cannot withstand."

**5.155.22**  "For there is no man in this world equal to me in valor; slaying your enemies in battle, I shall give them to you, O Phalguna."

**5.155.23**  Addressed thus, in the presence of the king of dharma and Keśava, and with all the lords of the earth and everyone else listening,

**5.155.24**  the wise son of Kuntī glanced at Vāsudeva and at the Pāṇḍava king of dharma, and spoke laughing in a tone of friendly warmth:

**5.155.25**  "Hero, when I was fighting the exceedingly mighty Gandharvas during the cattle-raid expedition, who was my ally, who was my friend then?

**5.155.26**  Likewise in that terrifying encounter in the Khandava forest, filled with gods and demons—who was my ally when I fought then?

**5.155.27**  In battle against the Nivātakavacas and the Kalakeya demons—who was my ally when I fought there?

**5.155.28**  Likewise in Virāṭa's city, when I fought many Kurus in battle—who was my ally then, dear friend?

**5.155.29**  Taking shelter in battle upon Rudra, Śakra, Vaiśravaṇa, Yama, Varuṇa, Pāvaka, Kṛpa, Droṇa, and Mādhava—

**5.155.30**  bearing the divine Gāṇḍīva bow, made of pure radiance and firm, furnished with inexhaustible arrows, and augmented by celestial weapons—

**5.155.31**  born in the lineage of the Kauravas, and above all as the son of Pāṇḍu, pointing to Droṇa as my teacher, I am the pupil who has Vāsudeva as ally.

**5.155.32**  How could one such as I speak the inglorious words 'I am afraid'—even were the thunderbolt-wielding Indra himself my enemy, O tiger among men?

**5.155.33**  I am not afraid, O mighty-armed one, nor do I have any need of an ally. Go wherever you wish, however you see fit, or remain elsewhere."

**5.155.34**  Then Rukmi turned back with his ocean-like army and went to Duryodhana in the same manner, O bull of the Bharatas.

**5.155.35**  And having approached him likewise, he said the same things to that king, and was equally rejected by him who thought himself a hero.

**5.155.36**  Two thus withdrew from that war, O great king: Rauhineya the Vṛṣṇi hero, and Rukmi the lord of the earth.

**5.155.37**  With Rāma departed on his pilgrimage to sacred fords, and the son of Bhīṣmaka likewise withdrawn, the Pāṇḍavas sat down once again to take counsel.

**5.155.38**  That assembly of the king of dharma, crowded with lords of the earth, shone resplendent — like the sky bright with stars and the moon, O Bharata.

**5.156.1**  Janamejaya said: When the armies had thus been arrayed on the field of Kurukṣetra, O bull among twice-born, what did the Kurus do, impelled by time?

**5.156.2**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: When those armies had thus been arrayed, O bull of the Bharatas, the great king Dhṛtarāṣṭra spoke these words to Sañjaya.

**5.156.3**  "Come, Sañjaya — tell me everything without remainder: whatever has occurred at the camp among the armies of the Kurus and Pāṇḍavas."

**5.156.4**  "I consider destiny supreme and human effort fruitless — for even though I know the evils of war and its ruinous consequences,"

**5.156.5**  "Yet I cannot restrain my son, whose judgment is warped and who plays at wicked dice, nor can I act for my own welfare."

**5.156.6**  "Indeed, O Sūta, a discernment of faults arises in me, but when it encounters Duryodhana, it turns back again."

**5.156.7**  "Things being so, O Sañjaya, whatever is destined shall come to pass. The duty of a warrior, it is said, is revered as the laying down of the body in battle."

**5.156.8**  Sañjaya said: "This inquiry of yours is appropriate, O great king, as befits you. Yet you ought not to fix this blame on Duryodhana alone. Hear me fully as I speak, O lord of the earth."

**5.156.9**  "A man who incurs misfortune through his own misconduct ought not to lay that sin upon destiny or upon time."

**5.156.10**  "O great king, a man who practices everything blameworthy among men, who commits what all the world condemns, deserves destruction by all the world."

**5.156.11**  "O foremost of men, the Pāṇḍavas endured insults, waiting on you, deceived with their ministers through the crooked dice-play."

**5.156.12**  "Hear from me in full what slaughter has occurred in battle — of horses, of elephants, and of kings of immeasurable splendor."

**5.156.13**  "Be steadfast, O great king, and having heard of the rise and fall of all the world as it truly occurred in this great war, do not become despondent."

**5.156.14**  "For a person is not truly the author of good or evil actions; a person is not free — he is driven like a wooden puppet."

**5.156.15**  "Some are directed by the Lord, some by chance, and others by the actions of former lives — this threefold force pulls men apart."

**5.157.1**  Sañjaya said: When the great-souled Pāṇḍavas had encamped on the Hiraṇvatī, O great king, Duryodhana — together with Karṇa, O Bharata —

**5.157.2**  — and with Śakuni the son of Subala, O king of kings, and with Duḥśāsana — having summoned Ulūka aside, O king, spoke thus:

**5.157.3**  "Go, Ulūka, son of Śakuni — go to the Pāṇḍavas together with the Somakas, and speak my words in the hearing of Vāsudeva."

**5.157.4**  "This war, terrible to all the world, which has been pondered over many years by the Pāṇḍavas and the Kurus alike, has now arrived."

**5.157.5**  "That great boast which Sañjaya spoke in the midst of the Kurus, O son of Kuntī — the time for it has come. Let everything be done as you have pledged."

**5.157.6**  "Remember the wrath, O Pāṇḍava — the seizure of the kingdom, the forest exile, and the humiliation of Draupadī — and be a man!"

**5.157.7**  "That for which a warrior woman bears a son in her womb has now arrived. Showing your strength, valor, heroism, and supreme skill with weapons in battle, make expiation for your wrath."

**5.157.8**  "Whose heart would not burst — one who has been tormented, impoverished, who has lived long in hardship, and who has been stripped of sovereignty?"

**5.157.9**  "Of a hero born in a noble house, whose kingdom was seized by one greedy for another's wealth — whose anger would not be kindled?"

**5.157.10**  "Let that great speech which was spoken be now demonstrated by deeds; the virtuous know that one who boasts but does not act is a base person."

**5.157.11**  "Two outcomes attend a warrior in battle: to remain under the power of enemies, or to recover the kingdom. Therefore, show your manhood!"

**5.157.12**  "Either defeat us and rule this earth, or be slain by us and go to the world of heroes."

**5.157.13**  "Remember the banishment from the kingdom, the suffering, the forest exile, O Pāṇḍava, and the humiliation of Kṛṣṇā — and be a man!"

**5.157.14**  "As unpleasant words were spoken and you went into exile again and again — show your indignation now, for indignation itself is manhood."

**5.157.15**  "Let your wrath, strength, valor, mastery of knowledge, and lightness of hand with weapons be seen here in the battle, O Pārtha — be a man!"

**5.157.16**  "And to that glutton Bhīmasena, O Ulūka — that dullard, that ignorant braggart — repeat my words more than once:"

**5.157.17**  "'What was sworn in the assembly hall by one who was powerless, O wolf-belly — let the blood of Duḥśāsana be drunk, if it can be done!'"

**5.157.18**  "The supply of iron is ready, Kurukṣetra is free of mud, your horses are well-fed, your warriors are paid — tomorrow fight together with Keśava!"

**5.158.1**  Sañjaya said: Having reached the camp of the Pāṇḍavas, Ulūka, son of Śakuni, was brought before Yudhiṣṭhira by the Pāṇḍava princes and addressed him.

**5.158.2**  "Knowing the protocols of envoys and the words I speak as instructed, you should not grow angry upon hearing Duryodhana's commission."

**5.158.3**  Yudhiṣṭhira said: "Ulūka, you have no cause for fear — speak without anxiety what is the view of the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, that greedy man of short-sighted vision."

**5.158.4**  Sañjaya said: Then, in the midst of the brilliant great-souled Pāṇḍavas, and of all the Sṛñjayas, and of the illustrious Kṛṣṇa —

**5.158.5**  — in the presence of Drupada with his sons, and of Virāṭa, and in the midst of all those lords of the earth, Ulūka spoke the following words:

**5.158.6**  "Thus spoke the great-minded king, son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, while the Kuru heroes listened. Understand this, O lord of men:"

**5.158.7**  "'You were defeated at dice, and Kṛṣṇā was dragged into the assembly hall. A man who considers himself a man ought to be able to feel indignation.'"

**5.158.8**  "'You were exiled from your home for twelve full years in the forest, and lived as servants in Virāṭa's court for one year.'"

**5.158.9**  "'Remember the wrath, the seizure of the kingdom, the forest exile, O Pāṇḍava, and the humiliation of Draupadī — and be a man!'"

**5.158.10**  "'What was sworn by the powerless Bhīmasena, O Pāṇḍava — let the blood of Duḥśāsana be drunk, if it can be done!'"

**5.158.11**  "'The supply of iron is ready, Kurukṣetra is free of mud, the road is even, the warriors are paid — tomorrow fight together with Keśava!'"

**5.158.12**  "'Why do you boast without having encountered Bhīṣma in battle — like a fool wishing to climb Mount Gandhamādana?'"

**5.158.13**  "'Without defeating Droṇa, the foremost of warriors, equal in battle to the lord of Śacī, how do you desire the kingdom here, O Pārtha?'"

**5.158.14**  "'The teacher who is supreme in the brahma-bow, who knows the distinction between the two Vedas, who bears the army unshaken and steadfast in battle, the unfailing one —'"

**5.158.15**  "'That you wish in delusion to conquer Droṇa in battle, O Pārtha — that is false. For we have never heard of Mount Meru being shaken by the wind.'"

**5.158.16**  "'The wind might carry off Mount Meru, or the sky might fall upon the earth, or the age might be reversed — if what you say about me were true.'"

**5.158.17**  "'Who, wishing to live, having once received the weapons of those two foe-crushers — be it elephant, horse, or man — would return safely home again?'"

**5.158.18**  "'How could one who is targeted by those two and who has come into contact with their terrible weapons escape alive from battle, even touching the earth with his feet?'"

**5.158.19**  "'Are you like a frog dwelling in a well, that you do not perceive this royal army assembled — invincible, brilliant as the army of the gods, protected by lords of men like the sky by the thirty gods?'"

**5.158.20**  "'— With the easterners, the westerners, the southerners, the northerners, the Kāmbojas, the Śakas, the Khashas, the Śālvas, the Matsyas, the people of the Kuru midlands, the Mlecchas, the Pulindas, the Dravidas, the Andhras, and the Kanchis —'"

**5.158.21**  "'— the vast flood of diverse peoples swelling in battle, unstoppable as the current of the Gaṅgā — and me standing in the midst of a force strong as elephants — do you wish to fight me, O dullard of little intelligence?'"

**5.158.22**  Having spoken thus to king Yudhiṣṭhira, the son of Dharma, Ulūka turned and again addressed Arjuna, the victorious one:

**5.158.23**  "Fight without boasting — why do you boast so much, Arjuna? Success in this comes in due course; it is not achieved by boasting."

**5.158.24**  "If deeds in the world were accomplished by boasting, O Dhanañjaya, then every person in want could boast greatly and have all their aims fulfilled."

**5.158.25**  "I know you have Vāsudeva as your ally; I know your bow Gāṇḍīva is as tall as a palm tree; I know all this — that there is no warrior like you — and yet knowing this, I take your kingdom."

**5.158.26**  "Yet one does not attain great success merely by the law of due course; for the Creator brings beings under his control through the mind alone."

**5.158.27**  "For thirteen years I enjoyed the kingdom while you lamented; I shall rule it again after slaying you along with your kinsmen."

**5.158.28**  "Where was your Gāṇḍīva then when you were staked and lost as a slave? Where was the strength of Bhīmasena then, O Phalguna?"

**5.158.29**  "From Bhīmasena with his mace and from Pārtha with his Gāṇḍīva — there was no deliverance for you at that time without the blameless Kṛṣṇā."

**5.158.30**  "That noble lady rescued you when you had fallen into slavery, you who were caught in inhuman conditions and reduced to servitude."

**5.158.31**  "What I called you — sesame seeds without kernel — that was the very truth; for Pārtha kept his braided hair tied up in the city of Virāṭa at that time."

**5.158.32**  "And Bhīmasena, O son of Kuntī, exhausted in the work of a cook in Virāṭa's great kitchen — that too speaks to my manhood."

**5.158.33**  "In this very way have warriors always punished a warrior — in rank, in girdle, and in braid — who flees in battle."

**5.158.34**  "Not out of fear of Vāsudeva, nor of you, O Phalguna, will I surrender the kingdom — fight together with Keśava!"

**5.158.35**  "Illusions, conjuror's tricks, sorcery, or terrifying spectacles — these counter-roarings carry no weight against me when I stand weapons in hand in battle."

**5.158.36**  "A thousand Vāsudevas or hundreds of Phalgunas — upon encountering me, whose arrows never miss, they will flee in all ten directions."

**5.158.37**  "Go meet Bhīṣma in battle — smash a mountain with your head! — cross this great unfathomable ocean of warriors with your own two arms!"

**5.158.38**  "— whose heights are Sharadwata, teeming with Viviṃśati as its fish, whose swells are Bṛhadbala, whose sea-monsters are Saumadatti —"

**5.158.39**  "— whose floods are Duḥśāsana, whose fish are Śala and Śalya, whose crocodiles are Suṣeṇa and Citrāyudha, whose mountain is Jayadratha, whose depths are Purumitra, whose eddies are Durmarṣaṇa, whose cataracts are Śakuni —"

**5.158.40**  "— the inexhaustible, ever-swelling torrent of weapons — when you plunge into it and emerge with mind exhausted and all kinsmen slain, then your mind will know torment."

**5.158.41**  "Then, O Pārtha, may your mind turn back from ruling the earth, as the impure are turned back from the third heaven — for to govern a kingdom is as impossible for you as reaching heaven is for one who has not practiced austerities."

**5.159.1**  Sañjaya said: Ulūka again spoke to Arjuna the words as he had been told, stinging him with verbal darts like one goading an angry venomous serpent.

**5.159.2**  Hearing those words, the Pāṇḍavas were greatly enraged — already deeply angered as they had been, now further provoked by Ulūka, son of Śakuni.

**5.159.3**  They could not remain seated; they flung their arms out and looked at one another like enraged venomous serpents.

**5.159.4**  Bhīmasena, with head bent down, looked up at Keśava with eyes reddened at the corners, hissing like a venomous serpent.

**5.159.5**  Seeing the son of the Wind-god distressed and deeply stricken by wrath, the descendant of Dāśārha, smiling slightly, replied to Ulūka, son of Śakuni.

**5.159.6**  Go quickly, O son of a gambler, and say to Suyodhana: your message has been heard, your purpose understood; let it be as you wish.

**5.159.7**  And tell Suyodhana this further word of mine: tomorrow you shall see what a man looks like — be a man if you can, O foolish-minded one.

**5.159.8**  You think, O fool, that Janārdana will not fight because he has been engaged by the Pārthas as a charioteer — and so you do not fear.

**5.159.9**  Even at the last moment it could happen that I, in my wrath, would burn all the kings as fire burns grass.

**5.159.10**  But at the command of Yudhiṣṭhira and of the great-souled Phalguna, I shall serve as charioteer to him who fights and who knows the self.

**5.159.11**  Whether you soar through the three worlds or burrow into the earth, at dawn you shall see Arjuna's chariot right before you, wherever you may be.

**5.159.12**  And as for what you regard as Bhīmasena's empty roar — let it be firmly fixed in your mind that Duḥśāsana's blood shall be drunk.

**5.159.13**  Pārtha does not even deign to look at you, nor does King Yudhiṣṭhira, nor Bhīmasena, nor the twins — you who speak so contrarily.

**5.160.1**  Sañjaya said: Upon hearing those words of Duryodhana, the bull of the Bharatas gazed at the son of the gambler with eyes that had turned deep crimson.

**5.160.2**  He, the great-glorious Guḍākeśa, glancing toward Keśava, stretched out his mighty arm and addressed the son of the gambler.

**5.160.3**  One who, relying on his own valor, challenges his foes fearlessly and to the full measure of his power — he is truly called a man.

**5.160.4**  But one who, relying on others' valor, challenges his foes — that mere kinsman of a kṣatriya, powerless on his own, is the lowest of men in the world.

**5.160.5**  You are such a one — you count others' valor as your own, a coward and a fool yourself, yet you wish to revile those greater than you.

**5.160.6**  You who have consecrated that elder — the wise counselor of all kings, master of his senses, of great intellect — to death, and then boast about it.

**5.160.7**  Your intention is known to us, O evil-minded defiler of your lineage — that the Pāṇḍavas will not kill Gāṅgeya out of compassion.

**5.160.8**  The one whose valor you lean on while you boast, O son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra — I shall slay Bhīṣma first, in the sight of all archers.

**5.160.9**  Go back, O son of the gambler, join the Bharatas and tell Suyodhana, son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, what Arjuna Savyasācī has said: at the end of this night the clash shall begin.

**5.160.10**  What he, of undaunted spirit and true to his vows, declared in the midst of the Kurus to their joy — that he would slay the armies of the Pāṇḍavas and the sons of Śalva — that burden now falls to me.

**5.160.11**  Were I to slay even Droṇa, you would not fear the Pāṇḍavas at all — so runs his thought: that your kingdom would then be securely won, and the Pāṇḍavas reduced to ruin.

**5.160.12**  But you, filled with arrogance, do not perceive the calamity that is unfolding upon yourself — therefore I shall be the one to slay, before your eyes in the midst of battle, that elder of the Kurus first.

**5.160.13**  At sunrise, having marshaled my forces and waited — with banner and chariot — guard the true-vowed one; for I shall, before your eyes, bring Bhīṣma down from his chariot with my arrows.

**5.160.14**  Tomorrow, when day breaks, Suyodhana shall learn the worth of all this boasting when he sees his grandfather tormented by the net of my arrows.

**5.160.15**  And as for what was said in the assembly hall by that short-sighted man — your brother Duḥśāsana — to the wrathful Bhīmasena,

**5.160.16**  O Suyodhana, that ignorant of dharma, that perpetual enemy, that evil-minded doer of cruelty — that oath taken truly shall be fulfilled ere long; of that I assure you.

**5.160.17**  For pride, arrogance, and wrathful harshness, for cruelty, insolence, and self-conceit —

**5.160.18**  — for ruthlessness and sharpness of tongue, for hatred of dharma, for transgression into unrighteousness, for speaking too harshly, and for disrespecting elders —

**5.160.19**  — and for your crooked ways and all your wrongdoing, you shall soon see the bitter fruit, O Suyodhana.

**5.160.20**  With me enraged and Vāsudeva beside me, O lord of men, on what grounds do you, fool, hold any hope of life or kingdom?

**5.160.21**  When Bhīṣma is laid low, and Droṇa likewise, and the son of the charioteer is slain, you shall be left without hope of life, kingdom, or sons.

**5.160.22**  Seeing your brothers slain and your sons too, O Suyodhana, when Bhīmasena strikes you down, you shall remember all your misdeeds.

**5.160.23**  Keśava will not make a second vow; I speak the truth to you — all of this shall indeed come to pass as truly said.

**5.160.24**  Thus addressed, O king, the son of the gambler took those words to heart, and, having received leave to depart, returned the same way he had come.

**5.160.25**  Turning back from the Pāṇḍavas, the son of the gambler went to the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra and reported everything exactly as it had been said, in the assembly of the Kurus.

**5.160.26**  Having heard the words of Keśava and Arjuna, that bull of the Bharatas addressed Duḥśāsana, Karṇa, and Śakuni.

**5.160.27**  He commanded the kings and their forces and the allied contingents that the entire army should stand ready and equipped before sunrise.

**5.160.28**  Then messengers dispatched by Karṇa went out in great haste — some in chariots, others on camels and mules, others on fine swift horses.

**5.160.29**  Under Karṇa's orders they swiftly circled the entire army, commanding all the kings: be in battle-readiness before sunrise.

**5.161.1**  Sañjaya said: Having heard Ulūka's words, Yudhiṣṭhira, son of Kuntī, set his army in motion with Dhṛṣṭadyumna at its head.

**5.161.2**  Comprising infantry, elephants, chariots, and masses of cavalry — a fourfold force, terrible and immovable as the earth itself.

**5.161.3**  Protected by Bhīmasena and the other great chariot-warriors including Arjuna, that formidable army under Dhṛṣṭadyumna's control was steady as the ocean's depth.

**5.161.4**  At the head of that army marched the great archer Dhṛṣṭadyumna of Pāñcāla, maddened for battle, eager to seize Droṇa, leading the divisions forward.

**5.161.5**  According to the strength and keenness of each, he assigned the chariot-warriors: Arjuna against the son of the charioteer, Bhīma against Duryodhana.

**5.161.6**  Nakula he assigned against Aśvatthāman, Śaibya against Kṛtavarman, and Yuyudhāna of the Vṛṣṇis against the king of Sindhu.

**5.161.7**  Śikhaṇḍin he appointed in the vanguard against Bhīṣma; Sahadeva against Śakuni; and Cekitāna against Śala.

**5.161.8**  Dhṛṣṭaketu he set against Śalya; Uttamaujā against Gautama; and the five sons of Draupadī he assigned against the five Trigarta chiefs.

**5.161.9**  Saubhadra he set against Vṛṣasena, and appointed other kings against the remaining foes — for Abhimanyu was deemed more capable in battle than even Arjuna himself.

**5.161.10**  Having distributed those warriors both individually and collectively in this way, that great archer of flame-like complexion assigned to himself the share that was Droṇa.

**5.161.11**  Then Dhṛṣṭadyumna, that great archer, supreme commander of the army, wise and resolute for battle, arrayed the formation in proper order.

**5.161.12**  He deployed the divisions of the Pāṇḍavas as they had been directed, and stood alert in the field of battle for the victory of the sons of Pāṇḍu.

**5.162.1**  Dhṛtarāṣṭra said: When Phalguna had vowed to slay Bhīṣma, O Sañjaya, what did my dull-witted sons — Duryodhana and the rest — do?

**5.162.2**  I see the son of Gaṅgā, that father-like elder, as already slain in battle by Pārtha, firm of bow, with Vāsudeva as his ally.

**5.162.3**  And that man of boundless wisdom, Bhīṣma, best of those who strike — what did he say upon hearing Pārtha's declaration?

**5.162.4**  And having assumed the generalship of the Kaurava forces, how did that bearer of the yoke, Gāṅgeya of great intellect and prowess, conduct himself?

**5.162.5**  Vaiśaṃpāyana said: Then Sañjaya reported all of that to him exactly as it had been spoken by Bhīṣma, the elder of the Kurus, of immeasurable splendor.

**5.162.6**  Sañjaya said: Having received the generalship, Bhīṣma son of Śantanu addressed Duryodhana with these words, as if to gladden him, O king.

**5.162.7**  Having saluted the young prince, the spear-bearing commander of the vanguard: I shall without doubt be your general today.

**5.162.8**  I am versed in the duties of commanding an army, in the various battle-formations, and in directing the work both of those in service and those not in service.

**5.162.9**  In marches and movements, in battle, and in consolidating gains — I know these deeply, O great king, as Bṛhaspati himself knows them.

**5.162.10**  Even the great formations of divine, Gandharva, and human type — with those I shall bewilder the Pāṇḍavas; let your fever of anxiety depart.

**5.162.11**  I shall fight with full commitment, protecting your army in accordance with the teachings of the treatises, O king; let your mental anguish pass.

**5.162.12**  Duryodhana said: I have no fear, O Gāṅgeya, O mighty-armed one, even from all the gods and demons together — this I tell you truly.

**5.162.13**  How much less when you, the irresistible, are installed as commander, and Droṇa, the tiger among men, who delights in battle, stands ready beside you.

**5.162.14**  With both of you, foremost of men, standing firm, my victory is not difficult, O best of Kurus — even the sovereignty of the gods would be certain.

**5.162.15**  I wish to hear a full accounting of the chariot-warriors — of the enemy's side and of ours, and likewise of the great chariot-warriors, O Kaurava.

**5.162.16**  For the grandsire is expert in the reckoning of enemy and allied forces alike; I wish to hear it together with all these lords of the earth.

**5.162.17**  Bhīṣma said: O lord of Gāndhāra, O king of kings, hear the count of the chariot-warriors in your own force — those who are chariot-warriors and those who are great chariot-warriors, O guardian of the earth.

**5.162.18**  Many thousands, ten-thousands, and ten-millions of chariots are in your army — but hear from me the account of the principal ones.

**5.162.19**  You yourself are foremost, a noble chariot-warrior, together with all your brothers — the hundred brothers beginning with Duḥśāsana.

**5.162.20**  All of them are trained in weapons, skilled in cutting and piercing, expert on chariot-platform and elephant-back, in mace-fighting, and in sword-and-shield.

**5.162.21**  They are controllers and attackers, trained in weapons and able to bear their loads; in archery they are disciples of Droṇa and of Kṛpa son of Śaradvat.

**5.162.22**  These high-spirited sons of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, their offenses done by the sons of Pāṇḍu weighing upon them, shall slay in battle the battle-maddened Pāñcālas.

**5.162.23**  Then I, O best of Bharatas, your supreme commander, shall destroy your enemies, reducing the Pāṇḍavas to naught — though it is not for me to extol my own qualities; you know me well.

**5.162.24**  Kṛtavarman of the Bhojas, a great chariot-warrior, the foremost of those who strike — he shall without doubt accomplish your objectives in battle.

**5.162.25**  Irresistible to those who know weapons, a far-shooter, firm of arms — he shall slay your enemies as great Indra slays the demons.

**5.162.26**  Śalya, the great archer, king of the Madras — I count him a great chariot-warrior; he is one who rivals Vāsudeva himself in battle after battle.

**5.162.27**  Forsaking his own nephews, Śalya that best of chariot-warriors shall fight on your side in battle against Kṛṣṇa, the bearer of discus and mace.

**5.162.28**  With onslaughts like the waves of the ocean, inundating his enemies as it were — Bhūriśravas, skilled in weapons, is also your devoted friend and ally.

**5.162.29**  Saumadatti, the great archer, commander of commanders of chariot divisions, shall bring about a very great diminishing of the strength of your foes.

**5.162.30**  The king of Sindhu, O great king, I count as double the worth of a chariot-warrior — he shall fight in battle, O king, with valor, best of chariot-warriors that he is.

**5.162.31**  Having been humiliated in the past during the abduction of Draupadī by the Pāṇḍavas, he, the slayer of enemy heroes, shall fight remembering that humiliation.

**5.162.32**  For he it was, O king, who once undertook a fearsome austerity and won a boon most difficult to obtain — to fight the Pāṇḍavas in battle.

**5.162.33**  That tiger among chariot-warriors, remembering that enmity in battle, will fight the Pāṇḍavas, my son, casting aside his own life that is so hard to cast away.

**5.163.1**  Bhīṣma said: Sudakṣiṇa the Kāmboja I reckon a chariot-warrior of single measure; desiring the fulfillment of your cause, he will fight the foe in battle.

**5.163.2**  The valor of this lion among chariot-warriors, spent in your cause, O best of kings, the Kurus shall behold in the fray as though it were Indra's own.

**5.163.3**  His ranks of chariots, O great king, manned by the Kāmbojas who strike with fierce and sudden speed, stretch on like a swarm of locusts.

**5.163.4**  Nīla, dweller in Māhiṣmatī, clad in dark-blue armor, will work with his chariot ranks a great slaughter of your enemies.

**5.163.5**  Having made enmity of old with Sahadeva, that prince will fight unceasingly in your cause, O king, O best of the Kurus.

**5.163.6**  Vinda and Anuvinda of Avanti, united, are foremost among chariot-warriors, accomplished in battle, my son, firm in might and valor.

**5.163.7**  These two tigers among men will consume the enemy host with mace and javelin, sword and iron shaft, and with lances hurled from their arms.

**5.163.8**  Eager for battle, sporting in the fray like two herd-leaders, they will range through the midst of the host, O great king, like Death itself.

**5.163.9**  The five Trigarta brothers I count exalted among chariot-warriors, who made their enmity with Pārtha then, in the city of Virāṭa.

**5.163.10**  Like sea-monsters, O lord of kings, churning the Gaṅgā with her high-tossing waves, they will throw the army of the Pārthas into turmoil in the fight.

**5.163.11**  These five are chariot-warriors, O lord of kings, with Satyaratha at their head; they will fight in battle, remembering what was done of old.

**5.163.12**  They remember the wrong done by the son of Pāṇḍu, the younger brother of Bhīmasena, who, conquering the quarters with his white-horsed car, O king, O Bhārata —

**5.163.13**  — closing in, these bearers of the warrior's burden will slay the great chariot-warriors of the Pārthas, the choicest of the mighty archers among the kṣatriyas.

**5.163.14**  Your son Lakṣmaṇa, and likewise the son of Duḥśāsana — both these tigers among men never turn back in battle.

**5.163.15**  Young and tender, both these princes are swift and spirited, skilled in the particulars of war and leaders in every kind of it.

**5.163.16**  These two chariot-warriors, O tiger among them, I reckon foremost; devoted to the kṣatriya's duty, those heroes will accomplish a mighty deed.

**5.163.17**  Daṇḍadhāra, O great king, is a chariot-warrior of single measure, a bull among men; reaching the battlefield, guarded by his own troops, he will fight.

**5.163.18**  And King Bṛhadbala of Kosala, foremost among chariot-warriors, I count a true chariot-warrior, my son, firm in onset and valor.

**5.163.19**  He will fight in the battle, rousing his own army to joy, a great archer wielding fearsome weapons, intent on the welfare of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son.

**5.163.20**  Kṛpa, son of Śaradvat, O king, is a leader among the leaders of chariot-bands; surrendering his own dear life, he will consume your foes.

**5.163.21**  He who was born from a clump of reeds, son of the great sage Gautama, the teacher Śaradvat — unconquerable like Kārttikeya.

**5.163.22**  He will range through the manifold host arrayed with diverse weapons and bows, my son, like fire in the battle, crushing it as he goes.

**5.164.1**  Bhīṣma said: Śakuni, your maternal uncle, O ruler of men, is a chariot-warrior of single measure; clinging to his enmity with the Pāṇḍavas, he will fight — of this there is no doubt.

**5.164.2**  His soldiers are hard to assail, irresistible in battle, armed for the most part with strange weapons, in swiftness equal to the speed of the wind.

**5.164.3**  The son of Droṇa, a great archer above all bowmen, fights with cunning craft in battle, sure of aim, a mighty chariot-warrior.

**5.164.4**  For his arrows, O great king, like those of the wielder of Gāṇḍīva, loosed from the bow, fly forth in unbroken streams.

**5.164.5**  This hero, foremost among chariot-warriors, I cannot fully reckon; this man of great renown could, if he wished, burn down the three worlds.

**5.164.6**  His wrath and fiery splendor were gathered through the austerity of the hermitage-dweller, and that noble-minded one was favored by Droṇa with celestial weapons.

**5.164.7**  Yet one great fault he has, on whose account, O bull of the Bharatas, I count him neither a chariot-warrior nor a great chariot-warrior, O best of kings.

**5.164.8**  Life is exceedingly dear to him; this brāhmaṇa ever longs to live. Yet none is his equal in either of the two armies.

**5.164.9**  With a single chariot he could slay even the army of the gods, and that mighty-framed one could split mountains with the thunder of his palms.

**5.164.10**  A hero of countless qualities, a striker of terrible splendor, irresistible like Yama staff in hand, he will range the field like Death itself.

**5.164.11**  In wrath like the fire at the world's end, lion-necked, of mighty mind — this man, O Bhārata, will bring the back of the war to its close.

**5.164.12**  But his father, of great splendor, though aged is finer than the young; that he will do a mighty deed in battle, of this I have no doubt.

**5.164.13**  Fanned by the wind of his weapons' speed, kindled by the host as by dry grass for fuel, he will burn up the armies of Pāṇḍu's son, fixed upon victory.

**5.164.14**  That bull among men, leader of the bands of chariot-leaders, the son of Bharadvāja, will do a fierce deed for your benefit.

**5.164.15**  The teacher and aged preceptor of all the consecrated kings, he could bring the Sṛñjayas to their end — yet Dhanaṃjaya is dear to him.

**5.164.16**  Never would this great archer slay Pārtha, the tireless doer, remembering his own blazing teaching and how it was conquered by Pārtha's virtues.

**5.164.17**  This hero ever praises the abundance of Pārtha's virtues; the son of Bharadvāja looks upon him as more than a son.

**5.164.18**  With a single chariot the mighty one could slay the gods, gandharvas, and dānavas, even were they joined as one in battle, with his celestial weapons.

**5.164.19**  Paurava, that tiger among kings, O king, your great chariot-warrior, I count a true chariot-warrior, O hero — a breaker of the cars of enemy heroes.

**5.164.20**  United with his own troops, scorching the enemy host, he will consume the Pāñcālas as fire whose path is black devours dry grass.

**5.164.21**  Satyavrata, foremost of chariot-warriors, a prince and a great chariot-warrior, will range through the enemy's host, O king, like Death itself.

**5.164.22**  His warriors, O lord of kings, in armor and arms of many colors, will range through the battle, striking down your enemies.

**5.164.23**  Vṛṣasena, foremost of your chariot-warriors, son of Karṇa and a great chariot-warrior, best of the mighty, will burn up the forces of your foes.

**5.164.24**  Jalasaṃdha of great splendor, O king, your foremost chariot-warrior, the Māgadha, slayer of enemy heroes, will yield up his life in battle.

**5.164.25**  He will fight in the battle, skilled upon the shoulders of an elephant, or by chariot — that mighty-armed one, wasting away the enemy host.

**5.164.26**  This chariot-warrior, O great king, I count a bull among men; in your cause he will lay down his life, together with his troops, in the great battle.

**5.164.27**  He fights with valor and with cunning craft in the fray, and free of fear, O king, he will do battle with your enemies.

**5.164.28**  Bāhlīka too is a great chariot-warrior and one who never turns back in battle; in the fight, O king, I count him a hero, the equal of Yama, son of the Sun.

**5.164.29**  For once he has reached the battle he would in no way turn back, O king, any more than the ever-moving wind, until he has struck down his foes in the fray.

**5.164.30**  Your commander Satyavat, O great king, a great chariot-warrior, of wondrous deeds in battle, is a chariot-warrior who breaks the cars of his enemies.

**5.164.31**  Beholding battle, there is in him no faltering whatever; smiling, he advances upon the foes who stand in his chariot's path.

**5.164.32**  Valiant against his enemies, this best of men will do, in the crush of battle, a very great deed worthy of the noble, in your cause.

**5.164.33**  Alāyudha, lord of the rākṣasas, cruel in deed and of great might, will slay your foes, O king, remembering the enmity of old.

**5.164.34**  He, foremost chariot-warrior of all the rākṣasa hosts, a master of illusion and firm in his hatred, will range through the battle.

**5.164.35**  Bhagadatta, lord of Prāgjyotiṣa, a valorous and mighty hero, foremost among wielders of the elephant-goad and skilled also upon the chariot —

**5.164.36**  With him the wielder of Gāṇḍīva once did battle for very many days, O king, both of them greedy for victory.

**5.164.37**  Then, O Gāndhāra, honoring the chastiser of Pāka as his friend, he made a compact with that great-souled son of Pāṇḍu.

**5.164.38**  He will fight in the battle, skilled upon the shoulders of his elephant, that king mounted as Vāsava mounted upon Airāvata among the gods.

**5.165.1**  Bhīṣma said: Acala and Vṛṣaka, two brothers united, are chariot-warriors of yours hard to assail; they will shatter your enemies.

**5.165.2**  Mighty tigers among men, firm in their wrath, strikers in battle, chiefs of the Gāndhāras, young, handsome, and of great strength.

**5.165.3**  Your ever-beloved friend, hardened to war, who incites you, O king, to the quarrel with the Pāṇḍavas —

**5.165.4**  — harsh, boastful, base, your Karṇa Vaikartana, your counselor, leader, and kinsman, proud and exceedingly puffed up.

**5.165.5**  He is neither a full chariot-warrior nor a great chariot-warrior, O king; parted from the armor born with him, witless, and parted too from his two celestial earrings, he is ever soft of heart.

**5.165.6**  By the curse of Rāma, by the word of a brāhmaṇa, and by the loss of his accoutrements, I therefore count him but half a chariot-warrior; once he meets Phalguna, he will not escape again alive.

**5.165.7**  Saṃjaya said: Then the mighty-armed Droṇa, best of weapon-bearers, spoke: "It is just as you have said; there is not a word of falsehood in it."

**5.165.8**  "In battle after battle he is seen overproud and turning away from the fight; soft-hearted and heedless is Karṇa, and therefore I too count him but half a chariot-warrior."

**5.165.9**  Hearing this, the son of Rādhā, his eyes wide with anger, O lord of kings, spoke to Bhīṣma, goading him with words as with a whip.

**5.165.10**  "Grandsire, you cut me at your pleasure with arrows of speech, though I am guiltless, ever out of hatred, at every step thus; yet I bear it all, every bit of it, for Duryodhana's sake."

**5.165.11**  "But you deem me unfit, as though I were a coward; in my eyes it is you who are but half a chariot-warrior — of that there is no doubt."

**5.165.12**  "I speak no falsehood before all the world, O son of Gaṅgā: you are forever the enemy of the Kurus, and the king does not perceive it."

**5.165.13**  "For who indeed, among kings equal and lofty in deed, would wreak such a slaying of another's spirit, eager to sow division before the battle, as you, under the guise of naming our qualities, seek to do us wrong?"

**5.165.14**  "Not by years, not by gray hairs, not by wealth, nor by kinsmen can the rank of a great chariot-warrior of the kṣatriya be reckoned, O Kaurava."

**5.165.15**  "The kṣatriya is held to rank by strength, the twice-born by their counsel, the vaiśyas are reckoned by their wealth, but the śūdras by seniority of age."

**5.165.16**  "At your own whim and by your own arbitrary grasp you make out chariot-warriors and great chariot-warriors thus, swayed by desire and hatred, out of delusion."

**5.165.17**  "O mighty-armed Duryodhana, let it be well and rightly considered; let this Bhīṣma of evil disposition be cast off, who works your harm."

**5.165.18**  "For an army once divided, O king, becomes hard to reunite — even one of hereditary loyalty, O tiger among men; how much more one raised from sundry sources?"

**5.165.19**  A division has arisen among these warriors on the eve of battle, O Bhārata, and our ardor is being plainly and grievously sapped.

**5.165.20**  What does Bhīṣma, dull of wit, know of the reckoning of chariot-warriors? I myself will hold back the host of the Pāṇḍavas.

**5.165.21**  Coming upon me, whose arrows never miss, the Pāṇḍavas with their Pāñcālas will scatter to the ten directions as bulls flee before a tiger.

**5.165.22**  What has the clash of battle, or counsels well spoken, to do with Bhīṣma, his vigor spent, feeble in spirit, befuddled by age?

**5.165.23**  For he is forever contending with the whole world, and in his vain conceit he esteems no other man at all.

**5.165.24**  "The aged are indeed to be heeded"—so the scriptures teach; yet not the overaged, for such are reckoned children once more.

**5.165.25**  I alone shall slay the Pāṇḍavas, of this there is no doubt; yet in a well-fought battle, O tiger among kings, the glory will fall to Bhīṣma.

**5.165.26**  You have made this Bhīṣma your commander, O lord of men; the honor will go to the commander, never in any way to the fighters.

**5.165.27**  While the son of Gaṅgā lives, O king, I will by no means fight; but once Bhīṣma is slain, I shall do battle against all the great chariot-warriors.

**5.166.1**  Bhīṣma said: This burden of mine that I take up is mighty, vast as the sea—the war of Dhṛtarāṣṭra's son, brooded upon for years on end.

**5.166.2**  Now that the hour has come, scorching and hair-raising, I must work no discord among us—and it is for that you live, O son of a sūta.

**5.166.3**  For old as I am, were I to put forth my strength this day, I could in battle cut short both your appetite for war and your very life, O son of a sūta.

**5.166.4**  When Rāma, the son of Jamadagni, loosed his great weapons against me, not a tremor touched me—and what then will you do to me?

**5.166.5**  The good, to be sure, do not praise this vaunting of one's own might; yet, stung as I am, I will speak to you, base wretch, blot upon your line.

**5.166.6**  At the bride-choice of the king of Kāśi, with but a single chariot I overcame the whole assembled warrior nobility and carried off the maidens by main force.

**5.166.7**  Thousands of such men, and of finer ones besides, were swept aside by me alone, together with their armies, upon the field of battle.

**5.166.8**  With you for their man of strife a great calamity has come upon the Kurus, for their destruction. Make your effort; prove yourself a man.

**5.166.9**  Fight Pārtha in battle, with whom you so vie—and I shall watch you come away unscathed from that combat, O most evil-minded one.

**5.166.10**  Saṃjaya said: Then the high-minded king, the son of Dhṛtarāṣṭra, spoke to him: Have regard for me, son of Gaṅgā, for a great task is upon us.

**5.166.11**  Let this above all be considered first—what is my highest good; for both of you, my lords, shall do great deeds for me.

**5.166.12**  And further I wish to hear of the foremost chariot-warriors among the foe, and who among them are atirathas, and likewise the leaders of chariot-bands.

**5.166.13**  I wish to hear the strength and weakness of my enemies, O Kaurava; for when night has brightened into dawn this war will begin.

**5.166.14**  Bhīṣma said: These chariot-warriors of yours have been numbered, O king, and likewise the atirathas; and now, O monarch, hear of the half-chariot warriors of the Pāṇḍavas.

**5.166.15**  If today you are eager, O king, to know the strength of the Pāṇḍavas—their tally of chariot-warriors, O mighty-armed one, together with these lords of the earth—

**5.166.16**  The king himself, that noble chariot-warrior, the son of Pāṇḍu, the joy of Kuntī—he will range through the battle like fire, my child, of this there is no doubt.

**5.166.17**  As for Bhīmasena, O lord of kings, he is reckoned a chariot-warrior eightfold over—proud, with the strength of ten thousand elephants; in his fierce energy he is no mere mortal.

**5.166.18**  The two sons of Mādrī are both chariot-warriors, bulls among men, in beauty like the Aśvins and endowed with like splendor.

**5.166.19**  Standing at the forefront of the host, remembering the wrongs done them, they will rage abroad like Rudra—of this I have no doubt.

**5.166.20**  All of them are great-souled, towering like śāla trunks, and in stature they exceed other men by a span's measure.

**5.166.21**  Lion-framed are all the sons of Pāṇḍu, of mighty strength; all have kept the vow of chastity and all are men of surpassing austerity.

**5.166.22**  Modest, tigers among men, fierce in strength like tigers; in speed, in the striking blow, and in the press of battle every one of them is more than human; all of them, O bull of the Bharatas, have subdued the kings of the earth in their conquest of the quarters.

**5.166.23**  And no men can ever match them in handling their weapons, maces, and arrows, O Kaurava—neither in stringing the bow, nor in lifting the heavy mace, nor in drawing the shaft to the head.

**5.166.24**  In speed, in striking the mark, in eating, in flinging up the dust of sport—even as boys they outdid you all.

**5.166.25**  Falling upon your army like tigers raging in strength, they will lay it waste in battle—let there be no encounter with them!

**5.166.26**  Each one of them, in battle, could slay all the lords of the earth—as you yourself saw, O king of kings, at the Rājasūya sacrifice.

**5.166.27**  Remembering the torment of Draupadī and the harsh words at the dicing, they will move through the battle like Death itself.

**5.166.28**  Red-eyed, with hair drawn into a knot, and having Nārāyaṇa for his ally—in neither army, O hero, is there a chariot-warrior like that one.

**5.166.29**  For never of old, neither among gods nor Dānavas nor serpents, nor among Rākṣasas nor Yakṣas—still less among men—

**5.166.30**  have I heard of any chariot, past or yet to come, O great king, so equipped as that of the wise Pārtha.

**5.166.31**  Vāsudeva for his charioteer, and Dhanaṃjaya himself for the warrior; the Gāṇḍīva for his divine bow, and those horses swift as the wind;

**5.166.32**  the impenetrable, divine armor, and the two inexhaustible great quivers; and the whole arsenal of weapons—those of Mahendra, of Rudra, and of Kubera;

**5.166.33**  those of Yama and of Varuṇa, and maces dreadful to behold, and the chief weapons headed by the thunderbolt, missiles of every kind.

**5.166.34**  Thousands of Dānavas, dwellers in the City of Gold, were slain by that single chariot in battle—what chariot-warrior is the equal of him?

**5.166.35**  In his fury this mighty-armed one, strong and true in valor, would slay your army even while he guards his own.

**5.166.36**  I, or else the teacher, might go forth against this Dhanaṃjaya; there is no third in either army, O king of kings, who could advance upon him as he pours down his torrents of arrows.

**5.166.37**  Like a storm-cloud at summer's end driven on by a great wind—so is the son of Kuntī when arrayed for war, with Vāsudeva for his ally; he is young and skilled, while we two are worn with age.

**5.166.38**  Saṃjaya said: Hearing this from Bhīṣma, the kings then felt their spirits sink, their stout arms ringed with golden armlets and smeared with sandal-paste.

**5.166.39**  Their minds in turmoil, they recalled the prowess of the sons of Pāṇḍu of old, as though they beheld it before their very eyes.

**5.167.1**  Bhīṣma said: The sons of Draupadī, O great king, are all five great chariot-warriors; and Uttara, the son of Virāṭa, I count a mighty chariot-warrior.

**5.167.2**  Abhimanyu, O great king, is a chief among the leaders of chariot-bands; in battle he may prove the equal of Pārtha or of Vāsudeva.

**5.167.3**  Quick with his weapons, fighting with brilliant variety, spirited and firm in valor, he will show his prowess, remembering the wrongs done to his father.

**5.167.4**  Sātyaki of the Mādhava line, a hero, a chief among the leaders of chariot-bands—he, foremost of the Vṛṣṇi champions, is unforgiving and has conquered all fear.

**5.167.5**  Uttamaujas too, O king, I count a mighty chariot-warrior; and Yudhāmanyu the valiant, a noble chariot-warrior, a bull among men.

**5.167.6**  These have many thousands of chariots, elephants, and horses; they will fight, casting away their lives out of love for the sons of Kuntī.

**5.167.7**  Together with the Pāṇḍavas, O king of kings, they will range through your armies, O Bhārata, like fire fanned by the wind, challenging one another.

**5.167.8**  Unconquerable in battle though aged, both Virāṭa and Drupada—great chariot-warriors of mighty valor—I count as bulls among men.

**5.167.9**  Old in years though they are, devoted to the warrior's duty, they will strive to the utmost of their power, holding to the path that heroes tread.

**5.167.10**  By their kinship, O king of kings, and through their lineage of valor and strength, these two great archers of noble conduct are both bound fast in the cords of affection.

**5.167.11**  When a sufficient cause arises, O bull among men, all men of mighty arms become either heroes or cowards.

**5.167.12**  These two, made one in purpose with Pārtha and steadfast in devotion, will labor to the utmost of their power, O lord of men, casting away their very lives.

**5.167.13**  Each at the head of an akṣauhiṇī, terrible in the fray, these two—guarding the bond of kinship—will do a great deed.

**5.167.14**  Heroes renowned in the world, great archers who have renounced all care for their lives, O Bhārata, guarding the trust placed in them, they will do a great deed.

**5.168.1**  Bhīṣma said: The son of the king of the Pāñcālas, O king, the conqueror of hostile cities, Śikhaṇḍin—him I count a foremost chariot-warrior on Pārtha's side, O Bhārata.

**5.168.2**  He will fight in the battle, undoing what was settled of old, spreading his great fame abroad through your armies, O Bhārata.

**5.168.3**  Many are his hosts, the Pāñcālas and the Prabhadrakas; and with that array of chariots he will do a great deed.

**5.168.4**  And Dhṛṣṭadyumna, commander of all their forces, O Bhārata, I count an atiratha, O king—the pupil of Droṇa, a great chariot-warrior.

**5.168.5**  He will fight in the battle, cutting down the foe in combat, like the blessed Lord of the Pināka in his wrath at the dissolution of an age.

**5.168.6**  Of his army of chariots, those who delight in war say that, for its multitude, it is like the ocean, as in the battle-array of the gods.

**5.168.7**  But Kṣatradharman, O king of kings, I count a half-chariot warrior, O monarch—the son of Dhṛṣṭadyumna, who by reason of his youth is not yet greatly seasoned.

**5.168.8**  The son of Śiśupāla, the heroic king of the Cedis, a great chariot-warrior and mighty archer—Dhṛṣṭaketu, a kinsman of the Pāṇḍava;

**5.168.9**  this valiant lord of the Cedis, together with his son, O Bhārata, a great chariot-warrior, will do a great and hard-wrought deed.

**5.168.10**  Devoted to the warrior's duty, a conqueror of hostile cities, I count Kṣatradeva, O king of kings, a foremost chariot-warrior among the Pāṇḍavas; and Jayanta, and Amitaujas, and Satyajit the great chariot-warrior.

**5.168.11**  Great chariot-warriors and great-souled, all the foremost of the Pāñcālas—they will fight in battle, my child, like elephants in fury.

**5.168.12**  Aja and Bhoja, both valiant, great chariot-warriors among the Pāṇḍavas, will strive to the utmost of their power in aid of the Pāṇḍavas—swift with their weapons, fighting with brilliant variety, skilled and firm in valor.

**5.168.13**  The five Kekaya brothers, O king of kings, frenzied for battle—all of them noble chariot-warriors, all with crimson banners.

**5.168.14**  The king of Kāśi, and Sukumāra, and Nīla, and that other king, and Sūryadatta, and Śaṅkha, and one named Madirāśva—

**5.168.15**  all these are noble chariot-warriors, all bearing the marks of battle, all masters of every weapon, all great-souled—so I judge them.

**5.168.16**  Vārdhakṣemi, O great king, I count a mighty chariot-warrior; and king Citrāyudha I count a foremost chariot-warrior, for he is a splendor in battle and is devoted to the Diademed One.

**5.168.17**  Cekitāna and Satyadhṛti, two great chariot-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas—these two, tigers among men and noble chariot-warriors, so I judge them.

**5.168.18**  And Vyāghradatta, O king of kings, and Candrasena, O Bhārata—both I count noble chariot-warriors of the Pāṇḍavas, beyond doubt.

**5.168.19**  And Senābindu, O lord of kings, called by name Krodhahantā, the slayer of wrath, who is the equal of Vāsudeva and of Bhīmasena, that overpowering one—he will fight here valiantly in battle against your soldiers.

**5.168.20**  Even as Your Honour reckons me, and Droṇa, and Kṛpa, so too must that one, glorious in battle, be reckoned the foremost of chariot-warriors.

**5.168.21**  The king of Kāśi, swiftest of all in his weaponry, praiseworthy, an excellent chariot-warrior, the conqueror of his enemies' cities—him I reckon a chariot-warrior of single measure.

**5.168.22**  And this one, valiant in war, must be reckoned a chariot-warrior of eightfold measure—Satyajit, glorious in battle, the young son of Drupada.

**5.168.23**  He has attained the rank of an atiratha, a super-warrior, reckoned the equal of Dhṛṣṭadyumna; desiring glory for the Pāṇḍavas, he will perform a supreme deed.

**5.168.24**  And this other great chariot-warrior, devoted and brave, is the king of the Pāṇḍyas, mighty in valour, a bearer of the Pāṇḍavas' burden.

**5.168.25**  Dṛḍhadhanvan, the great archer, a foremost chariot-warrior of the Pāṇḍavas, is Śreṇimat, O best of the Kurus; and there is the king Vasudāna—both of these I reckon super-warriors, O scorcher of foes.

**5.169.1**  Bhīṣma said: Rocamāna, O great king, a great chariot-warrior of the Pāṇḍavas, will fight in battle like an immortal against the enemy's hosts, O descendant of Bharata.

**5.169.2**  Purujit Kuntibhoja, the great archer of mighty strength, the maternal uncle of Bhīmasena—him too I reckon a super-warrior.

**5.169.3**  This hero, a great archer, accomplished and skilful, a versatile fighter and powerful—him I reckon a bull among chariot-warriors.

**5.169.4**  For he will fight valiantly, as Maghavat does against the Dānavas; and his warriors are all renowned and well-versed in war.

**5.169.5**  For his nephews' sake that hero, the king, devoted to what is dear and good for the Pāṇḍavas, will perform a very great deed in the encounter.

**5.169.6**  The son of Bhīmasena, O great king, the son of Hiḍimbā, lord of the rākṣasas, master of many illusions, a leader of leaders of chariot-bands—him I reckon highly.

**5.169.7**  He will fight in battle with illusions, my son, loving the fray, and so too the brave rākṣasas, his obedient companions.

**5.169.8**  These and many others, lords of various lands, have assembled for the Pāṇḍava's cause, with Vāsudeva at their head.

**5.169.9**  These, O king, are the principal chariot-warriors and super-warriors of the great-souled Pāṇḍava, and those reckoned half-warriors as well.

**5.169.10**  They will lead in battle Yudhiṣṭhira's dreadful army, O king, guarded by the hero Kirīṭin as by the great Indra himself.

**5.169.11**  Against these, O hero, who advance upon you seeking victory, I shall fight in battle, longing either for victory or for death in the fray.

**5.169.12**  And Pārtha and Vāsudeva, wielders of the discus and the Gāṇḍīva, those most excellent of men, I shall meet as the sun and moon meeting at twilight.

**5.169.13**  And those noble chariot-warriors, the soldiers of Pāṇḍu's son—those too, together with their hosts, I shall face at the forefront of battle.

**5.169.14**  These chariot-warriors and super-warriors I have named to you according to their rank, O king; and likewise certain half-warriors among them, O lord of the Kurus.

**5.169.15**  Arjuna and Vāsudeva and whatever other kings are there—all of them I shall hold off, so long as I have sight, O descendant of Bharata.

**5.169.16**  But the Pāñcāla Śikhaṇḍin, O mighty-armed one, I shall not slay, even when I see him with arrow raised and fighting back against me in battle.

**5.169.17**  The world knows this, that I, wishing to do what pleased my father, renounced the kingdom that had come to me and held fast to the vow of celibacy.

**5.169.18**  I consecrated Citrāṅgada to the kingship of the Kurus, and I consecrated the child Vicitravīrya to the office of crown prince.

**5.169.19**  Having proclaimed my devotion to my vow before all the kings on earth, I would never slay a woman, nor by any means one who was once a woman.

**5.169.20**  For that Śikhaṇḍin was once a woman, O king, if you have heard it: having first been a maiden, he was born a man; with him I shall not fight, O descendant of Bharata.

**5.169.21**  But all the other kings I shall slay, O bull of the Bharatas, whomever I meet in battle—save the sons of Kuntī, O king.

**5.170.1**  Duryodhana said: For what reason, O best of the Bharatas, would you not slay Śikhaṇḍin, even seeing him in battle with arrow raised and bent on assault?

**5.170.2**  Having said before, O mighty-armed one, 'I shall slay the Pāṇḍavas together with the Somakas,' O son of Gaṅgā—tell me of this, grandfather.

**5.170.3**  Bhīṣma said: Hear, Duryodhana, together with these lords of the earth, the tale of why I would not slay Śikhaṇḍin even when I see him in battle.

**5.170.4**  The great king, my father Śaṃtanu, bull of the Bharatas, righteous in soul, reached his appointed end in due time, O bull among men.

**5.170.5**  Then I, O best of the Bharatas, keeping my vow, consecrated my brother Citrāṅgada to the great kingship.

**5.170.6**  And when he met his death, abiding by Satyavatī's wish, I consecrated Vicitravīrya as king according to the rites.

**5.170.7**  Though consecrated by me, O lord of kings, and though my younger, rightly so, the righteous Vicitravīrya still looked to me for guidance.

**5.170.8**  Wishing then to bring about his marriage, my son, I set my mind on it, thinking, 'from a family worthy of him.'

**5.170.9**  Then I heard, O mighty-armed one, that three maidens at a self-choice ceremony, all of matchless beauty, daughters of the king of Kāśi—Ambā, and Ambikā, and Ambālikā the third.

**5.170.10**  And the kings of the earth were summoned, O bull of the Bharatas. Ambā was the eldest of them, Ambikā the middle one, and Ambālikā, O lord of kings, was the youngest of those royal maidens.

**5.170.11**  I, going to the city of the king of Kāśi with but a single chariot, saw those three maidens, O mighty-armed one, well adorned, and the kings, the assembled princes, O lord of the earth.

**5.170.12**  Then, challenging all those kings who stood ready for battle, I made those maidens mount my chariot, O bull of the Bharatas.

**5.170.13**  Knowing that they were to be won by valour as their price, having set them upon my chariot, I addressed all the princes gathered there, again and again: 'Bhīṣma, the son of Śaṃtanu, carries off the maidens!'

**5.170.14**  'Strive, all of you, with utmost might to wrest them free, O princes; for by force I carry them off, even as you look on, O rulers of men!'

**5.170.15**  Then those guardians of the earth sprang up with weapons raised, crying in fury 'To arms! To arms!' and urged on their charioteers.

**5.170.16**  Those kings rushed forth with weapons raised—in chariots resembling clouds, the elephant-fighters upon elephants, and others upon horses bred for the saddle.

**5.170.17**  Then those kings, every one of them, O lord of peoples, surrounded me on all sides with a great host of chariots.

**5.170.18**  Them I held off with a mighty rain of arrows, and I vanquished all those kings, as the king of the gods vanquishes the Dānavas.

**5.170.19**  Of those who rushed upon me, their bright banners adorned with gold, I cast each one to the earth with a single arrow apiece.

**5.170.20**  And their horses and their elephants and their charioteers I felled in the fray with blazing arrows, laughing the while, O bull among men.

**5.170.21**  Seeing that deftness of mine, they turned back and were broken; and then, having conquered those rulers of the earth, I came to Hāstinapura.

**5.170.22**  Then those maidens, O descendant of Bharata, won for my brother's sake, and that deed too, O mighty-armed one, I reported to Satyavatī.

**5.171.1**  Bhīṣma said: Then, O best of the Bharatas, approaching my mother, the mother of a hero, the daughter of the fisherman, and clasping her feet, I spoke these words.

**5.171.2**  'These daughters of the king of Kāśi, won at the price of valour, I have gained for Vicitravīrya's sake, having vanquished the kings.'

**5.171.3**  Then, O king, smelling my head and with tears brimming in her eyes, Satyavatī said, rejoicing: 'By good fortune, my son, you have triumphed!'

**5.171.4**  But when, with Satyavatī's consent, the wedding was at hand, the eldest daughter of the king of Kāśi, abashed, spoke these words.

**5.171.5**  'Bhīṣma, you are a knower of dharma, well-versed in all the scriptures; having heard my words, which are righteous, you ought to act on them here.'

**5.171.6**  'The lord of Śālva was once chosen by me in my heart as my groom, and by him too I was chosen before, in secret, unknown to my father.'

**5.171.7**  'How, O king, having studied the scriptures, would you keep me in your house, Bhīṣma, when my heart longs for another—you, of all men, being a Kaurava?'

**5.171.8**  'Having weighed this and resolved it in your mind, O bull of the Bharatas, whatever is fitting for you, that, mighty-armed one, you ought to undertake here.'

**5.171.9**  'Plainly the king of Śālva awaits me, O lord of peoples. Show mercy to me, O mighty-armed one, best of the upholders of dharma; for you, O hero, are known throughout the earth as true to your vows—so have we heard.'

**5.172.1**  Bhīṣma said: Then, having taken leave of Kālī Satyavatī, and of the ministers and the brāhmaṇas and likewise the house-priests, I gave leave to the eldest maiden, Ambā, O ruler of men.

**5.172.2**  Given leave, that maiden went to the city of the lord of Śālva, guarded by aged brāhmaṇas and attended by her nurse; and having crossed that road, she came before the king.

**5.172.3**  Coming before that king, Śālva, she spoke these words: 'I have come, O mighty-armed one, seeking you, O greatly radiant one.'

**5.172.4**  The lord of Śālva said to her, with something like a smile, O lord of peoples: 'I do not desire you for my wife, O fair-complexioned one, since you have already belonged to another.'

**5.172.5**  'Go, gentle lady, back there into the presence of the descendant of Bharata; I do not want you, who were seized by force by Bhīṣma.'

**5.172.6**  'For you, having been won by Bhīṣma, were then led away content—he laid hands on you in that great battle, having vanquished the lords of the earth; I do not desire you for my wife, O fair-complexioned one, since you have belonged to another before.'

**5.172.7**  'How should a king such as I take into his house a woman who has belonged to another—one of discerning knowledge, who lays down the law of conduct for others? Go where you will, gentle lady; let not this time of yours slip past you.'

**5.172.8**  Ambā said to him, O king, tormented by the arrows of the bodiless one, Kāma: 'Speak not thus, O guardian of the earth; this is by no means so.'

**5.172.9**  'I was not led away content by Bhīṣma, O harrower of foes; I was carried off by force, weeping, when he had put the lords of the earth to flight.'

**5.172.10**  'Take me to yourself, O lord of Śālva—a devoted girl, innocent; for the abandonment of those devoted to one is not praised in the codes of dharma.'

**5.172.11**  'I, having taken my leave of the son of Gaṅgā, who never turns back in battle, and being granted leave by him, have come to your house alone.'

**5.172.12**  'That mighty-armed Bhīṣma does not desire me, O lord of peoples; Bhīṣma's whole undertaking was for his brother's sake—so I have heard.'

**5.172.13**  'My two sisters who were carried off, Ambikā and Ambālikā, O king—the son of Gaṅgā gave them to his younger brother Vicitravīrya.'

**5.172.14**  'As, O lord of Śālva, I think of no other man at all but you, O tiger among men—so do I touch my own head in oath.'

**5.172.15**  'Not having belonged to another, O lord of kings, have I come before you. I speak the truth, Śālva—by that truth I touch my own self in oath.'

**5.172.16**  'Take me to yourself, O wide-eyed one—a maiden come of her own accord, who has belonged to no other, O lord of kings, longing for your favour.'

**5.172.17**  But Śālva cast off that daughter of the king of Kāśi as she spoke thus, O best of the Bharatas, as a serpent casts off its worn-out skin.

**5.172.18**  Though entreated thus by her with words of many kinds, O sinless one, the lord of Śālva put no faith in the maiden, O bull of the Bharatas.

**5.172.19**  Then that eldest daughter of the king of Kāśi, possessed by anguish, spoke with tearful eyes, in a voice broken by weeping.

**5.172.20**  'Forsaken by you, I shall go wherever I may, O lord of peoples; wherever that be, may the good be my refuges, even as I have spoken the truth.'

**5.172.21**  But as she was speaking thus, the cruel king of Śālva then cast her off utterly, O Kaurava, as she lamented piteously.

**5.172.22**  "Go, go!" Śālva said to her again and again. "I fear Bhīṣma, fair-hipped one, and you are Bhīṣma's prize."

**5.172.23**  Thus addressed by Śālva, that man of short sight, she went forth from the city, wretched, weeping like an osprey.

**5.173.1**  Bhīṣma said: As she went out from the city, O Bhārata, she thought: "On this earth there is no maiden more wretchedly placed than I. Bereft of my kinsmen, I am spurned by Śālva as well."

**5.173.2**  "Nor can I go back to the city named for the elephant, for I was released by Bhīṣma on Śālva's account."

**5.173.3**  "Whom then shall I blame—myself, or the unassailable Bhīṣma, or my foolish father, who arranged for me a self-choice of husband?"

**5.173.4**  "This is a fault of my own making, that long ago, when the deadly battle was joined, I did not leap from Bhīṣma's chariot for Śālva's sake. This is the fruit ripened of that, that I have come to ruin like a fool."

**5.173.5**  "A curse on Bhīṣma, and a curse on my dull-witted, foolish father, by whom I was flung forth like a woman for sale, with valor as the bride-price."

**5.173.6**  "A curse on me, a curse on King Śālva, and a curse on the Creator too, by whose ill-conduct I have come to this utmost calamity."

**5.173.7**  "In every way a mortal reaps his own allotted portions; yet the fountainhead of this misfortune of mine is Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu."

**5.173.8**  "I now see that vengeance must be wrought upon Bhīṣma, whether by austerity or by war—for I deem him the cause of my sorrow. Yet what king would dare to conquer Bhīṣma in battle?"

**5.173.9**  Having thus resolved, she went forth from the city to the hermitage of holy-natured, great-souled ascetics; and there she passed the night, surrounded by the ascetics.

**5.173.10**  And she told them all that had befallen her, O mighty-armed Bhārata, fully and in detail—she of the bright smile told of her seizure, her release, and her dismissal by Śālva.

**5.173.11**  Now there was there a great Brāhmaṇa of firm vows, Śaikhāvatya, grown old in austerity, a master of the forest-lore.

**5.173.12**  To her in her distress that sage Śaikhāvatya, of great austerity, spoke—to the virtuous, sighing girl wholly given over to grief and sorrow:

**5.173.13**  "Things being so, good lady, what can be done by ascetics—by the blessed great-souled ones who dwell in the hermitage and are ever devoted to austerity?"

**5.173.14**  And she said to him, O king: "Let a favor be granted me. I wish to go forth as a recluse here; I shall practice austerity hard to perform."

**5.173.15**  "Surely these evil deeds were done by me, deluded, in former bodies; this is the certain fruit of them."

**5.173.16**  "I cannot bear to go back to my own people, O ascetics—rejected, joyless, and spurned by Śālva."

**5.173.17**  "I wish to be taught the ascetic life here by you, O sinless ones who are like gods. Let your compassion rest upon me."

**5.173.18**  He comforted the maiden with examples and with reasons drawn from scripture, soothed her, and together with the other Brāhmaṇas pledged to undertake her cause.

**5.174.1**  Bhīṣma said: Then all those ascetics, righteous men, became intent upon the matter, pondering concerning that maiden, "What is to be done?"

**5.174.2**  Some of the ascetics said, "Let her be taken to her father's house"; some of the foremost of the twice-born set their minds on rebuking us.

**5.174.3**  Some thought that they should go to the lord of Śālva and constrain him; others resolved against it, since she had been rejected by him.

**5.174.4**  "Things being so, good lady, what can be done by us, the wise?" And then all those ascetics of firm vows spoke again:

**5.174.5**  "Enough of renunciation here, good lady; hear our wholesome counsel. Go from here—blessings on you—to your own father's dwelling."

**5.174.6**  "Your father the king will see to whatever comes next. There you shall dwell in happiness, fair one, possessed of every comfort. No other refuge would be fitting for you, good lady, as your father is."

**5.174.7**  "A husband or a father is a woman's refuge, fair-complexioned one: when her fortunes are even, the husband is her refuge; but in adversity, the father is her refuge."

**5.174.8**  "The renunciant life is full of hardship, especially for a delicate woman—and by nature for a king's daughter, and for a maiden such as you, O proud lady."

**5.174.9**  "Good lady, fair one, many dangers exist for one dwelling in a hermitage; these would not be so in your father's house."

**5.174.10**  Then the Brāhmaṇas spoke this word to her, the ascetic woman: "Seeing you here alone in this deserted, dense forest, great kings will woo you; therefore do not set your mind on this."

**5.174.11**  Ambā said: "I cannot return to the city of Kāśi, to my father's house. I would become an object of contempt to my kinsmen—there is no doubt of it."

**5.174.12**  "In a different way I dwelt in my childhood in my father's house, O ascetics. I shall not go—blessings on you—to where my father is. I wish to perform austerity, guarded by ascetics."

**5.174.13**  "So that in the next world too there may not be for me this same great ruin and misfortune, O best of Brāhmaṇas—therefore shall I practice austerity."

**5.174.14**  Bhīṣma said: While those Brāhmaṇas were thus pondering this way and that, the royal sage Hotravāhana, an ascetic, came to that forest.

**5.174.15**  Then all those ascetics honored that king with the rites of hospitality—with welcome and the rest, with a seat and with water.

**5.174.16**  When he was seated and rested and listening, the forest-dwellers once again took up their talk concerning the maiden.

**5.174.17**  Hearing that story of Ambā and of the king of Kāśi, O Bhārata, he—the father of her mother—rose up trembling, and taking the maiden upon his lap, he comforted her, O lord.

**5.174.18**  He asked her in full about the origin of her misfortune from the beginning, and she related to him in detail all that had happened.

**5.174.19**  Then that royal sage was filled with grief and sorrow, and that one of great austerity resolved in his mind upon the deed to be done.

**5.174.20**  And trembling, in deep sorrow, he said to the afflicted maiden: "Do not go to your father's house, good lady; for I am your mother's father."

**5.174.21**  "I shall cut away your sorrow; depend upon me, little daughter. Enough has your mind, my child, been thus withered with grief."

**5.174.22**  "Go at my word to Rāma, son of Jamadagni, the ascetic. Rāma will take away your great grief and sorrow; he will slay Bhīṣma in battle if he will not heed his word."

**5.174.23**  "Go to that best of the Bhārgavas, whose splendor equals the fire of doom. That one of great austerity will set you again upon an even path."

**5.174.24**  Then, shedding tears aloud again and again, she spoke to her mother's father, Hotravāhana:

**5.174.25**  "Having bowed my head, I shall go at your command. But can I now even behold that noble one, renowned throughout the world?"

**5.174.26**  "And how shall that Bhārgava destroy my keen sorrow? This I wish to hear, and then indeed I shall go there."

**5.175.1**  Hotravāhana said: "You shall see Rāma, son of Jamadagni, my child, in the great forest, engaged in fierce austerity—true to his word, of mighty strength."

**5.175.2**  "On Mahendra, the best of mountains, sages versed in the Vedas ever attend upon Rāma, and Gandharvas and Apsarases too."

**5.175.3**  "Go there—blessings on you—and speak to him this word of mine, having first bowed your head to him, grown old in austerity, of firm vows."

**5.175.4**  "And tell him further, good lady, whatever purpose you have in mind; when I am named, Rāma will do all of it for you."

**5.175.5**  "Rāma is my friend, my child, bound to me in affection, and my well-wisher—the heroic son of Jamadagni, the foremost of all who bear weapons."

**5.175.6**  While the king Hotravāhana was thus speaking to the maiden, Akṛtavraṇa appeared, the dear companion of Rāma.

**5.175.7**  Then all those sages rose up by the thousands, and that aged king Hotravāhana, of the Sṛñjayas, as well.

**5.175.8**  Then, having greeted one another in due form, those forest-dwellers, O best of the Bharatas, sat down together, surrounding him.

**5.175.9**  Then they told one tale after another, delightful, lovely and sublime, O great king, full of love and joy and gladness.

**5.175.10**  Then, at the end of the talk, the great-souled royal sage Hotravāhana asked Akṛtavraṇa about Rāma, foremost of the great sages:

**5.175.11**  "Where now, O mighty-armed Akṛtavraṇa, is the valiant son of Jamadagni? Can he be seen—he, the best of those who know the Vedas?"
