**5.175.12**  Akṛtavraṇa said: "Rāma is ever speaking of you yourself, my lord—'the Sṛñjaya is my dear friend, a royal sage,' so he says, O king."

**5.175.13**  "It is my thought that Rāma will be here tomorrow at dawn; you shall see him coming here, eager for the sight of you."

**5.175.14**  "And this maiden, O royal sage—for what purpose has she come to the forest? Whose is she, and what is she to you? I wish to know."

**5.175.15**  Hotravāhana said: "This is my daughter's daughter, my lord—the fair daughter of the king of Kāśi. The eldest, she stood at the self-choice along with her sisters, O sinless one."

**5.175.16**  "This eldest daughter of the lord of Kāśi is known as Ambā; the other two, the younger, are Ambikā and Ambālikā, O you whose wealth is austerity."

**5.175.17**  "The kingly warrior-host of the kings had gathered then in the city of Kāśi; on account of the maidens, O Brahmā-sage, there was a great festival there."

**5.175.18**  "Then, it is said, the mighty Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu, of great splendor, scattered the kings and carried off those three maidens."

**5.175.19**  "Having conquered the rulers of the earth, Bhīṣma of pure soul came then to the city named for the elephant together with the maidens, O Bhārata."

**5.175.20**  "Having then made them known to Satyavatī, the lord forthwith commanded that they be married to his brother Vicitravīrya."

**5.175.21**  "Then, seeing the marriage preparations, this maiden, the one who had been won, spoke there to the son of Gaṅgā in the midst of the counselors, O bull among the twice-born:"

**5.175.22**  "'O hero, the lord of Śālva has been chosen by me in my heart as my husband; you ought not, O knower of dharma, to give me away when my heart is set on another.'"

**5.175.23**  "Having heard that word, Bhīṣma, after taking counsel with the ministers and reaching a decision, released her, abiding by the judgment of Satyavatī."

**5.175.24**  "Permitted then by Bhīṣma, this maiden, glad at heart, went to Śālva, lord of Saubha, O Brāhmaṇa, and in due time spoke these words:"

**5.175.25**  "'I have been released by Bhīṣma; grant me my rightful place. You, O bull among kings, were earlier chosen by me in my heart.'"

**5.175.26**  "But Śālva refused her too, suspicious of her conduct. So she has come to the penance-grove, devoted utterly to the ascetic life."

**5.175.27**  "And I recognized her by the recital of her lineage. As for the source of this sorrow, she holds Bhīṣma alone to be its cause here."

**5.175.28**  Ambā said: "Reverend one, it is even so, just as the king says—Hotravāhana the Sṛñjaya, the begetter of my mother's body."

**5.175.29**  "For I cannot bear to return to my own city, O you whose wealth is austerity—both from fear of dishonor and from shame, O great sage."

**5.175.30**  "But whatever the reverend Rāma shall bid me, O best of the twice-born, that is the deed most fit for me to do—such, reverend sir, is my resolve."

**5.176.1**  Akṛtavraṇa said: "This is a twofold sorrow, good lady; for which of the two do you wish redress to be wrought, O frail one? Tell me that, my child."

**5.176.2**  "If, good lady, the lord of Saubha is to be constrained in your judgment, the great-souled Rāma will constrain him for your welfare."

**5.176.3**  "But if you wish to see that Bhīṣma, son of the river, conquered in battle by the wise Rāma, the Bhārgava would do that too."

**5.176.4**  "Having heard the word of the Sṛñjaya and your own, O bright-smiling one, let what is next to be done in this matter be considered this very day."

**5.176.5**  Ambā said: I was carried off by Bhīṣma, holy one, who did not know my heart; for Bhīṣma does not know, O brāhmaṇa, that my mind was set upon Śālva.

**5.176.6**  Weighing this in your mind, let your honour himself reach the decision rightly, and let the remedy be made accordingly.

**5.176.7**  Whether against Bhīṣma, that tiger of the Kurus, or else against King Śālva, or indeed against them both, O brāhmaṇa—do what is fitting.

**5.176.8**  For I have laid before you the root of my sorrow just as it is; in this matter, holy one, you ought to provide a remedy by sound reason.

**5.176.9**  Akṛtavraṇa said: This is fitting, gentle and lovely lady, that you should speak thus in regard to what is right; now hear these words of mine.

**5.176.10**  If the son of the River had not borne you off to the City named for the Elephant, then Śālva, O timid one, urged on by Rāma, would have received you with bowed head.

**5.176.11**  Because you were won by him, gentle lady, because you were carried off, O passionate one, therefore is King Śālva in doubt concerning you, slender-waisted one.

**5.176.12**  Bhīṣma too is proud of his manhood and exults in his victories; therefore it is fitting that you have redress exacted from Bhīṣma.

**5.176.13**  Ambā said: In my heart too, O brāhmaṇa, this great longing ever stirs—that I might have Bhīṣma slain in battle, this and nothing else, always.

**5.176.14**  Whether Bhīṣma or King Śālva—whomever you find at fault—chastise that one, O mighty-armed one, on whose account I am so deeply afflicted.

**5.176.15**  Bhīṣma said: As they were thus conversing, that day passed away, and the night too, O best of the Bharatas, with its breezes pleasantly cool and warm.

**5.176.16**  Then Rāma appeared, blazing as it were with his splendour, surrounded by his disciples, O king—the sage wearing matted locks and garments of bark.

**5.176.17**  Bow in hand, undaunted in spirit, bearing a sword and an axe, stainless, O tiger among kings, he approached the Sṛñjaya king.

**5.176.18**  Then the ascetics, seeing him, and that king of great austerity, all stood with palms joined together—and so too that suffering maiden.

**5.176.19**  Calmly they honoured the descendant of Bhṛgu with the honey-offering; and, duly worshipped, he sat down together with them.

**5.176.20**  Then the two of them recounted to each other the things of bygone days—both that royal sage of the Sṛñjayas and the son of Jamadagni, O Bhārata.

**5.176.21**  Then, when the talk was done, the royal sage at the right moment spoke to Rāma, that mighty best of the Bhṛgus, in sweet and meaningful words.

**5.176.22**  Rāma, this is my daughter's daughter, the child of the king of Kāśi, O lord; hear her cause just as it truly stands, O you skilled in counsel.

**5.176.23**  "Let her tell it fully," Rāma answered him; and then she approached Rāma, who blazed like fire itself.

**5.176.24**  And that fair maiden, saluting Rāma's feet with her head and touching them with hands like lotus petals, stood before him.

**5.176.25**  Stricken with grief, her eyes troubled with tears, she wept, and sought refuge with the son of Bhṛgu, the giver of refuge.

**5.176.26**  Rāma said: As you are to this Sṛñjaya king, so are you to me, O daughter of a king. Tell me the sorrow of your heart; I shall do your bidding.

**5.176.27**  Ambā said: Holy one of mighty vows, this day I have come to you for refuge; lift me out of the dreadful ocean whose mire is sorrow, O lord.

**5.176.28**  Bhīṣma said: Beholding her beauty, her tender youth, and her exceeding delicacy, Rāma fell to brooding thought.

**5.176.29**  "What will she say?" Thus the best of the Bhṛgus pondered; long did Rāma meditate, overwhelmed with compassion.

**5.176.30**  "Tell it," Rāma said to her again; and she of the bright smile recounted everything just as it truly was to the descendant of Bhṛgu.

**5.176.31**  Hearing then the words of the king's daughter, the son of Jamadagni, having settled the certain conclusion of the matter, said to her of the fair hips:

**5.176.32**  I shall send word to Bhīṣma, best of the Kurus, O passionate lady; that ruler of men, on hearing me, will carry out my righteous command.

**5.176.33**  But if the son of Jāhnavī will not do as I bid, then in battle, gentle lady, I shall burn him up, ministers and all, with the fire of my weapons.

**5.176.34**  Or else, O king's daughter, if your mind turns away from that, then I shall instead set the heroic lord of Śālva to this task.

**5.176.35**  Ambā said: I was let go by Bhīṣma, O son of Bhṛgu, as soon as he heard that my heart had long before been resolved upon King Śālva.

**5.176.36**  Going to the lord of Saubha, I spoke words hard to utter; but he would not receive me back, mistrustful of my conduct.

**5.176.37**  Weighing all this with your own judgement, O son of Bhṛgu, you ought to consider what course of action is here the fitting one.

**5.176.38**  The root of this calamity of mine is Bhīṣma of the mighty vow, by whom I was then seized up by force and brought under his power.

**5.176.39**  Slay Bhīṣma, O mighty-armed one, on whose account such sorrow has befallen me; for his sake, O tiger of the Bhṛgus, I wander on in utmost misery.

**5.176.40**  For he is greedy, and proud, and exultant in victory, O descendant of Bhṛgu; therefore it is fitting that you exact redress upon him, O sinless one.

**5.176.41**  This was the resolve that arose in my heart, O lord, when I was being carried off by the Bhārata: that I would have him of the mighty vow slain.

**5.176.42**  Therefore fulfil this longing of mine this day, O sinless Rāma; slay Bhīṣma, mighty-armed one, as Purandara slew Vṛtra.

**5.177.1**  Bhīṣma said: Thus addressed with "Slay Bhīṣma," O lord, Rāma then spoke to the weeping maiden, who urged him again and again:

**5.177.2**  O daughter of Kāśi, willingly I take up no weapon, lovely lady, save for the sake of the knowers of the Veda. What else can I do for you?

**5.177.3**  By my word both Bhīṣma and Śālva, O queenly one, shall become obedient to my will, O flawless-limbed one; that I shall do—grieve not.

**5.177.4**  But by no means whatever shall I take up a weapon, O passionate lady, except at the bidding of the brāhmaṇas; this is the vow I have made.

**5.177.5**  Ambā said: My sorrow must by all means be taken away by your honour; and since it is Bhīṣma who has begotten it for me, slay him, O lord, without delay.

**5.177.6**  Rāma said: Speak once more, maiden of Kāśi: Bhīṣma, though himself worthy to be revered, will at my word take hold of both your feet with his head.

**5.177.7**  Ambā said: Slay Bhīṣma in battle, Rāma, if you wish to do me a kindness; and if that was promised, you ought here to make it come true.

**5.177.8**  Bhīṣma said: As the two of them, Rāma and Ambā, were thus conversing, O king, Akṛtavraṇa spoke these words to the son of Jamadagni:

**5.177.9**  O mighty-armed one, you ought not to abandon a maiden who has come to you for refuge; slay Bhīṣma in battle, Rāma, as one slays a roaring demon.

**5.177.10**  If Bhīṣma, summoned by you to battle, Rāma, O great sage, should say "I am vanquished," or should do your bidding,

**5.177.11**  then this maiden's cause would be accomplished, O son of Bhṛgu, and your word, O hero, would be made true and fulfilled, O lord.

**5.177.12**  And this too was the vow you once made, Rāma, O great sage, when, having conquered all the kṣatriyas, you gave your pledge before the brāhmaṇas:

**5.177.13**  "If in battle a brāhmaṇa, a kṣatriya, a vaiśya, or even a śūdra shall prove a hater of the brāhmaṇas, him will I slay"—so you swore, O descendant of Bhṛgu.

**5.177.14**  "Of those who come to me for refuge, frightened and longing to live, I shall never be able, so long as I live, to forsake any."

**5.177.15**  "And whoever shall vanquish in battle the whole kṣatriya host assembled, him too, that arrogant one, will I slay"—so you swore, O descendant of Bhṛgu.

**5.177.16**  Now Bhīṣma, the upholder of the Kuru line, is just such a conqueror, Rāma; meet him and fight with him in combat, O son of Bhṛgu.

**5.177.17**  Rāma said: I remember the vow I made of old, O best of sages, and I shall act accordingly—yet so that the end may first be gained by conciliation.

**5.177.18**  This is a great matter, O brāhmaṇa, that dwells in the heart of the maiden of Kāśi; I shall go there myself, taking the maiden, to where he is.

**5.177.19**  If Bhīṣma, who glories in battle, will not do as I say, then I shall slay that overweening one; such is my settled resolve.

**5.177.20**  For the arrows loosed by me do not cling fast in the bodies of living beings—as is known to you from of old, in my battles with the kṣatriyas.

**5.177.21**  Bhīṣma said: Having spoken thus, Rāma then, together with those expounders of the Veda, resolved upon setting forth, and the great-souled one rose up.

**5.177.22**  Then those ascetics, having passed that night there, having offered into the fire and murmured their prayers, set out with the desire to have me slain.

**5.177.23**  Then Rāma, together with those bulls among brāhmaṇas, came to Kurukṣetra, O great king, along with the maiden, O Bhārata.

**5.177.24**  Then they all encamped, having taken up their station along the Sarasvatī—those great-souled ascetics, with the best of the Bhṛgus at their head.

**5.178.1**  Bhīṣma said: Then, on the third day, taking his stand upon level ground, he of the mighty vow sent word to me, O king: "I have arrived."

**5.178.2**  Hearing that the mighty one had come to the border of the realm, I went swiftly forth to meet him with joy—that lord who was a treasury of splendour.

**5.178.3**  Putting a cow before me, O king of kings, surrounded by brāhmaṇas, by priests of the sacrifice godlike in their nature, and likewise by household chaplains.

**5.178.4**  Seeing me come to him, the valiant son of Jamadagni accepted that worship and spoke these words:

**5.178.5**  Bhīṣma, with what intent was this daughter of the king of Kāśi brought here by you against her will, and then again sent away?

**5.178.6**  For by you she has been made to fall from the winning of righteousness, in its higher and lower forms; and who indeed could now take her, whom you have laid hands upon?

**5.178.7**  For she has been rejected by Śālva, saying "she was carried off by you," O Bhārata; therefore, at my command, take her back, O Bhārata.

**5.178.8**  Let this king's daughter, O tiger among men, attain her own proper estate; it is not fitting that this slight should be put upon her by you, a king, O sinless one.

**5.178.9**  Then, looking upon him whose mind was ill at ease, I said: I shall in no way give her again to my brother, O brāhmaṇa.

**5.178.10**  "I belong to Śālva," she declared to me here long ago, O descendant of Bhṛgu; and granted leave by me, she went off toward the city of Saubha.

**5.178.11**  Neither out of fear, nor out of pity, nor out of greed, nor from desire of gain shall I abandon the kṣatriya's law; such is the vow I have laid upon myself.

**5.178.12**  Then Rāma, his eyes troubled with wrath, said to me: If you will not do as these words of mine require, O bull of the Kurus,

**5.178.13**  "I shall slay you this very day, ministers and all"—so again and again, in his fury, Rāma spoke, his eyes troubled with wrath.

**5.178.14**  With pleasing words I again and again entreated that subduer of foes, that tiger of the Bhṛgus; yet he would not be appeased.

**5.178.15**  Bowing my head once more to that best of brāhmaṇas, I said: What is the reason for which you wish to fight with me here?

**5.178.16**  The fourfold science of missile and weapon was taught to me, when I was a boy, by you yourself, O mighty-armed one; I am your pupil, O descendant of Bhṛgu.

**5.178.17**  Then Rāma, his eyes red with anger, said to me: You know me to be your teacher, Bhīṣma, and yet you will not take back this daughter of the king of Kāśi to please me, O Kaurava, O lord of the earth?

**5.178.18**  For in no other way is there peace for you, O joy of the Kurus; take her, mighty-armed one, and preserve your own line. For she, made to fall by you, can win no husband.

**5.178.19**  To Rāma, conqueror of hostile cities, as he spoke thus, I replied: "This shall not come to pass, O brahmin-sage; why do you trouble yourself?"

**5.178.20**  Looking upon your ancient standing as my teacher, O son of Jamadagni, I seek your favor, O blessed one; for she was renounced by me long ago.

**5.178.21**  For who, knowing the grievous faults that ruin women, would ever house a woman whose heart belongs to another, one who dwells like a serpent within his home?

**5.178.22**  Not even out of fear of Vāsava would I abandon my duty, O greatly radiant one. Be gracious, or else do without delay whatever you intend.

**5.178.23**  And this verse too, O pure-souled and mighty lord, is heard in the ancient lore, sung by the great-minded, great-souled Marutta:

**5.178.24**  "Even a teacher who is arrogant, who knows not right action from wrong, who has strayed onto a crooked path—him it is proper to chastise."

**5.178.25**  You, deemed my teacher, I have honored exceedingly out of affection; yet you know not the conduct befitting a teacher—therefore I shall fight with you.

**5.178.26**  I would not slay a teacher in battle, and least of all a brahmin, and most of all one grown great in austerity—thus have I borne with you.

**5.178.27**  But when a brahmin, like a kinsman of the warrior caste, is seen with arrow drawn, whoever in wrath should slay him in battle as he fights and does not flee—upon him no guilt of brahmin-slaughter falls: such is the settled rule in the sacred laws.

**5.178.28**  I am a kṣatriya, abiding in the duty of kṣatriyas, O you whose wealth is penance. The man who deals with another just as that other deals with him incurs no unrighteousness and attains what is best.

**5.178.29**  He who is competent in matters of gain or of duty, who knows place and time, even when fallen into doubt and adversity, fares better than one who acts without due reflection.

**5.178.30**  Since in this doubtful matter you proceed according to your own notion of justice, therefore I shall join battle with you, Rāma, in this great fight. Behold the might of my arms and my more-than-human valor.

**5.178.31**  Even with things come to this pass, O delight of the Bhṛgus, I shall do what lies in my power. On Kurukṣetra I shall fight with you, O brahmin; in single combat, Rāma, as you wish—make ready, O great sage.

**5.178.32**  There, Rāma, slain by me and bristling with a hundred shafts, purified by weapons in that great battle, you shall win the worlds you have conquered.

**5.178.33**  So go now, turn back toward Kurukṣetra, O lover of battle. There I shall come to you for combat, O mighty-armed one whose wealth is penance.

**5.178.34**  And in that very place where once, Rāma, you performed the purifying rite for your father, there too, having slain you, I shall perform the purifying rite, O Bhārgava.

**5.178.35**  Go there, Rāma, in haste, O you maddened for battle; I shall strip away your old pride, you who are a brahmin but in name.

**5.178.36**  And as for your repeated boast in assemblies, Rāma—'By me alone were the kṣatriyas of the world subdued'—hear now my answer to that.

**5.178.37**  In those days there was born no Bhīṣma, nor any kṣatriya of my measure, who could have destroyed your battle-pride and your desire.

**5.178.38**  But now that Bhīṣma is born, O mighty-armed one, conqueror of hostile cities—I shall undo your pride in battle, Rāma, of that there is no doubt.

**5.179.1**  Bhīṣma said: Then Rāma, as if laughing, said to me, O Bhārata: "By good fortune, Bhīṣma, you wish to do battle with me in the field."

**5.179.2**  "Here I go, O Kaurava, to Kurukṣetra together with you; I shall make good my word. Come there, O scorcher of foes."

**5.179.3**  "There may your mother Jāhnavī behold you slain by me, Bhīṣma, bristling with a hundred shafts, made food for vultures, herons, and crows."

**5.179.4**  "Gazing piteously upon you slain by me, let the goddess, attended by Siddhas and Cāraṇas, weep this day, O prince."

**5.179.5**  "Undeserving of such grief is that blessed river, daughter of Bhagīratha, who bore you—a fool, sick with the lust for war."

**5.179.6**  "Come, go with me, Bhīṣma; let the battle take place this very day. Take up all your gear—chariot and the rest—O Kaurava, bull of the Bharatas."

**5.179.7**  To Rāma, conqueror of hostile cities, as he spoke thus, I bowed my head, O king, and said, "So be it."

**5.179.8**  Having said this, Rāma set off for Kurukṣetra eager to fight; and I, entering the city, made it known to Satyavatī.

**5.179.9**  Then, the rites of blessing performed and welcomed by my mother, having had the twice-born pronounce the auspicious day and the benediction, O greatly radiant one—

**5.179.10**  —mounting a gleaming silver chariot drawn by white horses, well equipped, sturdily framed, draped about with tiger-skins,

**5.179.11**  furnished with great weapons and provided with every appurtenance, O king—that chariot, with a hero of noble birth, learned in the science of horses,

**5.179.12**  yoked with a well-bred charioteer many times proven in action—I, mailed in white armor that lent splendor to my frame,

**5.179.13**  taking up a white bow, set forth, O best of the Bharatas, with a white parasol held above my head,

**5.179.14**  fanned with white yak-tail whisks, O lord of men, clad in white, turbaned in white, adorned with ornaments all of white,

**5.179.15**  hymned with blessings of victory, I went forth from the City named for the Elephant and came to Kurukṣetra, the field of battle, O bull of the Bharatas.

**5.179.16**  Urged on by that charioteer toward the great fight, those horses, swift as thought and wind, bore me swiftly onward, O king.

**5.179.17**  Having reached Kurukṣetra, both I and the valorous Rāma at once advanced against each other for combat, O king.

**5.179.18**  Then I took my stand within sight of Rāma, that supreme ascetic, and taking up my finest conch, I blew upon it a mighty blast.

**5.179.19**  Then, O king, the twice-born and the ascetics dwelling in the forest, and the gods with their hosts of seers, looked on at that wondrous combat.

**5.179.20**  Then again and again heavenly garlands appeared, and heavenly music sounded, and gathered banks of cloud.

**5.179.21**  Then all those ascetics, the followers of the Bhārgava, became onlookers, ringing about the field of battle.

**5.179.22**  Then the goddess, my mother, who seeks the welfare of all beings, appearing in her own form, said to me, O king: "What is this you intend to do?"

**5.179.23**  "I shall go to the son of Jamadagni and entreat him, O upholder of the Kurus, again and again: 'Do not fight with Bhīṣma, who is your pupil.'"

**5.179.24**  "Do not, do not, my son, persist thus in fighting in battle with a brahmin, the son of Jamadagni"—so she reproached me.

**5.179.25**  "Is not Rāma known to you, my son—the slayer of kṣatriyas, whose valor equals Hara's—that you wish to do battle with him?"

**5.179.26**  Then, saluting the goddess with joined palms, I told her all, O best of the Bharatas—everything that had befallen at the bride-choosing,

**5.179.27**  and how Rāma had earlier been entreated by me, O king of kings, and what the long-standing desire of the daughter of the king of Kāśi had been.

**5.179.28**  Then my mother, the great river-goddess, went up to Rāma and for my sake sought to appease that seer, the Bhārgava, saying, "Do not fight with Bhīṣma, who is your pupil."

**5.179.29**  But he said to her as she pleaded, "Turn Bhīṣma back instead, for he will not do my will"—and so I came up against him.

**5.179.30**  Saṃjaya said: Then Gaṅgā, out of love for her son, approached Bhīṣma once more; but he, his eyes troubled with wrath, would not heed her word.

**5.179.31**  Then the righteous one, foremost of the Bhṛgus, great in austerity, came into view, and that best of the twice-born once again summoned him to battle.

**5.180.1**  Bhīṣma said: As he stood ready in the field, I answered him, smiling, as it were: "Standing on the ground, I cannot bring myself to fight you while I am mounted upon a chariot."

**5.180.2**  "Mount a chariot, O hero, and gird on armor, O mighty-armed Rāma, if you wish to do battle with me in the field."

**5.180.3**  Then Rāma said to me, smiling on the field of battle: "The earth is my chariot, Bhīṣma; the Vedas are my horses, like fine steeds."

**5.180.4**  "The Wind is my charioteer, and the mothers of the Vedas are my armor; well sheathed in these, I shall fight in the field, O delight of the Kurus."

**5.180.5**  Speaking thus, O Gāndhāra, Rāma of unfailing valor enveloped me on every side with a vast multitude of arrows.

**5.180.6**  Then I beheld the son of Jamadagni stationed upon a heavenly chariot, bearing every weapon, splendid, of a wondrous aspect beyond compare,

**5.180.7**  fashioned by thought, holy, spacious as a city, yoked with heavenly steeds, fully arrayed and adorned with gold,

**5.180.8**  and with a banner, O mighty-armed one, marked by the emblem of the moon. A bowman, his quiver bound on, his lizard-skin guard and finger-guards fastened—

**5.180.9**  and there Akṛtavraṇa, the friend of the Bhārgava, knower of the Vedas and exceedingly dear to him, served as charioteer for the warrior eager to fight.

**5.180.10**  As he summoned me to battle, the Bhārgava seemed to gladden my heart, crying out again and again, "Come on, advance!"

**5.180.11**  That Rāma—like the rising sun, unassailable, of mighty strength, the ender of the kṣatriyas—I, alone, advanced upon, alone.

**5.180.12**  Then, at a distance of three arrow-falls, I reined in my horses, and descending and laying aside my bow, I went on foot toward that best of seers,

**5.180.13**  approaching Rāma, that best of the twice-born, to do him honor; and saluting him in due form, I spoke these noble words:

**5.180.14**  "I shall fight with you in battle, Rāma—with one greater and superior, a teacher of righteous conduct. Wish me victory, O lord."

**5.180.15**  Rāma said: "Even so it is, O best of the Kurus; thus should one act who desires prosperity. For this is the duty, O mighty-armed one, of those who fight with their superiors."

**5.180.16**  "I would have cursed you had you not come thus, O lord of the people. Fight on, then, in the field, intent and steadfast, O Kaurava."

**5.180.17**  "Yet I do not wish you victory, for I am here to conquer you. Go, fight by the rule of righteousness; I am pleased with your conduct."

**5.180.18**  Bhīṣma said: Then, bowing to him, I quickly mounted my chariot and once more blew upon my gold-adorned conch in the field.

**5.180.19**  Then battle was joined between me and him, O Bhārata, for very many days, O king, each seeking to vanquish the other.

**5.180.20**  In that combat he struck me first with shafts fledged with heron-feathers—nine hundred and sixty arrows blazing like fire.

**5.180.21**  By them my four horses and my charioteer too were checked, O lord of the people; yet I stood firm in the fray, clad in mail.

**5.180.22**  And bowing to the gods and to the brahmins, O Bhārata, I answered him, smiling, as it were, as he stood ready in the field:

**5.180.23**  "Your teacherhood has been honored by me, even though you have overstepped all bounds; yet hear from me further, O brahmin, what is fitting in the gathering of righteousness."

**5.180.24**  "The Vedas that dwell within your body, your great brahmin-hood, and the mighty austerity you have practiced—at these I do not strike."

**5.180.25**  "I strike at the warrior's office, which you, Rāma, have assumed; for a brahmin passes into the state of a kṣatriya by the lifting of weapons."

**5.180.26**  "Behold the prowess of my bow, behold the strength of my arms. Lo, hero, I split your bow in two, arrow and all."

**5.180.27**  I let fly at him a sharp broad-headed shaft, O bull of the Bharatas; by it the tip of his bow was severed and fell to the ground.

**5.180.28**  And nine hundred arrows, straight-jointed and fledged with heron-feathers, I sent flying at the chariot of the son of Jamadagni.

**5.180.29**  Lodged then in his body and stirred by the wind, those arrows quivered, dripping blood, like serpents.

**5.180.30**  His every limb drenched in blood, streaming gore from his wounds, Rāma shone then, O king, like Mount Meru pouring forth its mineral ores.

**5.180.31**  Like an aśoka tree at winter's end adorned with clusters of red blossoms, Rāma shone then, O king, in places resembling a kiṃśuka in flower.

**5.180.32**  Then, taking up another bow, Rāma, filled with wrath, rained down upon me arrows keenly sharpened and fletched with gold.

**5.180.33**  Reaching me, those fearsome shafts, piercing my vital parts in many places, made me tremble — of mighty speed, like serpents, fire, and poison.

**5.180.34**  Then I, steadying myself once more in the battle, in my wrath covered Rāma with arrows by the hundred.

**5.180.35**  Sorely afflicted by those sharp arrows, blazing like fire and the sun and like venomous snakes, Rāma seemed to grow faint of mind.

**5.180.36**  Then, overcome with pity, reproaching myself within myself, I cried, 'Shame, shame upon this battle and upon the warrior's calling!' O bull of the Bharatas.

**5.180.37**  And again and again I said, O king, swept away by the surge of grief: 'Alas, what an evil deed I have done through this warrior's office!'

**5.180.38**  'My teacher, a brāhmaṇa, a man of righteous soul, is thus tormented by my arrows!' — and so I struck no longer at the son of Jamadagni, O Bhārata.

**5.180.39**  Then, having scorched the earth, the thousand-rayed Pūṣan, the sun, went down to his setting as the day waned, and with that the fighting ceased.

**5.181.1**  Bhīṣma said: Then, O lord of peoples, my charioteer, esteemed for his skill, drew out the arrowheads from himself, from the horses, and from me.

**5.181.2**  When dawn broke and the sun had risen, with the horses bathed, rested, watered, and unwearied, the battle began again.

**5.181.3**  Seeing me come swiftly on, armored and standing in my chariot, the valiant Rāma made his own car fully ready for the fray.

**5.181.4**  Then I, seeing Rāma advancing, eager for combat, set aside my excellent bow and at once stepped down from my chariot.

**5.181.5**  Having saluted him, I mounted my car again, O Bhārata, and, eager to fight and free of fear, took my stand before the son of Jamadagni.

**5.181.6**  Then he covered me with a great downpour of arrows, and I in turn covered him, as he rained his shafts, with a downpour of my own.

**5.181.7**  But the son of Jamadagni, full of wrath, once more sent winged shafts against me, O king, like serpents with blazing mouths.

**5.181.8**  Those I cut down with sharp broad-headed arrows, by hundreds and by thousands, O king, cleaving them in the air again and again.

**5.181.9**  Then the valiant son of Jamadagni loosed celestial weapons against me, and these I warded off.

**5.181.10**  With weapon meeting weapon, O mighty-armed one, as he sought to gain the upper hand, a great roar arose in the heavens on every side.

**5.181.11**  Then I employed the wind-weapon against the son of Jamadagni, but Rāma struck it back with the weapon of the Guhyakas, O Bhārata.

**5.181.12**  Then I, with proper incantation, sent forth the fire-weapon, but the mighty Rāma turned mine aside with the weapon of Varuṇa.

**5.181.13**  Thus I warded off Rāma's celestial weapons, and Rāma too — blazing, knower of divine arms, subduer of foes — warded off mine.

**5.181.14**  Then, turning me to his left, O king, that foremost of brāhmaṇas, the mighty son of Jamadagni, in his fury pierced me in the chest.

**5.181.15**  Then I, O best of the Bharatas, sank down upon my noble car, and the charioteer, seeing me overcome with faintness and sorely afflicted by Rāma's arrow, swiftly bore me away a krośa's distance, O best of the Bharatas.

**5.181.16**  Then, seeing me borne off, grievously wounded and senseless, Rāma's followers — Akṛtavraṇa foremost among them, and the maiden of Kāśī — all rejoiced and raised a shout, O Bhārata.

**5.181.17**  Then, when I had regained my senses, I knew my situation and said to the charioteer: 'Drive on, charioteer, to where Rāma is; I am ready, my pain has passed.'

**5.181.18**  Then the charioteer bore me on with steeds most splendid, as if dancing in their stride, O Kaurava, and equal to the wind in their swiftness.

**5.181.19**  Then, reaching Rāma, O Kaurava, in great agitation I covered that agitated foe with a net of arrows, longing for victory.

**5.181.20**  But as those straight-flying arrows came on, Rāma swiftly cut each single one with three arrows of his own in that battle.

**5.181.21**  Then all those well-sharpened arrows of mine were shattered, cleft in two by Rāma's shafts, by the hundred in that great battle.

**5.181.22**  Then again I loosed at the son of Jamadagni, at Rāma, a blazing, bright shaft equal to Death itself, seeking to slay him.

**5.181.23**  Struck hard by it and brought under the power of that arrow's stroke, Rāma suddenly swooned and fell to the ground.

**5.181.24**  Then, when Rāma had sunk to the earth, all the world cried out in dismay, and the whole creation grew troubled, O Bhārata, as if at the falling of the sun.

**5.181.25**  Then, deeply alarmed, all those ascetics rich in penance, together with the maiden of Kāśī, rushed at once toward the delight of the Bhṛgus.

**5.181.26**  Embracing him, they gently revived him then with hands cooled by water and with blessings for his victory, O Kaurava.

**5.181.27**  Then Rāma, still reeling, rose and spoke to me, fitting an arrow to his bow: 'Stand fast, Bhīṣma — you are as good as slain!'

**5.181.28**  Loosed, it fell swiftly upon my left side in that great battle, by which I was sorely shaken, reeling like a tree about to topple.

**5.181.29**  Then, having slain my horses in that great battle with his swift-weapon, O king, he confidently covered me with arrows fletched with feathers.

**5.181.30**  Then I too loosed in battle the swift-weapon that none could withstand, O mighty-armed one; and the arrows, Rāma's and mine alike, swiftly met and held the space between us, veiling the whole sky on every side.

**5.181.31**  The sun could not shine through, veiled by that web of arrows; and the wind, caught in that space between, roared as though pent in by clouds.

**5.181.32**  Then from the churning of the wind, from the rays of the sun, from the scorching heat, and from their own nature, the arrows kindled into fire.

**5.181.33**  Those arrows, set ablaze by the fire risen from themselves, then all fell to the earth, O king, reduced to ashes.

**5.181.34**  Then by hundreds of thousands, by millions and tens of millions, by ten-thousands, by hundred-billions and thousand-billions, O Kaurava, Rāma in his wrath swiftly rained his arrows down upon me.

**5.181.35**  Then I too, with arrows like venomous serpents, cut those down in battle, O king, and brought them to earth like so many snakes.

**5.181.36**  Thus did that battle go, O best of the Bharatas; and when the hour of twilight had passed, my teacher withdrew.

**5.182.1**  Bhīṣma said: When I had joined battle with Rāma once more, the next day there was again a fight most terrible and tumultuous, O best of the Bharatas.

**5.182.2**  Then the hero, knower of celestial weapons, that righteous and mighty lord, deployed divine arms in great number, day after day.

**5.182.3**  Those weapons I scattered with counter-weapons, weapon against weapon, O Bhārata, in that tumultuous battle, having set aside this life so hard to give up.

**5.182.4**  When his weapons had been struck down again and again by mine, the Bhārgava of mighty splendor grew wrathful, having himself cast away all care for life in that combat.

**5.182.5**  Then the great-souled son of Jamadagni, his weapons blocked, hurled a spear of fearsome form, blazing like a meteor loosed by Time, its point aflame, wrapping the worlds in its glare.

**5.182.6**  Then, as it came on, blazing like the sun at the world's end, I cut it with flaming arrows into three pieces and brought it down to earth; whereupon a wind blew, fragrant and holy.

**5.182.7**  When it was cut down, Rāma, ablaze with anger, hurled twelve more fearsome spears; their form, O Bhārata, could not be told, so dazzling were they and so swift.

**5.182.8**  Indeed I was bewildered to behold them, from every quarter, like great meteors of fire, of varied form, blazing with fierce splendor, like the twelve suns at the world's dissolution.

**5.182.9**  Then, spreading out a net made of arrows, sighting them and piercing them with that net of shafts, O king, I loosed twelve arrows in the battle, and so beat down those spears of fearsome form.

**5.182.10**  Then the great-souled son of Jamadagni flung yet other fearsome spears with golden shafts, richly wrought and bound with bands of gold, blazing each like a great meteor.

**5.182.11**  Those fierce spears too I warded off with my shield and struck down in battle with my sword, O lord of men; and with celestial arrows I rained upon the son of Jamadagni's divine horses and his charioteer.

**5.182.12**  Seeing his gold-wrought spears, like serpents new-sloughed of their skins, cut to pieces, the great-souled crusher of the Haihaya lord, seized by wrath, brought forth a celestial weapon.

**5.182.13**  Then ranks of blazing arrows came on, fierce as swarms of locusts, and densely covered my body, my horses, my charioteer, and my whole chariot.

**5.182.14**  My chariot was thickly studded with arrows on every side, and so too the horses and the charioteer, O king; the yoke, the pole, and likewise the wheels, and the axle as well, were cut by the arrows and broke.

**5.182.15**  Then, when that downpour of arrows had passed, I rained back a flood of shafts upon my teacher; and he, that mass of brahminic power, wounded by my missiles, poured ceaselessly much blood from his body.

**5.182.16**  As Rāma was sorely tormented by my web of arrows, so was I most grievously and deeply wounded; then the battle ceased in the late afternoon, as the sun sought the mountain of its setting.

**5.183.1**  Bhīṣma said: Then at dawn, O lord of kings, when the sun had risen clear, the battle between the Bhārgava and me began once more.

**5.183.2**  Then, standing in his wheeling chariot, Rāma, best of warriors, rained showers of arrows upon me, as Śakra rains upon a mountain.

**5.183.3**  Struck by that downpour of arrows, my charioteer, who was my friend, fell to the floor of the chariot, casting my heart into grief.

**5.183.4**  Then that charioteer of mine, overcome by deep and great faintness, fell to the ground from the blows of the arrows and swooned away.

**5.183.5**  Then the charioteer, sorely afflicted by Rāma's arrows, gave up his life within a moment, O lord of kings; and fear entered into me then.

**5.183.6**  Then, when my charioteer had been slain, O king, as I, distracted in mind, was loosing my arrows, Rāma sent against me shafts that were equal to Death.

**5.183.7**  Then, while I was distraught at the loss of my charioteer and in disarray, the Bhārgava, drawing his powerful bow, struck me hard with an arrow.

**5.183.8**  It fell, O king, between my collarbones — that blood-drinking shaft — and along with me, O lord of kings, it went down to the surface of the earth.

**5.183.9**  Believing me slain, then, O bull of the Bharatas, Rāma roared aloud like a thundercloud and rejoiced again and again.

**5.183.10**  And when I had fallen thus, O king, Rāma, filled with joy, raised a great shout together with those his followers.

**5.183.11**  But the Kauravas who were there, standing at my side, and the people who had come, eager to behold that battle, fell into utmost anguish when I was struck down.

**5.183.12**  Then, fallen, O lion among kings, I beheld eight brāhmaṇas, radiant as the sun and fire; they stood surrounding me on every side and, in the midst of the battlefield, upheld me with their own arms.

**5.183.13**  Guarded by those brāhmaṇas, I touched not the ground but remained held aloft in the air by those brāhmaṇas as by kinsmen, as if sleeping in mid-air, sprinkled with drops of water.

**5.183.14**  Then those brāhmaṇas, O king, holding me up, all said together, 'Fear not,' and again and again, 'May it be well with you.'

**5.183.15**  Then, refreshed by their words, I rose up at once and saw my mother, the best of rivers, standing in my chariot.

**5.183.16**  And my horses had been held in check by her, the great river, in the midst of the battle, O lord of the Kurus; and I, having honored my mother's feet and likewise Ārṣṭiṣeṇa, mounted my chariot.

**5.183.17**  She had guarded my chariot, my horses, and all my gear; and I, with palms joined in reverence, then took my leave of her again.

**5.183.18**  Then I myself, taking up the reins of those wind-swift horses, fought on against the son of Jamadagni as the day waned, O Bhārata.

**5.183.19**  Then, O best of the Bharatas, in that battle I loosed against Rāma a swift and mighty arrow that would cleave his heart.

**5.183.20**  Then Rāma, sorely afflicted by the arrow's force, sank to the earth upon his knees, let his bow fall, and passed into a swoon.

**5.183.21**  And when Rāma, giver of many thousand gifts, had fallen, the rain-clouds veiled the sky, pouring down blood in abundance.

**5.183.22**  Meteors fell by the hundred, with thunderclaps and tremors, and Svarbhānu suddenly swallowed up the blazing sun.

**5.183.23**  Harsh winds blew, the earth itself shook, and vultures, baḍa-birds, and herons wheeled down, filled with glee.

**5.183.24**  In a quarter of the sky that blazed, a jackal howled grimly again and again, and kettledrums, untouched by any hand, resounded with a deafening din.

**5.183.25**  Such was the dreadful array of portents, O most excellent of the Bharatas, when the great-souled Rāma, well-nigh senseless, had sunk to the ground.

**5.183.26**  Then the sun, its orb of rays grown dim and sunk deep within heaped clouds of dust, went down to its setting; night drew on with its pleasant, cooling breeze, and so we two withdrew from battle.

**5.183.27**  Thus, O king, the withdrawal came about; and again at clear dawn the fearsome combat resumed, day after day for twenty days, and likewise for three days more.

**5.184.1**  Bhīṣma said: Then in the night, O lord of kings, bowing my head, I paid homage in full to the brāhmaṇas, to the ancestors, and to the gods.

**5.184.2**  And to the beings that roam by night and to the Night herself, O lord of peoples; then, reaching my couch in solitude, I pondered within my mind.

**5.184.3**  This grievous combat of mine with the son of Jamadagni has gone on now for very many days, and it bears the gravest peril.

**5.184.4**  And I am unable in the forefront of the fray to conquer Rāma of mighty valor, that brāhmaṇa son of Jamadagni, vast in strength.

**5.184.5**  If the resplendent son of Jamadagni can be vanquished by me, let the gracious gods reveal it to me this night.

**5.184.6**  Then in the night, O lord of kings, wounded by arrows, I fell asleep upon my right side, as the hour of dawn drew near.

**5.184.7**  Then by those very foremost of brāhmaṇas, by whom I had been raised when I fell from my chariot and upheld, I was comforted with the words, "Fear not."

**5.184.8**  Those same ones, O great king, came to me in a dream-vision, surrounded me, and spoke this word—mark it well, O scion of the Kurus.

**5.184.9**  "Arise, fear not, O son of Gaṅgā; there is no danger whatsoever for you. We guard you, O tiger among men, for you are to us as our own body."

**5.184.10**  "Rāma, the son of Jamadagni, shall in no way conquer you in battle; it is you who shall vanquish Rāma in the fray, O bull of the Bharatas."

**5.184.11**  "This well-beloved weapon you shall recognize anew, for it was known to you as well in your former bearing of a body."

**5.184.12**  "It is the weapon of Prajāpati, fashioned by Viśvakarman, called Prasvāpa, the Inducer of Sleep, O Bhārata; not even Rāma knows it, nor any man anywhere upon the earth."

**5.184.13**  "Call it to mind, O mighty-armed one, and fix it firmly to your bow; nor shall Rāma meet his end by that weapon, O ruler of men."

**5.184.14**  "Nor shall you ever incur any sin thereby, O giver of honor; the son of Jamadagni, oppressed by the force of your shaft, shall but fall asleep."

**5.184.15**  "Then, having vanquished him, you yourself shall raise him up again in the fray, O Bhīṣma, by the cherished weapon of Awakening."

**5.184.16**  "Do thus, O Kaurava, mounting your chariot at dawn; whether a man lie sleeping or dead, we deem the two to be alike."

**5.184.17**  "And Rāma must never die, O king; therefore let this Prasvāpa, which has now arisen to you, be put to use."

**5.184.18**  Having so spoken, O king, those most excellent of the twice-born vanished, all eight of them alike in form, all of radiant aspect.

**5.185.1**  Bhīṣma said: Then, when the night had passed, I awoke, O Bhārata; and recalling that dream, I was filled with the highest joy.

**5.185.2**  Then the battle began between him and me, O Bhārata—tumultuous, marvelous, and making the hair of all beings stand on end.

**5.185.3**  Then the Bhārgava rained upon me a downpour made of arrows, and I warded it off with a net of shafts, O Bhārata.

**5.185.4**  Then that great ascetic, wrought to the height of fury, with the same wrath as the day before, hurled a spear at me.

**5.185.5**  Its touch was like the thunderbolt of Indra, its splendor like the rod of Yama, blazing like fire in the fight and licking out flames on every side.

**5.185.6**  Then, O tiger of the Bharatas, like a blazing meteor coursing through the sky, that spear struck me swiftly upon the region of the shoulder, O Bhārata.

**5.185.7**  Then my blood flowed dreadfully, like the red-ochre ore from a mountainside, O mighty-armed one, where Rāma had wounded me, O you whose eyes are red as blood.

**5.185.8**  Then I, filled with vehement wrath, sent against the son of Jamadagni an arrow that resembled death itself and was like the venom of a serpent.

**5.185.9**  Struck thereby upon the forehead, that heroic and most excellent of the twice-born shone, O great king, like a mountain crowned with a peak.

**5.185.10**  In fury he rounded upon me, and drawing back with might a dreadful arrow, the destroyer of foes, like Time the all-ending, he fitted it to his bow.

**5.185.11**  That fierce shaft fell upon my breast, hissing like a serpent; and then, O king, I sank to the earth, drenched in blood.

**5.185.12**  But regaining my senses once more, I hurled at the wise son of Jamadagni a gleaming spear, blazing like the thunderbolt.

**5.185.13**  It fell upon the shoulder of that foremost of the twice-born; he grew faint, O king, and a trembling came over him.

**5.185.14**  Then his friend, the brāhmaṇa Akṛtavraṇa, great in austerity, embraced him and comforted him in many ways with gracious words.

**5.185.15**  Then Rāma, recovered and filled with wrath and indignation, that hero of mighty vows, brought forth the supreme weapon of Brahmā.

**5.185.16**  Then, to counter it, the same most excellent Brahmā weapon, loosed by me, blazed forth, showing as it were the end of an age.

**5.185.17**  Between those two Brahmā weapons there came a clash in mid-course, before either could reach Rāma or me, O most excellent of the Bharatas.

**5.185.18**  Then in the sky there appeared nothing but a sheer blaze of fire, and all beings fell into anguish, O lord of peoples.

**5.185.19**  The seers, together with the gandharvas and the gods, O Bhārata, fell into the utmost torment, oppressed by the fiery splendor of those weapons.

**5.185.20**  Then the earth, with its mountains, forests, and trees, trembled, and the scorched creatures fell into the deepest despair.

**5.185.21**  The firmament blazed, O king, and the ten quarters were wreathed in smoke; nor could the beings that course the heavens hold their place in the sky.

**5.185.22**  Then, while the world with its gods, asuras, and rākṣasas cried out in dismay, I, thinking, "This is the moment," resolved to employ the weapon, O Bhārata.

**5.185.23**  The cherished Prasvāpa weapon, by the word of those expounders of the Veda; and that weapon, when I called it to mind, shone forth within my thought.

**5.186.1**  Bhīṣma said: Then a great clamor of "Halā! Halā!" arose in the heavens, O king: "Bhīṣma, loose not the Prasvāpa!"—so it cried, O delight of the Kurus.

**5.186.2**  Yet I was fixing that weapon upon the descendant of Bhṛgu; and as I was setting the Prasvāpa, Nārada spoke these words to me.

**5.186.3**  "Behold, O Kaurava, the hosts of the gods stand yonder in the sky; they restrain you this day—do not employ the Prasvāpa."

**5.186.4**  "Rāma is an ascetic, devoted to the brāhmaṇas, himself a brāhmaṇa, and your preceptor; do him no dishonor in any way, O Kaurava."

**5.186.5**  Then I beheld those eight expounders of the Veda standing in the heavens; smiling upon me, O lord of kings, they gently spoke this word.

**5.186.6**  "As Nārada bids you, O best of the Bharatas, so do; for this is the highest good of the worlds, O bull of the Bharatas."

**5.186.7**  Then, withdrawing that sleep-bringing weapon in the fray, I kindled forth the Brahmā weapon in that battle, according to the rite.

**5.186.8**  Then Rāma, O prince, was enraged; seeing that weapon withdrawn, he suddenly let fall these words: "I, dull of wit, am conquered by Bhīṣma!"

**5.186.9**  Then the son of Jamadagni saw his father, and his father's father, and yet another beyond him; they stood encircling him and spoke to him then with words of comfort.

**5.186.10**  "Never again, dear child, commit such a rash deed in any way—to enter into combat with Bhīṣma, and a kṣatriya at that."

**5.186.11**  "For battle is the duty of a kṣatriya, O delight of the Bhṛgus; but the study of scripture and the practice of vows are the supreme wealth of brāhmaṇas."

**5.186.12**  "For some particular cause this most fierce bearing of arms was once enjoined upon you by us, and that task you have indeed accomplished."

**5.186.13**  "Dear child, enough of this; your clash in combat with Bhīṣma is sufficient, O mighty-armed one—withdraw now from the battle here."

**5.186.14**  "Enough is this bearing of the bow by you—blessings upon you; lay it aside, O unassailable one, and devote yourself to austerity, O Bhārgava."

**5.186.15**  "This Bhīṣma, son of Śaṃtanu, has been restrained by all the gods, who urged him, 'Withdraw from this battle.'"

**5.186.16**  "'Do not fight with Rāma, your preceptor,' they said again and again, 'for Rāma ought not in justice to be vanquished by you in battle, O scion of the Kurus; show honor, O son of Gaṅgā, to the brāhmaṇa upon the field of war.'"

**5.186.17**  "And we are your elders; therefore we restrain you. Bhīṣma is one among the Vasus; by good fortune you yet live, O little son."

**5.186.18**  "This son of Gaṅgā, the son of Śaṃtanu, is a Vasu of great renown; how could he be vanquished by you in battle, O Rāma? Withdraw, then."

**5.186.19**  "Arjuna, the best of the Pāṇḍavas, the mighty son of Purandara—he is Nara, the heroic Prajāpati, the ancient god, the everlasting one."

**5.186.20**  "Renowned through the three worlds as Savyasācin, valiant—he has been ordained by the Self-existent as the death of Bhīṣma when his time shall come."

**5.186.21**  Thus addressed by his forefathers, Rāma spoke this to them: "I will not turn back in battle—such is the vow I have laid upon myself."

**5.186.22**  "Never before have I turned back in the forefront of battle. Let the son of the river-goddess be turned from the fight if you will, O grandsires; but I shall in no way withdraw from this combat."

**5.186.23**  Then those sages, O king, with Ṛcīka at their head, together with Nārada, drew near and spoke this word.

**5.186.24**  "Withdraw from the battle, dear child; show honor to these most excellent of the twice-born." But I said "No" to them, out of regard for the duty of a kṣatriya.

**5.186.25**  "This is my vow before the world: never in any way will I turn my back upon battle and withdraw, to be struck with arrows from behind."

**5.186.26**  "Not out of greed, not out of meanness, not out of fear, nor for any gain's sake would I forsake the eternal law—such is my settled resolve."

**5.186.27**  Then all those sages, with Nārada at their head, O king, and Bhāgīrathī, my mother, came into the midst of the battlefield.

**5.186.28**  Still I stood, my arrow taken up, bow in hand, my resolve as firm as ever, ready to fight in the fray; then they, gathering together once more, spoke to Rāma, the descendant of Bhṛgu, upon the field of battle.

**5.186.29**  "The hearts of brāhmaṇas are soft as fresh butter; be at peace, O Bhārgava. Rāma, Rāma, withdraw from this battle, O best of the twice-born; for Bhīṣma is not to be slain by you, nor you by Bhīṣma, O Bhārgava."

**5.186.30**  Speaking thus, all those forefathers barred the field of battle and made the descendant of Bhṛgu lay down his weapon.

**5.186.31**  Then once more I beheld those eight expounders of the Veda, blazing like eight planets newly risen.

**5.186.32**  With affection they spoke this word to me as I stood in the battle: 'Approach Rāma, O mighty-armed one; honor your teacher and act for the welfare of the worlds.'

**5.186.33**  Seeing that Rāma had been turned back by the words of his well-wishers, I too, acting for the welfare of the worlds, took up that counsel.

**5.186.34**  Then, sorely wounded, I came up to Rāma and saluted him; and Rāma, that great ascetic, smiling upon me with affection, said:

**5.186.35**  'There is no warrior walking the earth equal to you in all this world. Go now, Bhīṣma; in this battle I have been greatly pleased by you.'

**5.186.36**  And in my very presence the descendant of Bhṛgu summoned that maiden and, in the midst of those ascetics, spoke to her in a sorrowful voice:

**5.187.1**  Rāma said: It is plain before the eyes of all the worlds, O fair lady, how with my utmost might I have performed great feats of prowess.

**5.187.2**  Yet even displaying my supreme weapons I am wholly unable in battle to gain the upper hand over Bhīṣma, the best of those who bear arms.

**5.187.3**  This is my utmost power, this my utmost strength. Go where you will, gentle one; what else can I do for you?

**5.187.4**  Turn to Bhīṣma himself for refuge; no other recourse is left to you. For I have been vanquished by Bhīṣma as he loosed his mighty weapons.

**5.187.5**  Bhīṣma said: Having spoken thus, the high-souled Rāma sighed and fell silent; then the maiden addressed the delight of the Bhṛgus:

**5.187.6**  'O blessed one, it is even so as your lordship has said: this Bhīṣma of noble mind is unconquerable in battle, even by the gods.'

**5.187.7**  'You have done my work to the full measure of your power and zeal, withholding in the fight neither your valor nor your manifold weapons.'

**5.187.8**  'Yet in the end he cannot be overcome in battle; and never again, by any means, will I go to this Bhīṣma.'

**5.187.9**  'But I shall go there, O treasury of austerity, whence I may myself fell Bhīṣma in battle, O foremost of the Bhṛgus.'

**5.187.10**  Having spoken thus, the maiden departed, her eyes troubled with wrath, her resolve fixed upon austerity, brooding on my death.

**5.187.11**  Then the best of the Bhṛgus, Rāma, together with those sages, took his leave of me and returned to Mahendra as he had come, O Bhārata.

**5.187.12**  Then, mounting my chariot and praised by the twice-born, I entered the city and reported to my mother Satyavatī all that had passed, O great king; and she rejoiced over me.

**5.187.13**  And I appointed wise men to the task of watching the maiden's doings; day by day they, set diligently upon my good and welfare, reported to me her goings, her words, and her acts.

**5.187.14**  From the very moment that maiden went to the forest, bent upon austerity, I became troubled and downcast, as though my senses had fled.

**5.187.15**  For no warrior could conquer me in battle by valor, my son, save one knowing the Veda, made keen by austerity and firm in his vow.

**5.187.16**  And this too, O king, I disclosed out of fear to Nārada and to Vyāsa, that the deed might be averted; and both of them said to me:

**5.187.17**  'You must not grieve, Bhīṣma, on account of the daughter of Kāśi. Who could venture by human effort to turn back fate?'

**5.187.18**  But that maiden, O great king, entering the circle of hermitages and taking her stand on the bank of the Yamunā, practiced superhuman austerity.

**5.187.19**  Without food, gaunt, withered, her hair matted, caked with dirt, that ascetic for six months lived on air alone, motionless as a post.

**5.187.20**  Reaching the bank of the Yamunā, the fair lady passed yet another full year standing in the water, taking no food at all.

**5.187.21**  And, fierce in her wrath, she passed yet another year upon a single withered leaf, poised upon the tips of her toes.

**5.187.22**  Thus for twelve years she scorched heaven and earth; and though her kinsmen sought to turn her back, she could by no means be dissuaded.

**5.187.23**  Then she went to the land of the Vatsas, frequented by Siddhas and Cāraṇas, to the hermitage of the high-souled ascetics of holy conduct.

**5.187.24**  There, in those holy regions, the daughter of Kāśi, her limbs bathed in the sacred waters, wandered day and night, roaming at her own will.

**5.187.25**  At the hermitage of Nanda, O great king, and then at the fair hermitage of Ulūka, and at the hermitage of Cyavana, and at the abode of Brahmā;

**5.187.26**  at Prayāga, the sacrificial ground of the gods, and in the forests of the gods, and in Bhogavatī, O king, and likewise at the hermitage of Kauśika;

**5.187.27**  at the hermitage of Māṇḍavya, O king, and at the hermitage of Dilīpa, and at the pool of Rāma, O Kaurava, and at the hermitage of Paila and Gārgya—

**5.187.28**  in all these holy fords, O lord of peoples, did the daughter of Kāśi then bathe her limbs, undertaking the fiercest austerity.

**5.187.29**  My mother, O scion of Kuru, rising up from the water, said to her: 'Why do you torment yourself, gentle one? Tell me the truth of this.'

**5.187.30**  Then she, the blameless one, with hands folded in reverence, answered her, O king: 'Bhīṣma was not conquered by Rāma in battle, O fair-eyed one.'

**5.187.31**  'What other could venture to conquer that lord of earth with arrow raised? Therefore I shall practice the most terrible austerity for the destruction of Bhīṣma.'

**5.187.32**  'I wander the earth, O goddess, that I may slay that king; let the fruit of this vow be mine in another body.'

**5.187.33**  Then the river that goes to the ocean said: 'You walk a crooked path, fair lady. This desire, O flawless of limb, you cannot attain, O weak one.'

**5.187.34**  'If, O daughter of Kāśi, you keep this vow for the destruction of Bhīṣma, and if while keeping the vow you shall give up your body, then you shall become a river, O fair one—crooked, watered only in the rains;'

**5.187.35**  'hard to ford and trackless, flowing in the rains but dry the eight months, full of fearsome crocodiles, dreadful, a terror to all creatures.'

**5.187.36**  Having spoken thus to the daughter of Kāśi, O king, my illustrious mother, the fair lady, turned back as if smiling.

**5.187.37**  Sometimes in the eighth month, sometimes in the tenth, that maiden of fair complexion would again not so much as taste water.

**5.187.38**  That daughter of the lord of Kāśi, O Kaurava, in her craving for holy fords, kept rushing here and there over the land of the Vatsas, again and again falling and running on.

**5.187.39**  And in the land of the Vatsas that river became renowned as Ambā, O Bhārata—watered in the rains, teeming with crocodiles, hard to ford, and winding.

**5.187.40**  By that austerity the maiden was born again from half of herself: she became both a river among the Vatsas, O king, and yet remained a maiden as well.

**5.188.1**  Bhīṣma said: Then all those ascetics, seeing her fixed in her resolve upon austerity, sought to turn her back, my son, and asked: 'What is your purpose?'

**5.188.2**  Then the maiden answered those seers grown old in austerity: 'I have been spurned by Bhīṣma, cast out from the estate of a wife.'

**5.188.3**  'My vow is for his slaying, not for any heavenly world, O treasuries of austerity. When I have slain Bhīṣma I shall find peace—such is my resolve.'

**5.188.4**  'On whose account I have entered upon this everlasting abode of sorrow, bereft of the world of a husband, neither woman nor man am I here.'

**5.188.5**  'Without slaying the son of Gaṅgā in battle I shall not turn back, O treasuries of austerity. This is the resolve in my heart, the very end for which this effort is undertaken.'

**5.188.6**  'Wearied utterly of womanhood, resolved upon manhood, I would requite myself upon Bhīṣma; I am not to be turned aside'—so she said again.

**5.188.7**  To that fair lady the god who bears the trident, the consort of Umā, showed himself in his own form, in the midst of those great seers.

**5.188.8**  Offered a boon, she chose my defeat; and the god answered that resolute woman: 'You shall slay him.'

**5.188.9**  Then once again the maiden spoke to Rudra: 'How, O god, can victory in battle be mine, a woman? And by my womanly nature my mind is wholly stilled, O consort of Umā.'

**5.188.10**  'The defeat of Bhīṣma has been promised by you, O lord of beings; so act, O bull-bannered one, that it may come true—that I, meeting Bhīṣma the son of Śaṃtanu in battle, may slay him.'

**5.188.11**  To that maiden Mahādeva, the bull-bannered, replied: 'My word, gentle one, speaks no untruth; it shall come true.'

**5.188.12**  'You shall slay Bhīṣma in battle and shall obtain manhood; and when you have passed into another body you shall remember all of this.'

**5.188.13**  'Born in the line of Drupada, you shall become a great chariot-warrior; swift with weapons, skilled in varied modes of fighting, you shall become highly honored.'

**5.188.14**  'Even as I have said, O blessed one, all this shall come to pass: in time, after some passing of the years, you shall become a man.'

**5.188.15**  Having spoken thus, the mighty-splendored, matted-haired, bull-bannered god vanished then and there before the very eyes of the brāhmaṇas.

**5.188.16**  Then, in the sight of those great seers, the blameless maiden of fair complexion gathered firewood from that forest;

**5.188.17**  and when she had built a great pyre and set fire to it, the flames blazing up, O great king, with a mind inflamed by wrath,

**5.188.18**  crying 'For the slaying of Bhīṣma!' the eldest daughter of Kāśi entered the fire, O king, beside the river Yamunā.

**5.189.1**  Duryodhana said: How, O son of Gaṅgā, did Śikhaṇḍin, having first been a maiden, become a man, O best in battle? Tell me that, grandsire.

**5.189.2**  Bhīṣma said: The wife of that lord of earth Drupada, O king of kings, his beloved chief queen, was childless, O lord of peoples.

**5.189.3**  At that very time the lord of earth Drupada, for the sake of offspring, O great king, propitiated Śaṃkara.

**5.189.4**  Resolved upon my destruction, he undertook terrible austerity and obtained from Mahādeva a daughter, though he had prayed, 'Let a son be mine.'

**5.189.5**  'O blessed one, I desire a son, to requite myself upon Bhīṣma'—thus he spoke, and the god of gods replied: 'A woman-and-man shall be yours.'

**5.189.6**  'Turn back, O ruler of earth; this shall never be otherwise.' And he, going to the city, said this to his wife:

**5.189.7**  'A great effort was made by me, O queen, through austerity, for the sake of a son; and I have been told by Śaṃbhu, "Having been a maiden, it shall become a man."'

**5.189.8**  'Though entreated again and again, Śiva said only, "It is so destined"; for it shall be no otherwise—what must be will be even thus.'

**5.189.9**  Then that resolute woman, the wife of King Drupada, having purified herself, in her season approached Drupada.

**5.189.10**  In due time she conceived by the son of Pṛṣata, through the natural means ordained by rule, O ruler of earth, as Nārada told me.

**5.189.11**  Then the lotus-eyed queen bore that child; and King Drupada, the mighty-armed, O delight of the Kurus, out of love for the child tended his dear wife with all comfort.

**5.189.12**  Then to that sonless king Drupada, lord of earth, was born a daughter of surpassing beauty, O ruler of men.

**5.189.13**  But the illustrious wife of that sonless king Drupada gave it out, O king of kings: 'A son has been born to me.'

**5.189.14**  Then King Drupada, O ruler of men, had all the rites proper to a son performed for her, concealed as she was, as if for a son.

**5.189.15**  And the chief queen of Drupada guarded the secret with all care, declaring 'It is a son'; for none in the city knew the truth save the son of Pṛṣata.

**5.189.16**  For, trusting in the word of the god of wondrous splendor, he concealed that daughter, and declared, 'It is a man.'

**5.189.17**  The king had all the birth-rites duly performed as for a male child, complete with their proper ordinances; and they knew her as Śikhaṇḍin.

**5.189.18**  I alone came to know the truth—through my spies, through the words of Nārada, through the speech of the gods, and through the austerities of Ambā.

**5.190.1**  Bhīṣma said: Drupada took the greatest pains over all that concerned his own child; and so, O lord of kings, that child attained excellence in writing and the other arts, and in the crafts, and in archery became a pupil of Droṇa.

**5.190.2**  The fair-complexioned mother of that child, O great king, then urged the king to seek a wife for the maiden as though for a son.

**5.190.3**  Then the son of Pṛṣata, seeing that the maiden had reached her youth and knowing her to be a woman, fell into anxiety together with his wife.

**5.190.4**  Drupada said: This maiden of mine, who increases my sorrow, has come to her youth; and I have kept her concealed in obedience to the word of the trident-bearer.

**5.190.5**  That word, O great queen, can in no way prove false; for why should the maker of the three worlds make it untrue?

**5.190.6**  His wife said: If it please you, O king, I will speak; hear my words. Hearing them now, O son of Pṛṣata, you may set about your own affair.

**5.190.7**  Let a wife be duly taken for this child, O king; my settled conviction is that the word of the god will come true.

**5.190.8**  Bhīṣma said: Then the husband and wife, having made their decision in that matter, sought as bride the daughter of the lord of the Daśārṇas.

**5.190.9**  Then King Drupada, that lion among kings, having reviewed all the kings by their lineage, sought as a wife for Śikhaṇḍin the daughter of the Daśārṇaka king.

**5.190.10**  The king known as Hiraṇyavarman, that Daśārṇaka, that protector of the earth, gave his daughter to that Śikhaṇḍin.

**5.190.11**  And that king Hiraṇyavarman was a great lord of the earth among the Daśārṇas—unassailable, master of a mighty army, lofty of mind.

**5.190.12**  When the marriage had been made, O best of kings, that maiden came to her youth, and the maiden Śikhaṇḍinī likewise.

**5.190.13**  Śikhaṇḍin, now wedded, returned again to Kāmpilya; and for some time that maiden did not learn that the other was a woman.

**5.190.14**  But the daughter of Hiraṇyavarman, having found out that Śikhaṇḍinī was a woman, in shame reported to her nurses and her companions that the daughter of the Pāñcāla king, that Śikhaṇḍinī, was indeed a maiden.

**5.190.15**  Then, O tiger among kings, those Daśārṇa nurses fell into the deepest distress out of grief, and sent word.

**5.190.16**  Then the messengers reported the whole deceit, just as it had happened, to the lord of the Daśārṇas; and that king flew into a rage.

**5.190.17**  Śikhaṇḍin too, O great king, lived in the royal household after the manner of a man, full of joy, in no way relishing womanhood.

**5.190.18**  Then after a few days, O bull of the Bharatas, hearing this, King Hiraṇyavarman, O lord of kings, was driven to anguish by his fury.

**5.190.19**  Then the Daśārṇaka king, filled with fierce wrath, dispatched a messenger to the dwelling of Drupada.

**5.190.20**  Then the messenger of Kāñcanavarman, coming to Drupada, drew him aside alone and spoke in secret these words.

**5.190.21**  The Daśārṇa king, O sovereign, sends you these words, enraged at the affront, deceived by you, O blameless one:

**5.190.22**  You hold me in contempt, O king—surely your counsel has been evil—in that out of folly you asked of me my daughter for the sake of your own daughter.

**5.190.23**  Receive now the fruit of that deceit, O fool. Stand firm—I shall root you out, together with your kinsmen and your ministers.

**5.191.1**  Bhīṣma said: When Drupada was thus addressed by the messenger, O king, his speech failed him, as though he were a thief caught in the act.

**5.191.2**  He made a strenuous effort, sending word through kinsmen with soothing assurances and through messengers of honeyed speech, declaring, "This is not so."

**5.191.3**  But that king, having once more learned the truth—that the child of the Pāñcāla was a maiden—set out in haste.

**5.191.4**  Then he sent word to his allies of measureless might of the deceit practised upon his daughter, on the testimony of the nurses.

**5.191.5**  Then that best of kings, mustering his forces, resolved to march against Drupada, O Bhārata.

**5.191.6**  Then King Hiraṇyavarman, O lord of kings, took counsel with his allies concerning the Pāñcāla sovereign.

**5.191.7**  There this was resolved among those high-souled kings: "If, O king, it prove true that Śikhaṇḍinī is a maiden, we shall bind the Pāñcāla king and bring him to our homes;

**5.191.8**  and having set up another king, another lord of men, among the Pāñcālas, we shall put to death King Drupada together with Śikhaṇḍin."

**5.191.9**  Then that lord again instructed a messenger, an envoy, and dispatched him to the son of Pṛṣata: "I shall slay you—stand firm!"

**5.191.10**  And that lord of men, timid by nature and conscious of guilt, King Drupada, ruler of the earth, was seized with sharp dread.

**5.191.11**  Having dismissed the Daśārṇa messenger, Drupada, worn with grief, came to his wife in private and the king spoke these words.

**5.191.12**  Possessed by great fear and stricken to the heart with sorrow, the Pāñcāla king said to the beloved mother of Śikhaṇḍin:

**5.191.13**  "My kinsman of mighty strength, King Hiraṇyavarman, will march against me in his wrath, dragging his host along.

**5.191.14**  What shall I now do, bewildered, concerning this maiden? Śikhaṇḍin, said to be your son, is suspected to be a maiden.

**5.191.15**  Having ascertained this as the truth, with his allies and his host of followers, deeming himself deceived, he wishes, it seems, to uproot me.

**5.191.16**  What in this is true, O fair-hipped one, and what is false? Tell me, O lovely one. Having heard your word, O auspicious one, I shall act accordingly.

**5.191.17**  For I am fallen into doubt, and this Śikhaṇḍinī is but a child; and you too, O queen, fair of hue, have come into great straits.

**5.191.18**  Tell me then the truth, as I ask, for our deliverance from all this, so that I may take measures in this matter, O fair-hipped, bright-smiling one; and do not fear for Śikhaṇḍin—I shall surely make provision there.

**5.191.19**  By my own deed, O lovely one, I have been deceived as to the office of a son; and the Daśārṇaka king too, that lord of the earth, has been deceived by me. So tell me, O blessed one, and I shall do in this what is best.

**5.191.20**  Though the king knew the truth, he urged it openly so as to make it plain to the other; and the queen replied to the lord of the earth.

**5.192.1**  Bhīṣma said: Then the mother of Śikhaṇḍin, O ruler of men, O mighty-armed one, declared the truth to her husband—that Śikhaṇḍinī was a maiden:

**5.192.2**  "Being without a son, O king, and through fear of my co-wives, I declared this maiden Śikhaṇḍinī, when she was born, to be a male.

**5.192.3**  And you, O best of men, approved this of me out of love; the rites of a son were performed for the maiden, O bull among kings, and a wife was taken for her by you, O king—the daughter of the lord of the Daśārṇas.

**5.192.4**  And it was you who said before, considering the meaning of the god's word, 'Having been a maiden, she shall become a man'—and so this was let be.

**5.192.5**  Hearing this, Drupada Yajñasena, O king, laid the whole truth before his counsellors versed in deliberation, and took counsel as to whatever was fitting for the protection of his people.

**5.192.6**  And having weighed the kinship-bond with that Daśārṇaka king, O lord of men—since he had himself wrought the deceit as it had happened—intent upon his counsel, he came to a resolve.

**5.192.7**  In that time of peril, O Bhārata, the king secured on every side his city—naturally well guarded and fully arrayed for defence, O lord of kings.

**5.192.8**  And the king, together with his wife, fell into the deepest distress over the strife with the lord of the Daśārṇas, O bull of the Bharatas.

**5.192.9**  "How may there be no great quarrel between me and my kinsman?"—thus reflecting in his mind, he then worshipped the gods.

**5.192.10**  Seeing him then, O king, devoted to the gods and engaged in worship, the queen, his wife, spoke these words.

**5.192.11**  "Reverence for the gods is ever true and esteemed by the good; and it may avail us, who have fallen into an ocean of grief, if we worship them earnestly.

**5.192.12**  Let all the gods be honoured with abundant gifts, and let the sacred fires too be fed with oblations, for the turning back of the Daśārṇa.

**5.192.13**  And ponder in your mind, O lord, his turning back without battle; by the grace of the gods all this shall come to pass.

**5.192.14**  And what has been deliberated by your ministers together with you, O wide-eyed one, for the saving of this city, O king, that too carry out accordingly.

**5.192.15**  For the divine, joined with human effort, succeeds full well, O king; but from the mutual opposition of the two there is no success at all."

**5.192.16**  "Therefore, having made arrangements in the city together with your counsellors, worship the gods to your heart's content, O lord of peoples."

**5.192.17**  Seeing the two thus conversing, given over wholly to grief, the maiden Śikhaṇḍinī, high-minded, was as if overcome with shame.

**5.192.18**  Then she thought, "It is on my account that these two are grieving"; and thereupon she set her mind upon ending her life.

**5.192.19**  Having thus made her resolve, utterly given over to grief, she left the palace and went to a dense, deserted forest,

**5.192.20**  guarded, O king, by a yakṣa rich in power, Sthūṇākarṇa; and for very dread of him men shun that forest.

**5.192.21**  There stood the dwelling of Sthūṇa, plastered with lime and clay, rich with the smoke of parched grain and frankincense, with high ramparts and gateway-arches.

**5.192.22**  Entering it, Śikhaṇḍin, the child of Drupada, O king, took no food for many days and wasted away her body.

**5.192.23**  The yakṣa Sthūṇa, gracious of eye, showed himself to her: "For what purpose is this endeavour of yours? I shall accomplish it—tell me, do not delay."

**5.192.24**  "It cannot be done," she said to the yakṣa again and again; and the guhyaka answered her, "I shall do it."

**5.192.25**  "I am a follower of the lord of wealth and a bestower of boons, O daughter of a king; even what may not be given, I shall give. Speak what you wish to ask."

**5.192.26**  Then Śikhaṇḍin laid the whole matter, entire, before that chief of yakṣas, Sthūṇākarṇa, O Bhārata:

**5.192.27**  "My father is in peril, O yakṣa; before long he will perish, for the lord of the Daśārṇas in his wrath will march against him—

**5.192.28**  a king of great might and great vigour, clad in golden armour. Therefore save me, O yakṣa, and my father and my mother.

**5.192.29**  For you have promised to take away my sorrow. Let me become a man, O yakṣa, blameless, by your grace.

**5.192.30**  Only until that king draws near my city—until then, O great yakṣa, O guhyaka, show me your favour."

**5.193.1**  Bhīṣma said: Hearing the words of Śikhaṇḍin, O bull of the Bharatas, that yakṣa, pressed by fate, reflected in his mind and spoke—for it was destined to be, and to my own sorrow, O Kaurava:

**5.193.2**  "Gentle one, I shall do your will, but mark my condition. For a certain space of time I shall give you this my own manhood; but you must return at the appointed time. This I tell you truly.

**5.193.3**  I am a lord whose will is fulfilled, a sky-ranger who can take any form at pleasure. By my grace save your city and all your kinsmen entire.

**5.193.4**  I shall bear your womanhood, O daughter of a king. Promise me truly, and I shall do you this kindness."

**5.193.5**  Śikhaṇḍin said: "I shall give back to you this manhood again, O blessed one; for a certain space of time bear my womanhood, O night-ranger.

**5.193.6**  When the Dāśārṇa king Hemavarman has gone back again, I shall become a maiden once more, and you shall become a man.

**5.193.7**  Bhīṣma said: Having spoken thus, the two of them, O king, made a pact there—that they would do no harm to one another—and then they exchanged their forms.

**5.193.8**  The yakṣa Sthūṇa took on the female sign, O lord of men, and Śikhaṇḍin received that blazing form of the yakṣa.

**5.193.9**  Then Śikhaṇḍin of the Pāñcālas, having attained manhood, O king, entered the city rejoicing, came to his father, and recounted to Drupada all that had befallen, just as it had happened.

**5.193.10**  When Drupada heard this from him, he conceived the greatest joy, and together with his wife he then called to mind the words of Maheśvara.

**5.193.11**  Then he sent word to the lord of the Daśārṇas, O king: "This son of mine is a man—let your honour believe me in this."

**5.193.12**  Thereupon the Dāśārṇa king came at once against Drupada, the king of the Pāñcālas, filled with grief and indignation.

**5.193.13**  Then, having reached Kāmpilya, the lord of the Daśārṇas, with due honour, dispatched as his envoy the foremost of those learned in the Veda.

**5.193.14**  "Say in my name, O envoy, to that Pāñcāla, the basest of kings: 'Since you, O evil-minded one, chose a maiden for the sake of my daughter, this very day you shall see the fruit of that arrogance—of this there is no doubt.'"

**5.193.15**  Thus addressed by him, O best of kings, that brāhmaṇa envoy, urged on by the Dāśārṇa king, set out for the city.

**5.193.16**  Then the chaplain came upon Drupada within the city, and the Pāñcāla king, O lord of kings, offered him a cow and the welcome-water, well honoured, together with Śikhaṇḍin.

**5.193.17**  He did not welcome that homage, but spoke these words, the very words that had been uttered by that heroic king Kāñcanavarman:

**5.193.18**  "Since I, O man of base conduct, have been deceived by you in the matter of my daughter, reap now the fruit of that sin you have wrought, O evil-minded one!"

**5.193.19**  "Give me battle, O king, this day at the front of the fray; at once I shall uproot you, together with your counsellors, your sons, and your kinsmen."

**5.193.20**  That speech, fraught with reproach, the king was made to hear, in the midst of his counsellors, from the chaplain who was envoy of the Daśārṇa lord.
